# Front Cover

->**[Exterior of the Volume]**<-

The manuscript is bound in a traditional style featuring **marbled paper** covers. The paper displays dark, swirling patterns—reminiscent of flowing ink or stone veins—set against a muted tan background <note>Paper marbling is a decorative technique achieved by floating pigments on water and transferring them to paper; it was a standard choice for durable yet attractive book covers in the 18th and 19th centuries.</note>.

The spine and the corners are reinforced with **worn, dark brown leather**. This "half-leather" binding shows significant signs of age and handling, particularly along the edges where the leather has softened and scuffed over time. There are no titles or inscriptions visible on the front of this volume.

--- Page 11 ---
->P R E F A C E.<-

While my work on **Eunapius** <note>Eunapius of Sardis (c. 347–414 AD) was a Greek sophist and historian; the author is likely referring to a critical edition of Eunapius's *Lives of the Philosophers*.</note> lingers under the **Leiden presses**, <note>original: "prelis Leidensibus." Leiden, in the Netherlands, was a world-renowned center for scholarly printing and home to one of Europe's oldest universities.</note> I turned my thoughts toward publishing another writer of philosophical history—and a notable one at that—**Marinus**, <note>Marinus of Neapolis was a 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who succeeded Proclus as head of the Academy in Athens.</note> who composed the *Life of Proclus* with considerable diligence. Since the time of **Fabricius**, <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a monumental German bibliographer whose work cataloged nearly all known classical literature.</note> no one had deemed Marinus worthy of a new edition. 

To make this plan more appealing to learned men, I intended to place Marinus’s short work as a preface to an unpublished commentary by **Proclus** <note>Proclus Lycius (412–485 AD) was the "successor" to Plato's Academy and one of the most influential philosophers of Late Antiquity.</note> on the ***Cratylus***, <note>One of Plato's dialogues, focusing on the philosophy of language and whether names are natural or conventional.</note> which I had transcribed for my own study. This follows the exact method once used by **Portus**, <note>Aemilius Portus (1550–1614), a famous scholar and editor of Greek texts.</note> who published this small book by Marinus as a precursor to Proclus’s *Platonic Theology*. 

However, Portus devoted almost no care to Marinus—whose work was not even circulating in its complete form at that time—whereas I have devoted a great deal. This project was shared with the publisher **Weigel** <note>Johann August Gottlob Weigel (1773–1846) was a prominent Leipzig book dealer and publisher specializing in the classics.</note> through my friend **Bast** <note>Friedrich Jakob Bast (1771–1811) was a diplomat and scholar of Greek manuscripts whose early death was a significant loss to the academic community.</note>—whose company I then enjoyed, but whose recent death I now deeply mourn. Weigel was immediately pleased with the plan. For to him, indeed...

<margin>8</margin>

<summary>In this preface, the editor explains his decision to publish a new, corrected edition of Marinus's biography of the philosopher Proclus as a companion to Proclus's own commentary on Plato's Cratylus. He notes that previous editions, such as those by Portus, were neglected or incomplete, and he pays tribute to his late friend and collaborator, Friedrich Jakob Bast.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus of Neapolis, Proclus, Life of Proclus, Cratylus, Aemilius Portus, Johann Albert Fabricius, Friedrich Jakob Bast, Leiden printing, Neoplatonism, classical scholarship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the publication of a philosophical text, specifically mentioning a change in plans involving a colleague named Weigel and an upcoming edition of Plato.</meta>

...be given as a companion, with which that adorned
edition of Plato he is planning will soon appear. Regarding this
change in our plans, Weigel <note>original: "Weigelius"; likely Erhard Weigel (1625–1699), a prominent German mathematician and teacher of Leibniz</note> revealed to me
what he thought, and I easily allowed myself to be
persuaded, as I could not bear to deny anything to
such an honorable man; and so Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis (5th century AD), the successor to Proclus; his biography of Proclus was often edited during this period</note> enters
alone, intending to invite readers to himself only by his
own merits—which are not very
conspicuous—and by the notes of his editor, which I wish
were more learned. Otherwise, in this part of my labor,
I have by no means neglected what depended on diligence.
In order to <term>review</term> <note>original: "recenserem"; refers to the process of "recension," where a scholar compares different versions of a text to fix errors</note> the author's words, I diligently [sought out] all
the aids I could from books, both printed
and handwritten.

<summary>The editor explains a shift in publication strategy, deciding to publish the work of Marinus separately from a planned edition of Plato following advice from Weigel. He expresses modesty regarding his own scholarly notes but emphasizes his diligent use of both printed and manuscript sources.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Marinus of Neapolis, Erhard Weigel, philology, textual criticism, book history, manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>The text is the ninth page of a preface (Praefatio) to a scholarly edition, likely of a classical text. The author is discussing the various historical editions they consulted to establish their own text.</meta>

-> P R E F A C E <-
IX

...compared. I have consulted editions—not all of them, certainly, but the more distinguished ones: the Zurich edition, the Hamburg edition by Portus <note>Aemilius Portus (1550–1614), a noted Greek scholar.</note>, the Lyon edition of 1626, the Fabrician edition, and the London edition, among which the Fabrician <note>Referring to the work of Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a prolific German bibliographer and scholar.</note> is preeminent. The Zurich edition of 1558 is the first of all, and it includes a Latin translation by an anonymous translator, along with twelve short notes by the same author. These notes are indeed of little importance, but I thought they should be reprinted lest the memory of them be lost. In fact, I believe they have only been printed twice: first in this extremely rare Zurich volume, and then in a Lyon edition of the Latin translation published in 1559 without the original text, which is no less rare. Since I could find nothing about this anonymous translator, I approached Morelli <note>Jacopo Morelli (1745–1819), the influential director of the Marciana Library in Venice.</note>, the most distinguished prefect of the Marciana Library and a leading authority in literary history. From him, I received this reply:

"I do not know who the Latin translator was, nor did Fabricius. It seems he achieved the result he desired through his efforts to hide his name; for Conrad Gessner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565) was a Swiss naturalist and bibliographer, often called the father of modern bibliography.</note> testifies in a Letter of 1562 to William Turner <note>William Turner (c. 1508–1568) was an English naturalist and theologian.</note> regarding the books he himself edited, which was printed in Zurich in 1566 along with his biography..."

<summary>The author lists the primary historical editions used for this work, highlighting a rare 1558 Zurich edition with an anonymous Latin translation and notes. He consults the librarian Morelli to attempt to identify the mystery translator, referencing the bibliographical records of Conrad Gessner.</summary>
<keywords>Zurich edition, Fabrician edition, bibliography, Jacopo Morelli, Conrad Gessner, book history, Latin translations, Marciana Library</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's chronological account of the publication history of Marinus’s biography of Proclus, following previous discussions of early Renaissance editions.</meta>

x                                  ->P R E F A C E.<-

“On the authority of Josiah Simmler <note>A Swiss scholar and biographer of Conrad Gessner</note> in his life of Gessner, he gave the books of Antoninus <note>The Emperor Marcus Aurelius, often referred to as Antoninus in older scholarly texts</note> and Marinus to his cousin Andreas to be printed in the year 1558, together with Latin translations—specifically, those of Wilhelm Xylander for the books of Antoninus, but for Marinus’s *Life of Proclus*, those of a certain friend of ours, a very learned young man who, out of modesty, did not wish his name to be expressed.” <note>original: "Gesneri auctore Iofia Simlero... nomen ſuum exprimi noluit."</note> Portus placed the Zurich <note>original: "Tigurenſem"</note> text and translation at the head of Proclus’s *Platonic Theology*, which he published in Hamburg in the year 1618, and, as I mentioned above, he spared himself any further labor <note>meaning he did not perform any new editorial work or corrections</note>. Nor was the diligence of the Lyons bookseller, Francis de la Bottière, any greater; he saw to the printing of Marinus in the year 1626. But he who followed in the year 1700, Fabricius, managed the matter better, though not perfectly. Since Marinus’s book was circulating in the hands of the public in a state where nearly half of it was missing, he supplied what was lacking for its completeness from a transcript of the Gudian manuscript <note>A significant manuscript from the collection of Marquard Gude, now held in Wolfenbüttel</note>, a copy of which Scherzer had provided to him; he translated this supplement into Latin, and added notes and an introduction. Yet the negligence of this otherwise incomparable man in this minor work was extreme; he carelessly omitted many things, and provided very absurd—

<summary>This page details the early printing history of Marinus’s "Life of Proclus," tracking how various 16th and 17th-century editors and booksellers handled the text before Fabricius attempted to complete it using new manuscript findings.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus of Neapolis, Proclus, Johann Albert Fabricius, printing history, philology, Wilhelm Xylander, Gudian manuscript, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page (XI) continues the author's preface, moving from a critique of previous printed editions to the description of the manuscript evidence used for this new edition of Marinus.</meta>

-> P R E F A C E. <-                                          11

...[various] <note>original: "-simas", the suffix of a word from the previous page, likely "pessimas" (worst) or "dubias" (doubtful)</note> readings he either proposed or carelessly left behind. I note this not to detract from the praise of that most excellent man <note>referring to the scholar Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736)</note>, for such envy is far from my nature, but so that I might speak the truth and find my readers more forgiving when I myself err, since even the great Fabricius was not free from fault. No one, as far as I know, has approached the work of Marinus since Fabricius; for the London edition of 1703 is a mere repetition of Fabricius’s. From this review of editions, one can see that only two can offer any critical use to a scholar: the first Zurich edition <note>The *editio princeps* published in 1559</note> and that of Fabricius.

I turned to manuscripts <term>codices: hand-written books from the era before printing</term>, from which I hoped for greater support; and I immediately began to consider the **Gudianus** manuscript, which Fabricius had used and which the Wolfenbüttel Library <note>The *Bibliotheca Guelferbytana*, now the Herzog August Library in Germany</note> now possesses. G. H. Schaefer, whom I am both happy and proud to call my friend, voluntarily took upon himself the labor of collation <term>collation: the line-by-line comparison of different manuscripts to identify variations in the text</term> and sent to me a variety of readings extracted with wonderful diligence, interspersed here and there with brief notes, which I have included here. This manuscript brought excellent help to Marinus; for not a few things that either Scherzer or Fabricius had written incorrectly were more accurately preserved in the original writing— <note>The text cuts off mid-word at "ha-", likely completing as "habentur" (are held/contained) on the following page</note>

<summary>The author evaluates previous editions of Marinus, noting that most are derivative, and introduces the Gudianus manuscript as a vital primary source for his new, more accurate version.</summary>

<keywords>Marinus, Johann Albert Fabricius, G. H. Schaefer, Wolfenbüttel Library, Gudianus codex, textual criticism, philology, manuscript collation</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This is page XII of the preface. It continues a discussion regarding the provenance and classification of specific Greek manuscripts.</meta>

->XII                                  P R E F A C E.<-

...possess. The Wolfenbüttel manuscript <note>original: "Guelferbytanus codex"</note> is marked as *number 78 Greek*; the paper is in quarto format <note>A book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</note>, the paper itself is of the common variety, and the handwriting is very recent. "Unless I am completely mistaken," (these are the words of the scholar Schaefer) "Gudius himself wrote this while traveling, perhaps following an Italian manuscript. What if it was the Medici manuscript?" This conjecture is most certainly true. Indeed, on the first leaf <note>original: "folio"</note> the following may be read: *Shelf <note>original: "Scamn." for "Scamnum," a bench or shelf</note> LXXXVI. Number 3. A manuscript on cotton paper <note>original: "charta bombycina," a type of paper made from cotton or silk fibers rather than wood pulp</note> in quarto, and it is preceded by Iamblichus's 'On the Life of Pythagoras,' written excellently and with corrections;* and the Medici manuscript—which the most learned Francesco de Furia, prefect of the Laurentian and Marucellian Libraries, most kindly provided for my use—is marked as *Case <note>original: "Plut." for "Pluteus," a combination of a bookshelf and a reading desk</note> LXXXVI. manuscript III.* and, among other things, it contains Iamblichus's work on Pythagoras. It is now clear that the Gudian book is a copy <term>apographum|A manuscript transcribed directly from an original or another copy</term> of the Medici manuscript: a fact which the editor of the Gudian catalog had also previously noted on page 5.

<summary>This page details the bibliographical detective work used to prove that a manuscript in the Wolfenbüttel collection is actually a direct copy of a specific manuscript held in the Medici library in Florence.</summary>
<keywords>Wolfenbüttel, Medici manuscript, Gudius, Francesco de Furia, Iamblichus, codicology, provenance, quarto, cotton paper</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's discussion of manuscript sources, specifically citing the catalog of Lucas Holste to establish the provenance and quality of a key manuscript containing works by Iamblichus and Marinus.</meta>

# PREFACE 
-> XIII <-

"...these words of Holstenius <note>Lucas Holste (1596–1661), a prominent German humanist and Vatican librarian.</note> appear in the catalog of those manuscripts—which I acquired long ago from the Burmann library <note>The library of Pieter Burman (1668–1741), a famous Dutch classical scholar.</note> along with another manuscript by Wilhelm Lang: ***Shelf LXXXVI, no. 3. Iamblichus’s On the Pythagorean Life and Philosophy, four books of which survive out of the nine, as appears from the index prefixed to this work; in large quarto. Attached to this is the Life of Proclus of Lycia by his successor, Marinus, of which only half had been published. This manuscript is the best and most accurate of all those I have seen (and I have seen and compared many), a fact which will eventually be proven by the work itself.*** This is the authentic text of Holstenius, not the corrupted and mutilated version that Lilienthal <note>Michael Lilienthal (1686–1750), a Prussian theologian and historian.</note> presented in his *Historical and Literary Selections*, printed in 1715 at Königsberg <note>Original: *Regiomonti*.</note> and Leipzig, Volume I, page 112."

I offer the greatest possible thanks, as is only right, to Furia <note>Francesco Furia (1777–1856), the librarian of the Laurentian Library in Florence.</note>, a most generous man, for his willingness to support my studies with such excellent resources; nor shall I leave unmentioned the man he took as a partner in his labor, Gasparo Bencini, a man most profoundly learned in Greek. I was likewise fortunate to experience the ready assistance of Giuseppe Vernazza, the most celebrated citizen of Turin— <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>The author quotes the scholar Lucas Holste to identify a high-quality manuscript of Iamblichus and Marinus, correcting a previous error by Lilienthal. He also expresses gratitude to contemporary collaborators Francesco Furia and Gasparo Bencini for their assistance.</summary>
<keywords>Lucas Holste, Iamblichus, Marinus, Francesco Furia, Gasparo Bencini, manuscripts, Pythagoras, Proclus, bibliography, philology</keywords>

XIV

->PREFACE<-

...the goodwill of the library's prefect. Since the Turin manuscript could not be physically brought for comparison <note>original: "conferri," a technical term for the scholarly collation or line-by-line comparison of two texts</note> due to the lack of the Fabricius edition, the prefect arranged for the entire work to be traced from start to finish <note>original: "a capite ad calcem," literally "from head to heel"</note> onto translucent paper. This was done so that I could work with a *facsimile* of the entire manuscript (as we call it now), or rather, so that I might handle it as if it were the original manuscript itself. I owe this miracle of amazing industry to a young man, Carlo Giuseppe Ludovico Costa of Castelnuovo Scrivia, who cultivates both the arts and literature with an equal zeal, of which I have seen excellent examples. 

This Turin manuscript is therefore described in the *Catalogue of Manuscripts of the Royal Turin University*, Volume 1, page 214, in these words (among others which I omit for the sake of brevity): *Manuscript 107. Paper, 16th century, consisting of 81 leaves, formerly belonging to Andreas Darmarius.* 

Hase <note>Carl Benedict Hase (1780–1864), a renowned German philologist and librarian in Paris</note>, whose scholarship is admired by all who are themselves learned, indicated to me that he believes the name should be read as *Darbarius*, not *Darmarius*. He further informed me that Andreas Darbarius, the <term>protospatharius</term> <note>A high-ranking Byzantine court title, originally meaning the "first sword-bearer" to the Emperor</note> of Andronikos the Younger, possessed many manuscript books. Quite a few of these, marked with his name written in his own hand, are now held in the Paris Library...

<meta>This page continues the editor's discussion of the manuscript sources used for this edition of Marinus’s work, specifically focusing on the age and quality of the texts found in Paris.</meta>

->P R E F A C E.<-                                        xv

...are preserved. If these observations are correct, it follows that this manuscript is two centuries older. The final judgment will rest with those who are able to inspect the manuscript themselves. 

I have obtained two manuscripts from the Parisian Library: one is the *Coislinianus* <note>original: "Coiſlinianum"; this refers to the famous Coislin collection of Greek manuscripts</note>, a source of the highest quality, written on parchment <term>parchment: animal skin prepared for writing, often more durable and prestigious than paper</term> and dating—according to the index of Montfaucon <note>Bernard de Montfaucon (1655–1741) was a Benedictine monk and a founding figure in the study of ancient handwriting</note>—to approximately the 10th century. 

The other is the *Vatican* manuscript, no. 1197, written on paper in the 16th century; like all editions produced before that of Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a massive figure in classical bibliography whose work the editor is updating here</note>, it is incomplete, ending at the twenty-second chapter. 

These were the resources at my disposal for reconstructing the words of Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis, a 5th-century philosopher and biographer of Proclus</note>. Whether I have used them skillfully, let the learned judge. I do not concern myself with others; I did not write for them, and if they mock the care bestowed upon these critical matters, I do not envy them their "wisdom." 

In my own observations, I have included those of Fabricius in addition to those of the Latin translator mentioned above. I did this because Fabricius’s book is extremely rare, and I feared that if I simply referred readers to it, they might not be able to find a copy. This was also the reason why I saw to it that the *Prolegomena* <term>Prolegomena: a scholarly introduction or preliminary treatise</term> of that distinguished man were printed once again. Furthermore, they are most worthy of reading, and contain many learned discussions regarding Marinus and Proclus...

<summary>The editor describes the two primary manuscripts used for this edition—the 10th-century parchment Coislinianus and a 16th-century paper Vatican manuscript—while defending the inclusion of rare notes by the scholar Fabricius.</summary>

XVI                ->P R A E F A T I O<-
<note>original: "PRAEFATIO" — Preface</note>

...I found things which, against my will, have given me the leisure to run out into this field. As for the fact that I have repeated the Latin version from the Fabrician edition <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a monumental German scholar known for his bibliographies of Greek and Latin literature.</note>, I have indeed done something hardly worthy of this current age, in which such aids for reading are looked down upon; it is especially out of place for this particular author, whom almost only the most learned men will desire to know. But if there is any fault in this, I lay it entirely upon the excellent Weigel <note>Johann August Gottlob Weigel (1773–1846), a prominent Leipzig publisher and bookseller.</note>, who thought an addition of this kind would be most useful; and, although he did not convince me as he did regarding Proclus's *Cratylus* <note>Proclus was a Neoplatonist philosopher; his commentary on Plato's "Cratylus" explores the nature of language.</note>, he nonetheless followed his own judgment.

Before I send my readers on to Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis was a philosopher and the biographer of his teacher, Proclus.</note> and my own notes, I wish to inform them that Schaefer <note>Gottfried Heinrich Schaefer (1764–1840), a renowned classical scholar known for his editorial precision.</note>, such is his great kindness, did not refuse the burden of reading the proof-sheets as they came off the press. He corrected them with the utmost accuracy—certainly more accurately than I could have done myself using my own eyes. 

Written at Paris, October 1813.

# JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS
## L E T T E R
### TO
### RICHARD BENTLEY.

->TO THE REVEREND AND MOST DISTINGUISHED MAN<-
->RICHARD BENTLEY,<-
->THE VARRO OF ENGLAND,<-
<note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE) was considered Rome's greatest scholar. Calling Bentley the "Varro of England" is a high compliment to his immense learning.</note>
->JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS SENDS GREETINGS.<-
<note>The original Latin "S." stands for "Salutem," a standard abbreviation for "sends greetings."</note>

While many have promised to publish the complete *Life of Proclus* <note>Proclus (412–485 CE) was a major Neoplatonist philosopher; this biography was written by his student Marinus.</note> and even more have wished to read it, I am now finally bringing it forth into the light of day under a lucky star. I felt it should be dedicated to no one more than to you. I have long admired your great learning and have been taught many excellent things from your writings published in both your native <note>original: "patrio," referring to English</note> and the Latin language; therefore, I wanted there to exist some public testimony of my respect for you. 

Furthermore, you seem to me to be a man of such great kindness that it would be a waste of words for me to try to excuse the modest nature of this gift. I myself am so far removed from flattery and rashness that I do not think I should burden you with lengthy praises at this time, yet I shall always hold your name in high regard. Farewell, most learned man, and continue to serve the cause of literature excellently, as you already do. 

Written at Hamburg, on the day before the Nones of April, in the Christian year 1700. <note>April 4th, 1700.</note>

<summary>A formal dedicatory letter from the German bibliographer Johann Albert Fabricius to the renowned English scholar Richard Bentley, presenting his new edition of the biography of the philosopher Proclus.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Albert Fabricius, Richard Bentley, Proclus, Neoplatonism, classical scholarship, Hamburg, 18th-century correspondence</keywords>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSE
## BY JOHANNES ALBERTUS FABRICIUS
->A 3<-

Before I release you, Reader—whoever you are, learned and kind—to the Philosopher himself, who is described for you in this present little book, a few things must be said beforehand, which perhaps it would not be out of place for you to be advised of. The first part of the *Life of Proclus* <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a hugely influential Neoplatonist philosopher who headed the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>, which you see, written by Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis was Proclus’s student and successor.</note>, saw the light of day long ago, published by *) Wilhelm Xylander <note>A German classical scholar (1532–1576) known for translating many Greek texts into Latin.</note>, a most excellent and learned man. By him, along with a translation by I know not which author, it was appended to the moral precepts of Marcus Antoninus <note>original: "Marci Antonini," referring to the Emperor Marcus Aurelius and his *Meditations*.</note>, with which that "Emperor of Philosophers" once wished to make himself more diligent in the pursuit of virtue. Aemilius Portus prefixed the same first part, with the same translation, to Proclus's six books **) *On Platonic Theology*. Since that time, both Lucas Holstenius, a preeminent man, and many other learned men within our memory and that of our fathers, having obtained the second and most excellent part—which is missing from the editions of Xylander and Portus—gave hope that it would be published. Since, however, whether by fate or other causes, they were prevented from being able to keep their promises, I hoped that I would find all the more favor among learned men with this edition, as it fulfills the long-held wishes of so many for this Ma— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Marinus," likely continuing on the next page.</note>

***

*) In Greek and Latin. Zurich, 1599, octavo. <note>original: "8." refers to the book size *octavo*, created by folding a sheet three times.</note> [Compare *Greek Bibliography*, volume 9, page 363.]

**) Hamburg, 1618, folio. <note>original: "fol." refers to a large book size made by folding a sheet once.</note> Besides these editions of the *Life of Proclus*, the Bodleian catalog <note>The catalog of the Bodleian Library at Oxford.</note> mentions also a Lyon edition from the year 1626, in octavo. [This edition does indeed exist. Marinus is appended to the *Commentaries* of Marcus Antoninus, with the text and translation repeated from Xylander's edition; but the notes of the Latin translator were omitted.]

--- Page 23 ---
# PREFACE BY JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS. XIX

I realized that the complete text written by Marinus had long been desired. Therefore, when *Heinrich Volckmar Scherzer*—son of the celebrated theologian of blessed memory <note>original Greek: τοῦ μακαρίτου</note>—shared with me several months ago a Greek transcript of the unpublished portion from the excellent Gudian manuscript [which the Wolfenbüttel library now possesses], I immediately took it upon myself to translate it into Latin and publish it. I thought it unworthy that such an outstanding monument of antiquity should struggle longer in the darkness, even if it is sprinkled here and there with certain unbelievable stories. 

I have kept the old translation of the first part, except in a few places where the previous translator seemed to me to wander too far from Marinus’s meaning. In those cases, I have seen fit to correct it, as I carefully point out in the brief notes I have added to the entire booklet. Finally, I have appended a catalog of Proclus's writings *), as a small sample of the *Greek Library* <note>The *Bibliotheca Graeca*, Fabricius's massive multi-volume bibliography of ancient Greek literature.</note>, which I have long been preparing and organizing for the public use of students, and which I have decided to send to the printing presses as soon as possible, God willing. Now I will provide a few introductory remarks, first regarding Marinus, then about the lineage, age, teachers, and successors of Proclus, and finally about the seven levels of virtues in which Marinus affirms and celebrates that his teacher excelled.

## I. REGARDING MARINUS OF NEAPOLIS.

The author of this biography, Marinus, was a disciple of Proclus and his successor in the chair <term>Athenian chair: the headship of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens</term> at Athens. He was also the teacher of Isidore **) in both Aristotelian philosophy and mathematical disciplines. His homeland was Neapolis <note>Now Nablus</note>, specifically Flavia Neapolis in Samaria or Palestine, near Mount ***) Gerizim †),

*) [This Catalog has been omitted in this edition. It can now be read in a more extensive and accurate form in the *Greek Library*, vol. IX, p. 373.]
**) See Photius, pages 231 and 550 of Hoeschel's edition. <note>Photius was a 9th-century Byzantine scholar and patriarch whose "Bibliotheca" preserved summaries of many lost ancient books.</note>
***) The same, page 562.
†) [Compare the *Bordeaux Itinerary*, edited by Wesseling, p. 587.]

->b 2<-

<summary>Johann Albert Fabricius introduces his new edition and translation of the "Life of Proclus" by Marinus, noting his use of a rare Greek manuscript from Wolfenbüttel. He begins a biographical sketch of Marinus, a Neoplatonist philosopher from Samaria who succeeded Proclus in Athens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion (Prolegomena) regarding the life and identity of Marinus of Neapolis, the successor to the philosopher Proclus. It focuses on distinguishing "our" Marinus from several other historical figures sharing the same name.</meta>

xxx PREFACE

Regarding the city <note>original: "de qua urbe"; likely referring to Neapolis/Nablus</note> of this Marinus, one should consult Henry Noris in his fifth dissertation on the *Epochs of the Syro-Macedonians*, and Jean Hardouin in his *Ancient Coins of Peoples and Cities Illustrated*. There appears to have been another Marinus—or as he is called by Justinian <insert>in Edict 13</insert> and Zonaras <insert>XIV. p. 55. C.</insert>, **Marianus**—who was a Praetorian Prefect <term>Praetorian Prefect: One of the most powerful administrative and judicial officials in the later Roman Empire</term> under the Emperor Anastasius. Although this Marinus is also called "the Syrian" by Evagrius <insert>III. 43.</insert>, John Malalas <insert>XVI. p. 122.</insert>, and Nicephorus <insert>XV. final chapter</insert>, and it is reported by many that he made use of the works of Proclus the Philosopher in a naval battle against the usurper <note>original: "Tyrannum"</note> Vitalian. However, we show below that this Proclus was a different person from our subject and lived later; furthermore, the incomparable Valesius <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French classical scholar</note> correctly observes and distinguishes between the two in his notes on chapter 42 of Evagrius’s third book, noting that the Praetorian Prefect was a Christian by religion, while our Marinus, the philosopher and rhetorician we are discussing, was a pagan.

There were also others famous by this name, such as **Marinus of Tyre**, whose *Geography* is praised and corrected throughout his own geographical work by Claudius Ptolemy <insert>cf. Greek Library vol. V. p. 272.</insert>. Likewise, there was **Marinus the Physician**, whose twenty books on anatomy Galen reports he summarized in a four-volume work <insert>in his book "On His Own Books"</insert> *). To say nothing of **Marinus of Apsilia** <insert>cf. Greek Library vol. IX. p. 371.</insert>, whom Theophanes mentions under Leo the Isaurian, and **Marinus, Bishop of Beirut**, praised by Evagrius in Book 3, Chapter 33, as well as several others mentioned by ecclesiastical writers and more recent authors. But regarding our Marinus, the author of the *Suda* <note>original: "Suidas"; a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> states that he wrote the *Life of Proclus* in both prose and verse. Furthermore, I find no one among the ancient writers who mentions this poetic version of Proclus's life by Marinus; it is so far lost that it has not reached us at all. The prose version, which he pursued and which alone has survived the ages, seems to have been composed in the first year **) after the death of Proclus.

***

*) Vol. IV. Greek edition of Basel, p. 364. <insert>cf. Greek Library vol. IX. p. 372.</insert>. Posthumius Marinus, a physician, is also mentioned in Pliny’s *Letters*, Book X, Letter 6.
**) That is, around the year of Christ 486.

<summary>This page distinguishes Marinus of Neapolis, the Neoplatonist biographer of Proclus, from other historical figures named Marinus, including a Christian prefect, a geographer, and a physician. It notes that while Marinus wrote about Proclus in both verse and prose, only the prose biography survives, likely written around 486 AD.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus of Neapolis, Proclus, Neoplatonism, Praetorian Prefect, Suda, Vitalian, ancient geography, history of medicine, 5th century</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion by Johannes Albertus Fabricius regarding the life and works of the Neoplatonist philosopher Marinus of Neapolis, specifically focusing on his biography of his teacher, Proclus.</meta>

->JOHANNES ALBERTUS FABRICIUS, SECTION 21<-

having passed, as we observe on page 92 [chapter 37]. Right at the beginning, Marinus says he felt it a religious duty <note>Marinus viewed his philosophical biography as a sacred task, reflecting the late Neoplatonic view of philosophers as "divine" figures.</note> that he alone among the many disciples of such a great man should not remain silent, nor fail to comment on the praises of his master. From this, one may rightly gather that other students of Proclus also celebrated the fame of the deceased with published writings. Indeed, it cannot be denied that Marinus himself applied much talent and diligence in handing down the life of his teacher. Thus, the author of the Greek epigram—which we have restored from the *Anthology* <note>The *Greek Anthology*, a collection of poems from across the classical and Byzantine periods.</note> on the last page of this booklet *)—rightly declares Proclus blessed for having such a fortunate herald of his virtues. 

But Marinus had also written other works, some of which we can commemorate here. They are as follows:

1. *Philosophical Inquiries* <note>original: Ζητήματα φιλοσόφων (Zētēmata philosophōn); Latin: Quaestiones Philosophorum</note>, which are mentioned in the *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note>. And perhaps it deserves the assent of the learned, who think that the entry for Marinus in the books of the *Suda*—which currently reads: *“He wrote the life of Proclus his teacher both in prose and in verse, and certain other philosophical inquiries”* <note>original: ἔγραψε βίον Πρόκλου τοῦ αὐτοῦ διδασκάλου καὶ καταλογάδην καὶ ἐπικῶς καὶ ἄλλα τινὰ φιλοσόφων ζητήματα</note>—should be rewritten in the following order: *“He wrote the life of Proclus his teacher, the Philosophical Inquiries, and certain other things both in prose and in verse.”* <note>original: ἔγραψε βίον Πρόκλου τοῦ αὐτοῦ διδασκάλου, φιλοσόφων ζητήματα, καὶ ἄλλα τινὰ καὶ καταλογάδην καὶ ἐπικῶς</note> For to tell the truth, I can hardly persuade myself that the life of Proclus was also described in verse by Marinus; **) secondly, it is stated awkwardly that he wrote “certain other philosophical inquiries,” since no mention of inquiries had preceded it. 

2. *A lengthy commentary on Plato’s Philebus* <note>original: Ὑπόμνημα πολύστιχον εἰς τὸν Πλάτωνος Φίληβον</note>. However, Marinus himself soon committed this work to the flames. After the death of Proclus, Marinus had given the work to Isidore <note>Isidore of Alexandria, another Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> to be read and judged; upon hearing the feedback, he understood that *“those things written by the teacher [Proclus] were sufficient,”* <note>original: ἱκανὰ εἶναι τὰ τοῦ διδασκάλου</note> [and thus his own were unnecessary].

*) <note>[See toward the end of the notes.]</note>
**) <note>[However, Fabricius himself was not so opposed to this opinion in his Greek Library, Vol. 9, page 371.]</note>

<summary>Fabricius discusses the literary output of Marinus of Neapolis, arguing for a correction to the historical record in the Suda regarding whether Marinus wrote Proclus's biography in verse, and noting Marinus's humble decision to burn his own commentary on Plato.</summary>

### JOHANNES ALBERTUS FABRICIUS, SECTION 23

...which Claude Hardy <note>Claude Hardy (1604–1678) was a French mathematician and linguist known for his editions of ancient scientific texts.</note> published in Greek and Latin <margin>Paris, 1625, in quarto. Besides this edition, the Bodleian Catalog also mentions a Latin version from Basel, 1536.</margin> [cf. *Greek Library*, vol. 9, p. 371]. Whether the author is truly our Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis was Proclus's successor and biographer.</note>, who was certainly well-versed in mathematical literature, or another man, I can no more say for certain than whether our Marinus wrote the *Introduction to Optics*, the *Book of Optics*, the *Book on Mirrors*, the *Introduction to Phenomena*, and the *Book of Phenomena*. These are all works recorded under the name of Marinus by Gerard Johannes Vossius <note>A celebrated Dutch scholar (1577–1649) who wrote extensively on the history of the sciences.</note> in his book *On the Mathematical Sciences*, page 442.

***

### II. ON THE AGE, ORIGIN, TEACHERS, AND SUCCESSORS OF PROCLUS.

Regarding the age in which Proclus lived, I find that learned men have fallen into error in two different ways: some make the philosopher older than is proper, while others make him more recent. It is not necessary to spend much effort refuting the former group, who claimed he was a contemporary of the Emperor Trajan <note>Trajan reigned 98–117 AD, roughly 300 years before Proclus was born.</note>. Their opinion rests on a manifest error: they confuse Plutarch of Athens with Plutarch of Chaeronea <note>Plutarch of Chaeronea (d. 120 AD) is the famous biographer of the *Lives*; Plutarch of Athens (d. 432 AD) was the Neoplatonist head of the Academy who actually taught Proclus.</note>. Indeed, when Stöffler, Schreckenfuchs <note>Johannes Stöffler and Erasmus Oswald Schreckenfuchs were influential Renaissance astronomers and mathematicians.</note>, and others read in the works of Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> that Proclus had been a pupil of Plutarch, they did not think of the Athenian, but of that other most celebrated Plutarch I mentioned. Thus, they made them contemporaries and wrongly dragged Proclus back to the times of Trajan.

Cedrenus <note>George Kedrenos, an 11th-century Byzantine historian.</note> errs no less obviously <margin>Or, in the text of Cedrenus, page 304, one should read *Priscus* <note>original: "Πρίσκος"</note> instead of *Proclus* <note>original: "Πρόκλος"</note>, as it appears to Lambecius in volume 7, page 50.</margin> when he asserts that our Proclus flourished under Julian <note>Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who reigned 361–363 AD.</note> and was a contemporary of Maximus and Libanius. It is more worthwhile to weigh the opinion of those who contend that Proclus lived nearly half a century later than what we gather from this *Life* written by Marinus. The leader of this group is Peter Lambecius <note>Peter Lambeck (1628–1680) was the librarian to the Holy Roman Emperor and a noted historian.</note>, who argues this point at length in the seventh book <margin>Pages 46 and following, and 404 and following.</margin> of his *Commentaries on the Library*...

***

<summary>Fabricius discusses the mathematical works attributed to Marinus and corrects historical errors by previous scholars who misdated Proclus by confusing his teacher, Plutarch of Athens, with the famous biographer Plutarch of Chaeronea.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus of Neapolis, Plutarch of Athens, chronology, Claude Hardy, Peter Lambecius, Neoplatonism, history of mathematics</keywords>

It is precisely what you see here:

This birth record, when compared with astronomical tables and <term>ephemerides</term> <note>tables showing the predicted positions of celestial bodies</note>, teaches us that Proclus—who was a citizen of Byzantium <note>modern-day Istanbul</note>, where the latitude <note>original: "elevatio poli"</note> is forty-three degrees, and who lived beyond controversy in the 5th century after the birth of Christ—was born in the year of Christ 412, on the 8th day of February. Marinus adds that the new moon immediately preceding his birth occurred at 8 degrees and 5 minutes in Aquarius. From the repeated testimony of the same author, we know that Proclus lived for seventy-five years (lunar years, specifically, which the Greeks used), so that he died in Athens in the Julian year of Christ 485, on the 17th day of April. This is further confirmed by a double argument from the same *Life of Proclus*...

<meta>This page continues Johann Albert Fabricius's scholarly investigation into the life and chronology of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, specifically focusing on the date of his death.</meta>

->JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS.          xxv<-

can be proven. For first, Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis was Proclus’s student and biographer</note> testifies that a very great eclipse of the sun occurred in the year preceding the death of Proclus; specifically, the one which is noted in the Rudolphine Tables <note>The *Tabulae Rudolphinae* (1627) by Johannes Kepler, used for astronomical calculations</note> as having occurred at 25 degrees and 39 minutes of Capricorn, on January 13th in the year of Christ 484. Then [in chapter 36], he affirms that Proclus died in the one hundred and twenty-fourth year after the reign of Julian. In this context, no one else can be understood except the Emperor Julian, even though I found this little note written in the margin of a manuscript: <margin>He is not speaking of Julian the Apostate, but of an archon <note>A magistrate or ruler in ancient Athens</note> chosen by lot in Athens. <note>original Greek: οὐ τὸν παραβάτην Ἰουλιανὸν λέγει, ἀλλ’ ἐν Ἀθήναις κληρωτὸν ἄρχοντα</note></margin> However, the time period agrees perfectly with Julian the Emperor. Nor is it surprising that the memory of the "Apostate" <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363), the last pagan Roman emperor</note> was most sacred to a pagan philosopher of Athens, such that he wished to reckon a kind of <term>Epoch</term> <note>A starting point for a system of dating</note> from his reign—for Julian had bestowed such great favors and honors upon the philosophers, and he himself had made no small progress in philosophy. Furthermore, there were no <term>kings</term> <note>original Greek: βασιλεῖς (basileis)</note> in Athens at that time, nor is the name of <term>kingship</term> <note>original Greek: βασιλείας (basileias)</note> casually attributed to Archons; indeed, in that very passage, Marinus clearly distinguishes and, as it were, contrasts this title with the Archon. It is therefore established, by evidence that in my opinion cannot be denied, concerning the age of Proclus. To this may be added that the name of Marinus, the successor of Proclus, was already famous under Leo the Thracian <note>Eastern Roman Emperor, reigned 457–474</note>, and Damascius wrote while Theodoric, king of the Goths, was holding Italy, at which time both Isidore and Marinus were already dead.

      Now then, let us see what compelled Lambecius <note>Peter Lambeck (1628–1680), a historian and librarian at Vienna</note> to believe that Proclus was only born in the year of Christ 443 under the Emperor Theodosius the Younger, and died in the year of Christ 518 or 519, after the Emperor Anastasius had already passed away. Doubtless, he observed it reported in Zonaras [book 14, page 55] that a certain Proclus burned the fleet of Vitalian the Thracian (who was attempting to seize power under Anastasius) using <term>burning mirrors</term> <note>speculis ustoriis; parabolic mirrors designed to focus sunlight to ignite ships</note> made of bronze. Furthermore, he noted from Theophanes [page 140] that Proclus the <term>interpreter of dreams</term> <note>original Greek: ὀνειροκρίτην (oneirokriten)</note> predicted imminent death to Anastasius, which Cedrenus also testifies to [page 362, D].

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the identity and historical reputation of Proclus, specifically whether he is the same person mentioned in Byzantine chronicles as a "philosopher from Asia."</meta>

->XXVI PREFACE<-

and the author of the **Paschal Chronicle**, which is commonly known as the **Alexandrian Chronicle**, on page 331, where this Proclus is called "the Asian Philosopher" <note>original Latin: *Proclus iste Asianus Philosophus*</note> [see the *Greek Library*, volume IX, page 369]. This reference was sufficient for Lambecius <note>Peter Lambeck (1624–1680), a prominent librarian and scholar</note> to persuade himself that this could be a discussion of no one else but our Proclus. And perhaps Zonaras <note>A 12th-century Byzantine chronicler</note> himself and the author of the Chronicle, when they were writing these things, thought of no one else. However, it does not yet follow from this that they were [not] in error in holding this opinion, or that they could not have been deceived.

I will not inquire now into the truth of the matter itself—whether the fleet of Vitalian <note>A Byzantine general who rebelled in the early 6th century</note> was burned, or even could have been burned, by **burning mirrors** <term>burning mirrors: concave or parabolic mirrors designed to focus sunlight onto a single point to ignite distant objects</term> suspended from the walls of Byzantium. This is similar to the device by which Diodorus Siculus, Galen, and Tzetzes [in *Chiliades* II, history 35] and others record that the fleet of Marcellus was harassed by Archimedes. Gabriel Naudé, in his treatise on military studies, Johann Daniel Major in *The Wandering Genius*, and Lothario Sarsi <note>A pseudonym for the Jesuit scholar Orazio Grassi</note> in his book *On the calculation of weights, the scale, and the plumb-line*, all categorize such accounts as incredible fictions. Sarsi remarks that these stories are told by historians with no more reliability than the claim that the Babylonians used to cook eggs by the mere "whirling of slings," as read in the *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> under the entry *whirling around* <note>original Greek: *peridinountes*</note>.

To these, add René Descartes in the second part of his *Letters*, letter 105. Perrault <note>Charles Perrault (1628–1703), famous for his fairy tales but also a participant in the "Quarrel of the Ancients and the Moderns"</note>—in his French work where he compares the ancients with the moderns, or rather contends that the moderns far excel the ancients in every kind of learning and art—acknowledges that Descartes demonstrated the **impossibility** <note>original Greek: *adynamian*</note> of Archimedes’ burning mirrors. Specifically concerning Proclus, Isaac Vossius <note>A 17th-century Dutch scholar</note> writes in his *Observations on the origin of gunpowder among Europeans*, page 88: "For," he says, "one must not believe Zonaras or others who write that the Gothic fleet was set on fire by the use of burning mirrors constructed by Proclus the Philosopher. For however many well-constructed mirrors you might bring together, you would never achieve a fire of that kind." And scarcely—

<meta>This page continues Fabricius's discussion regarding the life and scientific reputation of the philosopher Proclus, specifically addressing the legend of him using mirrors to burn a hostile fleet.</meta>

->IO. ALB. FABRICIUS. XXVII<-

No one among the ancient authors reports this about Proclus besides Zonaras <note>Joannes Zonaras, a 12th-century Byzantine chronicler and theologian.</note>, even though Evagrius is cited in support of that opinion by Gerard Johann Vossius on page 270 of *On the Mathematical Sciences*, and Evagrius and Marcellinus are cited by Nicolaus Alemannus in his notes to Procopius, page 102. Furthermore, Evagrius and Paul the Deacon are cited by the Reverend Johann Andreas Schmidt in his *Life of Archimedes*, page 42. However, neither Evagrius, nor Marcellinus, nor Paul the Deacon mentions even a single word about a fleet being burned and devastated by Proclus with the help of **burning mirrors** <note>speculorum ustoriorum: concave mirrors used to focus sunlight to ignite objects at a distance.</note>. Even in Vossius's judgment, it is more correct that an old, unpublished Chronicle ascribes this entire feat to **sulfurous fire**. The same is done by John Malalas <note>A 6th-century Byzantine chronicler from Antioch.</note>, whose words are as follows:

<note>original: "Ὁ δὲ βασιλεὺς Ἀναστάσιος πρώην μὲν ἦν μεταστειλάμενος διὰ Μαρίνου τὸν φιλόσοφον Πρόκλον τὸν Ἀθηναῖον, ἄνδρα περιβόητον, καὶ εἶπεν αὐτῷ ὁ βασιλεὺς Ἀναστάσιος κ. τ. λ."</note>

*Now the Emperor Anastasius, having previously summoned through Marinus the philosopher Proclus of Athens, a man of great renown, the Emperor Anastasius said to him... etc.*

The Emperor, having first summoned Proclus the Athenian philosopher—a man of great name—through Marinus, said to him: "Tell me, Philosopher, what am I to do about this dog <note>Vitalian, a general who led a major revolt against Emperor Anastasius I between 513–515 AD.</note> (Vitalian), who so disturbs me and the Republic?" Proclus replied to him: "Do not lose heart, Emperor, for as soon as **) you send some armed men against him, he will take flight without delay." The Emperor therefore commanded Marinus the Syrian, the Prefect, who was standing by while he spoke with Proclus, to himself attack Vitalian, who had taken up a position with his forces in the region of Constantinople. Then the philosopher Proclus addressed Marinus the Syrian in the presence of the Emperor in this manner: "You," he said, "who are about to attack Vitalian, I wish you to take what I shall now give you." The philosopher then ordered a huge weight of **unrefined sulfur** <note>vivi sulphuris: "living" or native sulfur, prized for its combustibility.</note> to be brought, which he gave to Marinus after it had been ground into the smallest pieces. "This," he said, "upon whatever roofs or ships you throw it, they will immediately burst into flames at the rising of the sun."

*) Part 2. *Chronography*, pages 120, 121.

--- Page 32 ---
XXVIII	PREFACE

[...to burn] you will see, and thus be consumed by fires. However, in whatever manner that fire of the Gothic fleet was ignited—the reason for which the Greeks perhaps attributed to burning mirrors the better to conceal the truth—I have no doubt that there was another Proclus <note>The author is distinguishing between Proclus the Neoplatonist philosopher and Proclus the engineer/magician who supposedly used mirrors to burn a fleet during the reign of Emperor Anastasius.</note> who assisted Anastasius, distinct from the Proclus whose life we present here. This is much the same as we showed above: that Marinus the prefect was a very different person from our Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis, the biographer and successor of Proclus.</note>. Indeed, beyond the chronological reasoning set out above, this conclusion is supported by the way of life of our Proclus, the silence of Marinus the biographer, and the disagreement and ignorance of the writers who report those stories about the "other" Proclus.

It is further established that several men were famous by the name of Proclus, such as Proclus the son of Isidore the philosopher, mentioned by Damascius in the writings of Photius; and Proclus the quaestor, son of Paul of Byzantium, "a prudent and shrewd man" <note>original Greek: ἄνδρα συνετὸν καὶ ἀγχίνουν (andra syneton kai anchinoun)</note>. This Proclus is celebrated with that praise by Theophanes, and Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> notes from Procopius's *Persian War* that he held great authority under Emperor Justin. An epigram regarding this same man exists in Book IV of the *Anthology*, in the excellent Wechel edition. 

I omit others from earlier times, such as Proclus Quintillian, whom the author of the *Paschal Chronicle* mentions as the Proconsul of Asia under the reign of Emperor Decius; and the Proclus who was Praetorian Prefect under Theodosius the Great and was beheaded in the fifteenth year of that Emperor’s reign. Even today in Constantinople, the base of the obelisk in the hippodrome near the temple of Sophia <note>Hagia Sophia</note> displays a Latin and Greek epigram mentioning this Proclus. Because George Dousa published the Latin one in a fragmented state in his *Constantinopolitan Journey*, I will set it down here in its entirety:

->*Once difficult to obey serene masters,*<-
->*And ordered to offer the palm to fallen tyrants,*<-
->*All things yield to Theodosius and his everlasting offspring:*<-
->*Thus, conquered and tamed within thirty days,*<-
->*I was raised into the high breezes under Proclus the judge.*<-
<note>This poem is inscribed on the base of the Obelisk of Theodosius. It describes the difficult feat of raising the ancient Egyptian granite monument, which was finally accomplished in 390 AD under the direction of the city prefect, Proclus.</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes the philosopher Proclus from several other historical figures of the same name, specifically an engineer credited with burning a fleet and various Roman officials. It cites historical sources and an inscription from an obelisk in Constantinople to prove these were different individuals.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Emperor Anastasius, Theodosius the Great, Constantinople, Obelisk of Theodosius, Photius, Suidas, Greek Anthology, historical biography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of historical figures named Proclus by the scholar Johann Albert Fabricius, moving from the primary subject (Proclus the Neoplatonist) to other men of the same name found in classical records.</meta>

# JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS. CHAPTER 29.

To these may be added two Stoic philosophers named Proclus, whose homeland was Mallus <note>original: "Mallotae"</note> [cf. *Greek Library* vol. IX, p. 365], a city in Cilicia. Our Proclus praises one of them in the third book of his *Commentary on the Timaeus*, page 166: "Therefore, the celestial things remain unchanged, just as among the ancients both Proclus of Mallus and Philonides have said, and among the more recent, all the Platonists following Plotinus." <note>original Greek: "ἄτρεπτα ἄρα μένει τὰ οὐράνια, ὥσπερ δὴ τῶν ἀρχαίων Πρόκλος τε ὁ Μαλλώτης καὶ Φιλονείδης εἰρήκασι, τῶν δὲ νεωτέρων οἱ ἀπὸ Πλωτίνου πάντες Πλατωνικοί."</note> The *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note> mentions both of them and teaches that the other wrote about the logical fallacies <term>sophisms; clever but false arguments used to deceive</term> of Diogenes, and a work against Epicurus. 

There was also Proclus of Naucratis, a Pompeian and the teacher of Philostratus (who provides more details about him *), who taught <term>eloquence; the art of persuasive speaking or rhetoric</term> at Athens. He possessed a memory so fortunate that even as a ninety-year-old man, he seemed to surpass Simonides <note>Simonides of Ceos was a Greek poet famous for inventing a legendary system of memory enhancement.</note>. 

Then there is Proclus of Rhegium, a physician of the **Methodic sect** <note>A school of ancient medicine that emphasized simple, practical treatments over complex theories of "humors."</note> mentioned by Galen [cf. *Greek Library* ibid. p. 369]. Likewise, there is Proclus Proculeius, the son of a certain Themision, a Syrian <term>hierophant; a priest who interprets sacred mysteries</term> from Laodicea, whose writings are listed by the *Suda* as: *Theology*, a *Commentary on the fable of Pandora in Hesiod*, a work on the *Golden Verses*, an *Introduction to the Arithmetic of Nicomachus of Gerasa*, and certain other works on geometric subjects. 

I omit Proclinus the Platonist, mentioned by Porphyry in his *Life of Plotinus* **), and Eutychius Proculus (or Proclus) of Sicca. The Emperor Marcus Antoninus <note>Better known as Marcus Aurelius.</note> used him as a teacher of Grammar and, in return for his instruction, granted him the dignity of the proconsulship. Trebellius Pollio, in his life of *Aemilianus the Tyrant*, calls him the most learned grammarian of his time. Furthermore, there is Julius Proclus, who was killed by the Emperor Commodus along with his family, as related by Lampridius [ch. 7, cf. *Greek Library* ibid.]. Finally, among the Christians, there is Proclus of Torpa—

***

The same author mentions on page 611 of the Morell edition [p. 617, Ol.] an "embassy" <note>original Greek: "πρεσβευτικὴν"</note> undertaken by this Proclus on behalf of all the teachers in Athens.

**) [Correction: Not Porphyry, but Longinus quoted in Porphyry's *Life of Plotinus*, ch. 20, and in the fragments of Longinus, p. 178, of the excellent edition we owe to the late Weiske.]

->xxx                      PROLEGOMENA<-

...Dropacion <note>The text suggests this may be a variant name or a title</note> the hair-remover <note>original: "alipilarium" — a servant responsible for removing body hair</note>, the steward of Evodia, about whom Samuel Petitus writes in his commentary on Attic laws (page 301 and following) and Stephanus Lemonius in his notes on *Various Sacred Matters* (page 940). There is also Proculus or Proclus the Montanist <note>A follower of Montanism, an early Christian movement</note> [cf. *Greek Bibliography* as above, p. 366], the founder of the Proclians and a vigorous opponent of the Valentinian heresy <note>A major Gnostic movement in early Christianity</note>, who is wrongly called "Patroclus" by Theodoret. <note>original: "Theodoritum" — a 5th-century bishop and historian</note> We also find Proclus, the Bishop of Constantinople, a writer of the fifth century [cf. *Greek Bibliography* vol. IX, p. 365] who was nearly a contemporary of our philosopher of the same name. There are many others whom I shall now refrain from mentioning, though I should note from Plutarch’s *Life of Coriolanus* [chapter 11, Coray edition] that the Romans gave the name "Proclus" to those born while their father was away or "at a distance" <note>original: "procul"</note>, just as they called those "Posthumus" who were born after their father was already deceased and "given to the earth" <note>original: "humi datum" — a play on the etymology of Posthumus</note>.

    To return to our Proclus: concerning his clan or relatives, Marinus <note>Proclus's biographer and successor</note> records nothing, mentioning only his parents Patricius and Marcella. It might further seem uncertain whether "Patricius" is a proper name or a title of rank <note>Patricius often denoted a high-ranking "patrician" in the later Roman Empire</note>. Marinus also observes that Proclus regarded Plutarch of Athens <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher, not the famous biographer mentioned earlier</note> as a grandfather—not because he was related to him by blood or was truly his grandfather, but because Plutarch had been the teacher of Syrianus, whom Proclus honored above all his other masters and revered like a parent. The family of that Plutarch is presented to us by Marinus and by Damascius in the works of Photius:

Proclus himself remained celibate throughout his entire life, following the examples of Thales, Pythagoras, Democritus, Plato, Zeno of Citium, Epicurus, Epictetus, and Apollonius of Tyana. While others approved of that Pythagorean rule, "that one should not go to any woman other than his own wife" <note>original: μὴ δεῖν παρ’ ἄλλην ἰέναι γυναῖκα ἢ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ</note>, Apollonius used to say that for himself it was rather commanded "neither to marry nor to ever enter into the company of sexual pleasures" <note>original: μήτ’ ἂν γῆμαι μήτ’ ἂν εἰς ὁμιλίαν ἀφικέσθαι ποτὲ Ἀφροδισίων; literally "the things of Aphrodite." Apollonius of Tyana was a first-century philosopher often compared to Jesus or Pythagoras for his miracle-working and asceticism.</note>, as Philostratus records in Book 1, Chapter 10 [Chapter 13, page 15 of the Olearius edition]. In his way of life and his doctrines, Proclus primarily followed the Pythagorean and Platonic decrees, though not those alone; rather, he believed a wise man should be an interpreter of all kinds of sacred rites and a <term>hierophant</term> <note>A high priest or official who explains sacred mysteries; Proclus was known for his "theurgy," or ritual practices intended to invoke the divine.</note> of the whole world <margin>***</margin>.

--- Page 36 ---
XXXII ->INTRODUCTION<-

...as Kuster noted.] Furthermore, in his writings, Proclus expressed and followed the thoughts not only of **Pagan** philosophers but also of **Christian** ones, if we believe an ancient writer who says, in the entry for *Dionysius* in the Suda <note>The Suda is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world</note>: *“It should be known that some of the external <note>meaning non-Christian or Pagan</note> wise men, and especially Proclus, have often used the theorems of the blessed Dionysius, and even his very phrasing.”* <note>original Greek: ἰστέον, ὡς τινες τῶν ἔξω σοφῶν καὶ μάλιστα Πρόκλος θεωρήμασι πολλάκις τοῦ μακαρίου Διονυσίου κέχρηται, καὶ αὐταῖς δὲ ξηραῖς ταῖς λέξεσι.</note> I must admit, however, that it seems far more credible to me that both Proclus and that "masked" Dionysius the Areopagite <note>The author refers to the "Pseudo-Dionysius," a 5th-century author who wrote under the name of Dionysius the Areopagite to gain apostolic authority</note> *) "plowed with the same Neoplatonic heifer" <note>A scholarly idiom meaning they drew from the same source material; an allusion to the biblical riddle of Samson</note>, rather than Proclus having learned from Dionysius and inserted his words into his own writings. Truly, whether Plotinus and the rest received certain Christian <term>dogmas</term> <note>established beliefs or doctrines</note> into their philosophy, or attempted to reconcile or refine them, does not seem to be something that should be rashly denied or dismissed.

Among the Pagan philosophers, Proclus attributed much not only to the Greeks but also to the **barbarians** <note>In this context, "barbarian" refers to non-Greek cultures, particularly those of the Near East</note>, especially to Orpheus and the **Chaldean Oracles**, as his own books and the writings of Marinus testify throughout. His masters in **Alexandria** were first Orion the Grammarian, Leonas the Rhetorician, Olympiodorus the Philosopher (by whom he was imbued with the teachings of Aristotle), and Heron the Mathematician. Then, in **Athens**, he studied under Syrianus, son of Philoxenus, and Plutarch, son of Nestorius; but he was especially close to Syrianus, because the other died not long after Proclus had arrived in Athens. Our author Proclus gladly acknowledges that he owes everything to this Syrianus, who was a native of Alexandria (not of Gaza, as Jonsius asserts on page 300 [III. 18. 1. p. 97.]).

***

*) Stillingfleet, a light of England recently extinguished by an untimely fate <note>Edward Stillingfleet (1635–1699) was a prominent English theologian and Bishop of Worcester</note>, in his *Response to Cressy's Apology concerning the Fanaticism of the Roman Church* (chapter 2, page 130), observes that among the disciples of Apollinaris—to whom the famous Cave attributed the writings of the Pseudo-Areopagite—there was one by the name of **Dionysius** and another by the name of **Timothy**. From this, on page 133, he not absurdly conjectures that this Dionysius published his writings under the name of that ancient Dionysius the Areopagite, having largely derived or "encrusted" them with ideas from Plotinus, Porphyry, Iamblichus, and similar authors; he then dedicated them to Timothy, his own **fellow student** <note>original Greek: συμφοιτητῇ</note>, who by a very happy coincidence shared the name of the ancient disciple of the Apostle Paul.

<keywords>Proclus, Pseudo-Dionysius, Neoplatonism, Syrianus, Alexandria, Athens, Stillingfleet, Chaldean Oracles, Aristotle</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of Syrianus, the teacher of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, as documented by Johann Albert Fabricius.</meta>

->JO. ALB. FABRICIUS.<- ->XXXIII<-

He <note>Referring to the philosopher Proclus</note> followed his profession and lived in close friendship with him for many years; he frequently praises him and diligently follows him in his commentaries. He eventually succeeded him in the chair <note>The academic chair of the Platonic Academy</note> at Athens and was finally buried with him in the same tomb in the Athenian suburbs, where Isidore the Philosopher <margin>Photius, page 564; add Lambeck, volume VII, page 404 and following.</margin> brought a funerary incense-burner <note>original: "ἐπιτάφιον θυμιατρίδα"</note> with him and honored his departed spirit with worship. How subtle a philosopher Syrianus was—a vigorous follower of Iamblichus—can be understood not only from those things which Proclus himself cites in many places, but also from his commentary on the *Metaphysics* of Aristotle. This work was published in Latin at Venice from the version by Hieronymus Bagolinus, and it appears in Greek in various libraries in Italy, and by the favor of Lucas Holstenius, in the Joannaeum Library of this city <note>Hamburg, where Fabricius lived and worked</note>. Whether those Greek works on Hermogenes <note>A 2nd-century rhetorician</note> published among the *Rhetores* by Aldus Manutius are by this same Syrianus, I do not yet dare to affirm. Concerning other writings attributed to him in the *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world</note>, I have spoken below in the catalog of Proclus's writings on page 110. [See *Bibliotheca Graeca*, volume IX, page 358.]

Perhaps this Syrianus is one of the pair or "chariot team" <note>original: "xynoride sive biga"</note> of Plutarchist wise men, whom Synesius either celebrates or criticizes in Letter 135 to his brother. However, the Proclus mentioned by the same Synesius in Letters 129 and 133—called at times "the wonderful Proclus" <note>original: "θαυμάσιος Πρόκλος"</note>, at times "father" <note>original: "πατὴρ"</note>, at times "most wonderful father" <note>original: "θαυμασιώτατος πατὴρ"</note>, and at times "companion Proclus" <note>original: "ἑταῖρος Πρόκλος"</note>—is certainly different from our Proclus and older in age, and perhaps a Christian. Among the pupils of Syrianus, Hermias of Alexandria is also praised by Damascius <margin>In Photius, page 554.</margin> and the *Suda*. His most excellent wife, Aedesia, was related by blood to Syrianus, who had intended to betroth her while she was still a virgin to Proclus <margin>*Suda* under "Aedesia," from Damascius.</margin>, had not some god or other forbidden him from the marriage. Furthermore, Domninus the Syrian was a pupil of Syrianus, concerning whom, besides Damascius and the *Suda*, Marinus speaks in chapter 26. Among others who emerged as pupils of Proclus...

...innumerable [others including] Heliodorus and Ammonius, the sons of Hermias, Hierius the son of Plutarch, Asclepiodotus, Zenodotus, Severianus, Ulpianus of Gaza, and especially Marinus and Hegias; of these, Marinus succeeded his master in the Athenian school, just as Isidore succeeded Marinus. <note>The "Athenian school" refers to the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, which Proclus headed in the 5th century.</note> Isidore had also heard <note>i.e., been a student of</note> Proclus <note>See Photius, page 571.</note>, and later, when Hegias declined this duty <note>See Photius, page 568.</note>, Isidore took Marinus’s place. According to Suidas, Agapius also studied under Marinus, while Simplicius, Asclepius of Tralles <note>See Jonsius, Book III, chapter 18, *On the Writers of Philosophical History*.</note>, and John Philoponus studied under Ammonius. We may perhaps clarify elsewhere where these excerpts were poorly understood by the translator. A very subtle work by the same Damascius, of no small size, titled *On First Principles* <note>original Greek: περὶ ἀρχῶν (*peri archon*)</note>, exists in manuscript form in the libraries of Italy, France, and England, as well as in the Johanneum of this city <note>Likely referring to the library of the Gymnasium Johanneum in Hamburg.</note> from the legacy of the great Lucas Holstenius. It is said that learned men in France are already striving to produce an edition of it, a merit in which we rejoice.

## III. ON THE DEGREES OF VIRTUES, ACCORDING TO WHICH MARINUS PRAISES PROCLUS.

Although, as it once rightly appeared to Menedemus of Eretria, and as Socrates teaches many in Plato’s *Protagoras*, virtue is a single thing—namely, the serious pursuit and habit of living rightly, which Apollophanes called <term>prudence</term> <note>original Latin: *prudentia*; here meaning practical wisdom or *phronesis*</note>—from which, as if from a glowing center, the most beautiful rays are scattered into all human actions; nevertheless, it has long pleased almost all other philosophers to distinguish various types of virtues from one another according to the diversity of their objects, and to call them by various names. I need not even speak of Aristotle, whose number of eleven <term>moral virtues</term> and five <term>intellectual virtues</term>...

***

<note>original footnote * : Photius page 571.</note>
<note>original footnote ** : Photius page 568.</note>
<note>original footnote *** : See Jonsius III. 18. on the writers of Philosophical History.</note>

<summary>This page details the succession of scholars in the Neoplatonic Academy, leading to Damascius, and begins a philosophical discussion on whether virtue is a single unity or a collection of distinct categories.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Athenian School, Proclus, Damascius, Marinus, Photius, categorization of virtue, Menedemus, Aristotle, moral virtues</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS.      xxxv

There is a very well-known little story in books on ethics, and it concerns neither Zeno <note>The founder of the Stoic school of philosophy.</note> nor Chrysippus <note>A massive contributor to Stoic logic and ethics.</note>—whom Plutarch, in his book *On Moral Virtue*, complains burdened philosophy with various and useless names for virtues—nor even the many more recent authors, such as Omeis, Werdenhagen, Erhard Weigel, and many others. These later writers increased the number of virtues to such an extent that merely remembering their names seems to be a task of great labor, if not a virtue in itself. Who is such a stranger to the study of Morals that the quartet of **cardinal virtues** <note>The four "hinge" virtues: Prudence, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance.</note> escapes them? I do not know if any other distinction is older, more widespread, or less deserving of criticism—provided we hold this as an unshakable principle: that these individual virtues, though called "cardinal," are hardly worthy of the name unless they flow from that common center I mentioned previously. Thus, a person should cultivate one virtue in such a way that they are simultaneously devoted to all the others.

Therefore, it seemed right—not only to Plato and Pythagoras, but also to the Stoics, the Epicureans, and the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle.</note>—and, not to mention only the Greek philosophers, also to the Hebrews, Egyptians, and Persians, that the entire virtue of man is correctly divided into four types or parts: the first being **prudence**, the second **fortitude** <note>Often translated as courage or strength of mind.</note>, the third **temperance**, and the fourth **justice**. The author of the book commonly believed to be Solomon <note>original: "Salomon"; refers to the *Wisdom of Solomon* in the Apocrypha.</note> commends this tetrad <note>A group of four.</note> of virtues as the most useful fruit of wisdom in the eighth chapter and section: for it teaches temperance, prudence, justice, and fortitu-

[Horizontal line]

<margin>*) *Marius Victorinus, in his commentary on the first book of Cicero's Rhetoric: Virtue, therefore, has four parts: prudence, justice, fortitude, and temperance. **Prudence** is the knowledge of things good and evil throughout all time. **Justice** is a state of mind so formed that it assigns dignity to every merit. **Fortitude** is the deliberate undertaking of dangers and the enduring of labors. **Temperance** is the mind’s victorious and modest mastery over useless and shameful things. We shall recognize the sub-parts of these categories in what follows.* [Regarding these four principal virtues, see the excellent remarks noted by Wyttenbach in his *Observations on Plutarch’s 'On Studying the Poets'*, Vol. 1, p. 275, compared with the note by Krebs, p. 224.]</margin>

                                        c 2

<summary>Fabricius discusses the historical classification of the four cardinal virtues, noting that while some philosophers overcomplicate ethics with lists of names, the core quartet remains a universal standard across Greek, Hebrew, and Persian thought.</summary>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature and classification of virtues, likely following a section on their historical or theological origins.</meta>

->xxxvi               PROLEGOMENA<-

...[longitu]dine <note>original: "-dinem"; this is the conclusion of a word from the previous page, likely "magnitudinem" (greatness) or "longitudinem" (length/duration)</note>, than which nothing is more useful in human life. Comparing these virtues with the four rivers of paradise, <term>Philo Judaeus</term> <note>A Jewish philosopher from Alexandria who sought to harmonize the Hebrew Bible with Greek philosophy.</note> explains in the first book of his *Allegories of the Sacred Laws* that **prudence** teaches what should be done in life; **fortitude** (or courage) teaches what must be endured; furthermore, you are advised by **temperance** on what should be chosen or avoided, and by **justice** on what should be granted to each person. 

Soon after, this same Philo—agreeing with **Plato** that our soul is tripartite <note>Divided into three parts: the rational, the spirited, and the desiring.</note>—says that **prudence** belongs to the *rational* part, which is stationed in the head as if in its own citadel. He states that the *irascible* <note>The "spirited" part of the soul, associated with anger, courage, and vigor.</note> part, located in the chest, is ruled and kept to its duty by **fortitude**. Finally, the *concupiscible* <note>The desiring part of the soul, associated with physical appetites.</note> part, which resides in the lower belly, is governed by **temperance**. If a man’s anger and desire rightly obey his reason, and these three parts of the soul thus breathe together in a desired harmony, Philo asserts that this is **justice**.

In a similar fashion, **Josephus**—in his book *On the Rule of Reason*, in which he most eloquently sang the praises of the Maccabean martyrs—affirms that wisdom is fourfold, and that these four parts are the very virtues we have begun to discuss. The Christians received this same "fourfold" system from the Hebrews. This is true not only of the Latins—as is clear from **Ambrose**, who is observed to be the first to have called them "cardinal virtues" <note>From the Latin "cardo" (hinge); the fundamental virtues upon which a moral life pivots.</note> in his fifth book on Luke; **Jerome** in his first chapter on Matthew; **Augustine** in book 19 of *The City of God*, chapter 20; and many others, especially the Scholastics and more recent writers—but also the Greeks. Among them, **Theodoret**, in the tenth chapter of his fifth book on *Compendium of Heretical Fables*, warns that it is our duty not only to expel vice entirely from our souls, but also to strive in turn to introduce virtue and to shine through its parts, decorating the beauty of the soul with the flowers of **temperance, justice, fortitude, and justice** <note>The original Latin repeats "iustitiae" (justice) twice here, likely a printing error where "prudentiae" (prudence) was intended.</note>. 

This fourfold distinction of virtue is approved even today by Eastern Christians, as is evident from the confession of the Greek Church, which was published not many years ago with its own translation by the great ornament of the Uppsala Academy, **Laurentius Normannus**. Indeed, long ago, **Proclus**, Bishop of Constantinople, in a letter which survives with the translation by **Dionysius Exiguus**... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; Dionysius Exiguus was a 6th-century monk best known as the inventor of the Anno Domini (AD) dating system.</note>

<summary>This page explores the philosophical and theological origins of the four cardinal virtues, tracing their development from Philo Judaeus and Plato through to the early Church Fathers and modern Eastern Christianity.</summary>
