<meta>This page continues the discussion of moral philosophy and the classification of virtues, specifically addressing the transition from classical "pagan" virtues to Christian ethics.</meta>

->J. A. FABRICIUS. XXXVII<-

In his study of heresies <note>original Latin: *haeresiologia*</note>, Johannes Herold criticizes the Armenians because they do not hesitate to limit themselves to these four virtues <note>The four "cardinal virtues" are Prudence, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance.</note>. He adds that he does not so much reject this "group of four" as he finds it difficult to endure that, despite being Christians, they constantly crawl upon the ground in the manner of pagans. They do not attempt, he argues, to lift their minds toward the sublimity of truly Christian virtues: hope, faith, and charity. Nevertheless, these Christian virtues could be included under the former ones: hope might be related to fortitude, faith to prudence, and charity to justice—of which charity can be seen as a kind of pinnacle. In this same way, the four virtues which a French writer *)—recently following the example of Geulincx <note>Arnold Geulincx (1624–1669) was a Flemish philosopher who emphasized that virtue consists in the "love of reason."</note>—recommended in his *Christian Ethics*, are clearly contained within these same categories: diligence under fortitude, accuracy under prudence, modesty (or as it is commonly called, humility) under temperance, and finally, obedience under justice.

But to let our discussion return to the pagans, it is appropriate first to speak of the Egyptians. An ancient writer expresses their view in a letter to Ammon, which Stobaeus cites under the name of Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure often associated with a syncretism of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth.</note> in his *Physical Excerpts* [Book I, chapter 52, section 4]: *The soul, then, O Ammon, is an essence perfect in itself from the beginning, choosing a life according to fate* <note>original Greek: ψυχὴ τοίνυν ἔστιν, ὦ Ἄμμων, οὐσία αὐτοτελὴς, ἐν ἀρχῇ κ. τ. λ.</note>, *and it draws to itself a reason similar to matter, possessing both anger and desire. And anger is matter. If this anger forms a habit directed toward the soul's understanding, it becomes fortitude and is not led away by fear. Desire, however, is led away. If desire forms a habit directed toward the soul’s reasoning, it becomes temperance and is not moved by pleasure, for reasoning fulfills the deficiency of desire. When both of these are in harmony, and have formed an equal habit, and both cling to the soul’s reasoning, then justice is born. Equal...*

__________________________________________________________________
*) Mr. Naudé in his *Evangelical Morality* <note>original French: *Morale Evangélique*</note> printed at Berlin, 1699.

XXXVIII          PROLEGOMENA

->for their *disposition indeed removes the excess of anger, and balances the deficiency of desire. But the principle is the intellectual essence existing through itself in its providential reason (from this providential reason <note>original: προνοητικῷ λόγῳ (pronoyētikō logō)</note> comes prudence), having command over its own reason. This essence commands and leads as a prince. Its providential reason acts as a counselor.*<-
Book XIV, page 41, edition by Francesco Patrizi, in *The New Philosophy of the Universe* <note>Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597) was a Venetian philosopher who sought to replace Aristotelianism with a Platonist framework.</note>.

Plato relates in the *First Alcibiades* that it was established by custom among the Persians that the king's firstborn son, at fourteen years of age, was entrusted to four masters. Clement of Alexandria also notes from Plato that these were called royal tutors. One was the *wisest*, by whom he was taught the Magic of Zoroaster and Oromasdes <note>Oromasdes is the Greek form of Ahura Mazda, the supreme deity of Zoroastrianism. Here, "Magic" refers to the priestly wisdom of the Magi.</note>—that is, the worship of the Gods and the art of ruling; the second was the *most just*, who would advise him to keep faith throughout his life and be a seeker of truth; the third was the *most temperate*, from whom he would learn to conquer pleasures and serve no vice; and finally the fourth was the *bravest*, who would make his spirit undaunted. In this Persian training, the late <note>original: ὁ μακαρίτης (ho makaritēs); a respectful Greek term used for a deceased person, similar to "the late" or "of blessed memory."</note> Jakob Thomasius *) judged, not without reason, that a significant trace of the four cardinal virtues <note>The cardinal virtues are Wisdom, Justice, Temperance, and Fortitude.</note> exists—even if it seems that Plato added those four titles (wisest, most just, most temperate, and bravest) from his own perspective, adapting them to the duties of royal tutors. It is likely that one performed the role of a teacher of theology and politics, another of ethics, and the fourth trained the king's son in military discipline.

Among the Greeks, Pythagoras seems to have been the first of all to mention this fourfold division, which Hierocles explains in his golden commentary on the *Golden Verses* <note>The *Golden Verses* are a collection of moral exhortations attributed to Pythagoras; Hierocles of Alexandria wrote a famous Neoplatonist commentary on them in the 5th century.</note>, page 28 ff. [page 64 in the Needham edition]: *The kinds of vices are indeed many. According to the rational part, etc.* <note>original: Κακίας δὲ πλείονα. Κατὰ μὲν τὸ λογικὸν κ. τ. λ. (Kakias de pleiona. Kata men to logikon k. t. l.)</note> *There are many types of vices. In the rational part of man...*

***

*) In Dissertation I on the four cardinal virtues. Leipzig, 1665.

<meta>This page continues a discussion from the previous page regarding the moral philosophy of the Pythagoreans, specifically how human failings correspond to a lack of specific virtues. The text likely began with a discussion on the "rational" part of the soul before this page's opening line.</meta>

->JO. ALB. FABRICIUS.<- ->XXXIX<-

[...of the] human [soul is] imprudence, in the irascible part there is timidity, in the appetitive part a pursuit of pleasure and riches, and finally, in all the faculties together, there is injustice. Thus, it appears that four kinds of virtues were necessary to avoid such evils: **prudence** in that faculty which uses reason, **fortitude** <note>or courage</note> in the movements of the spirit, **continence** <note>or self-control/temperance</note> in the appetitive force, and finally **justice**, in which all these faculties are held together as the most perfect virtue, containing the other virtues within itself as its own parts. 

The fragments of Theages, Archytas, and Metopus the Pythagoreans—preserved by Stobaeus <note>a 5th-century compiler of Greek extracts</note>—agree entirely with Hierocles. For some unknown reason, these fragments were considered by Conringius <note>Hermann Conring, a 17th-century German scholar</note> to be forgeries fabricated in the second century after Christ. However, it is not clear for whose benefit anyone would have forged them at that time; furthermore, they are written in the *Doric dialect* <note>a regional variant of Ancient Greek associated with the Spartans and early Pythagoreans</note> familiar to those Pythagoreans, and they contain nothing foreign to the ancient Pythagorean doctrines. Hierocles adds further on page 94 that the goal of the *Golden Verses* <note>a collection of moral exhortations attributed to Pythagoras</note> was to hand down and recommend that "tetrad of practical virtues" <note>original: τετρὰς illa πρακτικῶν ἀρετῶν</note> to be expressed through diligent care and just observation in words and deeds.

What if the Pythagorean *tetractys* <note>a mystical symbol consisting of ten points arranged in a triangle, representing the number ten and the four elements</note> itself, celebrated in so many voices and writings, encompasses the mystery of these four virtues? These virtues correspond to the parts of the soul, imitating divine perfections and derived from the Deity alone as their source. 

Moreover, I find that *boldness* <note>original Greek: τόλμαν (tolman)</note> or fortitude was called the **dyad** <note>the number two</note> by the Pythagoreans; prudence was the **triad** <note>the number three</note>; and justice was the **tetrad** <note>the number four</note> or the "evenly even" number, as can be seen in the *Pythagorean Denarius* of Johannes Meursius. However, I confess I do not know from where the most elegant Caelius Calcagninus drew his claim—in Book V, Letter 8—that Pythagoras attributed the **monad** <note>the number one</note> to prudence, the **dyad** to fortitude, the **triad** to temperance, and finally the **tetrad** to justice.

Furthermore, the reader may see those four virtues explained according to the mind of the Pythagoreans, if they wish, in the *Pythagorean Ethics* of Daniel Omeisius. Since, however, Pythagoras followed the parts of the soul in the numbering of the virtues, as seen from Hierocles...

<summary>This page analyzes the Pythagorean system of the four cardinal virtues (prudence, fortitude, temperance, and justice), linking them to specific faculties of the soul and numerical symbols like the Tetractys. It also defends the authenticity of ancient Pythagorean fragments against claims of forgery by later scholars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the history of the cardinal virtues, moving from the Pythagorean view of the soul to the broader consensus among classical philosophers like Aristotle and the Stoics.</meta>

->XL	PREFACE<-

and I have observed in the rest that, as Laertius <note>Diogenes Laertius, a biographer of Greek philosophers</note> testifies, according to the opinion of Pythagoras the soul is composed of **mind**, **intellect**, and **spirit** <note>original: "νοῦν, φρένας, ϑυμὸν" (noun, phrenas, thymon)</note>. We might perhaps be able to reconcile this with what has already been said in the following way: the **mind**—that is, the theoretical intellect—claims for itself the **justice** that must be prescribed to the other parts; the **intellect**—or practical understanding—claims **prudence**; and the **spirit**—the irrational part—is divided again into two, namely **anger** (or high-spiritedness) and **desire**. Of these latter two, **fortitude** belongs to one, and **temperance** to the other.

	It would be a long task to speak of the remaining philosophers, nearly all of whom approved of that fourfold distinction of virtue. Not even *Aristotle* himself is an exception, as is clear from Book 10, Chapter 8 of the *Nicomachean Ethics* and Book 7, Chapter 1 of the *Politics*, although elsewhere he divides the virtues differently and, as he himself believes, more accurately. On this matter, one should consult the later dissertation by *Jakob Thomasius* <note>A German academic and teacher of Leibniz</note> concerning the four cardinal virtues, which is entirely based on *Aristotle's* opinion; it is also illustrated by *Henri Estienne* in his booklet <note>original: "Henrico Stephano"</note> <insert>*) This appeared with his *The Prince’s Guiding Muse* at Basel, 1590, in octavo format.</insert> on the difference between the Ethics of Aristotle, the historians, and the poets. Regarding the Stoics, the facts are clear from *Plutarch's* book in which he collected the disagreements and contradictions of those philosophers; from that source, one can also recover the different explanations of this "four-fold group" <note>The four cardinal virtues</note> provided by *Zeno*, *Cleanthes*, and others. Furthermore, besides *Posidonius* (whom *Laertius* mentions) and *Panaetius* (found in the first book of *Cicero's* *On Duties*), *Marcus Aurelius Antoninus* also—in Book 5, Section 12 of his moral precepts *To Himself* <note>Known today as the *Meditations*</note>—states that virtue is the only true good and enumerates these four: **prudence**, **temperance**, **justice**, and **fortitude**. I omit the spurious booklet on the four cardinal virtues which circulates under the name of *Seneca* <note>This was actually a work by Martin of Braga, though long attributed to Seneca the Younger</note>, as it contains a "patchwork" <note>original: "centones," referring to a literary work made of snippets from other texts</note> of passages from his writings stitched together rather poorly. I also omit what *Gaspar Schoppe* discusses regarding the <term>tetrad</term> <note>original: "tetracty," usually a Pythagorean term for the number ten, but here applied to the set of four virtues</note> of virtues according to the Stoic mind in Chapter 123, page 47 of his *Elements of Stoic Moral Philosophy*. He explains the opinion of *Aristo* in these words...

<summary>This page discusses how Pythagoras's tripartite division of the soul aligns with the four cardinal virtues and lists various classical authorities—including Aristotle, the Stoics, and Marcus Aurelius—who support this fourfold classification.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Aristotle, Stoics, Marcus Aurelius, cardinal virtues, justice, prudence, fortitude, temperance, Jakob Thomasius, soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Albertus Fabricius’s scholarly survey of ancient philosophical views on the four cardinal virtues, moving from the Stoic Aristo of Chios to Epicurus, and finally to the extensive Platonic tradition.</meta>

JO[HANNES] ALB[ERTUS] FABRICIUS.                                        41

Galen, in the seventh book of *On the Doctrines of Hippocrates and Plato*, page 310, writes: 
<note>original: "νομίσας γοῦν ὁ Ἀρίστων μίαν εἶναι τῆς ψυχῆς δύναμιν, ᾗ λογιζόμεθα, καὶ τὴν ἀρετὴν τῆς ψυχῆς, ἔθετο μίαν ἐπιστήμην ἀγαθῶν καὶ κακῶν..."</note>
"Aristo <note>Aristo of Chios (3rd century BCE), a Stoic philosopher who argued that all virtues are actually just one single state of mind.</note>, believing that there is but one power of the soul by which we reason, and likewise one virtue of the soul, defined it as a single knowledge of good and evil. Thus, when it is necessary to choose the good and flee the evil, he calls this knowledge **temperance**; when it involves doing the good and not doing the evil, he calls it **prudence**; it is **courage** when it is bold toward some things and flees others; and when it distributes to each according to their worth, it is **justice**. In a word, when the soul possesses knowledge of good and evil apart from action, it is **wisdom** and **science**; but when it reaches the various arrangements of life, it takes on the multiple names mentioned before: being called prudence, temperance, justice, and courage." 

That even Epicurus recognized this same fourfold set of virtues was noted long ago by the most learned [Pierre] Gassendi <note>A 17th-century French philosopher and priest who sought to reconcile Epicureanism with Christianity.</note>, whose very rich observations on the moral philosophy of that "Gargettian Philosopher" <note>A common nickname for Epicurus, derived from his birthplace, Gargettus.</note> require no further addition from us at present. 

There remain Socrates and Plato, whom this division of virtues especially pleased, as is evident from the fourth and sixth books of the *Republic*, the *Euthydemus*, the *Laches*, and the first book of the *Laws*; as well as in Diogenes Laertius's biography of Plato, Alcinous in chapter 1, Apuleius in the second book of *On the Doctrine of Plato*, and—not to mention others—finally our own Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 CE) was one of the last major Greek philosophers and a head of the Neoplatonist Academy in Athens.</note> in his dissertations on the *Republic*, page 407 and following. 

Although sometimes, as in the *Meno*, the Philosopher [Plato] adds others, such as **magnificence**, **teachability**, and **memory**, these last two are not so much moral <term>virtues|In this context, moral virtues refer to character traits developed through habit, while physical faculties are innate abilities.</term> as they are excellent natural faculties. 

The later Platonists follow their master with one voice, but as in other matters, so too in this doctrine of the four virtues, they added not a few things to Plato’s reflections. Among these additions, it is no small thing that they established several <term>levels|The Neoplatonists believed that virtues existed in a hierarchy, progressing from basic human behavior to the divine state of the soul.</term> or "orders" of these virtues: some identified three levels, others four, and finally others seven or eight. Of these, the first level consists of the four cardinal virtues in their **Physical** state; the second is **Ethical**; the third **Political**; the fourth **Purifying** [Purgatorial]; and the fifth belongs to the virtues of the soul that is already purified and cleansed...

<summary>This page discusses the historical classification of the four cardinal virtues (prudence, temperance, courage, and justice), citing Galen's account of the Stoic Aristo and tracing the concept through Epicurus, Plato, and the Neoplatonists.</summary>
<keywords>cardinal virtues, Aristo of Chios, Galen, Plato, Neoplatonism, Pierre Gassendi, moral philosophy, hierarchy of virtues</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
XLII INTRODUCTION

of the soul, the sixth being *Theoretical*, the seventh *Theurgic* <term>Theurgic: related to "theurgy," a Neoplatonic practice of rituals intended to unite the soul with the divine</term>, and finally the highest, which is scarcely—if at all—accessible to man: the *Divine*. Marinus <note>Marinus of Neapolis, a philosopher and biographer of Proclus</note> refers to this final level after mentioning the previous ones, writing as follows on page 4 [chapter 3]: "keeping silent about those even higher, as they are already ranked above the human level." <note>original Greek: τὰς δὲ ἔτι ἀνωτέρω σιωπήσαντες ὡς καὶ ὑπὲρ ἄνθρωπον ἤδη τεταγμένας</note>

Indeed, Aristotle does not dare to attribute virtues to God in Book 10, Chapter 8 of the *Nicomachean Ethics*, where he contends that God’s only "work" <note>original Greek: ἔργον (ergon)</note> is contemplation: *For what other actions could be attributed to him? Those of justice? It would truly be ridiculous if we were to say he makes exchanges, or returns deposits, and other things of that kind. Or actions of courage? How then would he endure terrors, or undergo danger to his life for the sake of what is honorable? This is truly absurd even to say. Or what of the acts of a liberal man?* (liberality being wisdom in distributing goods) *To whom and what will he give? For it would be absurd if we were to attribute money to him or something similar—if he himself needed such things for life, yet gave them to others for the sake of honor. And how will he be temperate, since he does not even have evil desires? All such actions are trivial and unworthy of God.* In a similar vein, Cotta argues from the opinion of Carneades in Cicero's <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, Roman statesman and philosopher</note> third book *On the Nature of the Gods*. However, while Cotta concludes from this that God does not exist, he also teaches that virtue falls upon God no less certainly than the fact that God exists. For although one might absurdly ascribe human weaknesses to the Deity—weaknesses which appear in our own virtues—one cannot deny that there are in God supreme perfections, of which the things we call virtues in ourselves are like shadows and outlines. It is in this sense that Origen, in the fourth book of *Against Celsus* (page 180), teaches that the virtue of God and man is the same—not in degree, but in aim and end, which is supreme perfection and rectitude, which man perhaps strives for...

[horizontal line]

*) Page 617, edition of Pierre Lescalopier, who refutes Cotta in his commentary.

->JOHANNES ALBERTUS FABRICIUS. XLIII<-

...[God] gives; God alone holds exactly and always and everywhere expresses it. This relates to the saying of Heraclitus reported by the same Origen in the sixth book, page 283: <note>original Greek: ἦθος γὰρ ἀνθρώπειον μὲν οὐκ ἔχει γνώμας, θεῖον δὲ ἔχει</note> *For the human character does not possess true insights, but the divine character does.* Perhaps the more sensible Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy that taught that virtue is the only good.</note> felt no differently, although Clement in the seventh book of his *Miscellanies* and Theodoret in his second discourse *On the End and Judgment* reject their opinion—which claims that the virtue of God and man is the same—as impious and most audacious. Plotinus, in the second book of the first *Ennead*, following Plato’s warning that we should strive to be like God through virtue, admits indeed that in God there are not <note>original Greek: τὰς πολιτικὰς λεγομένας ἀρετὰς</note> *what are called the civic virtues*: <term>prudence</term> <note>original: φρόνησιν; here meaning practical wisdom regarding the rational mind</note>, <term>courage</term> <note>original: ἀνδρίαν; regarding the spirited or emotional part of the soul</note>, <term>temperance</term> <note>original: σωφροσύνην; the harmony of the desiring part of the soul with the rational part</note>, or <term>justice</term> <note>original: δικαιοσύνην; defined here as each part of the soul performing its own proper function in ruling or being ruled</note>. Yet he soon adds that in Him there are certain higher things, like the models and "ideas" of the virtues. Macrobius, in the first book of his commentary on the *Dream of Scipio*, chapter 8, following in Plotinus’s footsteps, explains this succinctly in these words: *If we believe the ideas of other things exist, much more must we believe that the ideas of the virtues exist in the divine mind. There, prudence is the divine mind itself; temperance is the fact that God is turned toward Himself in perpetual focus; fortitude is that He is always the same and never changes; justice is that He is never diverted from the eternal continuation of His work by any everlasting law.*

Moreover, although Marinus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and biographer of Proclus.</note> mentions a rank even higher than the <term>Theoretical and Theurgical virtues</term> <note>Theoretical virtues relate to contemplation and study; Theurgical virtues relate to "divine work" or rituals intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> (as we have observed), and nothing can be higher than the divine ones, nevertheless, these Theoretical and Theurgical virtues are also frequently called "divine." Indeed, you may even see the virtues of the "purified soul" called divine, so that the old Pythagorean saying seems to apply to all of these: 
-><note>original Greek: Ταῦτά σε τῆς θείας ἀρετῆς εἰς ἴχνια θήσει</note> *These things will set you on the tracks of divine virtue.*<-
Truly, those virtues were called "practical" by those who needed them for human life—that is, the ethical and political virtues—and finally "purgatory" <note>From the Latin *purgatoriae*, meaning cleansing or purifying the soul from earthly distractions.</note> virtues, which are higher than the political but still ranked below the divine. The divine virtues of contemplation...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the classification of virtues within Platonist philosophy, specifically focusing on the distinction between practical, theoretical, and theurgic virtues.</meta>

->XLIV                PROLEGOMENA<-

...of the Platonists, as Hierocles teaches [p. 10 of Needham's edition]: "We find that practical philosophy is called human virtue, whereas theoretical philosophy is celebrated as divine virtue." <note>original: "τὴν μὲν πρακτικὴν φιλοσοφίαν ἀνθρωπίνην ἀρετὴν εὑρίσκομεν λεγομένην, τὴν δὲ θεωρητικὴν θείαν ἀρετὴν ὑμνουμένην"</note>. The reason is that they believed contemplation to be the chief or even the sole work <term>work (Greek: ἔργον / ergon): the characteristic function or activity of a being</term> of God, as we have already demonstrated above that Aristotle also felt. Akin to these contemplative virtues are the virtues of the purified soul and the theurgic virtues <term>theurgic (theurgical): rituals or practices intended to invoke the presence of gods or achieve union with the divine</term>. But there is as much difference between the first two types [the practical and the theoretical] as there is between Daemons <note>In this context, "Daemons" refers to the Greek *daimones*, intermediary spirits between gods and mortals, not "demons" in the modern sense.</note> (or Angels) and God, even if both are superior to man, and even Daemons are referred to as "divine matters" <note>original: "τὰ θεῖα"</note>. 

Certainly Iamblichus, in his book *On the Egyptian Mysteries*, suggests that the name "daemonic" <note>original: "δαιμονίων"</note>—a term Plato uses throughout—properly applies to those who possess the virtues of a soul already purified and cleansed; just as those distinguished by theoretical virtues are properly called "divine" <note>original: "θεῖοι"</note>. These are the people who are, as Synesius praises Herculianus in Letter 139: "entirely spectators of reality, having crossed over the virtues of the beginning of mortals." <note>original: "ὅλον θεωροὶ τῶν ὄντων καὶ τῆς τῶν θνητῶν ἀρχῆς τὰς ἀρετὰς διαβάντες"</note>. In this sense, Epictetus calls Diogenes and Heraclitus "divine" <note>original: "θείους"</note> in chapter 21 of the *Enchiridion*. 

Through theurgic virtues, men were made not only "divine," but even "fathers of God" <term>fathers of God (Greek: Θεοπάτορες / theopatores): a term used by some Neoplatonists to describe a sage who has attained such a high level of spiritual power that they can "bring forth" or manifest divine presence</term>, if we believe those good men who seem to have invented this to oppose the miracle-workers and the wonder-making <note>original: "θαυματοποιῷ"</note> faith of the Christians. Certainly, the accounts of the theurgic actions of Apollonius of Tyana, Iamblichus, and others found in the writings of Philostratus, Eunapius, Photius, and Suidas are marvelous—as are the theurgic effects of Proclus reported by our own Marinus. But however marvelous they are, they are certainly just as little credible. 

Yet Proclus himself admitted that in his own time, the Egyptian Heraiscus had advanced more deeply in this theurgic virtue. Concerning Heraiscus, Damascius (in Photius, page 588) and Suidas (under the entries for "judge" <note>original: "διαγνώμων"</note> and "Heraiscus") report certain paradoxes. One such example is that upon approaching any idol, he could immediately recognize whether it was animated by a God or not, and what kind of God inhabited the image. For unless the statue <note>original: "τὸ ἄγαλμα"</note> was "soulless and without a share of divine inspiration," <note>original: "ἄψυχον... καὶ ἄμοιρον θείας ἐπιπνοίας"</note> Heraiscus would be immediately seized by enthusiasm <note>In the ancient sense: being possessed or inspired by a god (enthusiasmos).</note> as the God moved him, and he would grow warm and be stirred in his innermost senses and soul.

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic hierarchy of virtues, contrasting human "practical" virtue with divine "theoretical" and "theurgic" virtues. It specifically details the mystical abilities of the philosopher Heraiscus to detect divine presence within religious statues.</summary>

->IO. ALB. FABRICIUS. <- ->XLV<-

[...continued from previous page] to have been. He was also able to distinguish a woman of prostituted modesty from an untouched virgin: for when such a woman spoke, his head immediately began to ache, <note>Fabricius is quoting a Greek source here.</note> because there was something in the nature of Heraiscus the philosopher that recoiled from the defilements of generation <note>Original Greek: ὅτι ἐνῆν τῇ τοῦ Ἡραΐσκου τοῦ φιλοσόφου φύσει καί τι τοὺς μολυσμοὺς τῆς γενέσεως ἀναινόμενον. In Neoplatonic thought, "generation" refers to the messy process of physical birth and material existence, which the "purified" philosopher finds repulsive.</note>. This is how the passage should be read in Photius, following the Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note>, and not "abolishing" <note>original Greek: *anairoumenon*</note>. There was something in the nature of the *Philosopher Heraiscus* that shrank from the stains of physical existence.

Furthermore, on the manner in which the "theoretical" virtues differ from the virtues of the purified soul—the "purgative," "political," and "ethical" virtues—Simplicius <note>A 6th-century philosopher and one of the last great pagan commentators on Aristotle and Plato.</note> teaches quite clearly in his *Commentary on the Enchiridion* <term>The "Manual" or "Handbook" of Stoic ethical advice</term> of Epictetus the Philosopher. This is in addition to the writings of Porphyry (in his *Sentences*, number 34), Plotinus, Macrobius, and especially our own Marinus. Simplicius says that Epictetus, in that work, recommends only the ethical and political virtues; he does not provide instruction for those who have already attained the other, more sublime types of virtue. 

Indeed, men who are destitute of all virtue—or rather, who are merely semblances of men, as most people are—have their souls completely submerged and oppressed by the filth of the body. Like brute animals, they are constantly driven off-course by pleasures and disturbances. However, through the ethical and political virtues, they are gradually led back to the human condition, so that the soul may command the body, and reason may dominate anger and desire. In this way, they can conduct themselves rightly in their private lives as well as in the Republic. 

*By these virtues, good men—as Macrobius says, following Plotinus* **) — *consult for the Republic and protect cities. By these, they venerate parents, love their children, and cherish those close to them. By these, they govern the safety of the citizens; by these, they protect allies with circumspect foresight, bind them with just liberality, and make others mindful of them through their merit. And it is the role of political prudence to direct everything one thinks or does universally according to the standard of reason, to will or do nothing except what is right, and to provide for human acts as if they were divine judges.* Prudence includes reason, intellect, circum[spection]...

*) Page 4 of the Heinsius edition.
**) Plotinus, *Enneads*, Book I, Chapter 2. Macrobius, Book I, Chapter 7, *On the Dream of Scipio*. Compare Plutarch’s book *On Ethical Virtue*.

XLVI               PROLEGOMENA

->...oversight, foresight, teachability, and caution. It is the nature of **Fortitude** to drive the soul above the fear of danger, to fear nothing except what is shameful, and to bravely endure both adversity and prosperity. Fortitude provides magnanimity, confidence, security, magnificence, constancy, endurance, and firmness. It is the nature of **Temperance** to desire nothing that brings regret, to never exceed the law of moderation, and to tame desire under the yoke of reason. Temperance is followed by modesty, reverence, abstinence, chastity, honesty, moderation, thrift, sobriety, and purity. It is the nature of **Justice** to preserve for each person what belongs to them. From justice come innocence, friendship, harmony, piety, religion, affection, and humanity. By these virtues, a good man is made first a ruler of himself and then a ruler of the State, governing human affairs justly and with foresight, while not abandoning divine matters. This is the **first and earthly <term>tetrad</term> of virtues** <note>original: "πρώτη καὶ περίγειος τετρακτὺς ἀρετῶν", referring to the four cardinal virtues: Prudence, Fortitude, Temperance, and Justice</note> according to Synesius in his 139th Letter.<-

But through the **purifying** <note>original: "καθαρτικὰς", or cathartic</note> or cleansing virtues, Simplicius teaches that a man is not merely content to subject desire and anger to reason, or the body to the soul. Instead, he clearly strives so that the soul, collected within itself, might escape the body and the body's passions; **it wishes to flee from the body and from bodily passions to gather into itself** <note>original: "φεύγειν ἀπὸ τοῦ σώματος βούλεται καὶ ἀπὸ τῶν σωματικῶν παθῶν εἰς ἑαυτὸν συννεύειν"</note>. This is what Marinus *) calls **to be released from the truly leaden weights of the material world, and to flee from the things found here** <note>original: "ἀπολύεσθαι τῶν τῆς γενέσεως ὄντως μολυβδίνων, καὶ φεύγειν τὰ ἐντεῦθεν". The "weights of generation" refers to the Neoplatonic idea that physical matter weighs down the soul.</note>. He who has already attained this state will truly be said to have reached the level of virtues which the same Macrobius calls those of the "purified and cleansed soul," and which Marinus discusses in chapter 21. Porphyry had said much regarding this type of virtue in his books *On the Ascent of the Soul*, from which Augustine brings several points to light in Book 10 of his most excellent work, *The City of God*. This is what the same Porphyry, in his *Sentences* **), calls the **death of the Philosophers**, the meditation upon which he affirms is Philosophy itself, according to—

_________________________________________________

*) Page 43. [chapter 18.]
**) Porphyry, *Sentences* 7. **Death is twofold: one is well-known, where the body is loosed from the soul; the other is that of the philosophers, where the soul is loosed from the body.** <note>original: "ὁ γοῦν θάνατος διπλοῦς, ὁ μὲν συνεγνωσμένος, λυομένου τοῦ σώματος ἀπὸ ψυχῆς· ὁ δὲ τῶν φιλοσόφων, λυομένης τῆς ψυχῆς ἀπὸ τοῦ σώματος."</note>

<meta>This page continues a discussion of Platonic and Socratic philosophy, specifically focusing on the concept of 'katharsis' or spiritual purification, citing the Neoplatonist commentator Olympiodorus.</meta>

# JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS. [space] 47

...Plato *) [and] Socrates. Since, however, the philosophers realized that that purification <note>original: "katharsin," referring to the cleansing of the soul from bodily passions</note> could be reached gradually in different ways, it might perhaps not be out of place here to record the words of Olympiodorus <note>A 6th-century philosopher in Alexandria known for his commentaries on Plato</note> concerning the three-fold purification—Pythagorean, Socratic, and Peripatetic or Stoic—taken from his unpublished commentary on Plato’s *Alcibiades*:

There are three modes of purification: Pythagorean, Socratic, and Peripatetic or Stoic. 

And the Stoic mode heals opposites through opposites: bringing desire to bear against anger <note>original: "thymos," often translated as spiritedness or wrath</note> and thus softening it, or anger against desire, thus strengthening it and leading it toward the more courageous, just like bent rods; when people wish to straighten them, they bend them in the opposite direction so that, from the reversal to the opposite, the proper proportion <note>original: "symmetron"</note> might appear. In this way, they also practiced bringing harmony to the soul by such a method. 

The Pythagorean mode is that which commands giving in slightly to the passions and tasting them as if with the tip of a finger—what physicians call "a little less." For they said that those who are inflamed by some passion do not turn away from it unless they act upon it. Thus Athena did in the case of Pandarus; when he wished to break his oath, she gave in to him <note>Referring to an episode in Homer's Iliad</note>, which is why in the end he is depicted being punished in the tongue, which had served as his instrument for the perjury. 

The Socratic mode of purification shifts from like things to like things; if someone is a lover of money, it says, "Learn what true self-sufficiency is"; if a lover of pleasure, "Learn what divine ease is," and simply all the things mentioned before. And this kind of method is superior to the others. For the first [Stoic] heals evil with evil—if indeed passion is healed by passion—while the second [Pythagorean] does not leave the soul unstained because of the contact with the passions. 

For one illustrating these same three kinds of purifications <note>original: "katharseon"</note>, please see Daniel Heinsius <note>A famous Dutch Renaissance scholar</note> in his book on the satires of Horace.

***

*) In the *Phaedo* and Book 7 of the *Republic*. Add Macrobius, Book I, chapter 13, on the *Dream [of Scipio]*; Julian, *Oration* 6, page 190; Nazianzen <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, a Church Father</note>, *Epistle* 70; and from more recent authors, Holstenius, page 48, on the *Life of Porphyry*; Cudworth <note>Ralph Cudworth, a Cambridge Platonist</note>, page 792; Johann Ludwig Fabricius, page 49 and following, *Theological Works*; Johannes Faes on Gyraldus, page 706 of the second edition.

<summary>This page records a passage from Olympiodorus describing three philosophical methods for purifying the soul: the Stoic method of balancing opposing passions, the Pythagorean method of moderate indulgence, and the superior Socratic method of redirecting desires toward divine virtues.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion regarding the Neoplatonic stages of soul purification, specifically drawing on the unpublished commentaries of Proclus to bridge the gap between human ethics and divine contemplation.</meta>

XLVIII          PROLEGOMENA

On pages 44 and following, our Proclus mentions three other more sublime types of soul purifications in his likewise unpublished commentary on the same *Alcibiades* with these words: "The true purification of the soul is threefold: one through the ritual arts <note>original: *telesthikē*; refers to theurgic rites intended to purify the spirit</note>, of which Socrates speaks in the *Phaedrus*; another through philosophy, of which much has been said in the *Phaedo* (for there, wisdom <note>original: *phronesis*</note> and each of the other virtues is called a kind of purification); and the third is through this science of <term>dialectic</term> <note>The Socratic method of logical questioning and refutation</note> (discussed in *Alcibiades I*), which leads to contradiction and refutes the inconsistency of doctrines, delivering us from <term>double ignorance</term> <note>A state of not knowing, and being unaware of one's own ignorance</note>." 

Just as the soul frees itself from the body through "purifying" <note>original: *kathartikas*</note> virtues, so through the "theoretical" virtues—which Porphyry calls virtues of the soul operating intellectually *)—it yearns to go even higher, striving to overcome its own nature and become superior to itself, "surpassing its own rational life," as Simplicius says in the mentioned passage, "and wishing to belong entirely to the higher powers." Not content with a life in accordance with reason or worthy of a human being, as great as that may be, it strives to belong to the Gods and to lead a divine life. In these states, it is a sacrilege for the passions or disturbances of the mind even to be named; while the ethical and political virtues soften these passions, and the purifying virtues now remove them, the virtues of the "purified soul" consign them to deep oblivion. 

However, the philosophers warn that two things must be carefully observed regarding these theoretical virtues: first, that those who have not yet aspired to the lower virtues should not rashly boast of or claim the higher ones for themselves. "And otherwise it is necessary," says Philo the Jew **) in his book *On Fugitives*, "that those who see fit to lay claim to divine rights should first fulfill human ones. For it is great folly to suppose one will reach the greater things while being unable to master the lesser." And a little before: "for the practical life must be a sort of preliminary contest <note>original: *proagōna*</note> to the more perfect contest of the theoretical life..."

***

*) Porphyry, *Sentences*, number 34, page 237, Cambridge edition.
**) Philo, page 356, Geneva edition. Compare his book *On the Contemplative Life*.

<summary>This page details Proclus's three paths to soul purification—ritual, philosophy, and dialectic—and emphasizes the Neoplatonic progression from human "political" virtues to divine "theoretical" ones. It includes a stern warning from Philo Judaeus that one must master human duties before attempting to reach for the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, soul purification, dialectic, Neoplatonism, Philo Judaeus, theoretical virtues, Porphyry, ethics, double ignorance, contemplative life</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Neoplatonic hierarchy of virtues as presented by Johann Albert Fabricius, likely commenting on Marinus’s biography of the philosopher Proclus. The previous context likely established the higher "theoretical" or contemplative virtues.</meta>

# J. ALB. FABRICIUS. 49

...it is good to have struggled first. <note>original Greek: καλὸν πρότερον διαθλῆσαι (kalon proteron diathlēsai)</note> The second point is that those who have been established in that sublime rank of the <term>theoretical life</term> <note>The "theoretical life" (bios theoretikos) refers to a life dedicated to contemplation and the study of divine truths, rather than "theory" in the modern sense.</note> should nevertheless remember that they do not yet dwell in the Elysian Fields, <note>In mythology, the paradise for the virtuous; here meaning a state of perfect, undisturbed peace.</note> but are still moving among men as long as they live this life. Therefore, they should bear with those who are weaker and learn that wise "art of Proteus" <note>Proteus was a sea-god capable of changing his shape; the "art of Proteus" refers to the ability to adapt one's behavior to suit different people or circumstances.</note> which Synesius praises in his 136th letter: they must learn **to live among men not in a divine manner, *) but in a political manner,** <note>original Greek: συνεῖναι τοῖς ἀνθρώποις οὐ θείως, ἀλλὰ πολιτικῶς. Here, "political" refers to civic or social engagement.</note> lest they deter others by their own paradoxical way of living and become a nuisance to them. Otherwise, they might fall into arrogance and an insolent contempt for others—only to be despised and mocked in return—and eventually be avoided, rendering the study of wisdom hateful and detested beyond reason.

It remains for us to speak briefly about the *physical virtues*, which are mentioned in the very first place by our author Marinus and complete the lowest level of the hierarchy. Properly speaking, these are not "virtues" that proceed from a person's reason and will, but rather endowments with which the human body is adorned by nature so that it may provide a convenient instrument for the soul. Such things certainly include health, a good condition of the senses, strength and agility, and finally an excellent appearance and a harmonious proportion of the limbs. This class of virtues is called by Proclus, in his commentary on the *Alcibiades*, **a sketch drawn by nature and an imperfect, final trace of the true good.** <note>original Greek: ἐσκιαγραφημένον ὑπὸ τῆς φύσεως καὶ ἀτελὲς ἔσχατον ἴχνος τοῦ ἀληθοῦς</note>

And let these remarks suffice concerning the four virtues and their various degrees, in which Marinus shows his teacher [Proclus] to have excelled—just as Ammianus Marcellinus wrote concerning the Emperor Julian in his 25th book, chapter 4.

***

*) Hierocles (on page 309) mentions two other types of practical life worthy of a philosopher: "Let us establish two forms of the practical: the **political** and the **telestic**; <note>The "telestic" life refers to the practice of sacred rites, initiations, and "theurgy" intended to purify the soul.</note> the one purifying us through the virtues, the other cutting away material fantasies through sacred methods." <note>original Greek: τοῦ δὲ πρακτικοῦ δύο θῶμεν εἴδη, πολιτικὸν καὶ τελεστικόν, τὸ μὲν διὰ τῶν ἀρετῶν ἀποκαθαῖρον ἡμᾶς, τὸ δὲ διὰ τῶν ἱερῶν μεθόδων τὰς ὑλικὰς φαντασίας ἀποτέμνον.</note> In this passage, he opposes the **telestic**—that is, a life occupied with performing sacred rites and mysteries—to the **political**, or a life lived according to the standard of moral virtues. Synesius, however, opposes to the political life a **divine** way of living, which consists in the constant exercise of the theoretical virtues.

<summary>Fabricius explains that philosophers must balance their high spiritual contemplations with social grace (the "art of Proteus") and defines "physical virtues" as natural bodily perfections that serve as tools for the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Proclus, Marinus, Synesius, Hierocles, hierarchy of virtues, theoretical life, political life, physical virtues, telestic rites</keywords>

->### LII PREFACE BY JOHANN ALBERT FABRICIUS.<-

For since there are, as the wise <note>original: "sapientes"; Fabricius refers here to the classical philosophers, particularly the Stoics and Platonists, who established the canon of the four virtues.</note> define them, four principal virtues—**temperance, prudence, justice, and fortitude**—and others that accompany them from the outside, such as **military skill, authority, good fortune** <term>Good fortune (felicitas): In a classical context, this refers to a divinely favored success or prosperity rather than mere personal happiness.</term>, **and liberality**, he <note>Referring to the philosopher Proclus, the subject of the biography.</note> cultivated all of them individually with intense zeal. The learned writer <note>Marinus of Neapolis, the author of the biography.</note> then pursues this in greater detail, believing that a man is abundantly praised when he is honored in such a way. Moreover, it was not unusual for Christian princes to be extolled with this same kind of praise; witness the old image of **Charles the Bald** <note>Charles the Bald (823–877) was a Carolingian Emperor; Fabricius is showing that classical "virtue" rhetoric persisted into the Christian Middle Ages.</note>, which features accompanying images of the four cardinal virtues, as mentioned by **Jean Mabillon** <note>A famous French Benedictine monk and scholar (1632–1707) who founded the study of ancient documents.</note> in Volume 1 of the *Italian Museum*, page 71 and following.

But at last, it is time for me to send you, Reader, to Marinus himself. I should first advise you that the divisions into chapters, which you see marking this *Life of Proclus*, are not ancient, but were recently added by me to make it easier to cite and find specific passages, and also for the pleasure of the readers. Most people, much like travelers on a journey, find themselves somehow delighted and refreshed by these kinds of intervals <note>Fabricius is explaining that he added chapter breaks to the original continuous ancient prose to make the text less daunting for 18th-century readers.</note>.

# MARINUS OF NEAPOLIS
## PROCLUS
### OR CONCERNING HAPPINESS
->1.<-

If I were looking toward the greatness of soul or the other merits <margin>p. 1.</margin> of Proclus, the philosopher who lived in our time, <margin>Fabric. Edition</margin> <note>This refers to the 1700 edition of this text by Johann Albert Fabricius, a landmark in classical scholarship.</note> and also toward the preparation and the power of speech possessed by those who ought to record his life—and if, in addition to these, I considered my own insignificance in words—it would have seemed right to me to keep silence. I would have preferred not to "jump beyond the line" <note>original: ὑπὲρ τὰ ἐσκαμμένα ἅλλεσθαι; a Greek proverb derived from the long jump, meaning to overreach or attempt something beyond one's capacity.</note> as the saying goes, nor to cast myself into such danger of speaking. 

But as it is, I am not measuring my efforts by that standard. Instead, I am mindful that even in the temples, those who approach the altars do not all perform their sacred rites with equal means. Some prepare themselves for communion with the gods to whom the altars belong <margin>p. 2.</margin> by means of bulls or goats and other such offerings, and indeed they craft their hymns more elegantly, some in meter and others without it. Others, however, have none of these things to bring forward, yet they offer only a sacrificial cake <term>sacrificial cake: a "popanon," a round, flat bread used in ancient Greek religious offerings</term> and perhaps a grain of frankincense, making their invocations with a brief address; yet they enjoy a favorable hearing no less than the others.

Considering these things—and further fearing, in the words of Ibycus <note>Ibycus was a Greek lyric poet; Marinus is paraphrasing his sentiment about the fear of failing a moral duty for the sake of human reputation.</note>, lest I might "exchange honor from men" by sinning—not against the gods, as he said, but against a wise man—I have decided to proceed. For I fear it might not even be holy for me alone among his companions <term>companions: "homilētōn," those who associated with and studied under a master</term> to remain silent, and not to record the truths about him according to my ability, even though I perhaps ought to speak before all the others. And perhaps I will not even lose the honor of men <margin>p. 3.</margin> (for they will not think at all that I am avoiding arrogance, but rather that I am silent through laziness...

<summary>In this opening passage, Marinus of Neapolis introduces his biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, humbly comparing his modest writing to a simple religious offering made to the gods.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus of Neapolis, Proclus, biography, Neoplatonism, religious sacrifice, Ibycus, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the biography of Proclus, the Neoplatonic philosopher, written by his student Marinus. The previous section likely established the author's motivation for writing.</meta>

2    **MARINUS' PROCLUS. 1. 2. 3.**

...some infirmity of the intellect or even some more terrible sickness of the soul, and so fail in my intended goal; for all these reasons, I have decided to fully commit myself to writing down some of the philosopher’s countless advantages during his life, and generally everything truly recorded about him.

**2.**
I shall begin my account not in the usual manner of professional writers, who arrange their discourse in order by chapters, but I will place the happiness <note>original: *eudaimonia*, often translated as "flourishing" or "well-being" rather than just a fleeting emotion.</note> of this blessed man as the most fitting foundation, I believe, for my words. For I consider him to have been the most fortunate of all men celebrated in recent history—I do not speak only of the happiness unique to the wise, even though he possessed this most exceptionally, nor merely that his virtue was sufficient for a good life; nor, on the other hand, do I mean only that "good luck" praised by the masses, even though this too was granted to him by fate more than to almost any other man. 
<margin>p. 4.</margin>
For he was abundantly supplied with all those things called "external goods." Rather, I wish to describe a certain perfect happiness, entirely without lack, composed of both of these aspects.

**3.**
First, then, we shall divide the <term>virtues</term> <note>The Neoplatonists viewed virtues as a hierarchy or ladder of spiritual development.</note> into their classes: natural, ethical, and political, and then those above these—the purifying and the theoretical—and those indeed called <term>theurgic</term> <note>From the Greek *theourgia*, meaning "god-work." These were ritual practices intended to purify the soul and unite it with the divine.</note>. 
<margin>p. 5.</margin>
We shall remain silent about those even higher than these, as they are ranked beyond human reach, and make our beginning from the natural virtues. These are the qualities that belong innately to those who have them from birth, and they were all present from the very beginning in this blessed man whom we celebrate; traces of them were clearly visible even in his final and <note>original: "ὀστρεώδει" (*ostreōdei*). This is a famous Platonic metaphor from the *Phaedrus*, comparing the physical body to an oyster shell that encases and restricts the soul.</note> "oyster-like" shell. First among these was a surpassing excellence of the senses, which they call "bodily wisdom," and especially of the most precious senses...

<summary>Marinus outlines his plan to praise the philosopher Proclus by focusing on his "perfect happiness," which combined internal virtue with external good fortune. He begins a systematic review of Proclus's virtues, starting with his natural, physical gifts and sharp senses.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Neoplatonism, eudaimonia, virtues, theurgy, Platonic metaphors, biography</keywords>

### MARINUS: THE LIFE OF PROCLUS, Chapter 3

->3<-

...senses of sight and hearing, which were indeed given by the gods to humanity for the sake of philosophy and well-being <note>The Greek term "eu einai" refers to living well or flourishing, not just surviving</note>; for him, these remained unimpaired throughout his entire life. The second [physical virtue] was a bodily strength, unaffected by winter storms and summer heat, and left unharmed by a meager <margin>p. 6.</margin> or neglected diet and those labors which he exhausted himself with by night and day, while praying, unfolding the doctrines <note>original: "dogmata"</note>, writing, and meeting with his associates—accomplishing each task with such intensity as if he were practicing that one thing alone. One might reasonably call such a power "physical courage."

A third bodily virtue belonged to him: that which is ranked under temperance <term>sophrosyne: a virtue of moderation, self-control, and excellence of mind</term>, which people think right to observe in relation to physical beauty—and with good reason. For just as temperance is observed in the harmony and agreement of the powers of the soul, so too is physical beauty seen in a certain symmetry of the body's organic parts. He was exceedingly lovely to look upon; <margin>p. 7.</margin> for not only was his symmetry well-proportioned, but the vital light <note>original: "phōs zōtikon"</note> blooming from his soul upon his body shone out with a wonderful brilliance, impossible to describe in words. Indeed, he was so beautiful to see that none of the painters were able to capture his likeness; although all the portraits of him in circulation are quite beautiful, they still fall far short of mimicking the truth of his actual form.

They wish for the fourth virtue, health, to correspond to justice <note>original: "dikaiosyne"</note> in the soul, and for this to be a kind of "justice in the body," just as the other is in the soul. For that state which makes the parts of the soul free from internal conflict is nothing other than justice; and that which, out of the disordered physical elements, makes them ordered and in agreement with one another, is called "health" even by the followers of Asclepius <note>The "Asclepiads" were a guild of physicians who traced their lineage to the god of healing, Asclepius</note>. And <margin>p. 8.</margin> this health had been so well-crafted in him from his very cradle that he could say that, however many times his body was led into illness, it was only twice or perhaps thrice in a long life of seventy-five whole years. A sufficient proof of this is what I myself observed in the...

# MARINUS’S *PROCLUS*, Chapters 4, 5, and 6

### 4. (continued)

...this natural <term>fitness <note>Greek: *epitedeiotes*. This refers to an inherent readiness or suitability of the soul to receive higher virtues.</note></term>, which one should call by no other name than virtue—and specifically, courage. From these very facts, it is surely already clear to everyone, even those who did not personally experience his natural genius, that from his earliest youth he loved justice. He was just and gentle, and in no way difficult to deal with, unsociable, or generally unjust. Indeed, he appeared to us to be naturally orderly, not a lover of money, nor stingy, nor a boaster, nor a coward.

### 5.

As for his quickness in learning and the fertility of his soul, perhaps it is superfluous to list them—especially for those who saw or heard how many beautiful teachings he was "pregnant" with, and how many he himself brought forth and gave out into the light <margin>p. 12</margin> for humanity. Indeed, he alone seemed to have drunk nothing from the "cup of forgetfulness" <note>A reference to the River Lethe in Greek mythology; souls were said to drink from it to forget their previous lives before being reborn. Marinus suggests Proclus retained divine knowledge.</note>. Because he possessed such a powerful memory, he never found learning difficult, nor did he suffer the plight of those who forget. Instead, he accepted himself as one who had a firm grasp on his studies, and he embraced only the activity related to them. He was far removed from a crude or harsh nature and was kindred to better things. In his social gatherings, his sacred feasts, and his other activities, he was refined and charming—and this without losing his dignity—always attracting those who spent time with him and sending them away in higher spirits.

### 6.

Being endowed from the beginning with all these and other natural gifts, <margin>p. 13</margin> he was born to his mother Marcella, who was joined in legal marriage to Patricius. Both of these parents were Lycians <note>Lycia was a region in what is now southern Turkey; Proclus’s family was part of the intellectual elite there.</note>, outstanding in both lineage and virtue. The patron goddess of Byzantium <note>The "city-protector" of Byzantium (Constantinople) was Athena. Marinus portrays the goddess herself as Proclus's spiritual midwife.</note> received him and, in a sense, acted as his midwife. She was the cause of his very existence, as he was born in her city; but later in time, she also took care of his well-being <note>The Greek contrasts *einai* (being/existence) with *eu einai* (well-being/living well), a classic Platonic distinction.</note> when he had already reached the age of a boy and a young man. Indeed, she appeared to him in a dream <margin>p. 14</margin> and urged him toward philosophy. From this, I believe, it happened that...

<summary>This passage details the natural virtues and intellectual brilliance of the philosopher Proclus, emphasizing his perfect memory and social grace. It also recounts his birth in Byzantium to noble Lycian parents and the divine intervention of the goddess Athena, who supposedly appeared in a dream to set him on the path of philosophy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Proclus by his pupil Marinus, moving from the circumstances of his birth to his early education and divine interventions in his youth.</meta>

6 ->MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. 5. 7. 8.<-

...and great was his kinship with the goddess <note>Athena, whom Proclus considered his patron and protector throughout his life</note>, so that he celebrated her rites in an exceptional way and pursued her laws with a more divinely inspired zeal. For a time, his parents brought him, after his birth, to their own fatherland, the sacred Xanthus of Apollo <note>An ancient city in Lycia, modern-day Turkey</note>, which had become his fatherland by a certain divine lot. For it was necessary, I believe, that he who was destined to lead all the sciences should share in the nourishment and education provided by the Leader of the Muses <note>Apollo, the god of music, prophecy, and healing, often called "Musagetes" or Leader of the Muses</note>. There, being guided by the most beautiful customs, he acquired the moral virtues, becoming accustomed to love what one ought to do and to turn away from what is not of such a kind.

                                7.

            At that time, his kinship with the beloved of the gods was also clearly demonstrated. p.15. For once, when his body was failing from a disease, and he was already in a difficult and incurable state, there appeared by his bed a boy who seemed exceptionally young and beautiful to look upon. One might have guessed that he was **Telesphorus** <note>original: "Τελεσφόρον" — the minor god of convalescence and son of Asclepius, typically depicted as a small hooded child</note> even before he spoke his name. Yet, having stated who he was and uttering his name, and having touched his head (for he stood somewhere there, resting by the pillow), he suddenly made him healthy from being sick, and thus he vanished from him. This divine event was granted to the youth because he was so beloved by the gods.

                                8.

            After having attended a grammarian in Lycia for a short time, he departed for Alexandria in Egypt, p.16. bringing with him even then the specific traits of moral virtue, by which he won over the teachers there. **Leonas** the Sophist, at least—an Isaurian, I believe, by birth, and well-regarded among the many of his profession in Alexandria—not only shared his own lectures with him but also saw fit to have him as a housemate, and arranged for him to live with his own wife and children, as if he had become a true son to him. He also made him known to those who held the reins of Egypt <note>The Roman provincial governors or high-ranking administrative officials</note>, who themselves admired the youth's natural character...

### MARINUS: THE LIFE OF PROCLUS (Chapters 8–9)

->Chapter 8 (Continued)<-

...keenness of mind and the <term>noble character</term> <note>original: "kalokagathia," a Greek ideal of being both beautiful in form and virtuous in character</note> of his disposition, they placed him among their most beloved. He also attended the school of the grammarian Orion, who was descended from the Egyptian priestly caste <margin>p. 17.</margin> and had studied the nuances of his craft with such moderation that he even labored to produce his own original writings, leaving behind useful works for those who followed him. Proclus also frequented the classes of the Latin schools <note>original: "Roman schools." In Alexandria, these schools taught the Latin language and Roman law to those pursuing careers in the imperial administration.</note>, and in a short time he showed great progress in these subjects as well. Indeed, he was initially being steered toward his father’s profession, in which the latter had become very famous, having properly practiced the legal art in the capital city <note>original: "the queen of cities," referring to Constantinople.</note>. It seemed that while he was young, he took great pleasure in rhetoric, having not yet tasted philosophical discourse; he gained a very high reputation in it, and was a source of great wonder both to his fellow students and to the teachers themselves, both for his eloquence and for the ease with which he mastered his lessons. <margin>p. 18.</margin> Furthermore, he possessed the habits and the studiousness of a teacher rather than a student.

->Chapter 9<-

While he was still a student, Leonas the Sophist arranged for Proclus to travel with him on a visit to Byzantium <note>modern-day Istanbul.</note>. Leonas made this trip as a favor to his friend Theodorus, who was then the governor of Alexandria, a man of refined character, magnificence, and a lover of philosophy. Although Proclus was still young, he followed his teacher quite eagerly so that his rhetorical studies would not be interrupted. But to speak more truly, a certain "Good Fortune" was leading him back again to the cause of his birth <note>Proclus was born in Constantinople; "Good Fortune" or Tyche is seen here as a divine guiding force.</note>. For while he was there, the Goddess <note>The Goddess Athena, who was the patron of both Athens and Proclus personally.</note> appeared to him and exhorted him toward philosophy, urging him to look toward the schools in Athens. <margin>p. 19.</margin> Having first returned to Alexandria, he said "farewell" to rhetoric and the other subjects he had previously been passionate about, and instead pursued the company of the philosophers there. He attended the lectures on Aristotle given by the philosopher Olympiodorus, whose fame was widespread; for mathematical studies, he entrusted himself to Heron, a god-fearing man who possessed a perfect mastery of the methods of education. These men also...

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus. Here, we see Proclus outgrowing his teachers in Alexandria and receiving a divine calling to move to Athens, the heart of the Platonic tradition.</meta>

8          MARINUS: PROCLUS. **9. 10.**

p.20. The men admired the character of the youth so much that **Olympiodorus** <note>Olympiodorus the Elder, a Peripatetic philosopher teaching in Alexandria</note>, who had a young daughter also raised in the study of philosophy, wished to betroth her to him. **Leon** <note>The OCR reads "Hero," but historically this refers to Leonas, the rhetorician who brought Proclus to Alexandria</note> also trusted him with all the secrets of his own **divine worship** <term>theosebeia: the practical, ritualistic side of religious life and piety</term>, and frequently invited him to share his hearth. While listening to Olympiodorus, a man of powerful speech whose ease and agility in lecturing made him difficult for most listeners to follow, Proclus would leave the session and recite from memory everything that had been discussed to his companions, using the very same words. This was despite the great length of the lectures, as I was told by one of his fellow students, **Ulpian of Gaza**, a man who also lived his life in a sufficiently philosophical manner. He also mastered **Aristotle’s** writings on logic with the greatest ease, even though they are difficult for those who encounter them even for a simple reading.

**10.**

After spending time studying with these men in Alexandria and benefiting from their company as much as their abilities allowed—and since, during a collective reading of a certain text, it seemed to him that they were no longer explaining the philosopher's <note>Aristotle's</note> thought in a worthy manner—he looked down upon those schools. At the same time, he remembered the **divine vision** and command he had received in Byzantium <note>Proclus reportedly had a vision of the goddess Athena urging him to travel to Athens</note>, and so he set sail for **Athens**. He was escorted by a procession of all the learned ones and the gods and **good spirits** <term>daimonon: in Greek philosophy, these are tutelary spirits or lesser divine beings that mediate between gods and humans</term> who oversee philosophy. For in order that the **Platonic succession** <term>diadoche: the continuous line of heads of the Platonic Academy</term> might be preserved pure and unadulterated, the gods led him to the **Overseer of Philosophy** <note>The goddess Athena</note>. This was clearly shown by the very circumstances of his arrival: the divine signs that occurred clearly foretold to him his inheritance from his father <note>likely referring to his spiritual lineage</note> and the "vote from above" regarding the succession. For when he put in at **Piraeus** <note>the harbor of Athens</note> and word of his arrival reached the city, **Nicolaus**—who later became famous for his skill as a sophist, but was at that time a student under the teachers in Athens—came down to the harbor as if to meet an old acquaintance, intending to welcome him and act as his guide...

->MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. Chapters 10, 11. Page 9<-

...countryman; for Nikolaos was also a Lycian. He then led him toward the city. Proclus felt the fatigue of the journey along the road, and when they were near the <term>Sokrateion</term> <note>A shrine or monument dedicated to the philosopher Socrates; its exact location in Athens is a subject of scholarly debate, but it was clearly a landmark at the time.</note>—not yet knowing or having heard that honors were paid to Socrates in that place—he asked Nikolaos to wait there for a short time and sit down, and also to provide him with some water if he could find it anywhere; for he was gripped by a great thirst, as he said. Nikolaos readily did this for him, and fetched it not from some other place, but from that very sacred spot; for the spring was not far from the statue of Socrates. As Proclus drank, <margin>p. 24</margin> Nikolaos—noticing the omen for the first time—remarked that he had been established at the <term>Sokrateion</term> and had drunk <term>Attic water</term> <note>The "water of Athens." This is seen as a symbolic baptism into the intellectual life of the city.</note> for the first time from that place. Proclus then rose up, offered a prayer of salutation, and proceeded toward the city. When he had ascended to the <term>Akron</term> <note>The Acropolis, the high "citadel" of Athens where the Parthenon stands.</note>, the gatekeeper met him at the entrance, just as he was about to place the keys upon the gates; the man said to him—and I shall speak the very words of the fellow: "Truly, if you had not come, I would have closed up." <note>original: „Ἀληθῶς, εἰ μὴ ἦλθες, ἔκλειον.“</note> What sign could have been clearer than this? It required no judgment from a <term>Polles</term> or a <term>Melampus</term> <note>Famous legendary seers and diviners of Greek mythology.</note> or anyone of their kind to interpret it.

### XI.

Having looked down upon rhetorical studies even here—though he was sought after by the rhetoricians as if he had come for that very purpose—he first encountered <margin>p. 25</margin> Syrianus, the son of Philoxenus, among the philosophers. <note>Syrianus was the head of the Platonic Academy and would become Proclus's beloved mentor.</note> Also present at the gathering was Lachares, a man who was deeply steeped in philosophical discourse and a fellow-student of the philosopher himself, yet who had inspired as much wonder for his own <term>sophistic</term> <note>The art of public speaking and rhetoric.</note> skill as Homer had for his poetry. As I said, he too was present; the time happened to be late afternoon. Indeed, while they were conversing, the sun was setting and the moon was appearing for the first time after its conjunction. <margin>p. 26</margin> They then tried to dismiss the young man, addressing him as a stranger, so that they might have leisure by themselves to worship the goddess <note>Likely referring to the goddess of the moon, such as Hecate or Artemis, whom the Neoplatonists held in high regard.</note>. But Proclus, having walked a little way off and seeing the moon appearing from that same house, took off his [sandals] right there... <note>Proclus removes his shoes as a sign of religious reverence, showing he already shares the same piety as the masters he just met.</note>

# MARINUS: PROCLUS. 11. 12.

...while they were looking on, he saluted the Goddess <note>The "Goddess" refers to Athena, the patron of Athens. Proclus’s arrival at the Acropolis was seen as a divine omen.</note>. At this, Lachares was struck with amazement by the young man's **boldness** <term>boldness</term> <note>original: *parrhesia*, meaning frankness or the courage to act/speak freely.</note>, and said to the philosopher Syrianus that this was the very thing divinely spoken by Plato regarding "great natures": "For such a person," he said, "will either become a great good, or at least the opposite." <note>A reference to Plato’s *Republic* (491e), suggesting that powerful minds are never mediocre; they are either exceptionally virtuous or exceptionally wicked.</note> These and other such signs—to mention but a few out of many—occurred to the philosopher by divine providence when he had just arrived in Athens.

<margin>p. 27.</margin>

### 12.

Syrianus then took him and introduced him to the **Great Plutarch**, the son of Nestorius. <note>Plutarch of Athens (died c. 432 AD) was the "Diadochos" or successor heading the Platonic Academy; he is distinct from the more famous biographer Plutarch of Chaeronea.</note> When Plutarch saw the youth, who was not yet twenty years old, and heard of his choice of life and his great yearning for the philosophical path, he was greatly pleased with him. He readily gave himself over to teaching the young man philosophical lessons, even though he was hindered by his advanced age; for he was already a very old man.

<margin>p. 28.</margin>

Proclus therefore read under him Aristotle’s *On the Soul* and Plato’s *Phaedo*. The great man even encouraged him to keep written records of what was said, using the young man's ambition as an instrument. He claimed that once the school notes were completed, they would be circulated as **Proclus’s Commentaries on the Phaedo**.

Furthermore, he delighted in the youth once he had seen his aptitude for noble things; he constantly called him "child" and made him a **sharer of his hearth** <note>Meaning he took Proclus into his own home to live as a member of the family.</note>. And when he saw that Proclus was persevering in his **abstinence from ensouled things** <term>abstinence from ensouled things</term> <note>A term for vegetarianism, common among Neoplatonists who believed that avoiding meat helped purify the soul and detached it from material concerns.</note>, he urged him not to abstain from these entirely, so that his body might remain a fit servant for the activities of the soul.

<margin>p. 29.</margin>

He gave these same instructions regarding the young man’s diet to the philosopher Syrianus. But Syrianus replied to the old man—as our **divine leader** <note>Marinus uses the term "divine head/leader" to refer to Syrianus, Proclus’s primary teacher.</note> used to recount to us—saying: "Let him learn as much as I wish while living temperately, and then, if he wants, let him die." In this way, his teachers cared for him in every respect.

The old man lived for barely two years after Proclus’s arrival, and as he was dying, he commended the young man to his successor, Syrianus, just as he did for his own grandson, Archiadas. And he...

# MARINUS’S PROCLUS. 12. 13. 14. 11

...having taken him in, he <note>Syrianus, the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens</note> not only assisted him even more greatly in his studies, but also made him a housemate for the future and a partner in the philosophical life. He had found Proclus to be the kind of student and successor he had long sought to have, one capable <margin>p. 30</margin> of receiving his vast number of teachings and divine doctrines.

### 13.

In less than two full years, Proclus read with him all of Aristotle’s treatises: the logical, ethical, political, and physical works, as well as the theological science <note>In this context, "theological science" refers to Aristotle's *Metaphysics*.</note> that transcends these. Having been sufficiently guided through these works—as if through certain preliminary rites <note>original: "proteleia"—rituals performed before a main initiation</note> and lesser mysteries—Syrianus then led him into the Platonic initiation <note>original: "mystagogia"—the guidance of a person into sacred mysteries</note>. He did not do this in a disorganized way or "with an over-stepping foot," as the oracle says, but rather enabled Proclus to behold <margin>p. 31</margin> the truly divine rites found in Plato with the clear eyes of the soul and the pure gaze of the intellect. 

By employing sleepless practice and diligence both night and day, and by writing down the teachings in summary form along with his own critiques, Proclus made such progress in a short time that by the age of twenty-eight, he had authored many other works, including his commentaries on the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the creation of the universe; Proclus's commentary on it remains one of the most significant works of late antiquity</note>, which are truly elegant and full of scientific knowledge. From this type of training, his character was even more finely adorned, as he had acquired the virtues alongside scientific understanding.

### 14.

Furthermore, he took up the political virtues from Aristotle’s political writings and from the *Laws* and *Republic* of Plato. <margin>p. 32</margin> However, so that he would not seem to be a man of words alone in these matters without ever touching upon action—and since he was personally prevented from engaging in politics because he was occupied with greater things—he encouraged Archiadas <note>A wealthy and influential Athenian who was a friend and patron of the philosophical school</note>, a friend of the gods, to take up this task. Proclus simultaneously taught him and guided him in the political virtues and their methods, and, like those who cheer on runners, he urged him to take charge of the common interests of his entire city...
