->12      MARINUS: THE LIFE OF PROCLUS. 14. 15.<-

[Proclus] encouraged him, and urged him to benefit each person individually in every form of virtue, but especially in the form of justice. And through his own actions, he inspired a certain zeal in him, demonstrating to him a spirit of generosity regarding money and a sense of magnificence <note>Greek: *megaloprepeia* — a virtue involving large-scale, noble spending for the public good</note> by giving gifts sometimes to friends, and at other times to relatives, to guests, <margin>p.33.</margin> and to fellow citizens. He proved himself in every way to be superior to the mere possession of wealth. He also bestowed no small gifts upon public works; and finally, following the example of Archiadas, he left properties to the cities—both to his own homeland <note>Proclus was born in Constantinople but raised in Xanthus, Lycia</note> and to the city of Athens. Such a man was the truth-loving Archiadas shown to be, both by his own nature and through his friendship with Proclus, so that the people of our own time, whenever they wish to speak of him, do not call him anything other than "the most pious Archiadas," speaking with auspicious words.

->15.<-

<margin>p.34.</margin> Sometimes the philosopher himself also took part in political deliberations. He attended the public assemblies of the city, introducing his opinions with great wisdom, and met with the governing officials regarding matters of justice. He did not merely encourage these officials, but in a certain way, he compelled them—using his "philosophical boldness" <term>Parrhesia: the Greek concept of speaking the truth fearlessly to those in power</term>—to grant what was right to every person. Furthermore, he took care of the general conduct of his students, ensuring they lived with moderation <note>Greek: *sophrosyne* — a state of self-control and balance</note>. He did not teach this through mere words, but rather practiced it through his own actions throughout his entire life, becoming a living model of moderation for others. 

As for the form of "political courage," he displayed it in a truly Herculean fashion. <margin>p.35.</margin> For when he found himself in the midst of a storm and a "triple-wave" <note>An ancient idiom meaning a massive, overwhelming danger or the peak of a crisis</note> of affairs, with "typhonic winds" <note>Metaphorical "storm-winds" representing political or religious persecution</note> blowing against the life of the law, this man swam through life with great weight and steadiness, even if it involved personal risk. Once, when he was being examined during a crisis caused by certain "sub-giants," <note>original: *hypogigantes* — Marinus uses this term to mock the anti-pagan authorities or mobs of the time, viewing them as spiritually crude enemies of the divine tradition</note> he departed from Athens just as he was, following the "divine revolution" <note>The movement of the heavens/stars, implying he took the event as a providential sign</note> of the universe. He made his journey to Asia, and this turned out to be for the greatest good. For it allowed him to avoid being left uninitiated in the older religious rites that were still preserved there...

# MARINUS, PROCLUS. 15. 16. 13

(15) They say that the **divine spirit** <note>original: "daimonion," a guiding spiritual force often mentioned by Socrates and Plato</note> engineered this journey abroad for him. He himself clearly recognized their local rituals, and if they had overlooked anything in their ceremonies due to the long passage of time, they were taught by him, as the philosopher guided them in the things of the gods more perfectly. <margin>p.36</margin> And while doing all these things and living according to them, he remained so well-hidden that not even the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, known for their disciplined and often private communal life</note>, who kept the command to **LIVE UNNOTICED** <note>original: "LATHE BIOSAS"—usually associated with Epicurus, though here attributed to the Pythagorean tradition of quiet living</note> of their leader unshaken, could surpass him. After spending only a year in Lydia, he returned again to Athens through the providence of the **philosophic goddess** <note>Athena, the patron goddess of wisdom and the city of Athens</note>. In this way, his quality of courage was established for him—by nature from the beginning, by habit, and afterwards by knowledge and the rational calculation of causes. In another way, he demonstrated his **political disposition** <term>political disposition: the virtue of civic duty and engagement in public affairs</term> through action, by writing to those in positions of power and thereby doing good for entire cities. <margin>p.37</margin> The witnesses to my words are those people who were well-treated—now the Athenians, and again the Argives and others from various nations.

16.

From this point, he also increased the pursuit of **logical studies**, both by aiding those who sought them and by demanding that the rulers provide stipends and other honors to each person according to their merit. He did not do these things without careful examination, nor as a personal favor, but he compelled those very people for whom he was concerned to attend intensely to their own studies, questioning and testing every detail of their work; for he was a capable judge of all matters. But if he found someone rather lazy in their own profession, he would rebuke them quite severely, so that he even seemed to be **most high-spirited** <note>original: "thymoeidestatos," meaning possessing a strong "thumos" or spirited, sometimes irritable nature</note> <margin>p.38</margin> and exceedingly **ambitious** <note>original: "philotimos," literally "a lover of honor"</note>, arising from his combined desire and ability to judge all things correctly. He was indeed ambitious, but he did not use ambition as a passion as others do. He was ambitious only toward virtue and the good; perhaps nothing great could ever happen among men without such energy. He was also high-spirited—I do not deny this—but at the same time he was gentle; for he would cease...

14      **MARINUS: THE LIFE OF PROCLUS**. 16. 17.

p.39. ...easily; and like the "turning of a potsherd" <note>original: "ὀστράκου περιστροφῇ" — an ancient Greek idiom referring to a swift and total reversal of direction or mind, like flipping a tile in a game</note>, he showed that his anger was as soft as wax. For in this way, so to speak, he would rebuke them, and then, moved by compassion, he would be led to benefit those very same people and intercede with the governors on their behalf.

17.

It is well that the unique quality of his compassion has come to my mind. For I believe that such a degree of it has been recorded in no other man. Although he never had experience of marriage or children—because he chose not to, even though many marriages were proposed to him that were outstanding in both lineage and wealth—remaining free of all these things, as I said, he took such care of all his companions and friends, and their respective children and wives,
p.40. as if he had become a kind of common father and the cause of their very existence; for he looked after the life of each person in every possible way. If ever one of his acquaintances was gripped by illness, he would first entreat the gods earnestly on their behalf with both rituals <note>original: "ἔργοις" (deeds/works), here referring to the performance of theurgic or religious rites</note> and hymns; then he would attend most carefully to the sufferer and gather the physicians, urging them to perform the requirements of their craft without delay. He himself would even suggest some further, exceptional treatment in these cases, and in this way he has already rescued many from the greatest dangers. How great also was his humanity toward the more capable of his servants can be seen by anyone who wishes from the will of the blessed man. But of all his acquaintances, he loved Archiadas most of all, and those related to him by birth—primarily
p.41. because of the lineage of the philosopher Plutarch <note>Plutarch of Athens (died c. 432 AD), the head of the Neoplatonist Academy and Proclus' teacher’s teacher</note>, but also because of their <term>Pythagorean friendship</term> <note>A technical term for the highest level of spiritual and intellectual bond, characterized by absolute harmony of will and the sharing of all life's aspects</note> which he established with Archiadas, having been his fellow-student and his teacher at the same time. For of the two kinds of friendship rarely recorded among the ancients, theirs seemed to be of the more earnest sort. For there was nothing Archiadas wanted that Proclus did not also want, nor conversely anything Proclus wanted that Archiadas did not.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus of Neapolis. Having previously discussed Proclus' civic virtues and his capacity for friendship, the text now transitions to his higher "purificatory" virtues and his ascetic lifestyle.</meta>

# MARINUS’ PROCLUS. 18. 19. 15

### 18.
<margin>p. 42</margin> Having now placed a fitting conclusion to the chapters concerning his **civic virtues**—which, in truth, were even greater than what has been recorded—and having sealed that account with his gift for friendship, let us move on to the **purificatory virtues**, which are of a different order than the civic ones. 

For while it is the task of the civic virtues to purify the soul in some measure and to prepare it to look after human affairs with detachment—so that it might achieve a "likeness to God," which is the soul's highest goal—not all virtues separate the soul from the body in the same way; rather, they do so to a greater or lesser degree. While the **civic purifications** <note>original: "πολιτικαὶ καθάρσεις"</note> bring order to those who remain in this world and make them better people by defining and measuring their anger and desires, <margin>p. 43</margin> and by removing false opinions entirely, the **purificatory virtues** that rank above these go further. 

They completely separate and release the soul from what are truly the "leaden weights" <note>A reference to Plato’s *Republic* (519b), where earthly desires and the sensory world are described as heavy leaden weights that pull the soul’s gaze downward toward the earth.</note> of the realm of becoming. They facilitate an unhindered escape from the things of this world. These are the very virtues that the philosopher Proclus practiced throughout his entire philosophical life. He taught them beautifully in his lectures—explaining what they are and how they come to be present in a human being—and he lived in the strictest accordance with them. 

He performed those actions which lead to the soul's separation: night and day he made use of **apotropaic rites** <note>Rituals intended to turn away evil or negative influences.</note>, ritual sprinklings, and other forms of purification. Sometimes he followed the **Orphic** traditions and sometimes the **Chaldean** <term>Chaldean: Referring to the *Chaldean Oracles*, a sacred text of the 2nd century AD that Neoplatonists believed contained divine revelations regarding the soul's ascent.</term> rites. <margin>p. 44</margin> Without hesitation, he would go down to the sea every month—sometimes even twice or three times in the same month. He endured these practices not only in the prime of his life, but he continued to observe them even as he reached the sunset of his days, fulfilling such customs as if they were sacred laws without ever skipping them.

### 19.
As for the necessary pleasures of food and drink, he treated them merely as a relief from pain, so that they would not trouble him; for he partook of very little. For the most part, he embraced **abstinence from animal flesh** <note>Proclus followed the Pythagorean and Platonic tradition of vegetarianism, believing that avoiding meat helped keep the soul "light" and unpolluted by the lower nature of animals.</note>. However, if ever a particularly pressing occasion called for the use of such things, he would only take...

It also touches on his ascetic diet and vegetarianism as a means to detach the soul from the material world.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Marinus of Neapolis, purificatory virtues, civic virtues, Orphic rites, Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, vegetarianism, ritual purification, leaden weights of generation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus. This section focuses on Proclus's religious practices and his final illness.</meta>

# MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. 19. 20.

p.45. ...he would taste even these for the sake of religious duty. He observed the fasts <term>fasts (original: "kasteias") — ritual purifications involving abstinence from certain foods or activities</term> of the Mother of the Gods <note>The "Mother of the Gods" refers to Cybele, a Phrygian goddess whose cult was central to Roman state religion</note>, which were practiced among the Romans or even earlier among the Phrygians, purifying himself every month. He also observed the Egyptian "unlucky days" <note>original: "apophradas" — days considered ill-omened in the Egyptian calendar when no business was conducted</note> even more strictly than the Egyptians themselves did. Moreover, he fasted on certain other days following a divine vision. On every "old and new" day <note>original: "enēn kai nean" — the last day of the lunar month</note> of the month, he remained without food, not even taking an evening meal. Just as he used to celebrate the New Moons <note>original: "noumenias"</note> with great splendor and sanctity, so too he continued to observe, in accordance with religious law, almost all the famous festivals of all peoples and the ancestral rites of each nation. He did not use these festivals as an excuse for rest or physical indulgence, as some others do, but rather for nights of vigil, the singing of hymns, and similar devotions.

p.46. This is clearly shown by his collection of hymns, which contains praises not only for those gods honored among the Greeks, but also

p.47. singing to **Marnas of Gaza** <note>A localized Syrian/Philistine deity often identified with Zeus</note>, to **Asclepius Leontouchos** <note>The "Lion-Holder," a specific title for the healing god Asclepius</note> of Ascalon, to **Thyandrites** (another god highly honored by the Arabs), to **Isis** who is still worshipped at Philae, and simply to all the others. Indeed, this most god-fearing man always had this saying ready at hand: "It is fitting for a philosopher not to be the worshiper of one single city, nor of the ancestral customs of a few, but to be the common <term>priest (original: "hierophanten") — literally 'one who shows holy things,' a high priest or initiator into sacred mysteries</term> of the whole world."

p.48. Such was the way his practices of self-restraint were prepared, both in a purifying and a holy manner.

## 20.

As for physical pains, he would banish them, or when they did occasionally strike, he bore them mildly and lessened them by refusing to let the highest part of himself suffer along with the body. The strength of his soul in the face of these pains was sufficiently demonstrated by his final illness.

p.49. For when he was weighed down by this sickness and gripped by intense pains, he tried to drive the suffering away. He would constantly command us to recite hymns, and while the hymns were being spoken, a total peace and stillness of his passions would occur. And what was even more marvelous: he still possessed a memory of the words being said, even though he had forgotten almost all human affairs because his <term>weakness (original: "pareseōs") — likely a reference to a stroke or paralytic exhaustion</term> had become so heavy upon him. For when we began to sing the hymns, he would complete the verses himself.

<summary>This passage details Proclus's role as a "universal priest" who honored various international deities and describes how he used sacred hymns to manage physical pain during his final illness.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Neoplatonism, Asceticism, Cybele, Egyptian religion, Marnas of Gaza, Isis, Hierophant, Philosophy and Health</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
# MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. 20. 21. 22. 17

...and the majority of the Orphic poems; for these also were things we used to read when we were with him. And he was not only so dispassionately disposed toward physical sufferings, but even more so <margin>p. 50.</margin> toward external circumstances that happened to him, and things that seemed to occur unexpectedly; so that he would say of every event that befell him: “These things are as they are; these things are customary.” This seemed to me an utterance worthy of memory, and a sufficient proof of the philosopher’s greatness of soul <note>original: "megalopsychia"—the classical virtue of "magnanimity" or having a "large soul" that is not shaken by trivialities</note>. He also restrained his anger as much as possible, so that it either did not move at all, or the rational soul was not the part that felt the anger; rather, the involuntary reaction belonged to another part <note>the lower, irrational soul</note>, and even this was slight and weak. Of natural sexual desires <note>original: "aphrodisiōn"</note>, he partook only as far, I believe, as the imagination, and even that was fleeting.

## 21.

And so, the soul of this blessed man, gathering and collecting itself from all parts into itself, <margin>p. 51.</margin> withdrew almost entirely from the body, even while it still seemed to be held by it. For to him, "wisdom" <note>original: "to phronein"</note> was no longer the "civic" kind—which is acting well regarding things that are changeable—but was pure intellect itself, acting on its own and turned toward itself, never sharing the body's opinions. "Temperance" was the refusal to associate with the lower self; it was not just the moderation of passions <term>metriopatheia: the Aristotelian ideal of feeling emotions in the "right" moderate amount</term>, but being entirely and in every way free from passion <term>apatheia: the Stoic and Neoplatonic goal of becoming unaffected by emotional disturbances</term>. "Courage" was not fearing the soul's departure from the body. Since reason and intellect were leading within him, and the lower parts no longer resisted, his whole life was adorned with "purificatory justice."

## 22.

Advancing from this form of virtue in an absolute and easy manner, and as if by some ritual step of initiation <note>original: "telestikon"</note>, <margin>p. 52.</margin> he ascended toward the greater virtues that lie beyond these, guided by a gifted nature and a scientific education. For having already been purified and rising above the world of generation <note>the material realm of birth, change, and decay</note>, and looking down upon those who merely "carry the wand" <note>original: "narthēkophorōn"; a reference to the Greek proverb "Many are the wand-bearers (ritual participants), but few are the mystics (true initiates)"</note> in this life, he began his sacred rites <note>original: "ebakkheue"; literally "to act as a Bacchic initiate" or to be in a state of divine frenzy</note> concerning the first principles...

<summary>This page describes the philosopher Proclus’s transition from "civic" virtues to "purificatory" and "theurgic" virtues, highlighting his extreme emotional detachment and his soul's withdrawal from bodily concerns.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Neoplatonism, Purificatory Virtues, Apatheia, Orphic Poems, Greatness of Soul, Temperance, Wisdom, World of Generation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his successor Marinus. The previous section likely described his ascent through the various levels of virtue, moving from the practical and political toward the contemplative and "theurgic" (divine-working) levels.</meta>

# 18 MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. 22. 23.

...[he poured] out, and he became an eyewitness of those truly blessed visions. He no longer arrived at knowledge of them through long-winded or demonstrative reasoning, but rather, as if by sight, he beheld the **p. 53.** archetypes <term>paradigms; the eternal blueprints of reality</term> within the Divine Mind through simple applications of his intellectual power. He acquired a virtue that one could no longer strictly call "prudence" <note>original: *phronēsis*; practical wisdom</note>, but would rather name "wisdom" <note>original: *sophia*; deep, theoretical insight</note>, or perhaps an even more venerable title.

Acting according to this virtue, the philosopher easily perceived all theology—both Greek and foreign <note>original: *barbarikēn*; referring to non-Greek traditions such as Egyptian or Chaldean</note>—including those truths veiled in mythical fictions. He brought these to light for those willing and able to follow, explaining everything with an inspired enthusiasm and bringing it all into harmony. He reviewed all the writings of the ancients; whatever was fruitful in them, **p. 54.** he adopted with critical judgment. However, if he found anything "wind-born" <note>original: *anemiaion*; a metaphor for a hollow or fruitless idea, like a bird's infertile "wind-egg"</note>, he rejected it entirely as a defect. Whatever was contrary to sound principles, he refuted through rigorous examination and debate.

In his lectures, he worked through every detail with both power and clarity, and he committed everything to writing. Laboring with immeasurable diligence, he delivered five lectures—sometimes even more—on the same day, and wrote about seven hundred lines of text. He also met with other philosophers as he walked, holding unrecorded evening discussions. All of this followed his nocturnal and sleepless devotions, and after **p. 55.** he had offered worship to the sun at its rising, at high noon, and as it moved toward its setting.

### 23.

He was also the father of many doctrines never known before, concerning the physical world, the intellect, and even more divine matters. For he was the first to establish that there is a class of souls capable of beholding many forms at once. This class was naturally placed in the middle, between the Divine Mind—which perceives everything simultaneously and with a single impulse—and those souls that must move from one form to another <note>this describes a hierarchy of consciousness: from the "all-at-once" divine mind to the "step-by-step" human mind</note>. Anyone who wishes may also encounter his other "offspring" <note>meaning his philosophical theories and books</note>...

MARINUS'S LIFE OF PROCLUS. 23. 24. 19

Anyone who goes through his works (which I have declined to mention at present, so as not to prolong this account by narrating every detail) will know that everything previously recorded about him is true. This is even more certain for anyone who actually saw him, had the good fortune to behold him, heard him lecturing, and listened to him delivering those most beautiful discourses when he celebrated the annual festivals of Plato and Socrates. For he did not seem to speak without divine inspiration <note>original: "epipnoia" — a state of being "breathed into" by a god, common in descriptions of Neoplatonic teachers</note>, and words literally resembling snowflakes <note>This is a classical allusion to Homer’s description of Odysseus's speech in the *Iliad*, suggesting words that are soft yet powerful and abundant.</note> poured forth from his temperate mouth. For his eyes <margin>p. 56.</margin> seemed to be filled with a kind of radiance, and the rest of his face shared in a divine illumination. On one occasion, a man of high standing in the state, who was truthful and otherwise worthy of respect (his name was Rufus), came to him while he was lecturing and saw a light dancing around Proclus's head. When Proclus brought his lecture to a close, Rufus stood up, bowed down before him <note>original: "prosekynēse" — a gesture of deep respect or even religious adoration</note>, and reported what he had seen of that divine vision, swearing an oath to its truth. This same Rufus later brought him a significant amount of gold after a period of hardship and Proclus's return from Asia; however, Proclus looked upon this with indifference and would not accept it at all.

### 24.
But let us return to our original purpose. Although we have not yet sufficiently recounted the details of his "theoretical wisdom" <note>In the Neoplatonic system, "theoretical" or "contemplative" virtues are higher than "political" or social virtues, focusing on the soul's relationship with the divine.</note>, it follows that we must speak of the <margin>p. 57.</margin> **Justice** that corresponds to this class of virtues. This kind of justice is no longer found in a plurality of parts, as were the virtues discussed before, nor in the agreement of one part with another; rather, it is defined as the soul’s "proper activity" <note>term: "oikeiopragia" — doing one’s own work. In Plato’s Republic, justice is each part of the soul doing its own job; here, it means the soul focusing entirely on its highest function.</note> by itself, involving only the rational soul. Nothing is more "proper" to the soul than to act toward the **Intellect** <note>term: "Nous" — the higher divine Mind or Intelligence in Platonic philosophy.</note> and toward God, which the philosopher did to an extraordinary degree. For even when he barely had leisure from his daily labors and finally committed his body to sleep, perhaps even then he was not outside the realm of thought. Nevertheless, because he would quickly shake off sleep as if it were a kind of idleness of the soul—and the time for morning prayers had not yet arrived because it was not long after... <margin>p. 58.</margin>

B 2

<summary>This passage describes Proclus's inspired teaching style, a miraculous vision of light around his head witnessed by a man named Rufus, and begins to define his "theoretical justice" as a total focus on the divine Intellect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Proclus by his student Marinus, specifically detailing his intellectual habits and his transition from civic virtues to the "divine" virtues of the Neoplatonic tradition.</meta>

->20 **MARINUS' PROCLUS.** Chapters 24, 25, 26<-

...during the night, while by himself on his bed, he would either compose hymns or find answers by examining doctrines; then, rising at daybreak <p.59.>, he would write them down.

### 25.
He possessed **Temperance** <note>original: *sophrosyne*—a key Greek virtue meaning moderation or self-control</note> which followed as a companion to these other qualities. This virtue is the inward turning of the soul toward the **Mind** <note>original: *nous*; in Neoplatonism, this refers to the divine Intellect or the realm of pure thought</note>, a disposition that is untouched and undistracted by all other worldly things. He also displayed **Courage** <note>original: *andreia*</note> as a perfect companion, emulating the "impassibility" <note>a state of being unaffected by emotion or physical pain</note> of that which he looked toward, for that divine nature is impassible by its very essence. In short, he lived according to the teachings of **Plotinus** <note>the 3rd-century founder of Neoplatonism</note>, not merely the life of a "good man" as defined by **civic virtue** <note>the standard moral excellence required of a citizen</note>, but <p.60.> leaving that behind and choosing instead the life of the gods. For his goal was to achieve likeness to the gods, not merely to good men.

### 26.
He lived with such virtues while still studying under the philosopher **Syrianus** <note>Proclus' predecessor as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens</note> and reviewing the treatises of the ancient thinkers. He received certain elements and, as it were, the seeds of **Orphic** <note>mystical teachings attributed to the mythical poet Orpheus</note> and **Chaldean** <note>refers to the *Chaldean Oracles*, a sacred text for late Platonists</note> theology from his teacher. This was because he did not have enough time to study the actual verses with him. Syrianus had proposed to explain either the works of Orpheus or the **Oracles** <p.61.> to both Proclus and the philosopher **Domninus** of Syria (Syrianus' other successor), and he gave them the choice of which to study. However, they did not reach an agreement, as they did not both choose the same subject: Domninus chose the works of Orpheus, while our Proclus chose the Oracles. This disagreement, along with the fact that the great Syrianus did not live much longer, prevented the joint study from happening. 

Nevertheless, Proclus took these starting points from his guide, as has been said. After Syrianus' death, he diligently studied his teacher's commentaries on Orpheus, as well as the countless writings of **Porphyry** and **Iamblichus** <note>two influential earlier Neoplatonist philosophers</note> regarding the Oracles and similar Chaldean works. <p.62.> Nurtured by these divine words, he ascended to the highest peaks of the virtues possible for a human soul—those which the inspired **Iamblichus**...

It also recounts a pivotal moment where Proclus and a fellow student, Domninus, split over whether to study Orphic or Chaldean theology under their master Syrianus.</summary>

# MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. 26. 27. 21

...[Iam]blichus called <note>original: "ὑπερφυῶς θεουργικὰς ἀπεκάλεσεν" — he called them "supernaturally theurgic"</note> wonderfully theurgic. And having gathered the explanations of the philosophers before him, he worked them out with the appropriate critical judgment; he also composed both the other <term>Chaldean</term><note>The "Chaldeans" in this context refer to a tradition of mystical and magical wisdom associated with the "Chaldean Oracles," a central text for late Neoplatonists.</note> hypotheses and the greatest of the commentaries on the God-given Oracles, completing them in five whole years; upon which he also saw that divine dream. For the great Plutarch <note>This refers to Plutarch of Athens, Proclus's teacher and the head of the Academy, not the more famous biographer Plutarch of Chaeronea.</note> seemed to predict to him in a dream that he would live as many years as there were <term>quaternions</term><note>A "quaternion" (tetrad) is a unit of manuscript production consisting of four folded sheets, making eight leaves or sixteen pages.</note> composed by him for the Oracles. <margin>p. 63.</margin> Having counted them, he found there were seventy. That the dream was divine was shown by the outcome at the end of his life. For he lived, as we said before, seventy-five years, but those [last] five were no longer in full strength. For his naturally healthy body—worn down by that excessively harsh and unbearable lifestyle, the frequent cold-water pourings, and similar endurances—began to decline after his seventieth year, so that he was more deficient regarding all his activities. For even in this state he would pray, and compose hymns, and write some things, and meet with companions <margin>p. 64.</margin>, though doing everything with less vigor; whence, remembering the dream, he marveled and would say each time that he had lived only seventy years. While he was thus weak, Hegias the Younger made him more eager, especially concerning the [philosophical] expositions, showing even from his youth clear signs of all the ancestral virtues and of the truly <note>original: "χρυσῆς... σειρᾶς" — referring to the "Golden Chain" of Platonic succession.</note> golden chain of the lineage from Solon. He [Hegias] therefore studied diligently with him both the Platonic works and the other theologies. And the old man <margin>p. 65.</margin> would teach him [mathematical] lines, and was greatly delighted, seeing the boy progressing by leaps and bounds <note>original: "κατὰ πῆχυν" — literally "by the cubit," a Greek idiom for rapid growth.</note> in each of the subjects. And thus his preparation concerning the matters of the Chaldeans has been told in a summary way.

Once, while I <note>Marinus, the author of this biography.</note> was reading the works of Orpheus with him, and hearing not only the things from Iamblichus and Syrianus...

<margin>p.67</margin> But since, as I said, from his leisure in such matters he provided himself with an even greater and more perfect virtue, the <term>theurgical: "Divine work" (theurgy), a system of ritual practices intended to purify the soul and unite it with the gods, moving beyond mere intellectual study.</term> virtue, and did not stop at the theoretical, nor lived according to only one of the two characteristics in divine matters—thinking only and reaching up toward the higher things—he now also took care of secondary things in a more divine way, and not according to the political manner mentioned before. For he used the <term>Chaldean: Referring to the "Chaldean Oracles," a sacred text of the 2nd century AD that combined Middle Platonic philosophy with ritual practice and was highly revered by Proclus.</term> assemblies and encounters, and the divine and ineffable <term>strophaloi: Literally "whirling things." These were spinning ritual tools, sometimes called "top-wheels," used by theurgists to invoke divine presence or manipulate cosmic forces.</term>. For <margin>p.68</margin> he had received these things, and had learned the invocations and their other uses from Asclepigenia, the daughter of Plutarch <note>This is Plutarch of Athens, a Neoplatonist philosopher, not the famous biographer Plutarch of Chaeronea.</note>. For with her, and her alone, were preserved from Nestorius the Great the secret rites <note>original: "Orgia"</note> and the entire theurgical training, passed down to her through her father. Before these things, the philosopher, being purified in order by the Chaldean purifications, encountered luminous visions of Hecate seen with his own eyes, as he himself mentions somewhere in his own writing. He even moved rain-showers, having moved a certain <term>iyngx: A ritual tool, traditionally a bird (the wryneck) tied to a wheel, but in theurgical practice, a spinning disc used to attract divine energy or influence the weather.</term> <margin>p.69</margin> in a fitting manner, and he freed Attica from terrible droughts. He also deposited charms <note>original: "phylakteria"</note> against earthquakes, and...

### 28.

He even tested the prophetic power of the <term>tripod</term><note>The tripod was a three-legged stand associated with the Oracle of Apollo at Delphi, used here to refer to the practice of obtaining divine oracles.</note>, and he produced verses regarding his own ultimate destiny. For it is said that when he was forty years old, he dreamt that he himself spoke these lines:

> "There the super-celestial, immortal radiance flits,
> Leaping forth from the fountain-head of the fire-resounding company,"

> And spreading its mind toward the upper air, whirling in flame,

In addition to these events, he clearly saw that he belonged to the <term>Hermaic chain</term><note>A "chain" (seira) refers to a spiritual lineage or succession of souls descending from a particular god—in this case, Hermes, the god of wisdom and interpretation.</note>, and he once believed in a dream that he possessed the soul of <term>Nicomachus the Pythagorean</term><note>A famous mathematician and music theorist from the 2nd century AD; Proclus believed he was a reincarnation of this thinker.</note>.

### 29.

<margin>p. 71.</margin>
One could say much more if they wished to dwell on the subject and recount the <term>theurgic</term><note>Theurgy refers to "god-working"—rituals intended to invoke the presence of gods or achieve divine effects, often considered a higher form of magic by late Neoplatonists.</note> activities of that blessed man. However, I shall mention just one other instance out of ten thousand; for it is truly a wonder even to hear.

Asclepigenia was the daughter of Archiadas and Plutarcha, and the wife of our benefactor Theagenes. While she was still a young girl living with her parents, she was seized by a grievous illness which the doctors were unable to cure. <margin>p. 72.</margin> Archiadas, who placed all his hopes for the future of his family in her, was distressed and in great pain, as was natural. When the doctors had given up hope, he came—as he usually did in the greatest emergencies—to his "last anchor," or rather to the philosopher as if to a good savior. He entreated Proclus and begged him to offer prayers for his daughter.

Proclus took with him the great Pericles of Lydia, a man who was also a philosopher, and went up to the <term>Asclepieion</term><note>The temple of Asclepius, the god of healing. In Athens, this temple was located on the south slope of the Acropolis.</note> to pray to the god on behalf of the sick girl. For the city was fortunate at that time, and still possessed the temple of the Savior <margin>p. 73.</margin> <insert>Asclepius</insert> in an undestroyed state. While Proclus was praying in the ancient manner, a sudden change appeared in the girl and she experienced an immediate recovery; for the Savior, being a god, healed her easily. # 24 MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. 29. 30. 31.

...he went to see **Asclepigenia** <note>Daughter of Plutarch of Athens and a teacher in the Neoplatonic school.</note> and found her recently delivered from the suffering that had gripped her body, and living in a state of good health. He performed such a deed here as elsewhere, by remaining hidden from the multitude and giving no pretext to those who wished to plot against him. 

p. 74. His house, where he lived, also assisted him in this; for among his other blessings, his dwelling was most suitable for him, having been inhabited by his "father" **Syrianus** <note>Proclus's immediate predecessor and teacher.</note> and his "grandfather," as he called him, **Plutarch of Athens**. It was a neighbor to the famous **Asclepieion** <note>The sanctuary of Asclepius, the god of healing.</note> associated with Sophocles, and the temple of **Dionysus** by the theater, 

p. 75. and it was visible from—or at least sensible to—the **Acropolis of Athena**.

## 30.

How he became dear to the **Goddess of Philosophy** herself <note>Athena, the patron goddess of Athens and wisdom.</note> was sufficiently shown by his choice of the philosophical life, which was of the kind that reason has already pointed out. But the goddess herself also clearly revealed this when her statue, which had been until then established in the **Parthenon**, was being removed by those who "move even the immovable." <note>A veiled reference to Christian authorities who removed the pagan statues from the Parthenon.</note> 

p. 76. For it seemed to the philosopher in a dream that a graceful woman visited him and announced that he must prepare his house as quickly as possible: **"For the Lady Athenais,"** she said, **"wishes to dwell with you."** <note>Athenais is a poetic name for the goddess Athena.</note> 

His intimacy with **Asclepius** was shown by his earlier deed, but he also convinced us during his final illness through a manifestation of the god. For while he was between sleep and waking, he saw a **serpent** <note>original: "δράκοντα" — The snake was the sacred animal and physical manifestation of Asclepius.</note> crawling around his head, the very place where the disease of paralysis had first taken hold. From this manifestation, he felt some relief from the illness; and if his eagerness and great desire for death had not prevented it, and if he had deemed his body worthy of proper care, I believe he would have become perfectly healthy again.

## 31.

p. 77. He also used to recount those things worthy of memory with deep feeling and not without tears. For he feared, when he was in his prime...

# MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. 31. 32. 25

...age, he feared lest the arthritic disease of his father—which is wont and accustomed for the most part to pass from fathers to children—might likewise come upon him; and he feared, I think, not without reason. For already before this—an event which should have been recounted earlier—he had felt such a pain, at which time another marvelous thing happened to him. Having been advised by certain people, he placed a so-called <term>ptygmation</term> <note>original: πτυγμάτιον; a small folded compress or medical bandage</note> upon his aching foot, and while he was lying upon his bed, ->p. 78<- a sparrow suddenly flew down and snatched the compress away. 

This omen was divine and truly <term>paeonian</term> <note>original: παιώνειος; derived from Paean, an epithet for healing deities like Apollo or Asclepius</note>, sufficient to instill confidence regarding the future. But, as I said, he was nonetheless gripped later on by a fear of the disease. Having supplicated the god concerning this and prayed for him to reveal something clear, while sleeping he saw a vision (it is a bold thing even to conceive of, but one must dare nonetheless and not shrink from bringing the truth to light). He saw, as it seemed, someone coming from Epidaurus <note>The site of the most famous sanctuary of Asclepius, the god of medicine</note> who leaned over his legs and, in an act of divine kindness, did not even refuse to kiss his knees. From that moment on, he continued through his entire life ->p. 79<- confident in this regard, and reached a deep old age without ever feeling such an affliction again.

## 32.

Furthermore, the god in Adrotta <note>A city in Lydia, Asia Minor</note> clearly demonstrated the kinship of this god-beloved man toward him. For the god graciously welcomed him with manifestations whenever he frequented the temple. Once, when Proclus was in doubt and prayed to learn who, or what gods, were those who frequented and were honored in that place—since even the locals did not hold the same accounts (some believing the sanctuary belonged to Asclepius and confirming this with many proofs; for indeed, "hearings" <note>original: ἀκοαὶ; likely referring to divine voices or auditory omens</note> are said to occur in that place, and a certain table ->p. 80<- is dedicated to the god, and healing oracles are given on each occasion, and those who approach are paradoxically saved from the greatest dangers; while others thought the **Dioscuri** <note>The "Heavenly Twins," Castor and Pollux</note> frequented the place, for some had already seen two youths, as it seemed, in a waking vision upon the road to Adrotta)...

# 26 MARINUS'S *LIFE OF PROCLUS*. 32. 33.

...very handsome, riding on horses and saying they were hurrying to the temple, so that they were perceived at first to be humans, but he was immediately convinced that the appearance was more divine. For when the men arrived at the temple, they were not reported to have been seen by those occupied around the temple when they inquired; rather, they suddenly vanished into the invisible even for those witnesses. <margin>p. 81</margin> Therefore, as has been said, while the philosopher <note>Proclus</note> was in doubt and could not disbelieve the reports, he prayed to the local gods to reveal their own identity to him. Because of his great sympathy for the divine, he was always moved to tears if he ever made mention to us of these things he saw, and of the divine praise spoken to him.

-> 33. <-

But if I should wish to go through everything in this way and describe his friendship with Pan, the son of Hermes, and the great favor and salvation he found in Athens from the god—and even more to recount the great good fortune he received from the Mother of the Gods, <note>The "Mother of the Gods" refers to Cybele, an ancient goddess whose mysteries Proclus practiced.</note> in which he especially took pride and greatly rejoiced—I might perhaps <margin>p. 84</margin> seem to be babbling to my readers, or even to be saying things that are unbelievable to some. For many and great were the things done and said to him every day by the goddess, and because of their number and unexpected nature, I do not even have my memory of them quite clearly organized for a written account now.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the philosopher Proclus by his student and successor, Marinus. The previous sections likely detailed Proclus's specific virtues and his philosophical education. # MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. 33. 34. 27

### 33.

If anyone longs to observe his skill and aptitude in this area <note>This refers to Proclus's ability to interpret religious mysteries and myths.</note> as well, let him take into his hands his **book on the Mother of the Gods**. <note>original: "μητρωακὴν βίβλον." This refers to a now-lost work by Proclus regarding the goddess Cybele, known as the Great Mother.</note> For he will see how, not without **divine inspiration**, <note>original: "κατακωχῆς" (katakōchēs), a state of being possessed or "held" by a divine power.</note> he revealed the entire theology concerning that Goddess. He also unfolded in a philosophical manner the other myths regarding her and **Attis** <note>Attis was the consort of Cybele; his myths often involved themes of grief and rebirth which Proclus interpreted as allegories for the soul.</note>—both those things done and those said—so that the ear is no longer disturbed by the jarring laments and the other secret things spoken there. <margin>p. 85</margin>

### 34.

Having run through, then, the actions and successes resulting from his **theurgical virtue**, <term>theurgy</term> <note>From the Greek "theourgia," meaning "god-working." It refers to rituals intended to invoke the presence of the divine or to purify the soul.</note> and having recounted how he was a man who, in all respects, was made equal to the virtues—the likes of which men have never seen in a very long time—let us now put a conclusion to our words about him. For us, the beginning has become not just a beginning, nor even (according to the proverb) "half of the whole," <note>A common Greek proverb attributed to Hesiod, suggesting that starting a task is the most important part.</note> but rather the **entirety of the whole**. 

For starting from his **happiness** <note>original: "εὐδαιμονίας" (eudaimonias), referring to the flourishing, blessed state of a life lived in accordance with virtue.</note> and proceeding through its center, we have returned to it once again. <margin>p. 86</margin> Through this discourse, we have presented the "goods" gifted to this excellent man by the gods and by **Providence** as a whole. We have declared their answering of his prayers, their divine appearances, their healings, and their other forms of assistance. We have also shown those things allotted to him by <term>Fate</term> and **Good Fortune**: his homeland, his parents, his physical excellence, his teachers, his friends, and his general abundance. We have demonstrated in this account how all these things excelled in magnitude and brilliance compared to those of all other men.

Furthermore, we have enumerated the advantages that arose from his own **deliberate choice** <term>proairesis</term> <note>A key philosophical term for the internal will or moral disposition that remains within an individual's control.</note> and did not simply come to him from the outside. For such were the achievements of his soul throughout its entire virtue. In short, we have shown the activity of his soul proceeding according to **perfect virtue**, <margin>p. 87</margin> and how it was sufficiently supplied with other "goods"—both divine and human—within a perfect life.

<summary>This page concludes the biography of Proclus, highlighting his inspired interpretation of the myths of Cybele and Attis, while summarizing his life as a perfect union of divine gifts (fate) and personal moral excellence.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Mother of the Gods, Cybele, Attis, Theurgy, Providence, Fate, Virtue, Eudaimonia, Neoplatonism</keywords>

28 MARINUS' PROCLUS. 35. 36.

### 35.
In order that those who possess a finer appreciation of beauty might be able to infer—from the configuration of the stars under which he was born—how the lot of his chosen life fell not among the last or the middle ranks, but among the very first, I have set out the positions of the planets as they were at his birth.

| Planet/Point | Sign | Degree - Minute |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| ☉ Sun | Aries | 16° - 26' |
| ☾ Moon | Gemini | 17° - 29' |
| ♄ Saturn | Taurus | 24° - 23' |
| ♃ Jupiter | Taurus | 24° - 41' |
| ♂ Mars | Sagittarius | 29° - 50' <note>original: "υ΄". In many manuscripts of this chart, this Greek numeral represents 50 minutes.</note> |
| ♀ Venus | Pisces | 23° |
| ☿ Mercury | Aquarius | 4° - 42' |
| <margin>p.88</margin> Ascendant | Aries | 8° - 19' |
| Midheaven | Capricorn | 4° - 42' |
| Ascending Node | Scorpio | 24° - 33' |
| Previous New Moon | Aquarius | 8° - 51' |

### 36.
He passed away in the 124th year from the reign of Julian <note>The author dates this from the beginning of the reign of the Emperor Julian the Apostate, roughly 485 AD.</note>, when Nicagoras the Younger was <term>archon|the chief magistrate of Athens</term> at Athens, on the 17th day of the month of Munychion according to the Athenians, and the 17th of April according to the Romans. His body was granted the honors of the traditional Athenian funeral rites, just as he himself had arranged while he was still alive. For this, too, was a characteristic of this blessed man—perhaps more than any other—namely, his knowledge of and careful attention to the rituals performed for the departed. He never neglected a single occasion for their customary <margin>p.89</margin> service; every year, on certain fixed days, he would make a circuit of the tombs of the <term>Attic Heroes|the legendary founders and protectors of the city of Athens</term>, the monuments of those who had lived as philosophers, and the graves of his other friends and acquaintances. There he performed the prescribed rites, not through an assistant, but acting in person. After the service for each individual, he would go to the Academy and there, in a specific location, appease the souls of his ancestors and his family as a whole. And again, in common...

<summary>This page preserves the birth horoscope of the philosopher Proclus and records the date of his death in 485 AD, emphasizing his devotion to traditional Athenian religious rituals and the veneration of deceased philosophers and ancestors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Proclus by his student Marinus, specifically detailing the philosopher's final arrangements, burial, and the celestial omens that preceded his death.</meta>

### MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. 36. 37. 38. 29

...to the souls of all those who have practiced philosophy <note>The author refers to Proclus’ practice of offering ritual honors to the memory of past thinkers</note> was poured out in another part. And after all these things, this most pious man, having designated a third place, performed the sacred rites for all the souls of men who had passed away. When his body had been laid out, as has been said, according to his own instructions, and was carried out by his companions, he was buried <margin>p. 90.</margin> in the eastern suburbs of the city <note>Athens</note> near Lycabettus, where the body of his teacher Syrianus <note>Proclus’ predecessor as head of the Platonic Academy</note> also lies. For Syrianus had commanded this of him while he was still alive, and for this reason, he had constructed the burial vault to be double. And even after his death, when the most holy Proclus was once deliberating whether it might be improper <margin>p. 91.</margin> to be buried there, Syrianus appeared to him in a dream and threatened him, specifically because he had even entertained such a thought. The following four-line epitaph was inscribed on the tomb, which Proclus himself composed:

->I, Proclus, was a Lycian by birth, whom Syrianus<-
->Nurtured here as the successor to his teaching;<-
->This common tomb has received the bodies of both;<-
->May one single place also be allotted to our souls.<-
<note>original: "Πρόκλος ἐγὼ γενόμην Λύκιος γένος..."—Proclus identifies himself by his ethnicity (Lycian, from modern-day Turkey) and his intellectual lineage.</note>

### 37.

There were also signs from heaven <term>divine omens|diosēmeiai</term> a year before his <margin>p. 92.</margin> death, such as a solar eclipse so clear that night seemed to occur during the day. For a deep darkness fell and the stars became visible. This occurred in the sign of Capricorn, at the eastern center <note>a specific astrological point in the sky at the time of the event</note>. The makers of almanacs also recorded another eclipse that was to occur when the first year after his death was completed. Such events appearing to happen in the heavens are said to be significant of what happens on earth; and for us, they clearly signaled the loss and, as it were, the departure of the light of philosophy.

### 38.

<margin>p. 93.</margin> Let these things, then, be sufficiently recorded by me concerning the philosopher. Let anyone who wishes also record the truth concerning the companions who were associated with him—

30          ### MARINUS' PROCLUS, Chapter 38

...to write; for many people came to him from many places. Some came only for the sake of mere listening <note>original: "ψιλῇ ἀκροάσει"; this refers to students who attended lectures without committing to the deep philosophical lifestyle</note>, but others became his devoted followers and fellow students for the sake of philosophy. Let the more industrious student enumerate his writings by their main headings. For my part, both because of my own conscience and so that I might fulfill my sacred duty toward his divine soul and the **good guardian spirit** <term>daimon</term> <note>original: "ἀγαθὸν δαίμονα"; in Neoplatonic thought, this is a guiding divinity or "genius" assigned to a person's soul at birth</note> that was allotted to it, I have been led to say these things. 

<margin>p. 94.</margin> Regarding his writings, I will say only this: that among all his other works, he always preferred his **commentaries on the Timaeus** <note>Plato’s dialogue concerning the nature of the physical world and the creator</note>. He was also very pleased with his commentaries on the *Theaetetus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the nature of knowledge</note>. He was even accustomed to say this often: "If I were the master, I would cause only the **Oracles** <term>Chaldean Oracles</term> <note>original: "τὰ Λόγια"; a collection of mystical verses from the 2nd century AD that Neoplatonists considered sacred scripture</note> and the *Timaeus* to be circulated among all the ancient books. The rest I would hide away from the men of today, because some people are actually harmed by encountering them randomly and without proper examination."

->***Proclus, or On Happiness***, has been completed with God’s help.<-

<summary>This final page of the biography concludes with Marinus’ reflections on Proclus' students and his most important philosophical works, specifically highlighting his reverence for Plato's Timaeus and the Chaldean Oracles. The text ends with a traditional closing formula expressing that the work is finished.</summary>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening preface to the biography of Proclus (412–485 AD), written by his student and successor, Marinus of Neapolis. Marinus establishes the "humility topes," a standard rhetorical opening where an author claims to be unworthy of their great subject.</meta>

->MARINUS OF NEAPOLIS<-
->**PROCLUS**<-

I.

If I had considered the greatness of soul, or the general dignity of **Proclus**, <margin>p. 1</margin> the philosopher of our time, and then considered what kind of preparation and what faculty of speech is required for those who take it upon themselves to describe his life—and finally, if I had looked upon my own poverty of speech—I would have judged it more correct to keep my peace. I would have, as the saying goes, *not leaped over the trenches* <note>original: "foffasque, quod aiunt, non tranfilire." A Greek and Latin proverb meaning to stay within the bounds of one's own abilities or not to overreach.</note> and declined the danger of such a grand oration.

However, I did not take such a precise account of my own reputation in this matter when I reflected that even in temples, those who approach the altars <margin>p. 2</margin> do not all perform equal sacrifices. Some offer bulls or goats and other things of that kind, taking care that they might not be unworthy of the kindness of the Gods to whom those altars belong. Similarly, some compose hymns with great elegance, some in verse and others in prose. But others, who have none of these things to offer, yet sacrifice perhaps only a small cake or a grain of incense and perform their invocations with a brief address, are heard no less than the others.

While I was reflecting on these things, I was fearful—as it is said in **Ibycus** <note>Ibycus was a Greek lyric poet of the 6th century BCE. The reference here is to a fragment where the poet fears offending the gods while seeking human glory.</note>—lest I should commit a fault, not against the gods (for so he said) but against a wise man, while seeking praise among men. Indeed, I do not believe it is right that I alone among his close associates <term>familiarium</term> <note>Referring to the inner circle of students in the Neoplatonic Academy.</note> should remain silent, nor that I should fail to report the truth about him to the best of my ability, for it is he <margin>p. 3</margin> who deserves to be spoken of above all others. And perhaps I would not even gain honor among men if I were silent, for they might not think that I wished to avoid a display of vanity, but rather...

<summary>Marinus of Neapolis begins his biography of the philosopher Proclus by humbly explaining why he feels compelled to write despite his own perceived lack of talent, comparing his modest tribute to a simple offering at a divine altar.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus of Neapolis, Neoplatonism, biography, humility, Ibycus, philosophy, late antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the biography of Proclus by his student and successor, Marinus of Neapolis. Marinus is transitioning from justifying his writing to discussing Proclus' innate qualities and the Neoplatonic hierarchy of virtues.</meta>

->32 MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. Chapters I, II, III.<-

...moved by a certain mental sluggishness, or even by a more serious ailment of the soul, they have fled from the task they set out to do. Moved by all these considerations, I felt I must undertake the task of recording some of this Philosopher’s <note>Referring to Proclus (412–485 AD), the influential head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens.</note> most illustrious deeds. Although his achievements are infinite, the things recorded about him are spoken with the greatest truth.

## II.

I will not begin in the usual manner of writers who strictly follow a set order of chapters. Instead, I believe it is most right to lay the foundation of my discourse upon the very happiness of this blessed man. Indeed, I judge him to have been the most fortunate of all those celebrated in the centuries before us. p. 4. I do not speak only of the "happiness of the wise"—though he possessed this above all others—nor do I mean simply that his physical health was excellently formed, nor even his good fortune, though these too fell to him more than to any other man. He certainly possessed a great abundance of those things called "external goods." Rather, I am speaking of a perfect happiness, complete in every part, and composed of all these elements together. 

First, then, we shall categorize the virtues into the natural, the moral, and the political, as well as the more sublime virtues—those concerned with **purification** <note>original: *purificatione*; referring to the "cathartic" virtues intended to cleanse the soul from the influence of the physical body.</note> and **contemplation**, and those through which one reaches a likeness to the gods. There are even virtues superior to these, p. 5. which are considered beyond the human level; however, we shall begin with the more natural virtues.

## III.

This blessed man whom we praise possessed all those qualities from birth—those which, if they are granted to us, we are born with. Traces of these were clearly visible even in his outermost "shell-like" covering <note>A famous Platonic metaphor where the physical body is compared to an oyster shell enclosing the soul.</note>. First, he possessed a singular integrity of the senses, which is called <term>bodily prudence<gloss>a philosophical term for the healthy and harmonious functioning of the physical senses</gloss></term>, and especially the "worthier" senses of sight and hearing. These were granted by the Gods for the sake of philosophy and for a more harmonious life...

<summary>Marinus begins his biography of Proclus by arguing that Proclus possessed a unique "perfect happiness" and begins a systematic review of his virtues, starting with his innate physical and sensory gifts.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus of Neapolis, Neoplatonism, virtues, bodily prudence, happiness, biography</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonic philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus, specifically detailing the physical virtues that mirrored his spiritual perfection.</meta>

-># MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. III. 33<-

(given to men) remained intact for him throughout his entire life. Second was his **bodily strength**, which was affected by no cold, nor could it be shaken or disturbed by any faulty or neglected regime of diet or labor <margin>p. 6.</margin> which he endured day and night while he prayed, turned through books, wrote himself, and gave attention to his friends; he performed all these tasks so diligently that he seemed to focus on each one alone. Perhaps one might rightly call such a faculty **bodily courage** <term>fortitudo corporis: the physical counterpart to the soul's virtue of bravery</term>. 

His third bodily virtue was **beauty**, which, when compared with temperance <note>original: "temperantia"; also known as moderation or self-control</note>, they think has some affinity with it; and rightly so. For just as we consider temperance to be a certain harmony and consensus of the soul’s faculties, so too this beauty in the body is understood from a certain congruence of the parts of the body with which we act. 

Moreover, he was most pleasant to look upon, because he was not only endowed with this excellent proportion, but the blooming of his soul shone out from his body like a **vital light** <note>original: "vitalis lucis"; Neoplatonists believed that a highly realized soul could physically illuminate the body</note> <margin>p. 7.</margin> to a degree that cannot be adequately explained in speech. Indeed, he was so beautiful that no painter could capture his likeness; and all his portraits that were circulated, though they were very beautiful, fell far short of the truth of the man they imitated. 

The fourth, namely **health**, they wish to correspond to that virtue which exists in the soul as **justice**. They say there is a certain justice in the body just as there is in the soul. For justice is nothing other than a certain habit keeping the parts of the soul in their proper duty. Thus, that which reconciles agreement and concord among the warring elements of the body is called "health" by physicians; this was so well established in Proclus from the cradle <note>original: "ab incunabulis"; meaning from his earliest infancy</note> <margin>p. 8.</margin> that he himself said it happened to him to be physically ill only twice or thrice in so long a life—namely, seventy-five whole years. Nay, this serves as sufficient proof, which I myself witnessed: when he was held by his final illness, he was entirely unaware of what those encroaching evils were that had invaded his body, because they were so unfamiliar to him.

->C<-

<summary>This passage describes the physical attributes of the philosopher Proclus, arguing that his strength, beauty, and health were outward reflections of his internal spiritual virtues like courage, temperance, and justice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Proclus by his student Marinus, moving from a discussion of Proclus’s physical attributes to his innate spiritual and intellectual virtues.</meta>

34 MARINUS’ *PROCLUS*. IV.

IV.

And although these are indeed "goods of the body," <note>In ancient philosophy, "goods" were often categorized into three types: goods of the soul (virtues), goods of the body (health, beauty), and external goods (wealth, status).</note> one might nevertheless call them the heralds and, as it were, the companions of those **forms** of perfect virtue into which we have divided his character. Now, regarding the primary gifts of his soul—those which fell to him by nature, without the labor of a teacher—and those parts of virtue: one would marvel at what kind and how great they were in him. These are the very qualities that Plato requires as the "elements of a philosophical nature." <note>Marinus is referencing Plato’s *Republic* (Book VI, 485a–487a), where Socrates lists the natural traits of a philosopher: love of truth, hatred of falsehood, temperance, and greatness of soul.</note>

<margin>p. 9.</margin> For he was endowed with a remarkable memory, a keen intellect, and magnificence; he was affable, a friend, and—so to speak—a kinsman to truth, justice, courage, and <term>temperance</term> <note>original Latin: *temperantia*; the virtue of self-control and moderation.</note>. By no means would he knowingly admit a lie; instead, he hated it most vehemently, loving the flight from falsehood and the pursuit of truth. And by Hercules! <note>original: *me hercle*; a common Roman oath expressing emphasis.</note> It was necessary that he pursued truth with such zeal, since he was supremely desirous of it from the time he was a boy—for truth is the principle of all goods for both Gods and men.

Moreover, that he despised the pleasures of the body and was a most devoted lover of temperance is proved by his desire—and indeed his great inclination—for thoroughly learning every branch of <margin>p. 10.</margin> the disciplines and studies. Such pursuits never allow beastly or <term>illiberal</term> <note>In this context, "illiberal" means "unworthy of a free person" or vulgar.</note> pleasure to take root, but instead can stir up a sincere delight within the soul itself, arising from its own nature.

Regarding greed, it cannot be expressed how far he was from it; even as a boy, he despised the riches of his parents (though they were very wealthy) because of his incredible zeal for philosophy. For this reason, he was far from both "illiberality" and the petty care of minor things, as he was most studious of the universe and of things both divine and human. Having obtained a "greatness of soul" from such a mind, he made nothing of human life; nor did he, like others, think that death was anything serious. Indeed, he did not fear any of the things that seemed formidable to others, possessing that natural disposition which is properly called by no other name than <term>fortitude</term> <note>Or "courage."</note>.

<margin>p. 11.</margin> From all these qualities, even to those who... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the innate virtues of the philosopher Proclus, asserting that he possessed the natural "philosophical temperament" described by Plato, including a love for truth, temperance, and courage. Marinus emphasizes that Proclus's intellectual hunger for philosophy naturally led him to disregard bodily pleasures and material wealth from a young age.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Plato’s Republic, philosophical nature, cardinal virtues, temperance, courage, Neoplatonism, biography</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his successor Marinus. The previous section likely introduced Proclus’s early character traits.</meta>

->MARINUS’S LIFE OF PROCLUS. CHAPTERS IV. V. VI. PAGE 35<-

...of that excellent disposition did not fail, I believe it is established that from his very boyhood he loved justice, and was just and easy to deal with, by no means difficult in social life or in contracts, nor, in short, unjust. To us, indeed, he certainly appeared to be modest and refined, not greedy, not ungenerous <note>original: "illiberalis" — meaning someone who lacks the qualities of a free-born gentleman, often specifically referring to stinginess.</note>, not arrogant, or timid by nature.

## V.

As for the goodness and richness of his intellect, would it not be superfluous to mention it? Especially among those who know and have heard that he was full of the most beautiful disciplines <note>The "disciplines" or "liberal arts" included grammar, rhetoric, logic, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note>, how many works he himself [margin]p. 12.[/margin] produced and brought to light for mankind, so that he alone seems to have drunk nothing from the cup of forgetfulness <note>original: "oblivionis poculo" — a reference to the mythical River Lethe, whose waters caused souls to forget their previous existence before reincarnation.</note>. Endowed with such a faculty of memory, he was never confused, nor did that which happens to those who forget happen to him; he never lost track of himself, as he was fully capable of retaining the disciplines and was one who took such great delight in their study. Furthermore, he was most distant from a rustic nature or one that is harsh and averse to the Muses <note>The Muses were the Greek goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences; to be "averse to the Muses" meant to be uncultured or boorish.</note>; instead, he was greatly inclined toward more refined things. Indeed, because of his singular politeness and festivity in common social gatherings, his sacred feasts, and other actions—while still preserving his integrity—he attracted his associates and always sent them away more cheerful.

## VI.

Being endowed from the [margin]p. 13.[/margin] beginning with all these and other gifts of nature, he was born to his mother Marcella and her lawfully wedded husband Patricius: both Lycians <note>Lycia was a region in southern Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey) known for its distinct culture and confederation of cities.</note>, excelling in lineage and virtue. The tutelary Goddess of Byzantium <note>Byzantium is modern-day Istanbul. The "tutelary goddess" refers to Athena (Minerva), the protector of the city.</note> received him at birth and cared for him like a midwife; she was the cause of his existence then, as he was born in her city. Later, she also provided for his well-being when he was already numbered among boys and youths. For she herself appeared to him in a dream <note>original: "per quietem" — literally "through rest," a common way to describe a vision received while sleeping.</note> and exhorted him to the study of philosophy. From this, clearly, there arose in him such an inclination toward the goddess that he performed her sacred rites with the greatest devotion, and with greater, so to speak— [margin]p. 14.[/margin]

C 2 <note>This is a "catchword," a printer's mark indicating the first syllable of the next page to ensure the sheets were bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page describes Proclus’s virtuous character, exceptional memory, and his divine calling to philosophy through a vision of the goddess Athena in his birthplace, Byzantium.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Neoplatonism, Athena, Byzantium, Lycia, Virtue, Memory, Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his successor Marinus. It follows the account of Proclus’s early childhood and his family's move back to their ancestral home.</meta>

36 ->MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. VI. VII. VIII.<-

...while she <note>Likely referring to his mother, Marcella, or the city of his birth, Byzantium, which was mentioned on the previous page</note> followed these customs with great devotion. Finally, his parents brought the boy back to their own homeland, Xanthus, which was consecrated to Apollo. This homeland itself fell to him through a certain divine providence, I believe, because it was fitting that he—who was destined to become the leader of all branches of knowledge—should be raised and educated under the guidance of the God who leads the Muses <note>Apollo Musagetes, the leader of the Muses and god of light, music, and prophecy</note>. There, having been instructed in the most refined manners, he attained the moral virtues, becoming accustomed to loving what was proper to do and fleeing from the opposite.

### VII.
Then truly the love of the Gods, which had accompanied him from birth in the highest degree, began to appear openly. For <margin>p. 15</margin> when, at one time, he was seized by a certain bodily illness, and so seriously indeed that it seemed he could hardly be cured, there stood by his bed a tall youth, who appeared quite young and beautiful in aspect. Even before he spoke his name, one might have thought he was Telesphorus <note>original: "Telesphorum" — a minor Greek deity associated with recovery from illness, often depicted as a child or youth accompanying the healing god Asclepius</note>. However, revealing who he was and pronouncing his name, he touched Proclus' head (for he was standing there, leaning his head against the boy's pillow) and immediately restored health to the sick boy, and then vanished. This divine presence, and that divine benevolence, was thus exhibited to the boy at that time.

### VIII.
In a short space of time, after he had applied himself to the study of Grammar <term>Grammar: In the ancient world, "Grammar" was a broad discipline involving the study of language, the interpretation of poets, and literary criticism</term> in Lycia, he set out for Alexandria in Egypt. He brought with him singular moral virtues, by which he moved even the teachers of that place to love him. For this reason, Leonas the Rhetorician—an Isaurian <note>A native of Isauria, a rugged region in South-Central Asia Minor</note>, I believe, by birth—who was famous among the many of his profession then in Alexandria, not only made him a partner in his own studies but even deigned to have him as a member of his household. He saw to it that Proclus <margin>p. 16</margin> took his meals together with his wife and children, just as if he had been his own true son. Furthermore, he ensured that Proclus became known to the leading men in Egypt, who were wonderfully delighted by the young man’s sharp intellect...

<summary>This page describes Proclus's childhood move to Lycia, a miraculous healing he experienced through the god Telesphorus, and his subsequent move to Alexandria to study under the rhetorician Leonas.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Xanthus, Apollo, Telesphorus, Alexandria, Leonas the Rhetorician, education, divine healing, Lycia</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
MARINUS’S PROCLUS. CHAPTERS VIII, IX. 37

Because of the elegance of his intellect and character, as well as his integrity, they held him among their dearest friends. He was also educated by Orion the Grammarian, whose ancestors among the Egyptians had held the office of the priesthood; Orion had progressed so far in the knowledge of his art <margin>p. 17.</margin> that he himself composed books and left them behind for the benefit of posterity. He also attended the schools of Roman teachers and made great progress in that language <note>Referring to Latin, which was essential for legal studies in the Roman Empire</note>; for in the beginning, he was led toward his father's profession, in which his father had been most distinguished, as he practiced law in the royal city <note>The "royal city" refers to Constantinople, the capital of the Eastern Roman Empire</note>. As a young man, he seemed to be vehemently delighted by the art of rhetoric, even before he had touched the writings of the Philosophers. He achieved great glory from this and earned the highest admiration from his fellow students and teachers, both because of the elegance of his speech and his speed of perception, and even more because he displayed the bearing and industry of a teacher <margin>p. 18.</margin> rather than a student.

IX.
While he was still frequenting the school, the Rhetorician Leonas joined Proclus to himself as a traveling companion, as he was about to set out for Byzantium. Leonas undertook this journey to please his friend Theodorus, who was then the Prefect of Alexandria, a sophisticated and magnificent man and a lover of philosophy. Proclus, although a youth, followed his teacher all the more eagerly so that he would not be forced to interrupt his studies. But, to speak more truly, a certain good fortune led him back to the origin of his birth. For once he had returned, the Goddess <note>Likely Athena, the patron of wisdom, whom Proclus claimed guided his life</note> exhorted him toward philosophy, so that he might also visit the schools of the Athenians. Having first returned <margin>p. 19.</margin> to Alexandria, and having bid farewell to Rhetoric and the other subjects he had previously studied, he devoted himself to the discourses of the philosophers who were then there. He frequented the school of Olympiodorus, a most famous philosopher, for the sake of imbibing Aristotelian doctrine; in the mathematical disciplines, however, he entrusted himself to Hero, a religious man who was [skilled in] those methods by which it was proper for such things to be taught...
