->38 MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. IX. X.<-

**IX.**

...most skilled. Indeed, these men were so delighted by the young man’s character <note>Proclus was noted for his virtue and intellect even in his youth.</note> that **Olympiodorus** <note>An important Neoplatonist philosopher teaching in Alexandria.</note> wished to betroth him to his own young daughter, who had herself been educated in literature. **Hero** <note>Another of Proclus's teachers, likely an expert in mathematics or religious rites.</note> also entrusted him with his entire religious system and took him on as a constant companion. When he listened to Olympiodorus—a man endowed with a great faculty for speaking, who, because of his speed in delivery and the gravity of his subjects, was understood by only a very few listeners—Proclus would leave after the crowd was dismissed and report everything that had been said to his fellow students. He repeated the lectures almost word for word, even though they were quite lengthy. This was told to me by one of his classmates, **Ulpianus of Gaza**, <p.21.> who himself spent a significant portion of his life in the study of philosophy. Furthermore, Proclus mastered the philosophical writings of **Aristotle** with great ease, even though these works are considered difficult for those who approach them for a simple reading.

**X.**

While he was applying himself to these studies in Alexandria and enjoying the familiarity of his teachers in those subjects they were able to provide, a turning point arrived. During the reading of a certain author that they were examining together, it seemed to Proclus that they were not interpreting the philosopher’s mind with the dignity the subject deserved. Consequently, he began to look down upon those schools. Mindful of a divine vision that had occurred to him in **Byzantium** <note>Modern-day Istanbul; Proclus had a vision of the goddess Athena there, urging him to pursue philosophy.</note> and the encouragement he had received, he made his way toward **Athens**, <p.22.> accompanied by the gods who preside over eloquence and philosophy, as well as by his own good spirits. 

For the purpose of preserving the authentic and uncorrupted **Succession of Plato**, <term>Succession (Diadoche): the continuous line of heads of the Platonic Academy in Athens, tracing back to Plato himself.</term> he was led by the gods to the head of the school of philosophy. This was clearly shown by the circumstances of his arrival; everything was divinely arranged to foreshadow that this office was destined for him by his "Father" or God, and that his vote for the Succession would be granted by heaven. For when he put into port at **Piraeus** <note>The primary port of Athens.</note> and his arrival was announced to the citizens, **Nicolaus**—who later flourished in the art of rhetoric but was then a student under the Athenian masters—went down to the shore <p.23.> as if to meet an old acquaintance. He intended to welcome him and receive him with the hospitality due to a fellow citizen; for Nicolaus was also a **Lycian**. <note>A native of Lycia, a region in southern Asia Minor where Proclus was raised.</note>

have drunk your first Attic <note>Attica is the region of Greece where Athens is located; "Attic water" implies he is being physically and spiritually nourished by the land of the philosophers.</note> water." Then he, rising and showing

about to set the bars across the doors, so that he even said to him 
(and I shall report the very words of the man himself): "Truly..."

40 MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. XI. XII.

greeted him. Here, Lachares, greatly admiring the young man’s confidence, said to the philosopher Syrianus: “This is that which Plato divinely said concerning great geniuses: <note>referring to Plato's *Republic* 491e, which suggests that powerful minds can become either exceptionally good or exceptionally bad depending on their education.</note> ‘This one,’ he said, ‘will either produce a great good, or its opposite.’” <margin>p. 27</margin> And such signs—to mention a few out of many—divinely occurred to the philosopher when he first arrived at Athens.

## XII.

Now Syrianus led him to the great Plutarch, the son of Nestorius, <note>Plutarch of Athens (died c. 432 AD), the Neoplatonist philosopher who re-established the Academy, not the more famous biographer of the same name.</note> who, when he saw the young man was not yet twenty years old and heard of his love and desire for the philosophical life, was greatly pleased with him. He immediately made Proclus a partner in his philosophical studies, even though his age almost prohibited it, for Plutarch was already a very old man. Therefore, he lectured to him on Aristotle’s <margin>p. 28</margin> *On the Soul* <term>De Anima; Aristotle’s foundational work on the nature of living things and the mind</term> and Plato’s *Phaedo* <term>A dialogue concerning the immortality of the soul</term>. The Great [Plutarch] encouraged him to write down what was being said, using the young man's ambition as an instrument; he told Proclus that if he completed those <term>scholia; lecture notes or commentaries</term>, it would come to pass that they would even be circulated as "Proclus' Commentaries on the *Phaedo*." And while he loved the young man for many other reasons—having recognized his zeal for all the best things—he even used to call him "son" and kept him as a member of his household. 

However, when he saw that Proclus was extremely temperate regarding [the eating of] animals, he urged him not to abstain from them entirely, so that his body might still be able to perform its natural <margin>p. 29</margin> functions. Plutarch also made this recommendation to Syrianus regarding the young man's diet. But Syrianus replied to the old man, as that divine soul (that is, Proclus) related to us: <note>The phrase "divine head" or "divine soul" was a common respectful way to refer to a teacher.</note> “Let him learn what I want with that frugality, and then, if he wishes, let him die.” So great was the care his masters had for him in all matters. The old man [Plutarch] lived for about two years after Proclus' arrival, and as he was dying, he commended the young man to his successor Syrianus, just as he did with Archiadas, his own grandson. After Syrianus took him in, he helped him greatly, not only in literature...

## MARINUS ON PROCLUS. XII. XIII. XIV. 41

but he also took him into his own home and made him a companion in his philosophical life. Syrianus did this because he had found in Proclus the kind of student and successor he had long sought—one who was capable <margin>p.30</margin> of mastering a vast range of learning, both human <note>likely referring to the liberal arts and literature</note> and divine.

### XIII.

Thus, in a period of less than two years, Proclus read all the works of Aristotle with Syrianus: the *Logic*, *Ethics*, *Politics*, *Physics*, and even his theological science <note>referring to Aristotle's *Metaphysics*</note>. Once he was sufficiently trained in these—as if by certain preliminary exercises and "minor mysteries"—Syrianus led him to the sacred discipline of Plato itself. He did this in a systematic order, not, as the saying goes, "with a skipping foot." <margin>p.31</margin> To ensure that Proclus could behold the true mysteries of Plato, Syrianus made sure he looked not with the clouded eyes of the soul, but with a pure and unblemished intellect. By devoting himself day and night to vigils, exercises, and study, and by writing concise summaries of the lectures using his own judgment, Proclus made such progress in a short time that by his twenty-eighth year, he had written many works, including those highly harmonious and scholarly commentaries on the *Timaeus* <note>Plato's dialogue on the creation of the universe</note>. Through this kind of education, his character also became more refined, as he grew in virtue just as he grew in knowledge.

### XIV.

He also mastered the "civil virtues" <note>the practical virtues required for life in a community</note> from Aristotle’s <margin>p.32</margin> books on politics and Plato’s *Laws* and *Republic*. However, Proclus did not want to appear to be merely contemplating these ideas without ever putting them into practice. Since he was personally hindered from managing public affairs because he was occupied with greater things <note>namely, his spiritual and philosophical teaching</note>, he encouraged the devout man Archiadas to take up that role. Proclus mentored Archiadas, explaining the principles and methods of political virtue to him. Just as those who cheer on runners, he urged Archiadas to lead the entire state for the common good and to bestow benefits on individual citizens privately, acting according to all the virtues, but especially according to justice—

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus. The previous section likely introduced Proclus’s social circle and his influence on his close friend and patron, Archiadas.</meta>

### 42 MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. XIV. XV.

And in practice, Proclus stirred a desire for imitation in Archiadas, p. 33. by demonstrating to him his own generosity regarding wealth and his magnificence. This was shown through his gifts to friends, relatives, guests, and fellow citizens; he proved that he was never conquered by greed, and he contributed to many public works. When Archiadas was dying, he appointed as his heirs the cities—both his own homeland <note>Archiadas was the grandson of Plutarch of Athens, but his specific ancestral "homeland" likely refers to the family's origins before settling in Athens.</note> and Athens. Indeed, Archiadas became such a lover of truth, both through the domestic habits of his family and through this friendship with Proclus, that our people, whenever they wish to mention him, refer to him with a celebratory voice as nothing less than "The Pious Archiadas."

### XV.

The Philosopher himself also engaged in civic consultations, p. 34. attending public assemblies where the affairs of the state were discussed. There, he would offer the most prudent opinions and confer with the <term>principibus</term> <note>original: "principibus"; here referring to the governors or leading magistrates of late Roman Athens.</note> regarding the law. He did not merely encourage them, but in a sense compelled them through his philosophical authority to "render to each his due" <note>This phrase, *suum cuique tribuere*, is a fundamental principle of Roman justice.</note>. He even took a public interest in the moral character of the Rulers, teaching them to govern with sobriety not only through his words but by the example of his own life, as he was a model of <term>temperantia</term> <note>Temperance or self-restraint, one of the four cardinal virtues.</note> for everyone else. 

Furthermore, he gave a truly **Herculean** demonstration of civic courage. For when he was tossed into a sea of great political storms, p. 35. with the winds of a pure life blowing fiercely against him, this man conducted his life with gravity and constancy, however much he was in peril. At one time, having been much harassed by the destructive and dangerous wickedness of violent men, he left Athens to avoid resisting fate and set out for Asia. This journey turned out to be to his great benefit: for God provided the opportunity for this departure so that Proclus might not remain ignorant of the more ancient <term>rituum</term> <note>Religious rites or sacred mysteries.</note> which were still preserved in that place. Indeed, he diligently learned about their customs, and they in turn benefited from his long-standing wisdom.

<summary>This passage describes the civic virtues of Proclus and his friend Archiadas, highlighting their generosity, justice, and Proclus's courageous decision to go into temporary exile in Asia to study ancient religious rites.</summary>

MARINUS' PROCLUS: XV. XVI. 43

they were taught those things which they had neglected over an interval of time, for the Philosopher <note>Proclus</note> surely reported on matters pertaining to the Gods with greater accuracy. <margin>p.36</margin> And although he did all these things and lived according to them, he nonetheless remained so well hidden—more so even than the Pythagoreans, who kept that precept of their founder, **Live unnoticed**, <note>original: Λάθε βιώσας (*Lathe biōsas*), a famous Epicurean maxim that Marinus here attributes to the Pythagorean tradition of silence and withdrawal.</note> unshaken. Having spent only a year in the regions of Lydia, he returned to Athens by the providence of the Goddess who presides over philosophy <note>Likely referring to Athena, the patron of Athens and wisdom.</note>. In this way, courage was perfected in him: first by nature, then by habit, and later by doctrine, which explains the causes of things. Likewise, he demonstrated in another way through his actions what use he could be in governing public affairs, for he wrote letters to the leading men and thus benefited entire cities. <margin>p.37</margin> My witnesses for this are those upon whom these benefits were bestowed—the Athenians, the Argives, and others of various nations.

XVI.

Furthermore, he greatly promoted the study of literature by this same method, taking up the care of those who practiced letters, demanding stipends from the rulers and ensuring that rewards were decreed to each according to their merit. And indeed, he did these things not randomly nor to curry favor, but he compelled those very people—since it was a matter of great concern to him—to ensure each was most diligent in their own profession, while he himself investigated and weighed their individual works. For he was a judge sufficiently equipped in all matters. But if he discovered anyone treating their studies too negligently, he would rebuke them most sharply; so much so that he even seemed extremely vehement and ambitious, <margin>p.38</margin> because he both wanted and was able to judge all things correctly. And he was certainly desirous of glory. Yet this was not a vice in him, as it is in most men, because he looked only toward good things and virtue. And perhaps nothing truly excellent could ever be achieved among men without this stirring of the soul. But he was also intense; I will not deny that. However, he was at the same time gentle, for he was very easily appeased, and in a moment his...

->### THE LIFE OF PROCLUS BY MARINUS. 16. 17.<-

<margin>p.39</margin> ...he showed his anger was like wax <note>original: "iram demonstrabat esse ceream"; an idiom meaning his temper was easily softened or melted away.</note> For at almost the same time, he would be entirely carried away in reprimanding someone—if I may put it that way—and yet be moved by a certain natural connection of the soul and a shared feeling of pain to do good for those very people, and to intercede on their behalf with the leaders <note>The Latin 'principes' refers to high-ranking Roman officials or local governors.</note>.

### 17.

Indeed, another specific example of that natural connection of his soul with others comes opportunely to my mind. For I do not think such a thing has been recorded of any other man. Although he was inexperienced in marriage and children (because he did not desire them, for even though many very noble and wealthy women were offered to him whom he could have married, he remained free from all of them, as I was saying), he nevertheless showed such great care for his household and all his friends, and their wives and children, as if he were <margin>p.40</margin> a sort of universal father <note>original: "communis aliquis pater"</note> and the very cause of their birth. He took a singular interest in the life of each one. 

For if ever any of his close associates were seized by illness, he would first of all earnestly entreat the Gods for them, both with sacrifices and with hymns; then he would be promptly present at the bedside of the sick person, calling together doctors and urging them to try whatever could be achieved by their art as quickly as possible. He himself would sometimes offer some unique insight among the doctors, and in this way he delivered many from great dangers. 

How great his kindness was even toward his servants <note>Likely referring to enslaved members of the household, though the term 'humanitas' suggests a gentle treatment.</note>, whom he employed extensively, anyone who wishes can understand from the blessed man’s will. Among all his associates, he loved Archiadas especially, along with his relatives, primarily for this <margin>p.41</margin> reason: that he traced his lineage back to Plutarch the Philosopher <note>This refers to Plutarch of Athens, the Neoplatonist teacher, rather than the famous biographer.</note>. Furthermore, he loved him because of the Pythagorean friendship <term>Pythagorean friendship: a deep, spiritual bond between students of philosophy based on the idea that "friends have all things in common."</term> which he had established with Archiadas, as he was both his companion in studies and his teacher. This second type of friendship, of the two we touched upon briefly above, seems to have been the strongest and most excellent between them. For Archiadas wanted nothing that Proclus did not also want, nor conversely did Proclus want anything that Archiadas did not.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his successor Marinus. We are transitioning from a discussion of Proclus’s social and civic "political" virtues to his "purifying" virtues—the spiritual practices intended to detach the soul from the physical body.</meta>

# MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. XVIII, XIX. 45

## XVIII.

Having now reached the goal for the types of political virtue <note>In the Neoplatonic hierarchy, "political virtues" are the foundational social ethics like justice and moderation that allow a person to function well in human society.</note>—which are lower than the true virtues—and having concluded with the topic of friendship, <margin>p. 42</margin> let us move on to the **purifying virtues**, which are distinct from the political ones. Although the political virtues also involve purging the soul in a certain way—and, as far as is possible, they ensure that the soul looks upon human affairs as if loosened and free, possessing a certain similarity to God, which is the highest goal of the soul—yet they do not all "separate" the soul in the same manner. Indeed, some separate it more, others less. 

While the political virtues are certainly types of purifications that adorn those who possess them even while they remain here on earth, making them better by ruling and moderating anger, desire, and all other passions, and by removing false opinions from their midst; <margin>p. 43</margin> those virtues superior to these, the **purifying virtues**, separate the soul entirely from this <term>leaden mass of the body</term> <note>original: "massa vere plumbea." A common Platonic metaphor for the physical body, which was seen as a heavy weight anchoring the divine soul to the material world.</note> and facilitate an easy departure from the things of this world. 

The Philosopher <note>Referring to Proclus.</note> applied himself to these very virtues throughout his entire life while engaged in Philosophy. He taught through his discourse what they were like and how they might be acquired by man, and he especially modeled his own life after them. He continually performed those actions by which the soul is separated: day and night he used prayers, ritual cleansings, and other purifications <margin>p. 44</margin> derived from both the **Orphic** and the **Chaldean** traditions <note>Proclus synthesized the mystical teachings of Orphism (ancient Greek mysteries) and the Chaldean Oracles (a 2nd-century text of "theurgy" or divine-working) into his philosophical system.</note>. He would also diligently go down to the sea every single month—sometimes even twice or three times in a month. He practiced these rites not only in the vigor of his youth, but also at the sunset of his life, observing that custom perpetually as if it were a set of established laws.

## XIX.

Regarding food and drink, he partook of only the necessary pleasures, just enough to avoid illness and so that he would not be troubled by them; for in these matters he was by far the most frugal. Above all, he loved <term>abstaining from animal flesh</term> <note>Proclus followed the Pythagorean and Platonic tradition of vegetarianism, believing that eating meat tethered the soul more closely to the lower, irrational nature of animals.</note>. But if ever a time happened where he was urged more forcefully to eat them...

<summary>This page describes Proclus’s transition from social ethics to "purifying" spiritual practices, detailing his ascetic lifestyle, ritual cleansings in the sea, and his strict vegetarian diet.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Purifying Virtues, Asceticism, Orphic traditions, Chaldean Oracles, Vegetarianism, Ritual purification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, moving from his personal habits of temperance to his universal approach to religious ritual and his final endurance of physical illness.</meta>

->46          THE LIFE OF PROCLUS BY MARINUS. XIX. XX.<-

<margin>p. 45</margin> ...as if he were merely tasting it, and then only sparingly and in the manner of a sampler. He purified himself every month according to the sacred rites celebrated by the Romans—and even earlier by the Phrygians—in honor of the Mother of the Gods <note>The goddess Cybele, a major deity in the Roman Empire originally from Phrygia.</note>. He observed the Egyptian "unlucky days" <note>original: "nefastos dies"—days considered spiritually inauspicious or forbidden for business in ancient calendars.</note> more diligently than even the Egyptians themselves did; moreover, he set aside certain days for special fasting because of certain visions he experienced. On the last day of the month, he would practice a fast, not even having dined the evening before. As for the New Moons, how splendidly and holily he celebrated them! Indeed, he performed the significant festivals of almost all nations <margin>p. 46</margin> according to the custom of each country with the proper sacrifices. For him, these were not—as they are for most people—an occasion for leisure or excess, but rather for constant prayers, hymns, and similar devotions. This is made clear by his own hymns, which contain praises not only for those gods whom the Greeks venerate, but they also celebrate Marnas of Gaza, <margin>p. 47</margin> Aesculapius Leontuchus of Ascalon <note>A healing god; 'Leontuchus' means 'Lion-Holder.'</note>, and another called Theandrites <note>A deity worshipped by the Arabs, whose name implies a "God-Man."</note>, whom the Arabs hold in great veneration, as well as Isis, who is worshipped at Philae—and, in short, all the others. For it was a very familiar saying of that most religious man that it is proper for a Philosopher not to care for the institutions and rituals of just one city, nor of only certain nations, but to be, in a universal sense, a <term>priest of the whole world</term> <note>original: "sacrorum antistitem"—often translated as a hierophant or overseer of sacred rites. This reflects Proclus's belief that a philosopher represents all humanity before the divine.</note>. Thus he was pure and holy <margin>p. 48</margin> insofar as it pertains to the virtue of temperance.

### XX.

Regarding pain, he tried to avoid it. But if it ever occurred, he bore it gently, and he lessened its impact by the fact that the "best part" of himself <note>Referring to the rational soul or intellect, which Neoplatonists believed could remain detached from bodily suffering.</note> was not affected by the pains at the same time. His final illness showed enough of his soul's fortitude in these matters. <margin>p. 49</margin> For when he was weighed down by it and racked by the most severe torments, he attempted to drive away such great pains. For this reason, he often commanded us to recite hymns; and when they were being recited, there was a total relief and soothing of the pain. What is even more wonderful is that he remembered the words of what he heard, even though he had forgotten almost all human affairs...

<summary>This page describes Proclus's rigorous religious life, highlighting his role as a "universal priest" who honored deities from many cultures, and notes his use of sacred hymns to manage pain during his final illness.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Cybele, Egyptian calendar, Marnas of Gaza, Theandrites, religious pluralism, healing hymns, Stoic endurance, Hierophant</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Proclus by his student Marinus, specifically detailing Proclus’s final days and his mastery over his physical and emotional states as he approached death. It follows descriptions of his ascetic lifestyle and intellectual achievements.</meta>

->MARINUS’S LIFE OF PROCLUS. CHAPTERS 20, 21. PAGE 47<-

...as the dissolution of his body grew more burdensome. For when we began to speak, he would complete the hymns and many of the poems of Orpheus <note>Orpheus was a legendary hero and musician; the Neoplatonists considered the "Orphic Hymns" to be sacred theological texts revealing the nature of the gods.</note>, for these were what we, who were present with him, were reciting. Nor was he merely so steadfast in enduring bodily ailments, but he was even more so regarding those misfortunes which occurred externally and which seemed to happen beyond expectation. Indeed, concerning each thing that happened, he would say: "Such are these things; <margin>p. 50</margin> they are customary." This seems to me—or rather, it seemed then—to be a memorable saying, and clear evidence of the greatness of this philosopher’s soul. He restrained anger as much as he could, so that it would either not be stirred up at all, or at least his reason would not give its consent; rather, the other part of the soul, which lacks reason <note>The "irrational soul" in Platonic thought includes the emotions and physical drives.</note>, might be moved slightly and lightly against his will. As for sexual desire <note>original: "venerem," referring to Venus/Aphrodite, the goddess of love and physical desire</note>, he dealt with it according to nature, but in such a way, of course, that it did not proceed beyond the lightest mental image.

### XXI.

<margin>p. 51</margin> Thus, gathering himself together from all parts and returning into himself, the soul of this blessed man departed in a certain way from the body, even while he still seemed to be held by it. For within him was <term>wisdom</term> <note>prudentia; here referring to the Platonic virtue of Phronesis or practical-spiritual wisdom.</note>—not of the "civic" kind <note>Civic virtues were the basic social ethics; Proclus is described as having reached the higher "purifying" virtues of a contemplative.</note>, which is concerned with the good administration of things that could be one way or another, but a wisdom pure in itself, namely in contemplation and in turning himself back into himself, never consenting to the body. Within him was <term>temperance</term>, because he did not touch evil things and was not affected even in a small degree, but rather was entirely free from all passions. Finally, there was <term>fortitude</term>, because he did not fear his own departure from the body. Since reason and mind held the sovereignty within him, and the lower faculties no longer opposed <term>purifying justice</term>, his entire life was adorned.

<summary>This passage describes the stoic and holy manner in which Proclus faced physical decline, emphasizing his use of Orphic hymns and his mastery over anger and desire. It concludes by categorizing his character through the "purifying virtues," showing him as a man whose soul had already begun to detach from the material world.</summary>

48 MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXII.

## XXII.

<margin>p.52.</margin> Having successfully completed and surpassed even this level of virtues, as if advancing through the highest and most mystical step, he ascended to the greatest and most perfect virtues. In this, he was guided by a fortunate nature and an education specifically designed for the pursuit of knowledge. Now purified, refined, and having conquered the circumstances of his own birth <note>Proclus and other Neoplatonists believed that through philosophy, a soul could transcend its "horoscope" or the material constraints of its physical birth.</note>, he looked down upon the empty boasters and those who merely carried the ritual wand <term>thyrsus-bearers: a reference to a Greek proverb quoted by Plato—"Many are the thyrsus-bearers, but few are the mystics"—meaning many pretend to be philosophers, but few truly understand.</term> of wisdom. He himself successfully entered the inner sanctuary of wisdom and became a true witness to the blessed visions found within.

<margin>p.53.</margin> He no longer required long-winded arguments or proofs to gather knowledge of these divine matters. Instead, he enjoyed direct, simple contemplation, gazing upon the pattern of the Divine Mind through the pure action of his own intellect. He attained a virtue that no one could accurately call "prudence" or "practical wisdom"; it is more rightly called "wisdom" <note>original: *sapientiam*</note>, or perhaps a name even more sacred. Guided by this, the Philosopher [Proclus] easily saw through the entire Theology of both the Greeks and the Barbarians <note>In this context, "Barbarians" refers to non-Greek ancient cultures like the Egyptians, Chaldeans, and Persians, whom Proclus believed shared a common divine truth.</note>, including the truths hidden behind the veil of mythical fables. He brought these truths into the clearest light for the benefit of those who are willing and able to understand them.

<margin>p.54.</margin> He interpreted each point with divine insight, demonstrating the harmony between these different traditions. After investigating all the writings of the ancients, he carefully adopted whatever he found to be of genuine quality and supported by general agreement. However, if he found anything superficial or empty, he rejected it entirely as flawed. Whatever contradicted established truths, he vigorously dismantled through diligent examination. He addressed these topics with both excellence and clarity in public gatherings and recorded everything in his books. He was a man of miraculous industry; he often completed five lectures in a single day, and sometimes more. He also wrote extensively, frequently producing up to seven hundred lines of text <note>The original uses the word *versuum* (verses/lines), referring to a standard unit of measurement for ancient manuscript length.</note>. Furthermore, he met with other philosophers, enjoyed their company, and held evening discussions with them after he had finished his daily writing. He accomplished all this with such dedication that...

<summary>This page describes Proclus reaching the peak of intellectual and mystical "theoretic" virtues, where he no longer needed logic but saw truth directly. It also highlights his immense productivity as a teacher and writer, and his ability to find a common truth between Greek and non-Greek religious traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, contemplative virtues, Greek theology, Barbarian theology, philosophical education, thyrsus-bearers, divine mind</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus. The previous section likely detailed Proclus's rigorous daily rituals and piety.</meta>

->MARINUS’S LIFE OF PROCLUS. CHAPTERS XXII, XXIII. 49.<-

...nor did he cease to worship the Gods even at night, remaining sleepless <note>original: "exsomnis"</note>, and he would diligently pray to the sun as it rose, reached its zenith <note>the highest point in the sky at midday</note>, and set. <margin>p. 55</margin>

### XXIII.

He was the first to introduce many doctrines <term>placitorum: philosophical principles or established opinions</term> that had occurred to no one before him, regarding both natural things and those that are <term>intelligible: things understood by the intellect rather than the physical senses</term> and divine. He was the first to teach that there is a specific class of souls that possess the power to contemplate multiple <term>forms: the "species" or eternal patterns of things</term> at the same time. He positioned this class, quite reasonably, as a middle point between the <term>Mind: the "Nous" or divine intellect</term>—which understands all things at once in a single, concentrated gaze—and those souls that are only able to focus their vision on one single form at a time. 

If anyone wishes to look through his writings, they will discover far more teachings unique to him <margin>p. 56</margin>. I have chosen to pass over listing them all now, so as not to make this account too long by chasing every detail. However, anyone who reads his works will easily see that what I have already shared about him is entirely true. This is even more certain for those who knew him personally, saw his face, and heard him interpreting and devoting himself to his excellent lectures, following the Platonic and Socratic teachings <note>original: "dogmata"</note> in his annual school sessions. 

Indeed, he seems to have been filled with divine inspiration. From his wise mouth, he brought forth words that were like the purest and thickest falling snow; his eyes seemed to glow with a brilliant splendor, and the rest of his face shone with a divine light. 

On one occasion, while he was teaching and interpreting, a man of great reputation in the state <note>original: "in rep. nominis," referring to the Roman government or public life</note> named Rufinus—a seeker of truth and a man worthy of respect—came upon him. <margin>p. 57</margin> Rufinus perceived a light flowing around Proclus’s head. When the Philosopher had finished his interpretation, Rufinus stood up and <term>venerated: showed deep religious respect or "adored"</term> him, and gave sworn testimony to the divine vision he had witnessed. This same Rufinus later offered him a great amount of gold when the war had ended... 

<summary>Marinus describes Proclus as a pioneer of Neoplatonic philosophy who introduced new theories on the nature of the soul and appeared physically transformed by divine light during his lectures.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Philosophy of the Soul, Rufinus, Divine Inspiration, Plato, Socrates, Late Antiquity</keywords>

->50 MARINUS'S PROCLUS. XXIII. XXIV. XXV.<-

...after he had returned from Asia once the danger had passed. But Proclus rejected this gift <note>This likely refers to a public office or financial endowment offered to him upon his return to Athens.</note> as well, and would by no means agree to accept it.

### XXIV.

But to return to the matters we proposed at the beginning: after having discussed the contemplative wisdom of the Philosopher <note>Marinus almost always refers to his teacher Proclus simply as "The Philosopher."</note>—though perhaps not as worthily as he deserves—it follows, **p.58**, that we should now also speak of the <term>justice</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, justice at this high level is not about social fairness, but the soul's proper internal alignment with the Divine.</note> which belongs to this specific class of virtues. For this kind of justice is not concerned with the distribution of goods or proportions, as were those lower virtues we mentioned before. We must also set aside that kind of justice which acts only upon itself, where everything is directed solely toward the rational soul. Instead, the unique characteristic of the justice we are discussing now is to refer every single action solely to the **Mind** <note>The "Mind" or *Nous* in Neoplatonism is the realm of eternal truths and divine ideas.</note> and to **God**. It is understood that the Philosopher achieved this in a truly extraordinary way. Indeed, he scarcely rested from his daily labors, refreshing his body with sleep only briefly; and perhaps even then, he was never truly free from meditating and contemplating. Certainly, he would shake off sleep as quickly as possible—treating it as a kind of numbness of the soul—and even before the time for morning prayers arrived (since a great part of the night had not yet passed), **p.59**, he would remain alone, reclining on his bed, either composing hymns or examining and searching out <term>doctrines</term> <note>original: "dogmata"</note> which he would then commit to writing after rising during the day.

### XXV.

In a similar way, he also pursued <term>temperance</term> <note>original: "temperantiam"; also known as *sophrosyne*, or moderation.</note>, which is closely related to these virtues. This temperance consists in the "turning back" of the soul toward the Mind; it allows itself to be neither touched nor moved by any other external things. Finally, he joined <term>fortitude</term> <note>original: "fortitudinem"; also translated as "courage."</note> to these as a companion in a perfect manner. With the greatest effort, he aspired to that "liberty" which knows no passions <note>The state of *apatheia*, or freedom from being driven by emotions and bodily desires.</note>, and which he observed to exist by nature within that **Divine Mind** which he contemplated. And so, through all these things...

->MARINUS'S LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXV. XXVI. 51<-

He lived the life of an exceptionally upright man, to which—as <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the foundational philosopher of Neoplatonism; he organized virtues into a hierarchy from civic to divine.</note> Plotinus says—even the <term>political virtues</term> <note>Also known as "civic virtues," these are the practical ethics involving justice, temperance, and courage required for living well in a human community.</note> can lead. Yet, leaving these far behind, he attempted to exchange that life for another, far more perfect one: that of the immortal Gods. <margin>p. 60</margin> For he longed to become like the Gods themselves, rather than merely like men endowed with virtue.

XXVI.

Furthermore, he had already made these virtues familiar to himself while he was still a frequent student of the philosopher <note>Syrianus was the head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens and Proclus’s beloved mentor.</note> Syrianus, and while he was examining and following the commentaries of the ancients. He drank in certain small elements and, as it were, the "seeds" of <term>Orphic and Chaldean</term> <note>The "Orphic" traditions were based on poems attributed to the mythical singer Orpheus, while the "Chaldean" refers to the *Chaldean Oracles*, a 2nd-century mystical text. Both were treated as sacred scripture by later Neoplatonists.</note> theology from the mouth of his teacher, because it was not granted to him to hear his master interpret the poems of Orpheus in their entirety. 

For Syrianus had promised Proclus and <note>Domninus of Larissa was a fellow student of Syrianus who often disagreed with Proclus on philosophical interpretations.</note> Domninus (a philosopher of the Syrian nation and likewise a later successor of Syrianus) that he would explain one of two things to them: either the Orphic writings or the <term>Oracles</term>, <margin>p. 61</margin> leaving the choice to them. However, these two men could not agree among themselves on which to choose: Domninus wanted the poems of Orpheus explained to him, while Proclus preferred the Oracles. This study was never completed, because not long afterward the great Syrianus departed from the living. 

Therefore, having drawn those first supports—as we have said—from the mouth of his teacher, Proclus applied himself with the greatest care to Syrianus's written commentaries on Orpheus. He was also constantly nourished by the voluminous works of <note>Porphyry and Iamblichus were earlier, highly influential Neoplatonists who wrote extensively on the integration of philosophy and divine ritual.</note> Porphyry and Iamblichus regarding the Oracles and similar Chaldean writings. Through these, he advanced as far as a human is permitted to the summit of the <margin>p. 62</margin> highest virtues, which the divine Iamblichus was accustomed to call, quite brilliantly and divinely, the <term>theurgic virtues</term>. <note>From the Greek 'theourgia' (god-working); these virtues relate to the practice of sacred rituals intended to unite the soul with the divine, surpassing mere intellectual understanding.</note> Consequently, by collecting the expositions of the philosophers who had preceded him in age...

<meta>This page continues Marinus's biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, moving into Chapter 26, which focuses on his scholarly output and his final years.</meta>

52 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; MARINUS'S LIFE OF PROCLUS. CHAPTER 26.

He condensed the commentaries into a single work, completing this task over five full years; concerning this work, he even dreamed this divine dream. It seemed to him, in the quiet of sleep, that the great Plutarch <note>original Latin: *Plutarchus*. This refers to Plutarch of Athens (died c. 432 AD), the head of the Neoplatonic Academy and Proclus’s predecessor once removed.</note> stood beside him, affirming that he would live as many years as the **tetrads**—or quaternions—he had composed on the Oracles. 

**p. 63.** After later calculating the number of these <term>tetrads</term> <note>From the Latin *tetrades sive quaterniones*. In manuscript production, these were "gatherings" or booklets of four folded sheets of parchment or paper.</note>, he discovered there were seventy. The outcome of the matter at the end of his life easily demonstrated that this dream was indeed divine. For he reached seventy-five years of age, as we have already mentioned above; however, during the last five years, he no longer enjoyed his full strength. 

His body—which was otherwise naturally well-constituted—began to languish after his seventieth year had passed. This was because it had been weakened by a harsh and scarcely tolerable regimen of diet, frequent ritual washings, and many fasts, so that he was much slower in performing all his duties than he had been before. 

**p. 64.** Nevertheless, even in this state, he did not cease dedicating his time to prayer and the composition of hymns; he likewise wrote several things and spent time with friends, but in every detail, he missed his former vigor. Mindful of his dream, he marveled at it and would often say that he had lived only seventy years. 

While he was dealing with such a weak body, a certain young man named Hegias made him much more spirited, especially regarding the interpretation of sacred writings. Hegias, from a tender age, displayed excellent signs of all kinds of ancestral virtues, and was indeed one of that "golden chain" <note>The "Golden Chain" refers to the spiritual and intellectual succession of Platonic philosophers, but here also implies a physical lineage.</note> descended from Solon <note>The famous lawgiver and poet of Athens (c. 638–558 BC).</note>. 

This youth remained most diligently at Proclus's side as he handed down the Platonic and other theologies. Proclus, now advanced in age, 

**p. 65.** was no small bit delighted to share his writings with the young man, after he realized that the youth was making <term>cubit-long progress</term> <note>original Latin: *cubitales profectus*. A metaphorical expression suggesting the student was making massive, measurable strides in his learning.</note> in every branch of teaching. 

Let these things be said briefly and concisely regarding the Philosopher's preparation for the **Chaldean Oracles** <note>A 2nd-century collection of mystical and philosophical utterances that the Neoplatonists considered to be divine revelation.</note>.

### MARINUS’S LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXVII. XXVIII. 53

## XXVII.

But I, having at one time read the verses of Orpheus <note>Orpheus: The legendary poet and prophet of ancient Greece, to whom a vast body of mystical and "theurgic" poetry was attributed by the Neoplatonists.</note> in his company, and having heard during his lectures not only the things found in the works of Iamblichus and Syrianus, but also many other things savoring of a more hidden theology, I asked the Philosopher <note>Proclus is frequently referred to simply as "the Philosopher."</note> not to leave even these divine poems unexplained. I received an answer, however, that although he had often <margin>p. 66</margin> thought about writing commentaries on the Orphic works, he had been strictly forbidden from doing so by more than one dream. For it seemed to him that Syrianus <note>Syrianus was Proclus’s beloved teacher and predecessor as head of the Academy in Athens.</note> was present, deterring him from his purpose with threats. Therefore, I attacked him with other "engines of war" <note>A metaphor for persistent persuasion.</note> and begged that he would at least annotate those passages in his master's books which he especially approved of. When the best of teachers had been persuaded by me and had written some notes on the front of each of Syrianus's commentaries, there was born for us this collection of all these notes, and even <term>scholia</term> <note>Scholia: Explanatory notes or comments written in the margins of a manuscript.</note> and commentaries on Orpheus of no small size; nonetheless, Proclus was not permitted to do the same for all that divine poetry and the complete Orphic Rhapsodies.

## XXVIII.

Since, as has been said, his incredible study of these matters had won for him a greater and more perfect degree of <term>theurgic virtue</term> <note>Theurgic virtue: The power to perform "theurgy" (divine work), a system of rituals intended to purify the soul and unite it with the divine, going beyond mere intellectual philosophy.</note>, <margin>p. 67</margin> he no longer stopped at the contemplative level. Nor was he content with either of the two kinds of divine properties, exerting his mind and thought only toward higher things; but he now also cared for the other kind of virtue, in that more divine way, and no longer according to the "civil" <note>Civil or political virtue: The social and ethical behavior of a good citizen, which Neoplatonists considered the most basic level of virtue.</note> method which was discussed above. For he made use of the gatherings and conversations of the Chaldeans <note>The "Chaldaean Oracles" were a sacred text of the later Neoplatonists, believed to contain divine revelations regarding the soul and the cosmos.</note>, and those "circumrotations" <note>original: "circumrotationibus." This refers to the use of the "iynx" or "strophalos"—a spinning disk or top used in theurgic rituals to call upon the gods.</note> which are superior even in speech. Having grasped these, <margin>p. 68</margin> he thoroughly learned their meanings and the rest of their use from Asclepigenia, the daughter of Plutarch. For she alone at that time preserved the knowledge handed down through her father from Nestorius...

54 MARINUS' LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXVIII. XXIX.

...the knowledge of the great <term>Orgia: secret sacred rites or mysteries</term> and the entire <term>theurgy: "god-working," a system of ritual intended to purify the soul and communicate with the divine</term> discipline. Even before this, the Philosopher <note>Proclus</note>, having been purified by <term>Chaldaic lustrations: ritual cleansings associated with the Chaldean Oracles, a core Neoplatonist text</term> in the proper ritual order, was himself present as an <term>epoptes: a witness of the highest grade of initiation</term> to the bright apparitions of <term>Hecate: a goddess of magic, crossroads, and the moon, central to theurgic practice</term>. He even mentions this himself somewhere in one of his own commentaries. Furthermore, by skillfully moving a certain *iynx* <note>original: "iynge" — a magical tool, often a spinning disk or sphere used to draw down divine influence</note> or "Hecatic sphere," he brought down rain and freed the region of Attica from a disastrous heatwave. <margin>p. 69.</margin> He also stopped earthquakes by placing protective charms, tested the power of the prophetic tripod, and composed verses about his own destiny. In his fortieth year, he seemed to hear these short verses in his sleep:

*There is an immortal splendor there, such as exists above the heavens,*
*Leaping forth from the fiery source of divine light.*

When he began his forty-second year, he seemed to speak these verses in a loud voice:

<margin>p. 70.</margin> *Behold, the soul seethes with the flames of divine fire,*
*And...*

->MARINUS’S PROCLUS. CHAPTERS XXIX. XXX. 55<-

...he was accustomed to do, he fled to the final anchor <note>original: "supremam ancoram"; a Greek and Latin idiom for a last resort</note>, which is to say, he approached the philosopher Proclus as his best savior and urged him tirelessly to intercede with the Deity on behalf of his daughter with his prayers without delay. <margin>p. 72</margin> He [Proclus] therefore, taking with him Pericles of Lydia <note>A fellow philosopher and friend of Proclus</note>, a man no less worthy of the title of Philosopher, went to the temple of Asclepius <note>The Greek god of medicine and healing</note> to pray to the God for the health of the sick girl. For at that time, the city [Athens] still happily enjoyed the presence of this divinity, and rejoiced that the temple of the Savior God was still intact and not yet laid waste. As soon as Proclus prayed according to the ancient rite, <margin>p. 73</margin> the girl immediately felt a great change and a relief from her illness; indeed, Asclepius restored her to health with no effort at all, as a God does. When the sacred rites were finished, Proclus went to Asclepigenia and found her liberated from the evils of the disease that had previously tormented her, now enjoying her health. Furthermore, this whole business was carried out by Proclus in secret from most others, so that he offered no sign of the matter to those who tried to scrutinize it. For this purpose, he used the house where he lived, which was most convenient. <margin>p. 74</margin> For besides his other fortunes, the house was also most fitting for him; it was the one where his "father" or teacher Syrianus and his "grandfather" (for so he did not hesitate to call him) Plutarch <note>Plutarch of Athens (died c. 432 AD), the Neoplatonist teacher, not the famous biographer</note> had once lived. It was situated near the shrine of Asclepius, made famous by Sophocles, and near the temple of Liber <note>The Roman name for the god Dionysus</note>, which is near the theater, looking out toward the high citadel of Minerva <note>The Acropolis of Athens, dedicated to the goddess Athena</note> or at least visible from it. <margin>p. 75</margin>

But she also demonstrated this clearly when her image, which had until then been dedicated in the Parthenon <note>The "Temple of the Virgin" on the Acropolis</note>...

56 MARINI PROCLVS. XXX. XXXI.

...they did not hesitate to move from their places even the most sacred and immovable things <note>original: "sanctissima quaevis atque immota loco movere." This refers to the historical removal of the great statue of Athena Parthenos from the Parthenon by authorities, likely during the Christianization of Athens.</note>. At that time, it was seen by the Philosopher <note>Marinus frequently refers to Proclus simply as "the Philosopher."</note> in a dream that a woman of noble form appeared to him, who warned him to prepare his house as quickly as possible: "For **Lady Minerva**," <note>Minerva is the Roman equivalent of the Greek goddess Athena.</note> she said, "decides to stay with you." Furthermore, how dear and familiar he was to **Aesculapius** <term>Aesculapius: The Latin name for Asklepios, the Greek god of medicine and healing.</term>—beyond those things which we related a little earlier—convinced us because he felt the presence of that same God during his final illness. For, while he was in a state between sleep and wakefulness, he saw a dragon <note>original: "draconem." In the cult of Asklepios, the god often appeared in the form of a sacred serpent to heal the sick.</note> crawling over his head, from which the disease began to grant him some relief; and so from this vision a certain soothing of his sickness was brought about, so that it would be believable that he might have been restored to full health, had not the desire for death which led him—and his great expectation of it—prevented this, or had he not been so indifferent toward the diligent care of his own body.

XXXI.

p.77. But he also related these other things worthy of being remembered, not indeed without tears of sympathy. For he had feared, as he grew older, that the arthritis which had afflicted his father—a disease which frequently loves and is accustomed to be passed down from parents to children—might also cause him distress. Nor, as it seems, did he fear this without cause. For already previously (a thing which I should have told earlier), he was scorched by pains of that kind, when another miraculous thing happened to him. According to the prescription of someone he had consulted, he placed a plaster <note>original: "emplastrum." A medicinal bandage or poultice.</note> upon his suffering foot, which, while he lay in bed, p.78. an unexpected bird flew in and snatched away. This was certainly a divine and truly health-bringing omen <note>original: "omen"</note> for Proclus, which could have persuaded him to be of good courage for the future. Nevertheless, as I have already said, he was nonetheless tormented by the fear of the disease. Therefore, he petitioned the God on this account, and prayed to him that he might be taught more clearly by him: and when he had fallen asleep after this, he saw (it is indeed a bold thing to say such a thing, or...)

# MARINUS ON PROCLUS. XXXI. XXXII. 57

...though one might hesitate to conceive of such things in the mind, we must nonetheless be daring and undaunted so that the truth may be brought to light. He saw the Epidaurian <note>original: "Epidaurium"; a reference to Asclepius, the god of medicine and healing, whose most famous sanctuary was at Epidaurus.</note> approaching him and contemplating his legs quite carefully; nor did the god disdain, out of his most compassionate love, <margin>p. 79</margin> to touch Proclus's knees with a kiss. After this vision, Proclus remained in good spirits for the rest of his life, and until his extreme old age, he was never again tormented by pains of that kind.

## XXXII.

Furthermore, the God who is worshipped by the people of Adrotta <note>A town in Lydia, in what is now modern-day Turkey.</note> demonstrated no less clearly how familiarly he was worshipped by that man who was so dear to the Gods. For the God kindly presented himself to be seen by Proclus more than once when he frequented the temple. Because there was a difference of opinion among the inhabitants on the matter, Proclus remained in doubt and wished to know which God—or indeed which Gods—dwelled and were worshipped in that place. Some believed that it was a temple of Asclepius, being persuaded of this by various signs; for it is said that voices are certainly heard in 그곳 that place, a table is consecrated to the God, <margin>p. 80</margin> and oracles regarding the recovery of health are given everywhere, and those who approach are miraculously delivered from the most serious life-threatening dangers. 

Others, however, were of the opinion that the <term>Dioscuri</term> <note>The "Youths of Zeus," the twin brothers Castor and Pollux, often associated with protection and horsemanship.</note> frequented that place. This was because there were people who, on the road leading toward Adrotta, seemed to have seen two young men of most beautiful form, riding very swiftly on horses, who said they were hurrying to the temple. These youths indeed bore the faces and features of men, but it was immediately believed that they were a more divine presence; for when the people had already reached the temple, the aforementioned young men appeared to those who were asking no such questions and were occupied with the sacred rites, but they soon withdrew themselves from sight again. 

Consequently, as the Philosopher remained in doubt regarding these matters, as has been said, <margin>p. 81</margin> and since he did not deny credit to these stories, he asked the <term>indigenous</term> <note>original: "indigetes"; refers to the local or native deities specific to a particular land or city.</note> Gods of the place that they might deign to manifest themselves and show who they were. Then, a God appeared to him in a dream...

<summary>This passage describes Proclus being miraculously cured of a chronic leg ailment by a vision of Asclepius and his subsequent attempt to identify the specific deities worshipped at a temple in Adrotta through divine revelation.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Asclepius, Adrotta, Dioscuri, healing miracle, Neoplatonism, divine visions, Castor and Pollux, ancient medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus by his student Marinus, specifically focusing on Proclus’s intimate relationship with the divine and the miraculous signs of favor he received.</meta>

# 58 MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXXII. XXXIII.

...and clearly spoke these words to him: <margin>p. 82</margin> *“What then? Have you not heard of Iamblichus* <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a highly influential philosopher whose works on theurgy and Plato shaped Proclus’s own school of thought.</note> *teaching who those two were, and his praise for Machaon and Podalirius?”* <note>Machaon and Podalirius were the sons of the healing god Asclepius; in Greek mythology, they were the physicians of the Greek army at Troy.</note> Furthermore, the God <note>Likely Asclepius appearing in a vision or dream-state to Proclus.</note> deigned to show our blessed teacher such great kindness that, just like those in theaters who sing the praises of others, the God presented himself. With a clear voice and a composed manner, extending his right hand—for why indeed should I not report the very words of the divinity?—the God did not hesitate to exclaim: ***“Proclus is the glory of the city.”*** <note>original Latin: "Proclus decus civitatis."</note> What greater testimony could there be <margin>p. 83</margin> of this most blessed man’s friendship with God? Indeed, because of the remarkable spiritual affinity <term>sympathy <gloss>Translated from "sympathiam," a technical term in Neoplatonism referring to the cosmic resonance or "feeling-together" that allows humans to communicate with the divine.</gloss></term> by which he was joined to the Gods, he could never hold back his tears whenever he mentioned this event to us or recounted the divine praise he had received.

### XXXIII.

If I wished to pursue and report every detail in a similar way regarding his familiarity with Pan <note>The god of the wild, shepherds, and nature, often associated with sudden inspiration or "panic" in its original divine sense.</note> (the son of Hermes), and the frequent proofs of kindness and manifold help which that deity deigned to bring to Proclus in Athens, and furthermore the kind of happiness...

<summary>This page recounts a divine encounter where the god Asclepius identifies Proclus as the "glory of the city," serving as proof of the philosopher's high spiritual standing. Marinus also begins to describe Proclus’s close relationship with the god Pan and the various forms of divine assistance he received in Athens.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Asclepius, Iamblichus, Machaon, Podalirius, Pan, divine visions, Neoplatonism, spiritual sympathy, Athens</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the detailed description of Proclus's supernatural and ritual abilities (theurgy) toward the concluding summary of his life and the divine favor he enjoyed.</meta>

->MARINUS’ LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXXIV. XXXV. 59<-

### XXXIV.

Since, therefore, we have now surveyed Proclus’ <term>theurgic virtue <note>original: "theurgicam"; theurgy refers to the Neoplatonic practice of "god-working" rituals intended to invoke divine presence or achieve union with the divine.</note></term> and his actions, as well as the successes he fortunately achieved in that realm, and have shown that he was second to none in every kind of virtue—the likes of whom mortals have certainly not seen for a very long time—it remains for us to bring our discourse about him to its conclusion. For this was already, even then, not merely the beginning of our speech, nor—as the proverb goes—<note>Referring to the ancient Greek proverb "the beginning is half of the whole," often attributed to Hesiod or Pythagoras.</note> "half of the whole," but the entire work itself. Having begun with the <term>happiness <note>original: "felicitate"; here meaning "eudaimonia," a state of blessedness or flourishing through wisdom.</note></term> of the philosopher, and having progressed through that same theme, our discourse now returns to it once more. This comes after we have set forth the blessings granted to this excellent man by the Gods and by Providence, having recounted how his prayers were promptly heard and how he beheld the <margin>p. 86.</margin> appearances of the Gods, as well as his healings and the other ways the Gods manifested their aid and concern for him. We have also explained those things which fell to him through good fate and auspicious fortune... <note>The original Latin text ends mid-word: "explicu[imus]".</note>

<summary>In this concluding chapter, Marinus summarizes Proclus's unparalleled virtues and the divine favor he received, asserting that Proclus reached the highest state of philosophical and ritual happiness.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, theurgy, Neoplatonism, virtue, divine providence, eudaimonia, Marinus of Neapolis, ancient philosophy</keywords>

->60 MARINUS’S LIFE OF PROCLUS. SECTIONS 35 AND 36.<-

...I have chosen to set out the chart of his birth <note>original: "schema geniturae"; this refers to his horoscope</note> in this place. It was as follows:

| Planet/Point | Position |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Sun** | 16° - 26' Aries |
| **Moon** | 17° - 29' Gemini |
| **Saturn** | 24° - 23' Taurus |
| **Jupiter** | 24° - 41' Taurus |
| **Mars** | 29° - 50' Sagittarius |
| **Venus** | 23° - [blank] Pisces |
| **Mercury** | 4° - 42' Aquarius |

| **Midheaven** <note>original: "Medium coeli"; the highest point in the sky at the moment of birth</note> | 4° - 42' Capricorn |
| **North Node** <note>original: "caput draconis," or the Head of the Dragon</note> | 24° - 33' Scorpio |
| **New Moon preceding birth** | 8° - 51' Aquarius |

### XXXVI.

Proclus departed from this life in the one hundred and twenty-fourth year after the reign of Julian <note>referring to Julian the Apostate, the last pagan Roman Emperor, whose reign ended in 363 AD; this places Proclus's death in 485 AD</note>, while Nicagora the younger was Archon <note>a chief magistrate</note> at Athens, on the seventeenth day of the Attic month Munychion, or the April of the Romans. His lifeless body was cared for according to the ancestral rite of the Athenians, just as he himself had commanded while he was still living. Indeed, if ever a man was truly blessed, he certainly possessed a careful knowledge and diligent concern for the duties owed to the departed. For he never neglected any part of their customary religion, but <margin>p. 89</margin> every year on certain days he visited the tombs of the Attic Heroes <note>legendary founding figures and protectors of Athens</note>, and then also the tombs of the Philosophers. Furthermore, he performed the due rites <note>original: "iusta fecit"</note> for the Spirits <note>original: "Manibus"; refers to the *Manes*, the ancestral spirits of the dead</note> of his other friends and associates, not through a proxy, but in person. Having thus offered the appropriate worship and honor to each, he proceeded to the Academy <note>the famous school founded by Plato</note>, where he individually appeased the Spirits of his ancestors and kinsmen in a certain place; then, in another part of that same Academy, he offered prayers collectively for the souls of all philosophers. After these religious duties were finished, this most excellent man, in a third location chosen for this purpose, performed sacred rites for the souls of all deceased human beings.

<summary>This page records the precise astrological birth chart of Proclus and describes his death in 485 AD, highlighting his meticulous observance of traditional Athenian funeral rites and his annual rituals honoring heroes, philosophers, and all human souls.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, astrology, horoscope, death of Proclus, Athens, Academy, funeral rites, Manes, Neoplatonism, Julian the Apostate</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, detailing his burial alongside his master Syrianus, the omens preceding his death, and the conclusion of Marinus's primary account.</meta>

->MARINUS’S LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXXVI — XXXVIII. 61<-

XXXVI.

Proclus’s body, therefore, was prepared according to his own instructions, as we have said. When his friends carried him out, he was buried in the eastern part of the suburbs near Lycabettus <note>Mount Lycabettus is a prominent limestone hill in Athens, visible from the Academy where Proclus taught.</note>, where the body of his teacher, Syrianus, also rests. Syrianus himself had requested this while he was still among the living, and for that reason, he had seen to it that a double burial chamber was constructed. p.90. Furthermore, when this most holy man <note>Proclus</note> was consulted about what he wanted done at his funeral—so that nothing should be unseemly or lacking in decorum—he asked for the flutes <note>original: *tibias*; ancient reed instruments often used in funeral processions</note> that a dream had warned him of, p.91. but desired nothing else. An epitaph was inscribed on the tomb, consisting of four verses, which he composed for himself to this effect:

   *I, Proclus the Lycian, lie here, your most devoted student*
      *And your successor, O Syrianus.*
   *Our bodies are hidden together in this common tomb,*
      *Just as the celestial temples now hold both our souls.*

XXXVII.

Nor were omens lacking in the year preceding his death, such as a solar eclipse so great that it seemed as if night were falling during the day. p.92. For the deepest darkness rushed in, so that the stars even appeared in the sky. This happened when the sun was in Capricorn, at the eastern horizon <note>In astrology, the "cardine" refers to one of the four principal points of the zodiacal chart, here the Ascendant.</note>. Furthermore, the writers of the *Almanacs* <term>Ephemeridum: tables predicting the positions of celestial bodies</term> noted another solar eclipse would occur exactly one year later. It is said that such "labors" of the celestial bodies are observed to portend events happening on earth; for us, certainly, the eclipse clearly signified the loss and, as it were, the failing of the great light of Philosophy.

XXXVIII.

And let these things narrated by me concerning the Philosopher suffice for now. p.93. Now, for others who wish to take this labor upon themselves, it will be up to them to record the truth about his disciples and friends. For many from everywhere...

<summary>This passage describes the burial of Proclus in a shared tomb with his predecessor Syrianus and recounts a dramatic solar eclipse that contemporaries viewed as a celestial omen of Proclus's death.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Syrianus, Lycabettus, epitaph, Neoplatonism, solar eclipse, omens, Athens, Philosophy, funeral rites</keywords>

62 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; MARINUS'S LIFE OF PROCLUS. XXXVIII.

It is well known that many visitors frequented his company; some were merely listeners, while others were devoted followers of his doctrine and became his close companions through their shared love of philosophy. I would hope that Proclus’s writings might be cataloged individually by someone more industrious than myself. For I have recorded these few things only to satisfy my own conscience, and to bear witness that I piously revere the divine soul of Proclus and his good genius <note>In Neoplatonism, a "genius" or *daimon* refers to a guiding spirit or the highest part of the soul</note>. Regarding the Philosopher's writings, therefore, I shall add nothing, except that I have heard his commentaries on the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue regarding the creation of the universe; Proclus's commentary on it is one of his most significant surviving works</note> were always preferred above all his other works.
<margin>p. 94.</margin> The commentaries he composed on the *Theaetetus* <note>Plato’s dialogue concerning the nature of knowledge</note> were also highly esteemed. He himself was often accustomed to say that, if it were up to him, he would preserve only the Oracles <note>referring to the *Chaldean Oracles*, a series of mystical texts that Neoplatonists considered divinely inspired</note> and the *Timaeus* out of all the writings of the ancients. 
"The rest," he said, "I would abolish entirely and withhold from the present age, because it happens that many are harmed <note>original: *offendi*; in this context, it implies being led into error or confusion</note> when they approach reading them rashly and without being properly prepared."

***

[The remainder of the page contains faint text which appears to be bleed-through from the reverse side of the leaf.]

<summary>Marinus explains that he will not provide a full bibliography of Proclus's works, but notes that Proclus considered the Timaeus and the Chaldean Oracles to be the only ancient texts essential for students, fearing other works might mislead the uneducated.</summary>

# NOTES ON MARINUS

**Page 1. Fabricius edition. [CONCERNING HAPPINESS]** <note>original: "ΠΕΡῚ ΕΥ̓ΔΑΙΜΟΝΊΑΣ"</note>] The Turin Manuscript <note>A significant 15th-century manuscript formerly held in the National Library of Turin.</note> reads "[Concerning] happiness" <note>original: "π. τῆς Ε."</note>.

**Page 1. [the preparation and]** <note>original: "τὴν παρασκευὴν καὶ"</note>] These words, which the Latin Translator had found in his own manuscript but which are absent from the printed editions, I have restored from the Coislin, Medici, Wolfenbüttel, and Turin manuscripts, and from the Dorvillian manuscript on Chariton, page 602.

**Page 1. [nothingness, it seemed to me it would be well to keep quiet]** <note>original: "οὐδένειαν, καλῶς ἂν ἔχειν ἔδοξέ μοι ἡσυχίαν ἄγειν"</note>] The Turin manuscript reads: "[you are nothing, it seemed to me well to lead]" <note>original: "οὐδὲν εἶ, κ. ἂν ἔδοξέ μοι ἄγειν"</note>.

**Page 1. [to jump beyond the dug-up line]** <note>original: "ὑπὲρ τὰ ἐσκαμμένα ἅλλεσθαι"</note>] "Proclus in book five [rather, book three], chapter thirty, writing on the Pentathlon says: the starting point from which the jump is made is called the *batēr* <term>batēr: the threshold or starting line for athletes</term>, while the end point is the 'dug-up area' <note>original: "τὰ ἐσκαμμένα"</note>. Thus, regarding those who leap beyond the boundary, the makers of proverbs say 'to jump beyond the dug-up line.' Many things concerning this proverb are noted in Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note>." **THE LATIN TRANSLATOR.** — "Besides Plato in the *Cratylus* [chapter 64, where Heindorf notes it], Pollux [III, chapter 30, § 6], Zenobius [Century VI, 23], Suidas [under 'beyond the dug-up line' <note>original: "ὑπὲρ τὰ ἐσκ."</note>, where Kuster notes it], and Apostolius [Century XIX, 92, page 249], Jerome mentions this proverb in his work *Against the Errors of John of Jerusalem*: 'When these matters are being sought, why do you, having omitted the points over which the fight is conducted, step out from the wrestling pit <note>original: "scammate"; referring to the sanded or dug area for athletes</note> and the place of the contest, and linger in foreign and far-removed disputes?'" **FABRICIUS.** — Eumathius *Hysmine* IV, page 133: "if he jumps beyond the dug-up line" <note>original: "ἂν δ' ὑπὲρ τὰ ἐσκαμμένα πηδᾷ"</note>; where Gaulminus notes it. Add Erasmus, *Adages* II. 1. 93; Ferrarius, *Proverbs* 41, and Basil in his critical epistle page 243, bringing forth new things.

**Page 1. [to cast a risk]** <note>original: "ἀναῤῥιπτεῖν κίνδυνον"; an idiom meaning 'to take a gamble' or 'run a risk'</note>] The Coislin, Turin, and Wolfenbüttel manuscripts read "to cast" <note>original: "ἀναῤῥιπτεῖν"</note>, which I preferred over the published "to cast" <note>original: "ἀναῤῥίπτειν"; the editor is choosing between two different Greek accentuations of the same word</note>. Suidas has "to cast," and Thucydides always uses it thus. Otherwise, the matter is very slight, and I could have kept the other form, had the best manuscripts not suggested otherwise. Plutarch in *Nicias* chapter 11: "he cast the risk concerning the ostracism." Agathias, *History* I, page 17 D: "about to cast the risk." Concerning this expression, see the learned men in *Greek Ellipses*, page 238 of that most excellent edition which Schaefer, a most distinguished man, edited and remarkably adorned. Cf. Abresch on Aeschylus, volume 1, page 156. Ammianus Marcellinus XXVI. 6, page 459.

<summary>This page contains philological commentary on the opening of Marinus’s biography of the philosopher Proclus, focusing on restoring the text from various manuscripts and explaining the athletic origin of the proverb "to jump beyond the line."</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, Fabricius, Greek philology, Greek proverbs, jumping beyond the line, manuscript variants, ancient athletics</keywords>

->64 NOTES ON MARINUS<-

"...or, thinking a cruel death more merciful than the evils by which he was afflicted, he suddenly cast the die of all dangers." <note>original Latin: "vel atrocem mortem clementiorem ratus malis quibus afflictabatur, aleam periculorum omnium iecit abrupte."</note>

**Page 1. "measuring or comparing"** <note>original Greek: παραμετρῶν (parametrōn)</note>] The Turin manuscript reads **"measuring around."** <note>original Greek: περιμετρῶν (perimetrōn)</note>

**Page 1. "by means of bulls"** <note>original Greek: διὰ ταύρων (dia taurōn)</note>] In the Turin manuscript there is a marginal note: <margin>The variety of ancient sacrifices.</margin> <note>original Greek: ποικιλότης τῶν πάλαι θυσιῶν</note>

**Page 2. "in meter"** <note>original Greek: ἐν μέτρῳ (en metrō)</note>] The manuscripts C, M, G, and T provide the word **"meter,"** which I have adopted in place of **"meters."** Shortly after, these same manuscripts gave the phrase **"without meter"** <note>original Greek: ἄνευ μέτρου (aneu metrou)</note> instead of **"without meters,"** where the manuscripts M and G also omit the word "but" <note>the Greek particle δὲ (de)</note>. The hymns of Aristides <note>Aelius Aristides, a 2nd-century Greek orator famous for his "prose hymns," which were speeches written in the style of poetry but without strict rhythmic constraints.</note> survive as an example of those written without meter. He himself says at the beginning of his *Hymn to Zeus*: "promising to recite a hymn to Zeus, and that without meter." <note>original Greek: ὑπισχνούμενος ὕμνον ἐρεῖν Διὸς, καὶ ταῦτα ἄνευ μέτρου</note>

**Page 2. "and only a sacrificial cake, and a grain, if it so happens, of frankincense"** <note>original Greek: πόπανον δὲ μόνον, καὶ χόνδρον, εἰ τύχοι, λιβανωτοῦ</note>] The manuscript T reads "grains if they happen." — Tibullus <note>The Roman poet Albius Tibullus, specifically referring to a passage in the "Panegyric of Messalla."</note> [Book 4, poem 1, line 14, and there the scholar Heyne]:

"And a small grain <term>mica|literally a crumb or grain; in Roman religion, it refers to the 'mola salsa,' a simple offering of salted meal used by those who could not afford expensive sacrifices</term> has appeased the Heavenly ones, nor does a bull always fall as a victim for them with gilded horns." <note>original Latin: "Parvaque Coelestes pacavit mica, nec illis semper inaurato taurus cadit hostia cornu." This passage emphasizes that the gods value the piety of the giver over the cost of the gift.</note>

FABRICIUS. — On Tibullus.
