NOTES ON MARINUS. 65

In the commentary on [Plato's] *Cratylus*, chapter 122, Isis is called **"Quick to Hear"** in a Mytilenean inscription which Villoison described:
->OCTATIA, DAUGHTER<-
->OF MARCUS,<-
->TO ISIS PELAGIA<-
->WHO HEARS PRAYERS<-
<note>The original Greek inscription: ΟΚΤΑΤΙΑ ΜΑΡΚΟΥ ΘΥΓΑΤΗΡ ΕΙΣΙΔΙ ΠΕΛΑΓΙΑ ΕΥΑΚΟΩ. "Pelagia" refers to Isis as a protector of the sea; "Euakoos" means "gracious listener."</note>
See the most learned man in the *Memoirs of the Academy of Inscriptions*, volume 47, page 306.

**P. 2. fearing, according to Ibycus, lest somehow]** Manuscripts CL and G read *dedeios* [fearing]. Manuscript T reads *dedeikos* [having shown]. An unpublished commentator on Philostratus’s *Images* (Book 1, chapter 2) notes: "It should be known that every perfect participle ending in *k* with a long penultimate vowel, if the *k* is removed, shortens that penultimate vowel: for example, *hestekos* [standing] becomes *hestaos*, *bebekos* [having gone] becomes *bebaos*, and *dedoikos* [fearing] becomes *dedios*." <note>This is a technical grammatical explanation for the specific form of the Greek word for 'fearing' used in the text.</note> — The manuscripts read *me ti* [lest somehow], which I have written instead of the *me toi* [lest indeed] found in the single Fabrician edition; for *me ti* is found in the others, such as in Plato and Synesius to be cited shortly. Damascius, using this passage of Ibycus in Suidas under the entry *me toi* [lest indeed], did indeed write *me toi*, but he must be corrected based on the words *amplakema* [offense] and *Ibykeion* [of Ibycus], where *me ti* [lest somehow] is more correct. — „Plato in the *Phaedrus* [page 242 C.]: *‘I was troubled, according to Ibycus, lest somehow [or rather 'me ti', which is the published reading] by offending the gods, I might receive honor from men in exchange.’* Plutarch also mentions this in *Table Talk*, Book 9 [page 748 C.], as does Suidas under the entry ‘lest indeed’ [*me toi*].“ — FABRICIUS. — „The sense seems somewhat obscure, but should be understood as follows: Ibycus feared lest, while seeking the glory of the common people, he might offend the Gods; Marinus, however, fears lest he offend not the Gods, but a wise man, while he strives for his own praise among men. The lyric poet Ibycus is mentioned in the proverb, *The Cranes of Ibycus* <note>A famous legend where the poet Ibycus, being murdered, called upon a flock of cranes to witness the crime; the cranes later circled over his killers in a theater, leading to their confession.</note> [found in Suidas under *Ibycus*. See also G. Canter, *New Readings* I. 12 and Jacobs on Antipater of Sidon, Epigram 78].“ — LATIN INTERPRETATION. — Whoever reads Ruhnken on Timaeus, page 90, will lack nothing concerning this most famous sentiment of Ibycus. Synesius, among other authors indicated by that most learned man, in Letter 105, page 247 A, says: *‘For this fear is not new, but very ancient: lest somehow by offending God, I find honor from men.’* Two manuscripts read *para theon* [beside God], from which I would restore *para theo* [with God]. Damascius indeed, in the entry *Ibykeion* in Suidas, has *peri theous* [concerning the gods], which Kuster approves, but the reading *para theois* [with the gods] is certainly to be preferred, as it is found in the entries for *offense* and *lest indeed*, as well as in Plato and our author [Marinus]. The commentator on Synesius says: *‘Ibycus the lyric poet says this.’*

**P. 2. (for I fear lest it not even be holy — to speak —)]** Manuscript T reads *phengesthai* [a misspelling of "to speak"]. — I felt I helped this intricate passage with a parenthesis. Agathias in the preface to the *History*, page 4 C: *fearing in some way*...

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations regarding Marinus's biography of Proclus, focusing on an inscription to Isis and a literary reference to the poet Ibycus. The text debates the precise wording and grammatical forms used by Marinus when quoting ancient sources about the fear of offending the gods while seeking human praise.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Ibycus, Mytilene inscription, Plato's Phaedrus, Synesius, textual criticism, Greek grammar, piety, honor, Cranes of Ibycus</keywords>

->66               NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

...it occurred to me, lest it might not be holy to leave such great works unrecorded. <note>original: "ἐπῆλθε, μή τι ἄρα οὐχ ὅσιον εἴη ἔργα οὕτω μέγιστα — καταλιπεῖν ἄμνηστα." This is an opening sentiment from the author, Marinus, expressing a religious or moral duty to document the life of his teacher, Proclus.</note>

**P. 3. due to a certain idleness of the mind]** <note>original: "δι’ ἀργίαν δέ τινα τῆς διανοίας"</note> The manuscripts C and T omit the word "certain." Below in chapter 24, he calls sleep "a certain idleness of the soul."

**P. 3. countless many]** <note>original: "μυρίων ὅσων"</note> Below in chapter 26, page 61, he uses the phrase "with countless many commentaries." Compare the scholar Schaefer’s notes on Dionysius, page 184.

**P. 3. recorded]** <note>original: "ἱστορημένων"</note> This is the spelling found in the Manuscripts and all Editions except for Fabricius’s, which uses "being recorded" <note>original: "ἱστορουμένων"</note>. Below in chapters 17 and 32, I have restored the same tense that Fabricius had wrongly altered.

**P. 3. I shall begin]** <note>original: "Ἄρξομαι"</note> LG <margin>The arrangement of the whole discourse is excellent.</margin> C [also notes]: The arrangement of the whole discourse is excellent. <note>These marginal notes from the manuscript sources (LG and C) praise the structure of the biography.</note>

**P. 3. foundation]** <note>original: "κρηπῖδα"</note> The Paris Manuscript spells this with a different accent, as do Xylander and the Leiden edition; however, Fabricius, Portus, and the CT manuscripts use the form with a circumflex accent, which seems more correct. A fragment of a dictionary edited by Hermann, likely authored by Nicephorus Gregoras (see Bast in Schoell’s *Repertory*, p. 414), states on p. 325: "The Attic writers shorten the final syllable of *karis* [shrimp], but others, lengthening the penultimate syllable, also lengthen the final; likewise *psēphis* [pebble], *krēpis* [foundation], and *teuthis* [squid]." Herodian, in his work *On Prosody* <note>rules for pronunciation and vowel length</note> also edited by Hermann, p. 447, says: "The letter *i* is lengthened especially in two-syllable words if the syllable before the end was long by nature or position: *kēkis* [gushing], *kēlis* [stain], etc." Compare Schaefer on Longus, p. 347, and Wernsdorf on Himerius, p. 184.

**P. 3. I shall lay a foundation]** <note>original: "κρηπῖδα — θήσομαι"</note> Euripides, in *The Madness of Heracles*, line 1261:
> Whenever the foundation of a race is not laid
> Correctly.
Maximus of Tyre, in Discourse 3.2: "just as a foundation is placed under the preservation of life."

**P. 4. frequently mentioned]** <note>original: "τεθρυλλημένων"</note> The manuscripts C and G spell this with only one 'l'.

**P. 4. the happiness of the wise]** <note>original: "τὴν τῶν σοφῶν εὐδαιμονίαν"</note> <gloss>He understands the "goods of the soul."</gloss> — LATIN INTERPRETER.

**P. 4. for good living]** <note>original: "πρὸς εὐζωΐαν"</note> <gloss>In Greek he says "euzōïan," which I refer to the physical happiness of the body, consisting of health, beauty, and strength. For "life" (*zōē*), as Ammonius testifies [in his work *On Synonyms*], is a term used even for plants and brute animals. Thus, in a human being, this "life" is common to both the soul and the body.</gloss> — LATIN INTERPRETER.

**P. 4. it had been well prepared]** <note>original: "εὐπαρεσκεύαστο"</note> Manuscript T reads "it had been prepared." G reads "well prepared," which is preferable. — Shortly after, where the text says "if to anyone" <note>original: "εἴπερ τῳ"</note>, the various editions print "if to any" <note>original: "εἴπερ τῷ"</note>, with the exception of Xylander’s edition.

**P. 4. First then indeed]** <note>original: "Πρῶτον δὴ οὖν"</note> Here, following the Leiden edition, I have placed the division for the third chapter; Fabricius had placed this division further down at the word "blood" <note>original: "αἵματος"</note>.

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations on the Greek text of Marinus’ "Life of Proclus," focusing on manuscript variations, correct grammatical accents, and the philosophical definitions of "happiness" and "living." The editor compares various historical editions and classical citations to justify his textual choices.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, philology, Greek grammar, prosody, happiness (eudaimonia), textual criticism, Fabricius, Euripides</keywords>

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 67

"some from birth," <note>original: "τινες ἐκ γενετῆς"</note> wrongly; for the words "certain ones" <note>original: "αἵ τινες"</note> belong together with "more natural." <note>original: "φυσικώτεραι"</note>

**P. 4. divided according to types**] <note>original: "κατὰ γένη διελόμενοι"</note> "Concerning these divisions of the virtues, it was spoken of in the introduction [§. 3.]" **FABRICIUS**. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, an 18th-century scholar who edited these texts.</note> Compare Olearius on Philostratus p. 243, and Wyttenbach on Plato’s *Phaedo* p. 216, 172.

**P. 4. ethical [virtues]**] <note>original: "ἠθικὰς"</note> Plutarch, in *On Education* chapter 4: "Character <note>original: "ἦθος" (ethos)</note> is long-lasting, and if one were to call the ethical virtues 'habitual,' <note>original: "ἐθικὰς" (ethikas)</note> they would not seem to be making a mistake." Xylander conjectured "Character is habit that is long-lasting," which pleased both Heusinger and Wolf. For my part, I confess that this conjecture is very likely, and I timidly propose my own: "Character is something long-lasting."

**P. 4. purificatory [virtues]**] <note>original: "καθαρτικάς"; in Neoplatonism, these are virtues that cleanse the soul from bodily passions.</note> "Concerning these, see Porphyry in Stobaeus [p. 47. Schow.] in the discourse on virtue." **LATIN TRANSLATOR.**

**P. 4. those so-called theurgic [virtues]**] <note>original: "οὕτω δὴ καλουμένας θεουργικὰς"; <term>Theurgy</term> refers to "god-working" or rituals intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> Concerning this redundant use of the word "so" <note>original: "οὕτω"</note> compare what those eminent men have observed, Bast in *Critical Letter* p. 37 and Schaefer in the *Appendix* to the same letter p. 7, and in *Meletemata* I. p. 14, 81.

**P. 5. they were kindred**] <note>original: "συγγενεῖς ἦσαν"</note> Thus all the Manuscripts and Editions before Fabricius, who, I know not why or from where, wrote "they were together." <note>original: "συνῆσαν"</note> Below in chapter 3: "he was kindred <note>original: "συγγενῶς"</note> to better things." In chapter 7: "showing he possessed much of the kindred <note>original: "συγγενὲς"</note> nature of the god-beloved."

**P. 5. oyster-like**] <note>original: "ὀστρεώδει"</note> "This is a circumlocution for the human body. A similar expression is found in Plato in the *Phaedrus* [p. 250 G]: 'pure and unmarked by this, which we now carry about and call a body, bound to it in the manner of an **oyster**.'" **LATIN TRANSLATOR.** — "The body is called an 'oyster' by Plato in the *Phaedrus*, by Arrian in the *Discourses of Epictetus* [IV. 7. 5.], etc." **FABRICIUS.** Themistius in *Oration* XXI p. 261 B: "for all such cowardice belongs to a small and unfree soul that excessively honors this **oyster**." The Valesian manuscript reads *ostrion*, which is more Attic, if you write it as *ostreion* with a diphthong. See the *Notes on Moeris* p. 285 and Schweighäuser on Athenaeus III. 44. Proclus, in his commentary on the *Cratylus* (ch. 60), called it the "oyster-like body." Compare Abresch on Aeschylus vol. 1. p. 526. Admetus in the *Epigram*:
->Earth, take the shell of Admetus; he himself has gone to God.<-
where see Jacobs.

**P. 5. it was indeed prudence**] <note>original: "ἦν δὴ φρόνησιν"</note> <margin>Coislinianus manuscript in the margin: "Fine sensitivity is analogous to prudence."</margin>

**P. 5. they have been gifted with the most precious senses**] <note>original: "τῶν τιμιωτάτων αἰσθήσεων — δεδώρηνται"</note> The printed editions read "it has been gifted," <note>original: "δεδώρηται" (singular)</note> which I changed [to the plural] based on manuscripts C, G, and T. — "Plato in the *Timaeus* E 2"

->68 NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

"[chapters 37, 38], and there see Proclus and Chalcidius [chapter 265]."
FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, the original editor of this text.</note> I will describe the passage from Plato: "Sight, in my opinion, has become the cause of the greatest benefit to us; and concerning voice and hearing, the same argument holds: they have been granted by the gods for these same purposes." <note>original Greek: ὄψις δὴ κατὰ τὸν ἐμὸν λόγον... from Plato’s *Timaeus* 47a.</note> Chalcidius: "There are, therefore, two principal senses, sight and hearing, both aiding philosophy."

   Page 5. *And the second strength*] CG <margin>strength is analogous to courage</margin>. From the Laurentian manuscript, *analogous* is noted.

   Page 5. *by storms and heat*] I have written this instead of *toils* <note>The Greek words for "heat" (kaumaton) and "toils" (kamaton) are nearly identical in spelling.</note>, following manuscripts LG and the Dorville manuscript on Charito page 500, with the support of Schaefer in *On Ellipses* page 86. Manuscripts CTV read *toils*, as do the printed editions. Aristides in *Sacred Tales* II, page 292: "For since I was brought from Italy, having suffered all kinds of things in my body from continuous toils and storms, such as I experienced departing through Thrace and Macedonia"; perhaps it should be *heat*. Themistius in *Oration II*, page 39 C: "A royal body is firmer than adamant through labors; thus it easily (Valesian manuscript: *most easily*) and readily endures (Codex: *presses*) want, is distressed by thirst, is spurred on by sleeping outdoors, is exposed to the storm, and yields to the heat." Stobaeus page 123: "Those who are well-disposed in body endure both heat and cold." Theano page 742: "to bear both hunger and thirst, and further, both cold and heat."

   Page 6. *neglected*] Manuscripts LG read *sun-baked* <note>original: ἡλημένης (hēlēmenēs), meaning "exposed to the sun," suggesting a different physical state than "neglected."</note>.

   Page 6. *both by night and by day*] Fabricius wrote *during the day* <note>original: μεθ’ ἡμέρας (meth' hēmeras)</note> following a correction by Desiderius Heraldus in *Iamblichus* page 150, who thought that "by night" and "by day" should have the same grammatical ending; but he was rightly rebuked by Gataker in his observations on Marcus Aurelius III, section 4, and Heraldus himself later deservedly regretted this emendation in his notes on Martial IV. 28. Fabricius likewise corrupted other passages of our author where "during the day" <note>original: μεθ’ ἡμέραν (meth' hēmeran)</note> was read, with one exception in chapter 18 which escaped his keen eye. Everywhere I have restored the ancient and pure reading. "During the day" <note>meth' hēmeran</note> signifies during the daylight hours. Suidas: *during the day*, instead of *in the day*. Charito IV. 6: "During the day, therefore, he kept watch over the woman"; where Dorville should be read. Aristaenetus, as well corrected by Pauw, I. 24: "neither by night, nor by day," where Abreschius page 150 collected similar examples. Aeschines *Against Ctesiphon* page 468: "those whom he perjures himself to by day, he says speak to him by night." Plutarch’s *Life of Theseus* 6; Lucian’s *The False Prophet* 2; Synesius *On Kingship* page 6 A; Polybius more than once, as is clear from the Index. Alciphron II. 4: "Menander surrounding me both by night and by day"; where Bergler and Wagner should be consulted. Mazaris, unpublished in—

NOTES ON MARINUS. 69

In the *Funeral Dialogue*, Codex 2991 A: "being with and conversing with the emperor by night and by day." In the same work: "For the memory of these things <note>referring to traumatic or haunting memories</note> wears down the body, and like a poisonous worm, it bites the soul by night and by day." Again: "caught once more by love for her, or rather, completely under her spell, I wallowed in her filthy body by night and by day." Plato, *Phaedrus* page 251 E: "he is neither able to sleep by night, nor to remain still by day, wherever he may be." Philip’s *Letter to Olympias*: "and by night and by day, look to what is profitable for the revenues of the empire." Codex 3002: "by night." Theano’s *Letter* page 742: "but you are dispirited both by night and by day." Lucian, *The Liar of Words* 31: "at night and in the darkness, but as for the daytime, away with it!" Himerius, *Oration* XXIII, §. 3: "worrying by night and by day." Wernsdorf notes: "perhaps it should be 'day' in the accusative case." Indeed, certainly so. In the work of Astrampsychus <note>an ancient author of a book on dream interpretation</note> page 6, the translator renders the word *hēmeras* as "during the day," as if it were put for the temporal phrase "by day," when in fact it is the genitive form of *hēmeros* <term>hēmeros: meaning calm, gentle, or tame</term>:

->Swimming in the sea indicates fierce grief;<-
->Swimming in the sea is a good dream by day.<-
" *Swimming in the sea indicates cruel pains.* "
" *Swimming in the sea during the day is a good dream.* "

I correct this to: "Swimming in a *fierce* sea indicates grief. — Swimming in a *calm* sea is a good dream." <note>The translator is correcting a previous scholar who mistook the word for "calm" (hēmeros) for the word for "day" (hēmera)</note>. *Swimming through a stormy sea indicates pains; swimming through a calm sea is a good dream.* I am surprised that D'Orville, who used the former verse in his notes on Chariton page 418, did not notice the error.

Page 6. **he was draining**] Heraldus illustrates this in the same place. Euripides, *Cyclops* 10: "and now I drain a labor greater than those." See Höpfner there, and at line 110. Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations* I. end: " *when he had drained all his labors* "; on which see Davis.

Page 6. **associating with companions**] The printed editions have "having associated with," which I changed based on the manuscripts C, L, G, and T. The word "others" is read in L, G, and T; concerning this substitution <note>the confusion between 'companions' (hetairois) and 'others' (heterois)</note> compare the note on Philostratus' *Heroicus* page 345, Wolf on Libanius’ *Letter* 1005, and Hermann on Orpheus 568. — And in many other words, the confusion of *ai* and *e* <note>the Greek letters alpha-iota and epsilon, which sounded similar</note> has produced errors in the writing. Himerius, *Oration* III. page 434: "the earth is crowned and rejoices not in anemones and violets and other flowers, but in the gifts of Dionysus and the grey-green crown of the form <note>original: "ideas"</note>": perhaps it should be "of Ida" <note>original: "Idaias"</note>, namely, belonging to the goddess Cybele. In Eunapius page 238, "little child" and "plain" are confused, as they are in these unpublished verses in Codex 3019, which bear the name of Philostratus, though he is an unknown man.

<meta>This page continues a series of scholarly annotations, transitioning from previous philosophical or biographical commentary into poetic evidence attributed to Philostratus.</meta>

70          NOTES ON MARINUS. <note>original: "NOTAE IN MARINVM." Marinus of Neapolis was a 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher and the successor to Proclus.</note>

->Verses of Philostratus<- <note>original: "Στίχοι τοῦ Φιλοστράτου." These lines are likely attributed to Philostratus the Athenian, a writer of the Roman imperial period known for his "Love Letters."</note>

Behold the bows, and the fire, and the arrows,
And the winged child of the tyrant $^1$). <note>The "child of the tyrant" is Eros (Cupid), often depicted in Greek poetry as a cruel or absolute ruler over the human heart.</note>
Where can I be hidden? Where can I turn? Where can I find refuge?
If I escape the flight of the arrows,
It is impossible for me to flee the fire as well.
And if I outrun even the fire itself $^2$),
The swift stroke of the wing finds me.
Therefore, having learned these three inescapable fates,
Eros, I entreat you: prevail with your wing alone $^3$),
And as you go before me and seize me,
Bend my neck and tread upon your humble slave.
I do not resist; do not burn me in vain.
I do not act wickedly; do not empty your quiver.
I do not escape; do not spread your wings.
With a submissive spirit... <note>The Greek text breaks off here with "Εὐγνω," likely part of the word "Εὐγνωμόνως" meaning submissively or gratefully.</note>

<summary>This page contains a poetic fragment attributed to Philostratus, illustrating the inescapable power of Eros (Love) through the metaphors of bows, fire, and wings. It serves as a literary illustration within a scholarly commentary on the philosopher Marinus.</summary>

<keywords>Philostratus, Eros, Marinus, Greek poetry, Cupid, Neoplatonism, Hellenistic literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philological discussion regarding the rare combination of physical beauty and moral or physical strength in classical heroes, citing Proclus and Dio Chrysostom.</meta>

...According to Proclus, these qualities are almost separable: beauty and strength. Dio Chrysostom, in Oration 29, page 544, states that there were only two men who were at once brave and exceptionally beautiful: <note>original: "duos tantum fuisse ait viros fortes simul et egregie pulchros"</note>

"One might say that among the exceptionally beautiful, only Theseus and Achilles were brave, and even in these two, <term>self-restraint</term> <note>original: "σωφροσύνη" (sophrosyne) — a Greek concept of excellence of character and soundness of mind, often translated as temperance or moderation.</note> was not entirely present. For if it were, the former would not have seized Helen by force, and the latter would not have engaged in such discord."

Manuscript 2958 provides a fuller and better reading: <note>original: "plenius et melius Codex 2958"</note> "and the other [Achilles] engaged in discord at Troy for the reasons he engaged in discord."

A little earlier he had said: "One might find, by looking back from the beginning at the most beautiful men of former times, that the majority of them displayed no work of bravery or of virtue."

The Manuscript correctly reads "most indeed" <note>original: "τοὺς δὴ πολλοὺς"</note> and "nothing of bravery—work..." <note>The text ends mid-word with "ἔργ-", likely the beginning of "ἔργον" (work/deed).</note>

<summary>This page discusses a classical debate over whether physical beauty and heroic bravery are usually found together, noting that even heroes like Achilles and Theseus lacked moral restraint. The author compares different manuscript versions of Dio Chrysostom's orations to establish the most accurate Greek text.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Dio Chrysostom, Achilles, Theseus, beauty, bravery, self-restraint, sophrosyne, philology, Codex 2958, Greek heroes</keywords>

Read from the Manuscript <note>The author uses "Codex" to refer to a handwritten manuscript</note> "was extended," <note>original: "ἐπετάθη"</note> as Abresch excellently conjectured in *Dilucidations on Thucydides*, page 788, dealing with this exchange of certain tenses in the verbs *to stretch* and *to arrange*. <note>original: "τείνειν" (teinein - to stretch) and "τάττειν" (tattein - to arrange)</note> Achilles Tatius, Book IV, 19, in his description of the Crocodile: "many teeth and <note>original: "τεταγμένοι"</note> arranged at great length." &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P. 7. of the writers**] The printed editions absurdly have "of the writings." <note>original: "γραφῶν" (graphon)</note> Manuscripts C and T have "of those writing," <note>original: "γραφόντων" (graphonton)</note> which I had to adopt. See especially Bast’s *Palaeographical Commentary*, pages 811 and 812. Just as Marinus used "writer" <note>original: "γραφὼν" (graphon)</note> for "author," <note>original: "γραφεύς" (grapheus)</note> so Lydus in *On Magistracies*, page 63, uses "the one writing" for "the author": "as is said by those writing in another language."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P. 7. And they want health as the fourth**] <margin>health is analogous to justice</margin> <note>In the Platonic tradition, bodily health was often seen as a physical parallel to the soul's virtue of justice</note> Manuscript L has "analogous" in the masculine. — Instead of "and," <note>original: "δὲ"</note> Manuscripts L and G have "indeed." <note>original: "δή"</note> Manuscript V has a spelling variant for "health." Manuscript C reads: "the fourth is health which they want," and so does T, but omitting the article "the."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P. 7. from the disordered**] Manuscripts L and G combine this into a single word. <note>original: "ἐξετάκτων"</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P. 7. And this one also thus**] I wrote "this one" <note>original: "αὕτη" (haute - feminine "this")</note> instead of "self/same." <note>original: "αὐτή" (aute)</note> Cassius Dio, Book XXXV, 25: "If neither this dishonor nor this flight grieves you": I would prefer "this" <note>original: "αὐτή"</note> both times. Achilles Tatius, Book VI, 16: "I am not called a Spartan woman. This is a pirate's insult; I have been robbed..."

### NOTES ON MARINUS, PAGE 73

"and the name" <note>original: "καὶ τοὔνομα"</note>. The word "this" <note>original: "αὕτη"</note> is a better reading found in the Venetian Codex 409 <note>A 14th-century manuscript preserved in the Marciana Library in Venice, often used by scholars to correct later copies.</note>. — Next, the manuscript T reads "from their very swaddling clothes" <note>original: "ἐκ σπαργάνων αὐτῶν"</note>, instead of "for him" <note>original: "αὐτῷ"</note>.

**Page 8.** "he might be led" <note>original: "ὑπαχθείη"</note>] The manuscripts L, G, C, and T read "he should be led" <note>original: "ὑπαχθῇ"</note>. <note>The editor is noting a subtle shift from the optative to the subjunctive mood in the Greek text.</note>

**Page 8.** "twice only or even thrice" <note>original: "δὶς μόνον ἢ καὶ τρὶς"</note>] The manuscript V reads "three" <note>original: "τρεῖς"</note>. — Compare Heliodorus <note>Author of the "Aethiopica," an ancient Greek novel.</note>, Book 2, chapter 19: "doing this once, and again, and a third time he was seen to be telling the truth" <note>original: "τοῦτο ποιῶν ἅπαξ μὲν, καὶ αὖθις, καὶ τρίτον ἐπαληθεύσας ὤφθη"</note>. The Venetian Codex 409 reads: "and doing this once, twice, and a third time..." <note>original: "καὶ τοῦτο π. ἅ. μ. καὶ δὶς, καὶ τρίτον"</note>. See also Achilles Tatius <note>An ancient Greek novelist known for "Leucippe and Clitophon."</note>, Book 3, chapter 17: "he struck the coffin perhaps twice and thrice" <note>original: "δίς που καὶ τρὶς ἐπάταξε τὴν σορόν"</note>; where the Venetian Codex 409 wrongly reads "three" <note>original: "τρεῖς"</note>.

**Page 8.** "for seventy-five full years" <note>original: "ἔτεσιν ὅλοις πέντε καὶ ἑβδομήκοντα"</note>] The manuscript T is without the word "and" <note>original: "καὶ"</note>. — "See below in chapter 26," notes Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), the classical scholar who edited this text.</note>. — Compare Chapter 12: "not even having reached a full twentieth year" <note>original: "οὐδὲ ὅλον εἰκοστὸν ἔτος ἔχοντα"</note>. Add to this chapter 13 and chapter 26.

**Page 8.** "by which he himself also" <note>original: "ᾧ καὶ αὐτὸς"</note>] The manuscripts C and T read "as" <note>original: "ὡς"</note>. — I remember a certain passage in the letters of Libanius... <note>Libanius was a prominent 4th-century Greek teacher of rhetoric; the author is using Libanius’s style to justify a specific reading in the text.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus's biography of the philosopher Proclus. The author is cross-referencing Marinus’s descriptions of Proclus's virtues with classical sources, primarily Plato's "Republic" and "Laws," to show how Proclus was depicted as the ideal Platonic philosopher.</meta>

74 NOTES ON MARINUS.

"...mindful, and eager to learn." <note>original Latin: "memores, et philomathes"</note> — LATIN INTERPRETATION. — "Plato in the sixth book of the *Republic* [ch. 3, Ast edition] and in the *Epinomis*." FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius was a famous 18th-century scholar who edited these texts.</note> Here are Plato's words: 
"Can you then in any way find fault with a pursuit <note>referring to philosophy</note> which a man could never sufficiently practice unless he were by nature of good memory, quick to learn, noble, gracious, and a friend and kinsman of truth, justice, courage, and temperance?"

**Page 9. he willingly accepted a lie, but utterly hated it]** <note>The author is discussing whether Proclus "accepted" or "rejected" falsehoods based on different manuscript copies.</note> Manuscripts C and T read "gave away" <note>original Greek: "προήκατο"</note> instead of "accepted." Regarding the phrase "willingly," <note>original Greek: "ἑκὼν εἶναι"</note> see the notes on Gregory of Corinth, page 74. — Plato, *Republic* VI, ch. 5: "Will such a person have any part in loving falsehood, or will he, on the contrary, hate it?"

**Page 9. he loved the truth]** The *Coislin Lexicon* 393 defines truth thus: "Truth: an assertion stating that which is as it is, and that which is not as it is not."

**Page 9. It was necessary, then, for one who intends to grasp the truth of that which is]** "Another reading: 'the truth that truly is.'" FABRICIUS. I do not know where he obtained this; elsewhere he indicates variant readings whose source is unknown to me. Plato, *Republic* VI, 5: "...nor would he cease from his passion until he grasped the nature of each thing as it is, with that part of the soul which is fit to grasp such a thing (and it is fit by being akin to it); having approached and joined with that which truly exists, and having begotten intellect and truth, he would know and truly live." — Our author <note>Marinus</note> in chapter 6 uses the same form of speech: "For it was necessary, I believe, for the one who intends..." and so on.

**Page 9. For Truth is the leader of all]** "Plutarch, *On Isis and Osiris* [at the beginning]: 'as there is nothing greater for a man to receive, nor anything more venerable for a god to bestow, than truth.'" FABRICIUS. These are the words of Plato in the *Laws*, page 605 F, which were also used by Proclus in *Platonic Theology*, ch. 21, page 55, and Eunapius, page 77: "If indeed the saying of Plato is fixed, that truth is the leader of all good things for the gods, and of all for men." See the Note there.

**Page 9. toward these indeed]** <note>The commentator is noting a small word-order change in the manuscripts.</note> Manuscripts L, G, C, and T read "indeed toward these." — Shortly after, "he had" <note>original Greek: "εἶχε"</note> instead of "he was having" <note>original Greek: "εἶχεν"</note> from manuscripts C and T. — "and of temperance" in manuscript V, instead of "but of temperance."

**Page 9. to present]** <note>original Greek: "παραστῆσαι"</note> Chapter 30: "the choice also presented it sufficiently." Chapter 34: "having presented all things differing in reason."

**Page 10. both desire and inclination]** <note>original Greek: "ἔφεσίς τε καὶ ῥοπή"</note> Chapter 12: "much desire for the life in philosophy." Synesius, *On Kingship*, page 6 B: "For I do not despair that his age will reach every improvement, if only someone would spur it <note>original Greek: "μυωπίσειεν"</note> toward a zeal for virtue (a Scholium <note>marginal note</note> in Manuscript 1038 says: 'would move it'). For youth is a strong inclination toward either side." Eunapius, page 30: "For he seemed well-disposed by nature toward an inclination for virtue."

**Page 10. of the soul itself by itself]** Fabricius reads "itself" <note>original Greek: "αὐτῆς"</note> as a possessive, which is found in manuscripts L, G, C, and T. Other editions and manuscript V read "itself" <note>original Greek: "αὐτήν"</note> as an object.

The author provides detailed textual criticism, noting variations between different Greek manuscripts (L, G, C, T, and V).</summary>

I added "to find" <note>εὑρεῖν</note> from Manuscript 2958.

I read it this way following Casaubon and Reiske <note>Isaac Casaubon and Johann Jacob Reiske, two famous scholars of classical literature</note>, not because they are certain and proven

76 NOTES ON MARINUS.

...thius to Procopius 1) in Letter 49: "Your golden offspring <note>Referring to his eloquent words or writings</note> were in everyone’s mouths." Synesius, Letter 138, page 275 D, to...

***

1) Photius <note>A 9th-century Byzantine scholar and Patriarch</note> in Volume 160, page 337, line 13, praises the speeches of this Procopius of Gaza <note>A famous Christian rhetorician and "Sophist" of the 5th/6th century</note> as "a thing worthy of zeal and imitation." Of these orations, most of which now seem to be lost, Villoison <note>A prominent French classical scholar</note> published one in *Anecdota*, Volume II, page 28—namely, a Panegyric to the Emperor Anastasius. I believed it would be no less worthwhile if I transcribed another oration of Procopius here, found in Codex 1038; though it is of a very light subject, it is not entirely to be looked down upon.

# <term>Ethopoeia</term> of Procopius the Sophist of Gaza.
<note>An *Ethopoeia* is a "character piece," a standard rhetorical exercise where the writer composes a speech in the voice of a specific person or type of person.</note>

### What words a shepherd might say when spring shines forth after a very severe winter.

O newer light, which I long desired to see but could not, for which only prayer remained! Now the sun is kind, the air has smoothed its brow <note>original: ὀφρῦς; the Greek suggests the sky has stopped "frowning"</note>, the breeze is moderate, the sea is tame, and the earth has mimicked the painter's art with its flowers. Now the Muses dance with the birds, the Graces with the meadows, Pan with the flocks, and the Loves <note>original: Ἔρωτες (Erotes)</note> with our thoughts. All things have turned around and become kind; a truce is perhaps made, and war is nowhere. Have courage now, goats and livestock, and greet the long-awaited grass! 

For previously, Zeus <note>Though a Christian, Procopius uses the traditional language of Greek mythology to describe the weather</note> poured himself out in abundance upon the earth, and this bright sun was hidden, besieged by the clouds. The air and the trees were shaken together by the winds, and the sea rose high and threatened war against the sailors <margin>variant: war-like</margin>, roaring like a lion, while the mountain torrents mimicked it, sweeping away whatever they met. Somehow, the traveler did not dare the road, nor the sailor his boat, nor the hunter his prey. There was no pasture, nor did the herds frequent it. Even the trees stood as if in mourning, shorn of their foliage. 

And I, and these poor little sheep who did no wrong, sat like prisoners in a cave as if in a dungeon. They ate dry leaves set aside from the summer, and their bleating was pitiful; I lived on cheese that showed nothing fresh <note>original: νεαρὸν; the editor notes the manuscript was corrected from a different form of the word</note>. The cold of the season was heavier <note>The manuscript mispelled the Greek word for "heavier"</note> than the "prison" itself; for I was not ready to move my feet, nor to stretch out my hands for what was needed, nor to blow into the reeds, nor to tune the flutes. One had to sleep even through the day, huddled among the livestock and finding comfort in their wool. No music was heard; even the talkative swallow slept, I think. Already, something of a spider’s web lurks in my pipe. Everything seemed to be silent, except that Zeus, flashing lightning, brought a terrible thundering, as if he were moving furniture or remodeling the heavens. 

I often reminded him in prayer: "Why, O Zeus, have we caused such grief? <note>Possible alternative reading: "Why have you grieved me so much?"</note> Stop clashing everything together, lest the city be shattered and a new one must be built. Has a multitude of Giants risen up against you in war again?" What more is there to say? For it did no good until he was persuaded of his own will. Perhaps he grew weary of moving everything and now sleeps after his long labor; thus he makes a truce, speaks, and seems to grow young again, having shed his old age like a skin <note>variant: having shed (marginal reading)</note>. Skip now, goats, and with your leaping...

<summary>This page contains a scholarly introduction and the full text of a Greek "Ethopoeia" (rhetorical character study) by Procopius of Gaza, depicting a shepherd's joy at the arrival of spring after a harsh winter. The editor highlights this as a rare example of the Sophist's lost work found in a specific manuscript (Codex 1038).</summary>

<keywords>Procopius of Gaza, Ethopoeia, rhetoric, spring, Zeus, pastoral life, Greek literature, Codex 1038, Photius, Villoison</keywords>

"What other fire-stick <note>original Greek: *pyreion*, a tool for creating fire by friction, used here as a metaphor for intellectual or spiritual sparking</note> [this term is explained by Bastius in his *Critical Letter*, page 141] having been rubbed against your sacred soul, might I use to give birth to a light-like offspring of the mind?" The same author <note>Synesius of Cyrene</note> in *On Dreams*, page 156 C: "Whoever, therefore, loves the reputation among men that follows hereafter, and is conscious of being able to give birth to immortal works upon writing tablets..."

...rise toward heaven; be filled with the meadow-grass, unless someone has been transformed into a plant through love and preserves his beauty even as he changed into that nature (a). Narcissus was beautiful, but he suffered a passion (b) unworthy of his beauty <note>Narcissus famously fell in love with his own reflection and was turned into a flower</note>. Hyacinthus was beautiful, but be more kind than Zephyrus <note>the West Wind, who killed Hyacinthus in a fit of jealous rage</note> and spare the beautiful youth. And if it should also be necessary to drink...

->78          NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

<note>Marinus of Neapolis was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a famous biography of his teacher, Proclus.</note> "Let him confidently pursue the writing that we have treated lawlessly; let him entrust himself to time." <note>original Greek: μετίτω τὴν παρανομουμένην ὑφ’ ἡμῶν συγγραφὴν θαῤῥῶν. ἑαυτὸν παρατιθέσθω τῷ χρόνῳ.</note> I would prefer to punctuate this, following Manuscripts 1038 and 831, after "writing": "writing. Confidently let him entrust himself..." <note>The author is suggesting a change in sentence structure to clarify the meaning of the Greek text.</note>

Julian, *Oration II*, page 50 A: "The father of those words." <note>original Greek: ὁ πατὴρ ἐκείνων τῶν λόγων.</note>

Photius, *Library*, code 41: "From which it is possible to conclude that John is the father of the book." <note>original Greek: ἐξ ὧν ἐστὶ συμβαλεῖν Ἰωάννην εἶναι τὸν πατέρα τοῦ βιβλίου. Photius was a 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and a prolific scholar.</note>

Themistius, *Oration XXV*, page 311: "In whatever form they [the words] are born from the beginning, such they remain for all all time." <note>original Greek: οἷοί περ ἂν ἐξ ἀρχῆς τεχθῶσι (οἱ λόγοι), τοιοῦτοι καὶ τὸν ἅπαντα μένουσι χρόνον.</note> The edition by Petau <note>Denis Pétau, 1583–1652, a Jesuit scholar</note> reads "placed" <note>original Greek: τεθῶσι</note> rather than "born." I found the reading used by Hardouin <note>Jean Hardouin, 1646–1729, a French scholar</note> in the Valesian Manuscript.

Themistius elsewhere, in *Oration XXIV*, page 301 C, in a passage which Bernard <note>Edward Bernard, 1638–1696, an English astronomer and philologist</note> most unsuccessfully attempted to amend in his notes to Synesius, page 207: "This is characteristic of our words: [they are] very free..." <note>original Greek: τοῦτο τοῖς ἡμετέροις πρόσεστι λόγοις· ἐλεύθεροι σφόδρ...</note>

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations on the text of Marinus, comparing various manuscript readings and punctuation choices from authors like Julian, Photius, and Themistius.</summary>

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 79

...justly or at the harbors <note>original: δικαίως ἢ ἐπὶ τοῖς λιμέσι</note>. The Valesian manuscript <note>A 17th-century manuscript collection named after the scholar Henri Valois</note> contains "rather" <note>original: μᾶλλον</note> before "justly," and appropriately "you might be mindful" <note>original: φρονοίητε</note> instead of "you might appear" <note>original: φανείητε</note>. Theophylactus Simocatta <note>A 7th-century Byzantine historian</note>, in *Epistle 21*: "for we might hear the Sirens or the Muses lamenting" <note>original: σειρήνων γὰρ ἢ μουσῶν ὀδυρομένων ἀκούσαιμεν</note>. Manuscript 2991 A has "the Sirens rather than the Muses" <note>original: σειρ. γαρ μᾶλλον η μ.</note>. And "we shall hear" <note>original: ἀκούσομεν</note> is better than "we might hear" <note>original: ἀκούσαιμεν</note>.

**P. 12. nothing of the drink of forgetfulness — to have drunk** <note>original: οὐδὲν τοῦ πόματος τῆς λήθης — πεπωκέναι</note>] The manuscripts CLG read: "nothing of that of forgetfulness to have drunk of the drink" <note>original: οὐδὲν τοῦ τῆς λ. ἐδ. πεπ. πόματος</note>. Manuscript T reads "nor of forgetfulness to have drunk of the drink" <note>original: οὐδὲ τῆς λ. ἐδ. πεπ. πομ.</note>. Manuscript V reads "to have fallen" <note>original: πεπτωκέναι</note>. Regarding this confusion of the perfect tenses of the verbs "to fall" <note>piptein</note> and "to drink" <note>pinein</note>, see Wyttenbach, *Library* Vol. II, part 1, page 102; which Heindorf could have remembered regarding the end of Lysias; and see also Brunck on *Lysistrata*, line 395. Compare Casaubon on Athenaeus, Book VI, chapter 10.

**P. 12. of the drink of forgetfulness** <note>original: τοῦ πόματος τῆς λήθης</note>] "It is called 'Birth-producing forgetfulness' <note>original: Γενεσιουργὸς λήθη</note> by Proclus. Cebes <note>An ancient philosopher whose 'Tablet' describes the journey of human life</note> in his *Tablet* [chapter 6] speaks of the cup of deception, wandering, and ignorance: 'everyone drinks,' he says, 'but some more, some less.' Similarly, Socrates in Plato. Proclus in his commentary on Plato's *Alcibiades*, praising the *Odyssey*, Book 10, verse 326." — **FABRICIUS.** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, the original editor of this text</note> Proclus in his commentary on the *Cratylus*, chapter 2: "things very clear to those who are not altogether dizzy with darkness and who have not drunk deeply of the water of forgetfulness" <note>original: ἀρίδηλα τοῖς μὴ παντάπασι σκοτοδινιῶσιν καὶ πολὺ τὸ τῆς λήθης ὕδωρ ἐμφορηθεῖσιν</note>. The same author in his *Hymn to the Muses*, verse 25:
*Nor may the baneful Spirit always hold me*
*Under the streams of forgetfulness while doing destructive things—*
where see the notes by Jacobs. — The unpublished Mazaris <note>A 15th-century Byzantine satirist</note> in his *Dialogue of the Dead*: "These things I remember and shall remember, whether I drink or do not drink the water of forgetfulness."

**P. 12. witty** <note>original: ἀστεῖον</note>] <margin>that Proclus was also full of wit</margin> <note>original: ὅτι καὶ εὐτράπελος ἦν Πρόκλος</note>

**P. 12. not without dignity** <note>original: οὐκ ἔξω τοῦ σεμνοῦ</note>] Compare the note on Philostratus' *Heroicus*, page 524. — Soon after, I preferred "he sent away" <note>original: ἀπέπεμπεν</note> at the end of the chapter as found in manuscripts L and G, rather than the published "he was sending away" <note>original: ἀπέπεμπτε</note>.

**P. 12. Marcella** <note>original: Μάρκελλα</note>] Manuscripts C and T have "Markella" <note>original: Μαρκέλλα</note>. — "Marcella" is more correct. Eunapius, page 10, writes "Marcella."

**P. 13. according to law** <note>original: κατὰ νόμον</note>] Manuscripts C and T have "according to the laws" <note>original: κ. νόμους</note>.

**P. 13. excelling in both lineage and virtue** <note>original: γένει τε καὶ ἀρετῇ ὑπερφέροντες</note>] Fabricius omitted the last word. Below in chapter 17: "those excelling in both lineage and wealth" <note>original: γένει τε καὶ πλούτῳ ὑπερέχόντων</note>. See Porson on Euripides' *Hecuba*, line 272. — They were Lycians from the city of Xanthus, as we shall see shortly.

**P. 13. the female guardian of Byzantium** <note>original: ἡ τοῦ Βυζαντίου πολιοῦχος</note>] Editions before Fabricius used the masculine "the" [ho] instead of "the" [hē] which Fabricius wrote, following the agreement of the manuscripts, except for V, which has the masculine. Manuscripts L and G have "guardian" <note>original: πολιοῦχος</note>. — "He indicates Minerva <note>Athena</note>, who is the tutelary Goddess of Byzantium, just as Apollo is the guardian God <note>original: θεὸς πολιοῦχος</note> of Xanthus, a city of Lycia. In the manuscript commentary of Olympiodorus on Plato's *Alcibiades*, the Gods are either 'hypercosmic' <note>beyond the physical world</note>, to whom our souls are attached and no longer in a body..."

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations on the biography of Proclus, debating specific word choices regarding the "drink of forgetfulness" and identifying Athena as the patron deity of Byzantium.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Lethe, forgetfulness, Fabricius, Byzantium, Athena, Xanthus, Lycia, textual criticism, Greek manuscripts</keywords>

,, or worldly ones <note>original: ἐγκόσμιοι</note>, to whom only bodies are attached. Of these again,
,, some are celestial <note>original: οὐράνιοι</note>, others ethereal or fiery <note>original: αἰθέριοι sive πύριοι</note>, others
,, aerial <note>original: ἀέριοι</note>, others aquatic <note>original: ἐνύδριοι</note>, others terrestrial <note>original: χθόνιοι</note>, others
,, sub-Tartarean <note>original: ὑποταρτάριοι</note>. Among the terrestrial ones <note>original: χθονίους</note>,
,, some are rulers of climates <note>original: κλιματάρχαι</note>, others city-protectors <note>original: πολιοῦχοι</note>, and finally
,, others are household gods <note>original: κατοικίδιοι</note>." FABRICIUS. — Hesychius: "City-protectors <note>original: πολιοῦχοι</note> are those who
save the city, and its rulers." Plutarch in *Life of Theseus*,
chapter 6: "For the Troezenians honor Poseidon exceedingly, and
this god is their city-protector <note>original: πολιοῦχος</note>." Compare Cuper’s *Observations* III.
16. p. 337., Spanheim on Callimachus’ *Hymn to the Bath of Pallas* 53, where he
discusses Minerva herself as a city-protector <note>original: πολιοῦχῳ</note>, and Niclas on *Geoponica* p. 3.

   P. 13. "became the cause of [his] being" <note>original: τοῦ εἶναι γέγονεν αἰτία</note>] Below in chapter 9: "A certain good Fortune
brought him back again to the cause of his birth."
Chapter 17: "becoming as a certain common father and the cause of being for them."

   P. 13. "looked out for / provided" <note>original: προὐνοήσατο</note>] Manuscript V reads "provided beforehand" <note>original: προενοήσατο</note>.

   P. 13. "already among the children — he was finishing" <note>original: εἰς παῖδας ἤδη — ἐτέλει</note>] Manuscripts CT read "still" <note>original: ἔτι</note>, not "already" <note>original: ἤδη</note>. —
Eunapius p. 58: "just now finishing among the youths" <note>original: εἰς ἐφήβους ἄρτι τελῶν</note>: where a note should be consulted.

   P. 14. "to him there also happened a great intimacy" <note>original: αὐτῷ συνέβη καὶ πολλὴ οἰκειότης</note>] Manuscripts CT omit "and/also" <note>original: καί</note>.
Manuscript T reads "happened" <note>original: συνέβη</note>.

   P. 14. "for a while" <note>original: τέως</note>] in the meantime. Herodian the Grammarian in *Philetaerus* p. 434: "The word
*téōs* <note>original: τέως</note> signifies 'until,' and 'before,' and 'as long as,' and 'in the 
meantime.'" Scholia on Plato p. 85: "and in the *Peace*, the same 
[Aristophanes] uses it instead of 'in the meantime.'" Compare 
Hesychius and the notes.

   P. 14. "under the god who leads the Muses" <note>original: ὑπὸ τῷ μουσηγέτῃ θεῷ</note>] Manuscript T reads "under the god who leads the Muses" <note>original: ὑ. τῶν μουσιγέτῃ θ.</note>
Himerius *Oration* III. 9: "Apollo is a friend to you and for this reason, 
I think, also like you. He is the Leader of the Muses <note>original: Μουσηγέτης</note> and he 
establishes the dances of the Muses throughout Boeotian Helicon."

   P. 14. "of both nurture and education" <note>original: τροφῆς τε καὶ παιδείας</note>] Manuscript V reads "education/play" <note>original: παιδίας</note>. — These 
words are frequently joined together. Plato, *Crito* 12: "concerning the 
nurture and education of the child." 5: "For either one should not have
children, or one should persevere in both nurturing and educating them."
Ibid. "to have reared and to have educated." 13: "For we, having 
begotten you, having reared you [I discuss the phrase 'to be born and reared' <note>original: γενέσθαι καὶ τραφῆναι</note>
in notes to Eunapius p. 200.], and having educated you." Add Aristophanes, 
*Clouds* 532. Isocrates, *Archidamus* 42. Julian, *Oration* I. p. 3 C. 4 D. 
having compared Gottleber on *Menexenus* p. 26. and Wyttenbach on 
Julian p. 8 D.

   P. 14. "what must be done" <note>original: ἃ δεῖ πράττειν</note>] Xylander reads "what he sings." Hamburg edition "what
was necessary." The Leiden edition and Fabricius read "what must," as in the Manuscripts. Manuscript T reads "which things indeed" <note>original: ἃ δή</note>, 
which is a common substitution. Philostratus, *Life of Apollonius* II. 11. 
p. 59: "for those indeed [who are] warlike and fond of battle, for whom indeed?" 
Codex 1696: "for whom is it necessary?" <note>original: τίνων δεῖ;</note> excellently: nor did the Latin 
interpreters think otherwise, as is clear from their own version. He disagrees

### NOTES ON MARINUS. 81

Indeed Olearius <note>Gottfried Olearius (1672–1715), a German philologist and theologian</note> says this, but he is mistaken. See Lennep on Phalaris, pages 297 and 357. The similarity of sound between the letters *eta* [η] and *ei* [ει] gave birth to this confusion <note>original Greek: *syngchysin*</note>. Schow only partially corrected the corrupted text of Heraclitus's *Allegories*, page 9. Heraclitus says, "You are witnesses of Homer's pious intention, because in exceptional sufferings, whenever the divinity guards the temple, since he himself is also divine." <note>original Greek: Ὑμεῖς... μάρτυρες ἐστὲ τῆς Ὁμήρου θεοσεβοῦς προαιρέσεως, ὅτι πάθεσιν ἐξαιρέτοις, ἐπὰν νεωκορῇ τὸ δαιμόνιον, ἐπεὶ καὐτός ἐστι θεῖος.</note> Schow correctly restores "is guardian of the temple" [*neokorei*] in place of "might be guardian of the temple" [*neokorē*] after the word "that" [*hoti*], but the word "of verses" [*epōn*], which he conjectures in place of the corrupted word "whenever" [*epan*], can please no one. I read it as: "that the divinity guards the temple of all [the gods] through exceptional sufferings," meaning all the Gods. Above he had said: "For what has been recorded in a holy manner equally and in common for all (the gods), why even speak of it?" The letter *eta* [η] can also be confused with *iota* [ι] due to the similarity of sound. Demetrius Cydonius, in *On Contempt for Death*, chapter 25, paragraph 5, writes: "thus indeed he makes a truce with ignorance through impure pleasures." I read this as: "thus *through* [dia] ignorance."

**P. 14. and to turn away]** Alongside this passage, the manuscripts L and G have in the margin: <margin>may you thus be blessed, Marinus.</margin>

**P. 14. kindred]** This word is not found in manuscripts V and T. See above, page 6.

**P. 15. appearing above]** „An anonymous person wrote these notes in my copy of Fabricius's edition: Thucydides, Book 4, chapters 93 and 96, uses the phrase 'they appeared above the hill.' It is used differently by Lucian in *Zeuxis*, chapter 9, volume 1, page 847, 81.“ — SCHAEFER.

**P. 15. And it was possible to liken him to — Telesphorus]** I wrote the word "but" [*de*] instead of the shortened form [*d'*], following manuscripts C, L, and T. — „Understand this to be Apollo, by whom even the 'year-bringing' or 'year-fulfilling' <term>telesphoros: a Greek epithet meaning bringing an end or fulfillment</term> year is often named in Homer [Iliad, book 19, line 32, etc.].“ — FABRICIUS. — Do not understand it as Apollo, but rather the God Telesphorus. Regarding him, see the *History of the Parisian Academy of Literature and Inscriptions*, volume 21, page 26. Examine the image of the hooded Telesphorus on coins in the work of the most illustrious Choiseul-Gouffier, *Travels in Greece*, volume 2, plate 5, number 12, and plate 11, numbers 9, 15, 17, 25. Compare Desmolets' *Memoirs*, volume 10, page 384. <note>Telesphorus was a minor healing deity, often shown as a small child wearing a cloak and hood, accompanying Asclepius.</note>

**P. 15. was]** Manuscripts L, G, C, and T read "was becoming." Immediately following, manuscript C reads "to this."

**P. 15. having attended a grammarian in Lycia]** Manuscripts L, G, C, and T read "in the Lyceum" <note>the famous school in Athens</note>. — I have explained the construction of "to attend [the school of] someone" [*phoitan tini*] in my notes on Philostratus’s *Heroicus*, page 521. Below, in chapter 9, the word is used on its own: "he, attending." At the end of chapter 8: "to fellow students." In chapter 9, it is constructed with the preposition "at" or "with": "he attends at Olympiodorus's [school]."

**P. 16. Leonas]** „Suidas [in the entry for Leonas] repeats these words about Leonas the Isaurian word-for-word [up to the word 'Egypt']. There is another Leonas, a Syrian boxer, mentioned by Artemidorus, book 4, chapter 82.“ — FABRICIUS. — Manuscript T spells it *Leonas*. Below in chapter 9, Leonas is mentioned again, where manuscript T correctly uses the spelling *Leonas*.

**P. 16. to those who hold the reins]** Suidas omits the word "the." — Theodoret, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 1, chapter 3: "Sylvester held the reins of the Roman Church." Leo the Deacon, *History*, Book 6, chapter 1.

82 NOTES ON MARINUS.

which we owe to Hasius, a most learned man: “John, surnamed Tzimiskes, girded on the reins of the kingdom” <note>original Greek: τὰς τῆς βασιλείας ἡνίας Ἰωάννης... ὑποζώννυται</note>. and in chapter 6, “while Polyeuctus guided the reins of the patriarchate for thirteen years” <note>original Greek: τοῦ δὲ Πολυεύκτου... τὰς τῆς πατριαρχείας ἡνίας ἰθύναντος</note>. — Immediately I wrote *hoì dē* <note>who indeed</note> instead of the published *hoi dē* <note>they indeed</note>, following the CT manuscript.

   P. 16. **He also attended the house of a grammarian**] This is an ellipsis <note>a grammatical term for when a word is omitted because it is understood from the context</note> of “house” or “home,” regarding which see the points indicated by Schaefer on Ellipses, page 105. Themistius, Oration 27, page 340 C: “But if you do not discover the mind of the epic, do you wish me to remind you of another speech, which you learned from teachers while still a youth?” The Valesian Manuscript <note>a specific hand-written copy of the text</note> reads: “did you learn in the teacher’s [house]?” Anonymous Epigram 754:
			but then for him alone
	save lovely virginity until Hades.
The poet addresses Itorius and a girl. Wesseling proposed *mounoi* <note>alone, plural</note>, which Jacobs likes; but I prefer *mounō* in the <term>dual number</term> <note>a Greek grammatical form used specifically for exactly two people or things</note>. Furthermore, since Muratori’s edition has *SŌSATEŌS*, it should be written as *sōsate hōs Aidou*: *hōs* <note>as far as</note> instead of *pros* <note>to/towards</note>, as is very often the case elsewhere. This ellipsis is also found in Epigram 63 by Leonidas of Tarentum, which does not seem entirely healthy <note>meaning the text may be corrupted or contain an error</note> to me:
	Being cheerful, row the path
	creeping toward Hades; for it is not difficult to travel,
	nor crooked, nor full of any wandering.
I propose “nor quite full of wandering” <note>Greek: οὐδ’ ἀνάπλεως πλάνης</note>. The context demands “nor” <note>oude</note>. Furthermore, the sounds *an* and *en* are often swapped in old manuscripts. Achilles Tatius, Book 4, chapter 4: “A Greek man placed the head in the middle of the beast's head.” The Venetian Manuscript 409 has *enethēken* <note>put in</note>, which should have been accepted as it was already known from the English book. How frequently the letters *os* and *a* were swapped with each other was shown by Bast in his *Paleographical Commentary*, page 773. Now you see how easy it is to restore “nor quite full” from the phrase “full of nothing.” Photius, *Library*, volume 163: “filled with Greek wandering.”

   P. 16. **Orion**] “Suidas says the same regarding Orion the grammarian. He is the one whom the author of the Great Etymological Dictionary frequently praises.” — FABRICIUS.

   P. 17. **In the pastimes of the Roman schools**] Manuscripts LGC read *didaskaliōn* <note>instructional/teaching-places</note>. Chapter 9 mentions “the pastimes at Athens.” Chapter 11 “of the rhetorical pastimes.” Add chapter 12.

   P. 17. **being as yet without a taste of philosophical discourses**] Manuscripts LGCT read “being yet.” — For “philosophical discourses,” compare the note on Eunapius, page 131. Lucian, *Nigrinus* 35: “to touch upon philosophical discourses.” See also Casaubon, *Readings on Theocritus*, chapter 1, page 15.

   P. 18. **might be hindered**] Manuscript V reads *parampodizoito*. Marinus had perhaps written *parempodizoito* <note>a more standard Greek form for being obstructed</note>.

NOTES ON MARINUS. 83

P. 18. **to the cause of his birth** <note>original: "ἐπὶ τὴν τῆς γενέσεως αἰτίαν"</note>] „Namely, his fatherland.“
FABRICIUS: Minerva rather. Immediately after, [the text says] **the goddess exhorted him.** <note>original: "προὔτρεψεν ἡ θεός"</note>
P. 19. **he attended upon the Aristotelian studies** <note>original: "φοιτᾷ ἐπὶ μὲν Ἀριστοτελικοῖς"</note>] I have written it thus according to the LGCT <note>likely an abbreviation for specific manuscript codices</note>. The editions prior to Fabricius gave **he attended indeed upon the Aristotelian...**; Fabricius gave **he attended indeed upon the Aristotelian discourses.** „I added the word *discourses* [logois],“ [Fabricius] says, „from Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world</note> [in the entry for *Olympiodorus*, where he describes these events, though not accurately, with the order and meaning disturbed]. Consult Lambecius, Book VI of the *Commentaries on the Imperial Library of Vienna*, p. 181. The same author translates it as: *for the sake of learning Aristotelian dialectic*. But to me, the phrase *Aristotelian discourses* seems to have a broader meaning. Previously, the Latin version read: *he frequented Olympiodorus for Aristotelian matters*. Furthermore, Lambecius is mistaken when he thinks this Olympiodorus, who was the teacher of Proclus, is the same person as the philosopher of the same name whose learned commentaries on certain books of Plato survive in manuscript form in various libraries. For in these commentaries, not only is Proclus himself praised, but also Damascius, who lived long after Proclus.“ FABRICIUS. — The addition of the word *discourses* was entirely unnecessary. Below in chapter 26, it says: **he studied diligently with him both in Platonic matters and the other theologies.**
— Scholia in the Coislin manuscript: **Olympiodorus initiated Proclus into philosophical discourses.**

P. 19. **whose fame is wide** <note>original: "οὗ κλέος εὐρύ"</note>] A Homeric formula. *Odyssey*, Book 1, line 344: **of the man whose fame is wide throughout Hellas and midmost Argos,** and often elsewhere.

P. 19. **To Heron — of the ways** <note>original: "Ἥρωνι — ὁδῶν"</note>] „In Suidas, where he excerpts these things regarding Heron [under the entry *Heron*], it is incorrectly published as *the ways of Alexander* [Kuster edited this as *of Alexandria* from a manuscript]. But Suidas intended to imply that Heron was an Alexandrian, and thus the word *Alexandrian* should be read; this word, having been moved from its place, should be restored after the name Heron. Lambecius treats this somewhat differently in Book VII, page 404. Furthermore, there were several Herons who were mathematicians, about whom I shall speak, God willing, in the *Greek Library* [Book 3, Chapter 24, Section 7].“ FABRICIUS. — Suidas: **concerning education.**
— Scholium in the Laurentian manuscript: **that he was a Cynic, he showed by saying 'even a dog'—not meaning impudence, but boldness of speech.** <note>The word "Cynic" is derived from the Greek word for dog; here the commentator explains it refers to the sect's characteristic "parrhesia" or frankness.</note> — Suidas attributes to Heron an **abridgment of the histories of Heraclides**, perhaps the very one we now possess.

P. 20. **having been led** <note>original: "ἡγμένον"</note>] Found in Suidas under the entry *egmenos*, without the author's name, which escaped Kuster's notice here, though not always; compare volume 3, page 718. See the note on Eunapius, page 157.

P. 20. **to take courage — of godliness** <note>original: "θαῤῥῆσαι — θεοσεβείας"</note>] Coislin Scholium: **This man, as it seems, was the cause for Proclus of all the destruction and**

<meta>This page continues a philological commentary on the "Life of Proclus" written by Marinus of Neapolis. The notes focus on textual variations between manuscripts (such as the Laurentian) and clarify technical terminology regarding memory, sorcery, and historical figures like Ulpian of Gaza.</meta>

84 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

"the interpreter of absurd spells and prophecies." <note>original: "τῆς περὶ τὰς ἀτόπους ἐπῳδὰς καὶ μαντείας ἐξηγήτης"</note> In the Laurentian manuscript, the last words are rendered thus: "and indeed an interpreter of visions." <note>original: "καὶ μὲν γανείας εἰσηγήτης"</note> Furia, a most learned man, aptly corrects this to "sorceries" <note>original: "μαγγανείας"</note>. Eunapius page 50 says: "the sorceries that deceive the senses and cast spells are the works of wonder-workers." <note>original: "αἱ δὲ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἀπατῶσαι μαγγανεῖαι καὶ γοητεύουσαι, θαυματοποιῶν ἔργα"</note> In the same author, "sorceries" <note>original: "μαγγανείας"</note> seems to have corrupted into "madness" <note>original: "μανίας"</note>. Compare Wyttenbach, *Critical Epistle*, page 45.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 20. **sharing the same hearth** <note>original: "ὁμόεστιον"</note>] Manuscripts LGC read "sharing a hearth" <note>original: "ὁμέστιον"</note>. Manuscript T reads "of the same rank" <note>original: "ὁμόστιον"</note>. The lexicon of Suidas interprets the reading *homestion*—which is not a bad one—as "living in the same house" or "under the same roof" <note>original: "ὁμόοικον, ὁμορόφιον"</note>. But our author Marinus confirms the published version in chapter 12: "he made him a sharer of his hearth." <note>original: "ὁμόεστιον ἐποιεῖτο"</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 20. **ease and dexterity** <note>original: "εὐκολίαν καὶ ἐντρέχειαν"</note>] Eunapius page 30: "of demonstrations that run on decorously and easily" <note>original: "τῶν ἐπιτρεχουσῶν κοσμίως καὶ εὐκόλως ἀποδείξεων"</note>; see the note there. The argument of Aristides' work *To Apellas* volume 1, page 67: "it has something of a rhythmic dexterity that runs through it wonderfully." <note>original: "θαυμασίως δὲ ἔχει καί τι τῆς εὐρυθμίας ἐντρέχον"</note>. Longinus, *On the Sublime* 44. 1: "sharp and dexterous natures" <note>original: "δριμεῖαί τε καὶ ἐντρεχεῖς φύσεις"</note>; here Toupius should be consulted. Add Ernesti's *Lexicon of Technology* under the entry for "ease" <note>original: "εὐκολία"</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 20. **the matters of deeds** <note>original: "τὰ τῶν πράξεων"</note>] See below in chapter 22, "he explained the deeds," <note>original: "ἐξηγεῖτο πράξεις"</note> where you may see the note.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 20. **he used to recall in those very words** <note>original: "ἀπεμνημόνευεν ἐπ’ αὐτῶν λέξεων"</note>] Manuscripts CT have "he recalled" <note>original: "ἀπεμνημόνευσεν"</note> [changing the tense from imperfect to aorist]: concerning this exchange between the aorist and imperfect tenses, I have spoken in my notes on Philostratus's *Heroicus* page 530. Eunapius page 107: "he used to teach" <note>original: "ἐπαίδευεν"</note>; the manuscript has "he taught" <note>original: "ἐπαίδευσεν"</note>. See the notes there. These passive tenses are also frequently swapped. Below in chapter 10, we will read "was revealed" in two different forms <note>original: "κατεμηνύθη and κατεμηνύετο"</note>. — **in those very words.** So also at the end of chapter 10: "in those very phrases." Add chapter 32. Casaubon, in his notes on the *Augustan History* page 37 D, collecting examples of certain men with prodigious memories, contends that Proclus surpasses them all, citing this passage.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 20. **Ulpian of Gaza** <note>original: "Οὐλπιανὸς ὁ Γαζαῖος"</note>] Manuscripts LGCT read **Olympianus**. This is a significant variation. However, I have not dared to change anything in such a doubtful matter. The lexicon of Suidas simply lists him as "Ulpian of Gaza." FABRICIUS <note>A reference to the scholar Johann Albert Fabricius</note> writes: "Regarding Ulpian of Gaza, Proclus’s fellow student, whom learned men—with less probability—suspect to be the same person whose commentaries on Demosthenes survive, one should consult Damascius in the *Life of Isidore* and Suidas who draws from him." But as I said, we should not immediately conclude that Ulpian of Gaza, mentioned simply by Marinus (and, it seems, by Suidas), is the same as the Ulpian who was the brother of Isidore. The matter is very ambiguous and uncertain. Various passages concerning different men named Ulpian were collected by Taylor in Reiske's *Greek Orators* volume 8, page 711.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 21. **Aristotle’s logical writings themselves** <note>original: "αὐτὰ δὲ τὰ Ἀριστοτέλους λογικὰ συγγράμματα"</note>] "In the Latin version it previously read: 'Aristotle's writings on the art of speaking.' But just as the word 'discourses' <note>original: "λόγοι"</note> was used above, so now the term 'logical' <note>original: "λογικὰ"</note> has a broader meaning than just dialectic or rhetoric—"

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 85

"...since it ought to be restricted." <note>original Latin: "cam restringi debeat"</note> FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a renowned German scholar known for his exhaustive bibliographies of Greek and Latin literature.</note> I hardly need to mention that I have provided the reading *he was learning* <note>original Greek: ἐξεμάνθανε (*exemanthane*)</note> based on Manuscript T, instead of the form ending in 'n' (*exemanthanen*).

Page 21. **to those who encounter [this book]** <note>original Greek: τοῖς ἐντυγχάνουσι (*tois entynchanousi*)</note>] I have discussed the use of the word *encounter* in the sense of "reading" at length in my notes on Eunapius, page 126. A certain Dio, five of whose unpublished letters appear twice in Manuscript 3002 (on page 86 and page 110), used this word in his third letter, addressed to Eusebius, which I will include here in its entirety: **"I think I see you in person when I read the letters you send; so, if you were to write to me more frequently, I would be very little distressed by your absence."** If I were to transcribe the other four letters here, I do not think anyone would be particularly upset with me.

->**Letters of Dio.**<-

->**1. Dio to Rusus.** <margin>1) Thus in one manuscript; but in the other the title is absent. Perhaps it should be "To Rufus," as in the second letter.</margin><-
I recommend to you the man delivering this letter. He is facing some difficulties due to the persistence of a legal opponent, though he himself does not wish to be a burden to his friends. In all other respects, as you yourself might observe and praise, he is a man of moderation and integrity. As for his family lineage and social standing, I do not need to ask you to investigate; he is well-established in those regards.

->**2. To Rufus.**<-
You already know my friend Herennius, but not yet as well as I would like. Indeed, I could perhaps not even now describe all his good qualities. It is worth testifying, however, that he has been a friend to us for a long time and has proven himself over the years. Regarding his studies, he was first a devoted student, but now he has even excelled. He is a fine orator <term>rhetor: a professional speaker or teacher of communication in the ancient world</term>, and he would become even better by spending time with you and being encouraged by you. While you do me many favors in many areas, you would favor me most of all by considering Herennius as one of your own.

->**3. To Eusebius, etc.**<-

->**4. To the same [Eusebius].** <margin>2) In the second manuscript source, the third and fourth letters have no headings.</margin><-
Who would not agree that the recent events involving "little dragons" and evils are the height of misery? <note>The term "little dragons" (*drakontia*) likely refers to a specific type of biting pain or perhaps a parasitic ailment known in antiquity, though here it is used to describe a sequence of misfortunes.</note> Yet these are human experiences that have happened to many people already. Therefore, it is necessary to endure these things and bear them willingly. For one must—even if the situation were different, and even if one were completely overwhelmed by suffering—look toward those who are still with us, so that one does not appear to have managed the tragedy well while neglecting the needs of the living.

[Horizontal line]

86 NOTES ON MARINUS.

### 5. To Sabinianus ¹).
I do not hesitate to write, nor is it out of some arrogance that I have kept silent about matters at hand; for indeed, I would admit to being the most miserable of men if, after practicing the art of rhetoric <note>original Greek: τὸ λέγειν, "the speaking"</note>, I were unwilling to send a letter and should neglect a friend with whom I once joined the dance of the Muses <note>A classical metaphor for sharing a liberal or philosophical education</note> and performed the sacred rites—those which are the holiest of all among the Greeks.

**Page 21. "for mere simple reading only"** <note>original Greek: πρὸς μόνην ψιλὴν ἀνάγνωσιν</note>] "That is, so that you may extract the meaning even after the fashion of a grammarian." <note>LATIN INTERPRETATION</note>. I much prefer the interpretation of Casaubon <note>Isaac Casaubon (1559–1614), a famous classical scholar</note>, who relates "for mere simple reading" to the phrase "he learned by heart" <note>original Greek: ἐξεμάνθανε</note>, and translates it as "readily understood" <note>original Latin: statim lectos</note>, using the words of Spartianus <note>One of the supposed authors of the *Augustan History* (Historia Augusta)</note>: see the *Augustan History*, page 37 D. One may also aptly compare Augustine in *Confessions* Book IV, chapter 16, who says, "the categories...

<meta>This page contains scholarly commentary by Jean François Boissonade on Marinus’ biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus. It focuses on philological corrections and historical identifications of figures and places in 5th-century Athens.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 87<-

**Page 22. succession** <note>original: "διαδοχὴ"</note>] The CT manuscript reads **teaching** <note>original: "διδαχὴ"</note>, which is worth noting; but **succession** is the only true reading. "Hence Proclus was preeminently <note>original: "κατ’ ἐξοχὴν"</note> called the **Successor** <note>original: "διάδοχος"</note>, namely the Platonic Successor, as the learned scholar [Daniel Heinsius, page 79] noted regarding Hesiod." — FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a previous editor of this text.</note> Nicias, in Athenaeus Book 13, chapter 61, had written a book **on the successions of the philosophers**. And a book by Sotion was circulated on the same subject. Immediately following, the manuscripts LGCT gave **they lead** <note>original: "ἄγουσιν"</note> instead of **they were leading** <note>original: "ἤγουσιν"</note>.

**Page 22. the overseer of philosophy** <note>original: "τὴν τῆς φιλοσοφίας ἔφορον"</note>] Fabricius thinks this refers to Athens. I believe it refers to Minerva <note>The Roman name for Athena, goddess of wisdom.</note>. Compare chapter 15: **by the providence of the philosophical goddess**; and chapter 30: **to the philosophical goddess herself**.

**Page 22. the preludes** <note>original: "τὰ προοίμια"</note>] Regarding the metaphorical sense of this word, see Lennep on Phalaris, page 141.

**Page 22. from the father** <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ πατρὸς"</note>] "Meaning Apollo." — FABRICIUS. I believe it refers not to Apollo, but to Plato. <note>Proclus and other Neoplatonists often referred to Plato in quasi-religious, paternal terms.</note>

**Page 22. put into port** <note>original: "κατῆρε"</note>] Timaeus’s Lexicon defines this as **he arrived by sea** <note>original: "κατέπλευσεν"</note>; see Ruhnken’s notes there. — Instead of **was disclosed** <note>original: "κατεμηνύθη"</note>, the manuscripts LGC read **he was disclosing**, which is incorrect. Manuscript T reads **it was being disclosed**. See above, page 84.

**Page 22. Nicholas**] "The *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> says more about this Nicholas of Lycia." — FABRICIUS. A note in the Coislinianus manuscript says: **his circulated works include rhetorical preliminary exercises** <note>original: "προγυμνάσματα"</note>. The *Suda* testifies to the same: **He wrote preliminary exercises and rhetorical declamations and certain other things.** The commentator on Philostratus's *On Heroes* (page 304) might be referring to this Nicholas: **George is superior to Nicholas in eloquence.** — Shortly after, instead of **he who later indeed**, manuscripts CGT read **he indeed later**. Then I wrote **renowned in the sophistic art** <note>original: "περιφανὴς ἐπὶ σοφιστικῇ"</note> based on CT, instead of the published **renowned in the sophistic [art]** <note>using the article "the" in Greek</note>. Compare chapter 11, "in the sophistic art" and "in the poetic art." See Schaefer’s *Miscellany*, page 4.

**Page 23. Lycian**] The *Suda* says: **Nicholas of Myra in Lycia.** For I join the two entries in the *Suda*, "Nicholas the Rhetorician" and "Nicholas of Myra." There was another Nicholas, a native of Myra under Constantine, whom the *Suda* also mentions.

**Page 23. around the Socrateion**] A small shrine <note>original: "sacellum"</note> dedicated to Socrates, a monument to a Hero. — "Diogenes Laertius in Book 2 [43, with Menagius's notes] and Tertullian in his *Apology* [chapter 14] mention a bronze statue of Socrates dedicated by the Athenians." — FABRICIUS. Themistius in Oration 20, page 239 C, testifies that the Athenians repented for what they had committed against Socrates, and **they exacted punishment** (restore the middle voice *epraxanto* from the Valerian Codex) **from Meletus for that trial; Anytus himself** (read *autos* with the Codex) **fled from the city, and the people of Heraclea in the Pontus stoned Anytus** (read *Anyton* from the Codex) **to death because of Socrates; and even now there is shown a tomb of Anytus** (read *Anytou* from the Codex, as the Latin translator intended) **there in the suburbs, not far from the sea, where they surrounded him** (the more corrected Codex... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page provides philological commentary on Marinus' biography of Proclus, correcting Greek manuscript readings and identifying Nicholas of Lycia and the "Socrateion" shrine in Athens.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Neoplatonism, Nicholas of Lycia, Socrates, Athens, philology, textual criticism, Suda, Themistius</keywords>
