### 88 NOTES ON MARINUS.

It has "at whom they even threw [stones]" <note>original: οὗπερ καὶ ἔβαλον</note>: concerning the use of "to throw" <note>original: βάλλω</note> instead of "to throw with stones" <note>original: λίθοις βάλλω</note>, compare the note to Philostratus’ *Heroica*, p. 436.) Anytus <note>One of the men who accused Socrates, leading to his execution.</note> [was treated thus] by the Heracleots. Dio Chrysostomus, *Oration LV*, p. 289: "but to Anytus, perhaps speaking to him personally." Codex 2958 reads: "but speaking to him personally," which is good: for by changing "to him" <note>original: αὐτῷ</note> into "to Anytus" <note>original: Ἀνύτῳ</note> we have the true hand of Dio. The words "to Anytus, perhaps" <note>original: Ἀνύτῳ τάχα</note> are the work of a critical scribe who, taking offense at the word "to him," modestly proposed "perhaps, to Anytus." Reiske, being a most ingenious conjecturer, had almost seen the truth.

**P. 23. "he was seized, as he said, by a great thirst"**] Chapter 17: "he was seized by a disease." See also chapters 29 and 31. — The form "he said" <note>original: ἔλεγε</note> instead of "he was saying" <note>original: ἔλεγεν</note> is from manuscript T.

**P. 24. "having prostrated"** <note>original: προσκυνήσας; this refers to the act of *proskynesis*, a deep bow or prostration before a divine or high-ranking figure.</note>] Concerning the verb "to prostrate/worship," compare what Goetzius indicated regarding Corippus, Book IV, line 131. Since a mention of Corippus has been introduced—an author not often called upon for testimony—I will use this opportunity to bring forward a beautiful observation by Valesius on this poet. Thus Corippus is edited in Book II, line 92:
-> "The pious words of the praying man pleased the Parent, <-
-> and the Son nodded to the petitioner, <-
-> and the Holy Spirit, the **ruler** <note>original: regnator</note>, said..." <-
Valesius, in the Durean book, most excellently restores it:
-> "...and the Holy Spirit said: **reign!**" <note>original: regnato</note> <-
"Reign" <note>original: regnato</note>, that is, "may you rule." <note>This is a subtle grammatical correction where a noun (ruler) is changed to an imperative verb (reign), altering the theological tone of the passage.</note>

**P. 24. "the keys"** <note>original: τὰς κλεῖς</note>] "Bars: thus we translate 'the keys.' For they do not always signify a body part <note>A reference to the collarbone, also called *kleis* in Greek.</note> or just keys, but also obstacles or bars, as Eustathius reminds us when he says: Homer calls the 'key of the doors' the bolt and the bar of the doors, specifically the crossbeam." **LATIN INTERPRETER.**

**P. 24. "of the little man"** <note>original: τοῦ ἀνθρωπίου; a diminutive form of 'man,' often used with a hint of pity or contempt.</note>] Thus manuscripts C and T for "of the man." Themistius, *Oration XXIV*, p. 306 D: "the races of **pitiful little horses**." The Valesian manuscript has "of horses," but the accepted reading is better.

**P. 24. "they were closing"** <note>original: ἔκλειον</note>] "By these words it was signified, according to Marinus' interpretation, that if Proclus had not come, the Platonic succession <note>The 'Golden Chain' of teachers leading the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note> would have already been terminated." **FABRICIUS.**

**P. 24. "of Polles or Melampus"**] "Regarding Polles of Aegium, the <term>augur</term> <note>A religious official who interpreted omens, particularly the flight of birds.</note>, Suidas should be consulted again. Those who think that 'of Polyidus' should be read in this place instead of 'of Polles' are mistaken, even though Apollodorus and others celebrate Polyidus' prophetic faculty. Concerning Melampus, it is discussed in the *Greek Library*, Book I, chapter 15." **FABRICIUS.** See regarding Melampus the index of writers by Reiff in his edition of Artemidorus. Nicephorus’ commentary on Synesius, p. 421 B: "This Melampus (Codex 2629 adds 'this man') was an Egyptian <term>sacred scribe</term> <note>original: ἱερογραμματεὺς; a high-ranking priest in Egypt skilled in hieroglyphs.</note>, a <term>hierophant</term> <note>An interpreter of sacred mysteries.</note>, and the best of diviners, etc." Suidas: "Melampus; of divination..."

NOTES ON MARINUS. 89

**Expert.** <note>original: εἰδήμων (eidēmōn)</note> “But the symbol of Melampus or Polletus was required.” <note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ σύμβολος Μελάμποδος ἢ Πόλλητος ἐδέετο. Melampus and Polletus were legendary seers in Greek mythology.</note> These men were the best at judging future events. Kuster, who discusses Melampus at length, did not fail to notice the similarity of Suidas’s words with those of Marinus. See also Foucher in *Memoirs of the Academy of Inscriptions*, volume 36, page 308.

P. 25. **highly contested**] <note>original: περιμάχητος (perimachētos)</note> Regarding this word, see Wyttenbach on Plutarch’s *On the Late Vengeance of the Deity*, page 60, and the reference there to Hemsterhuis on Lucian’s *The Contemplants*, chapter 11, volume 3, page 389. Synesius, in Letter 67, page 211 D, writes: “This was the point of contention for our brothers, and now for others as well, the most devout bishops Dioscorus and Paul.” <note>Synesius (c. 373–414 AD) was a philosopher and Bishop of Cyrene.</note> Codex 1031 deletes the word **the** <note>original: τὸ (to)</note> by placing two dots over it; I would certainly approve of this.

P. 25. **he meets Syrianus, the first of the philosophers**] <note>Syrianus was a Neoplatonist philosopher and the teacher of Proclus.</note> Codex T reads **firstly**. <note>original: πρῶτον (proton)</note> “Spoken of Syrianus in the Prolegomena <note>introductory remarks</note> to this *Life*.” FABRICIUS.

P. 25. **Lachares**] “Concerning Lachares, besides Damascius in the *Life of Isidore* [page 1045] and Suidas in his own entry, the same Suidas should be consulted under the entries for **Superianus** and **Metrophanes**.” FABRICIUS. A scholium <note>marginal commaentary</note> in the Coislinian manuscript: “This Lachares is the one who worked out the rhetorical meters.”

P. 25. **of the late evening**] <note>original: ὁ τῆς δείλης ὀψίας (ho tēs deilēs opsias)</note> Fabricius had omitted the article **the**. <note>original: ὁ (ho)</note> See Graevius on Lucian’s *The Cock*, chapter 22, and Ruhnken on Timaeus, page 75. Synesius, in Letter 51: “Setting out from Phycus at the beginning of dawn, we reached the Erythraian Gulf in the late evening; we stayed only as long as it took to drink water and to take on water.” <note>original: ὕδωρ πιεῖν καὶ ὑδρεύσασθαι (hydōr piein kai hydreusasthai)</note> Codex 1031 reads **to make water**, <note>original: ὕδωρ ποιεῖν (hydōr poiein). This is a common scribal error or a crude joke, substituting 'making water' (urinating) for 'drinking water.'</note> which is worth noting and, it seems, should be preferred; I will add, however, that the letter **o** was disturbed and almost canceled by the scribe. The same words, **to make** <note>original: ποιεῖν</note> and **to drink**, <note>original: πιεῖν</note> are swapped in Themistius, Oration 21, page 260 D, where instead of “to make the ladle into an amphora,” Valesius’s manuscript incorrectly has “to drink.” In Lucian’s *Saturnalia*, chapter 4: “and indeed both to drink to one’s greatest pleasure and to seem more musical than another at the same time during the banquet.” Codex 2600 displays **to make**, poorly, and lacks that intrusive word **at the same time**. Likewise in chapter 9: “he could not drink when thirsty”; the manuscript again incorrectly reads **to make**. I will remove a similar error from a fragment of Cassius Dio (163). Popilius had demanded that Viriathus hand over his weapons, but “he did not make <note>original: ἐποίησεν (epoiesen)</note> himself or the rest of the crowd submit to this.” Ursinus suspected the word **made**, and rightly so, if I am not mistaken. I propose **persuaded**, <note>original: ἔπεισεν (epeisen)</note> which is a very easy change, requiring almost no alteration of letters. In the letter to Brutus from Cos (Letter 14): “neither the capture of the Rhodians would have struck us with fear if we were not of your mind, nor would the prosperity of the Lycians along with their flattery have persuaded <note>original: ἔπεισεν</note> us”; the margin of Codex 1038 has **made**. <note>original: ἐποίησεν</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s "Life of Proclus," focusing on reconciling manuscript variations and clarifying historical references to the Neoplatonic school in Athens.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 91<-

**P. 27. "we were born":** <note>original: "δ' ἔφυμεν"</note> "both from a mother," which is the text found in the published version of Pindar, *Nemean Ode* VI.

**P. 27. "led against this":** <note>original: "ἤγε τούτῳ ἐναντίον"</note> Manuscript T reads "or indeed." <note>original: "ἤτι γε"</note> Immediately after, instead of "and such things," Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a prolific German scholar and bibliographer who edited this text.</note> incorrectly wrote "and yet these things." I recall a passage in Athenaeus [Book XIII, 82] where the phrase "and yet" <note>original: "καί τοι"</note> similarly sits in error: "Sophocles, having heard this, composed such an epigram against him, using the story about the Sun and Boreas [the North Wind], and yet <note>original: "καί τοι"</note> hinting at his adultery." The most distinguished editor preferred "and indeed," but the fault will be more easily cured by reading "and somewhat." <note>original: "καί τι"</note>

**P. 27. "to the great Plutarch, son of Nestorius":** <note>original: "τῷ μεγάλῳ Πλουτάρχῳ τῷ Νεστορίου"</note> The *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> under the entry for *Nicolaus* says: "a rhetorician, an acquaintance of Plutarch and Proclus; I mean Plutarch, surnamed the son of Nestorius." Add the same from the entry for *Plutarch son of Nestorius*.

**P. 27. "to give himself over":** <note>original: "ἑαυτὸν ἐπιδοῦναι"</note> I have added the pronoun based on manuscripts L, G, C, and T.

**P. 28. "the great":** <note>original: "ὁ μέγας"</note> " 'The Great' is not a proper name, as the translator seems to imply, but an honorary title <term>elogium (an honorific title or short laudatory inscription)</term> for Plutarch of Athens, which Damascius also attributes to him in the *Life of Isidore*." **FABRICIUS**. And further down in chapter 26, that Plutarch is called "the great," and likewise in Damascius, whose passage see cited below for chapter 26 regarding Hegias. Nestorius is also called "the great" in chapter 28, as is Pericles the Lydian in chapter 29. Similarly, "the all-great Plato" <note>original: "ὁ παμμέγας Πλάτων"</note> appears in Themistius, *Oration* II, page 31 B, where the Valesian Codex has "the great."

**P. 28. "to write down what was said":** <note>original: "ἀπογράφεσθαι τὰ λεγόμενα"</note> Chapter 13: "writing down what was said summarily."

**P. 28. "he rejoiced":** <note>original: "δὲ ἔχαιρε"</note> Manuscripts L, G, C, and T read "he rejoiced" <note>original: "δ' ἔχ." - noting the elision of the vowel.</note>.

**P. 28. "to abstain entirely":** <note>original: "πάντη ἀπέχεσθαι"</note> "Thus, Pythagoras did not abstain entirely from living things, nor did he command others to abstain, as Diogenes Laertius [VIII. 20] and Athenaeus [X. 13] testify on the authority of Aristoxenus." **FABRICIUS**. See below in chapter 19 what is narrated regarding Proclus's abstinence from eating living things <note>i.e., meat</note>.

**P. 29. "living":** <note>original: "διαιτώμενον"</note> Manuscripts C and T read "living" <note>original: "διαιτώμενος"</note>. Soon after, instead of "if he wished, he might die," I have accepted the reading of Codices L, G, and C. In manuscript T, it reads "if he wishes, he might die."

**P. 29. "he introduced the young man":** <note>original: "τὸν νέον συνίστη"</note> Manuscripts L and G read "introduced" <note>original: "συνέστη"</note>. C and T read "he introduced the young man."

**P. 29. "the grandson Archiadas":** <note>original: "τὸν ἔγγονον Ἀρχιάδαν"</note> "It appears from chapter 29 below that Archiadas was the son-in-law of Plutarch. Others read 'descendant' <note>original: "ἔκγονον"</note> in this place instead of 'grandson,' <note>original: "ἔγγονον"</note> which is the same thing." **FABRICIUS**.

**P. 29. "more greatly":** <note>original: "μειζόνως"</note> Manuscripts C and T read "greater" <note>original: "μεῖζον"</note>. G reads "more greatly" <note>original: "μαζόνως"</note>.

**P. 30. "treatises":** <note>original: "πραγματείας"</note> Chapter 19: "the treatise <note>or study</note> of his hymns." Chapter 23: "to one going through his treatises." Add Chapter 26. Upton and Schaefer have illustrated this word in that sense in Dionysius [of Halicarnassus] *On Composition*, page 14; also Diodorus Siculus, Book I, at the beginning.

It also discusses Neoplatonic and Pythagorean dietary practices regarding abstinence from meat.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s biography of the philosopher Proclus. The previous pages likely discussed the various stages of Proclus's education and the specific terminology used by Marinus to describe his philosophical works.</meta>

92 ->NOTES ON MARINUS<-

...they provide the most excellent experience through this <term>treatise|original: πραγματείας (pragmateias). In ancient Greek, this refers to a systematic study, a professional work, or a formal piece of writing.</term> to those who read it. The same author in Book 3: "We did not, however, suppose that their treatises were worked out according to what is useful and possible." Herodian, Book 2, Chapter 15, Section 6: "Making the life of Severus the subject-matter of the entire treatise." Longinus, Fragment 5, Section 1: "Some even attempted through writing to treat the things that seemed right to them."

**Page 30. ethical, political** <note>original: ἠθικὰς, πολιτικὰς</note>] Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a renowned classical scholar who produced an earlier edition of Marinus.</note> had omitted these, as well as the words **through these sufficiently just as** <note>original: διὰ τούτων ἱκανῶς ὥσπερ</note> shortly after.

**Page 30. theological** <note>original: θεολογικὴν</note>] "Metaphysical." FABRICIUS. <note>In the Neoplatonic tradition, "theology" was often used interchangeably with "metaphysics," the study of ultimate reality and the divine.</note>

**Page 30. preliminary rites** <note>original: προτελείων (proteleion). These were initial ceremonies or sacrifices performed before a major event, particularly before initiation into religious mysteries.</note>] "Among the Athenians in the <term>Mysteries|The Eleusinian Mysteries, secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</term>, those whose names were inscribed in the register of those to be initiated, after they [had waited] for at least six months, from the twentieth of the month <term>Boedromion|An Attic month corresponding roughly to September/October.</term>

It also begins a detailed explanation of the "preliminary rites" associated with Athenian religious initiations mentioned in the text.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a philological analysis of the Greek word for "mind" (nous), specifically focusing on rare or variant declension patterns found in ancient and Byzantine literature.</meta>

*nos*, *noi*, *noa*. <note>original: "ὸς, νοῒ, νόα"</note> Neophytus Ducas <note>a prominent 18th-century Greek scholar and priest</note> in his grammar *Terpsithea*
page 16, records these two forms among **nouns declined in different ways**. 
Synesius <note>a 4th-century philosopher-bishop</note> in *On Dreams* page 131 C. would have written: **saying that the gods are nothing other than intellects**; Nicephorus <note>referring to Nicephorus Gregoras, who wrote commentaries on Synesius</note> in the Scholia <note>scholarly commentaries written in the margins of ancient manuscripts</note> added these things: **Mind; <term>minds</term> <note>using the variant form νόας</note>, intellectual substances.** 
Astrampsychus <note>a legendary figure to whom various oracular and magical texts were attributed</note> page 5:
**Having sailed through the mud of the mind, perceive the harm.**
and page 7:
**A clear spring dissolves the pains of the mind.**
The Suda <note>a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>: **A wax impression; the imagination of the mind**; which, as Toup <note>Jonathan Toup, an 18th-century English philologist</note> has already noted, he took from Photius's <note>a 9th-century Patriarch and scholar</note> summary of Damascius <note>the last head of the School of Athens</note> page 1029, line 51, but he added **of the mind**. 
Cyril in the *Life of Euthymius* page 11: **with an undistracted mind**. 
And even earlier, Paul had said in 1 Corinthians 1:10: **in the same mind and in the same judgment.** 
So Nic... <note>The text likely continues with "Nicephorus" or another author starting with "Nic"</note>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and explanatory notes on Marinus's biography of Proclus, specifically focusing on grammatical debates regarding the word "mind" (nous) and citations from various commentators.</meta>

94 ->NOTES ON MARINUS<-

...[we say] <note>original: γωμεν, though the author suggests λέγομεν (legomen)</note>, as some do, that they are pure minds, but the daemons, being of the soul, are participants in mind; even more so, the pure minds are undisturbed and unmixed with sensible things. <note>This passage discusses the hierarchy of beings in Neoplatonic thought, distinguishing between "pure minds" (divine intelligences) and "daemons" who participate in that intelligence.</note> He who wrote *of mind* <note>original: νοῦ (nou)</note> perhaps did not write *minds* <note>original: νόας (noas)</note>. Maximus indeed, the Scholiast <note>An ancient or medieval commentator.</note> on Dionysius the Areopagite, page 2, records these things: "Even the philosophers among the Greeks call the intellectual or angelic powers 'minds' [original: *noas*]... but they are called 'minds' [original: *noes*] for the sake of a more euphonious declension, since from the singular *the mind* [original: *ho nous*], *of the mind* [original: *tou nou*], the plural should have been *the minds* [original: *hoi noi*]. But for the sake of euphony, from another nominative singular, it became *the minds* [original: *hoi noes*]. For this is a habit of the Attics <note>Inhabitants of Athens or speakers of the Attic dialect.</note>, who say 'Aristophanes' and 'Socrates' [in the plural] as *Aristophanai* and *Socratai* instead of *Aristophaneis* and *Socrates*. There are also those who say *hoi nooi*, as if from *ho noos, tou noou*... Thus according to the Ionians: *nous, noos, noes*, like *chrous* (skin), *chroos*, and the plural *chroes*; for the declension is Ionic." Yet I would almost believe that because of this *nou*, Porphyry wrote *nous*, and we have just seen that Synesius, a most elegant philosopher, wrote *nous* and not *noas* like Nicephorus. A Greek man, Polyzois Kontos, who thought he was composing a certain epic in the style and manner of Homer, made this verse:
->And God taught him, and raised up his mind [original: *no’*] for him:<-
which is not to be tolerated in a small work shaped according to the standard of the ancients. <note>The author is criticizing the poet for using a non-standard, unclassical contraction of the word for "mind."</note> Corayus of Smyrna <note>Adamantios Korais (1748–1833), a major figure in the Greek Enlightenment.</note>, a man reflecting the learning of ancient Greece in the preface which he placed before the Greek version of Beccaria, page 13, correctly wrote *of the mind* [original: *noos*], since he was using the style of more recent writers. It is pleasing to produce the passage, for the book is not easily found: "the abuse [of pleasures] flees from necessary labors, softens the body, gradually unnerves the powers of the soul, and later blinds the eyes of the mind."

**Page 31. during the day**] Fabricius usually uses *meth’ hēmeras* <note>A different grammatical case for "during the day."</note>, which I ought to have corrected. The Hamburg margin <note>A reference to a specific annotated copy of the text.</note>: perhaps *meth’ hēmeras*: incorrect. Compare page 68.

**Page 31. with judgment**] The same expression will occur again below in chapter 22.

**Page 31. the elegant [works] on the Timaeus**] "That Marinus, in the final chapter, testifies that these commentaries of Proclus on the *Timaeus* <note>Plato's famous dialogue on the creation of the universe.</note>, which are still extant, were preferred over all his other writings." — FABRICIUS. — The term *glaphyros* [elegant/refined] is illustrated by Ernesti in his *Technical Lexicon*. Compare Schaefer on Dionysius of Halicarnassus, page 309.

**Page 31. upbringing**] Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note>: *Hēgmenōs* means "in an educated manner," and *agōgē* means "education" or "upbringing."

**Page 31. even more**] Bastius learnedly illustrates this expression and "still more" in his *Critical Letter*, page 184. Plotinus, *Enneads* I. 6.

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations regarding the proper Greek declension of the word "mind" (nous) and clarifies specific terminology used in Marinus's biography of the philosopher Proclus.</summary>
<keywords>grammar, Neoplatonism, Proclus, Timaeus, Fabricius, Adamantios Korais, philology, Greek dialects</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s biography of the philosopher Proclus. The author is meticulously comparing different manuscript versions (referred to by abbreviations like CT, V, and G) and citing parallel passages from other ancient Greek authors to justify specific word choices in the text.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 95<-

**6.** "The soul, therefore, having been led up to the intellect, is directed toward that which is more beautiful." <note>original: ψυχὴ οὖν ἀναχθεῖσα πρὸς νοῦν, ἐπὶ τὸ μᾶλλόν ἐστι καλόν</note>
Plutarch, *Customs of the Spartans*, vol. II, p. 239 C: "whoever among them might endure more and more while being beaten." <note>original: ὅστις αὐτῶν ἐπὶ πλέον τε καὶ μᾶλλον καρτερήσειε τυπτόμενος</note> Someone might say these words are perhaps not most appropriately applied here. I myself thought that the preposition "on" <note>the Greek "epi"</note> could be silently repeated before "more." I would not wish to stubbornly maintain that the edition of Olympiodorus in Photius, p. 177, 56, which reads "as he was raised to an even greater extent of power," <note>original: ὡς ἐπὶ πλεῖστον ἔτι μᾶλλον ἤρθη δυνάμεως</note> is perfectly sound. Themistius, *Oration IV*, p. 57 A: "and still most of all compelling you with the argument." The Valesian manuscript reads: "especially." <note>original: ἐπιμάλιστα</note>

**Page 31. was fully adorned]** I have changed the published reading "was adorned" <note>original: ἐκοσμεῖτο</note> to "was fully adorned" <note>original: κατεκοσμεῖτο</note> based on manuscripts C and T.

**Page 32. of the writings]** Fabricius had omitted this.

**Page 32. because of the fact that also concerning]** Manuscript V lacks "also." <note>the Greek word "kai"</note>

**Page 32. toward this]** Manuscripts C and T read "at this." — Shortly after, in the phrase **of virtue**, I added the article "the" <note>the Greek "tēs"</note> based on manuscripts L, G, C, and T.

**Page 32. just like those who cheer on the runners]** The eminent scholar Wyttenbach cites and illustrates this in his notes on Plato’s *Phaedo*, p. 126. Eunapius, p. 72, preceded Marinus with this image: "just as with the prize-winners, there are those who cheer and urge them on." See the note there.

**Page 33. magnificence]** "Regarding Archiadas's restraint concerning the loss of wealth, the same Suidas [in the entry *Archiadas*] reports several things from Damascius [as it indeed seems]." <note>original: De Archiadae continentia circa opum iacturam...</note> — FABRICIUS.

**Page 33. at one time — at another time]** Manuscript G reads "when — when." <note>A subtle change in Greek from "hote" (when) to "ote" (at one time)</note> Consult Schaefer on Theocritus, *Idyll I*, 36.

**Page 33. to foreigners and citizens]** These words are constantly joined together. Plutarch, *Consolation*, § 34: "most gentle to both foreigners and citizens." Plato, *Apology*, § 17: "I will do these things for both the younger and the older, whomever I meet, and for both foreigner and citizen." Also Plato’s *Theaetetus*, § 3. Philostratus, *Lives of the Sophists*, II. 1. § 3. p. 549: "in the Kerameikos, providing drink for citizens and foreigners alike." Anonymous Epigram 357:
*A citizen and a stranger are always dear to me —*
Leonidas of Tarentum, Epigram 85, in the Epitaph for Aristocrates:
*He knew how to act kindly toward both strangers and locals... O lovely earth, may you hold such a dead man.*
It should read "what kind of dead man you hold," <note>changing "toion" (such) to "poion" (what kind) and "echois" (may you hold) to "echeis" (you hold)</note> which will be clear to anyone inspecting the whole epigram. Planudes and the Vatican manuscript read "you hold." The corrupt word "such" <note>toion</note> reminds me of the highly corrupt "such" <note>toiēn</note> in Achilles Tatius, II. 22: "It is time for such a one, she said, that you should also fear spiders." I read from the Venetian manuscript 409: "It is time, **therefore**, she said." <note>original: ὥρα τοίνυν, ἔφη</note>

**Page 33. to show in every way]** Fabricius reads "to everyone." Manuscript T reads "all things."

**Page 33. after Archiadas]** "The meaning is ambiguous. For it can be understood such that either the heirs of Archiadas [endowed] those cities..." <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page</note>

<summary>This page provides philological commentary on Marinus's biography of Proclus, focusing on word choices regarding virtue, public kindness, and the treatment of foreigners. The author compares multiple ancient manuscripts and classical parallels (Plato, Plutarch) to establish the most accurate text.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, Archiadas, Greek manuscripts, philology, textual criticism, Plutarch, Plato, hospitality, citizens and foreigners</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus. The previous page likely discussed Proclus's will and bequests.</meta>

### 96 NOTES ON MARINUS

"...had substituted, or that, besides what he bequeathed to Archiadas, he left to the cities. We have set down the former interpretation as more probable." **LATIN INTERPRETATION:** "This can also be understood as: those things which he did not bequeath to Archiadas, he left to the cities." **FABRICIUS:** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a famous classical scholar and bibliographer.</note> I would prefer the sense proposed by Fabricius.

**P. 33. from one's own home]** <note>original: "oikothen"</note> Perhaps to be translated as: *spontaneously, by one's own motion and internal mental force*; as seen in Himerius, Oration 23, section 12: "For you kept away from harmful things of your own accord <note>original: "oikothen"</note>, often not waiting for the person giving the orders." Pindar, *Olympian Odes* 3. 79: "He touches the pillars of Heracles from his own home <note>meaning: by his own power</note>." Hierocles in *The Golden Verses*, page 278, edited by Warren: "To those who have set out from their own resources <note>original: "oikothen"</note> toward the release from evils." Upton rashly disturbed this passage in his notes on Arrian’s *Epistle to Gellius*, and was rightly rebuked by Schweighäuser, a most learned man.

**P. 34. in common]** <note>original: "koinois"</note> The editions, except for the Hamburg one, read "of common" <note>original: "koines"</note>; and Manuscript V also has "of common." But manuscripts L, G, C, and T have "in common." Shortly after, by a similar error, instead of "of the" <note>feminine singular</note>, Xylander and the Lyon edition have "to the" <note>masculine/neuter plural</note>. Below in chapter 34, Fabricius wrote "of the" instead of "to the," and in chapter 35, manuscript G incorrectly has "from the" <note>plural</note> instead of "from the" <note>singular</note>. Alciphron, Book II, Letter 4, page 327: "Menander, long ago defeated by my sufferings and love, and now having even added judgment to those things which I am more involved in, fearing the short-lived nature of passionate friendship." The sense, which is intricate when using the word "which" <note>original: "hois," plural</note>, becomes clear by reading "whose" <note>original: "hes," singular</note> from Codex 2832: namely, "whose judgment."

**P. 34. being present]** <note>original: "paragignomenos"</note> Thus Xylander, the Lyon edition, and manuscripts V, L, G, C, and T. In Fabricius and the Hamburg edition, it is spelled "paraginomenos" <note>a common orthographic variation in Greek dropping the second 'g'</note>.

**P. 34. of those who read]** <note>original: "ton anaginoskonton"</note> Thus all the Manuscripts and Editions, except for that of Fabricius, who substituted "of the rulers" <note>original: "archonton"</note>, with this little note: "In previous editions 'of those who read' was printed, but the translator reads 'of the rulers'." For my part, I have restored the original reading, not because I thought it was perfect, but because the conjecture of "rulers" did not seem sufficiently sound or safe in itself. — Immediately following, Manuscript V has "he was neglected."

**P. 34. prototype]** <note>original: "protypoma"</note> Compare the note to Eunapius, page 206.

**P. 34. for in a storm and a triple-wave]** <note>original: "en zale gar p. kai trikumia"</note> Manuscript V omits "for." — Aristaenetus, Book I, Letter 17: "You were a partner in my desire and you toss about the manner of my instability in the fashion of a triple-wave <note>The 'triple-wave' or 'trikumia' refers to the Greek belief that every third wave (or tenth, in other traditions) was larger and more dangerous—a 'rogue wave'.</note>." I am surprised that Abresch changed this accepted reading, especially since he cites passages from Philo that thoroughly explain and confirm it. Chariton, Book III, Chapter 2, page 58: "His soul was being submerged, but he forced himself to surface, as if from the triple-wave of passion." See Dorville on this passage. Aristides, *Sacred Tales*, Book I, page 274: "By the triple-waves surrounding the body." Aeschylus, *Prometheus Bound*, line 1023.

<summary>This page contains scholarly notes on the Greek text of Marinus's "Life of Proclus," focusing on textual variants and the specific meanings of philosophical and metaphorical terms like "oikothen" (spontaneously) and "trikumia" (a great swell or triple-wave).</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, philology, textual criticism, Fabricius, Himerius, Pindar, Greek metaphors, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the "Life of Proclus" by Marinus of Samaria. The previous pages likely discussed Proclus's early life and education; this page focuses on specific poetic metaphors and textual corrections regarding Proclus’s struggles and his "lawful life."</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 97<-

"What sort of storm and inescapable triple-wave of evils has overcome you."
<note>original: "Οἷός σε χειμὼν καὶ κακῶν τρικυμία Ἔπεισ᾽ ἄφυκτος." This is a quote from Euripides' play *Hippolytus*, line 822. The "triple-wave" refers to a Greek maritime belief that every third wave—or sometimes the ninth—was the largest and most dangerous.</note>
Compare Euripides, *Hippolytus* 822. Alcaeus, cited in the Scholia <note>ancient explanatory comments</note> to Pindar’s *Isthmian Odes* I. 52, also calls misfortune a "storm and a triple-wave."

**Page 35. and of typhoon-like winds** <note>original: "καὶ πνευμάτων τυφωνείων"</note>] Fabricius omitted the words "and of winds." — Manuscript TC reads "typhonian." — Hermias, in Ruhnken’s edition of *Timaeus* page 260: "They are accustomed to speak of typhonian winds or typhonian thunderbolts or lightning." Damascius, in the *Suda* <note>a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> under the entry "Sarapion": "For the life of one man was like that of Cronus or Zeus, but the other’s was Typhonian, a beast even more complex than Typhon, and more deeply smoldering." Who does not immediately recognize Plato's words from the *Phaedrus* § 8? Ruhnken, in his notes to *Timaeus* page 251, thinks that in Plato he read "even more smoldering" <note>original: "μᾶλλον ἔτι τεθυμμένον"</note>: and Fabricius read it this way when citing the Philosopher’s words in his notes to Sextus Empiricus, page 72. Heindorf did not use the passages of Fabricius and Ruhnken with total accuracy. Compare Ruhnken in the same work, pages 260 and 261.

**Page 35. in the lawful life** <note>original: "τῇ ἐννόμῳ ζωῇ"</note>] "I preferred this reading over what was previously read, 'in law'." — **FABRICIUS**. Manuscript T reads "lawful."

**Page 35. he swam through life** <note>original: "τὸν βίον διενήξατο"</note>] Proclus, *Hymn to Minerva*, line 47:
"Grant calm breezes to my life as it sails along."
<note>original: "δὸς βιότῳ πλώοντι γαληνιώοντας ἀήτας"</note>
Mitscherlich thinks Marinus had this passage in mind. Antipater of Sidon, *Epigram* 72:
"O you who, with song and with love,
Have sailed through all of life."
<note>original: "ὦ σὺν ἀοιδᾷ Πάντα διαπλώσας καὶ σὺν ἔρωτι βίον"</note>
Consult Jacobs on this passage. Plato, *Phaedo* 36: "to sail through life" <note>original: "διαπλεῦσαι τὸν βίον"</note>; see Wyttenbach page 227. Compare Abresch’s notes to Cattier, page 76.

**Page 35. vulture-giants** <note>original: "γυπογιγάντων"</note>] "Marinus hints at the fury and power of Proclus’s enemies with this word. Perhaps some of our Philosopher’s own fellow citizens, living in Athens, were causing him trouble, as he was Lycian by birth. For the Lycians were called 'Giants'." — **FABRICIUS**. Fabricius is referring to a passage in Hesychius: "Gigantia: Lycia was formerly called this, and its inhabitants, Giants." — Following this, manuscript CT reads "he had" <note>original: "εἶχε"</note> instead of "he was having" <note>original: "εἶχεν"</note>.

**Page 35. he recognized** <note>original: "ἐπεγίγνωσκε"</note>] LG reads "he recognized" <note>original: "ἐπεγίγνωσκεν"</note> instead of the published "he knew" <note>original: "ἐπεγίνωσκεν"</note>. Manuscript T reads "he foreknew."

**Page 35. if anything** <note>original: "εἴ τι"</note>] "It should be read this way, not 'still' <note>original: "ἔτι"</note> as in previous editions." — **FABRICIUS**. The Hamburg and Leiden editions indeed have "still," but Xylander reads "if anything," as do the Manuscripts.

**Page 36. all these things** <note>original: "ταῦτα πάντα"</note>] Manuscript CT reads "all these things" <note>original: "πάντα ταῦτα"</note> in reverse order. Below in chapter 22: "all these things." I remember Themistius's *Oration* II, page 33 C, published thus: "All these things have been written by the philosopher, holding nothing more precious..."

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the life and works of Marinus, focusing on specific textual variations and the philosophical motto "Live unnoticed."</meta>

# 98 NOTES ON MARINUS.

"to the one prophesying" <note>original Greek: μαντευομένῳ</note>. The Valesian Manuscript <note>The Codex Valesianus is a 17th-century collection of excerpts from historical texts</note> reads "not at all less," a reading that the scholar Petavius <note>Dionysius Petavius (1583–1652) was a renowned French Jesuit scholar</note> had already arrived at through his own scholarly conjecture.

Page 36. **"Live unnoticed"** <note>original Greek: τὸ λάθε βιώσας. A famous Epicurean maxim advising against seeking public fame.</note>] "Live in such a way that no one knows you have lived. Regarding this saying, Plutarch <note>Plutarch was a Greek philosopher and biographer; he wrote an essay specifically titled "Is 'Live Unnoticed' a Wise Precept?"</note> discusses at length whether it is stated correctly." — LATIN INTERPRETER.

"This saying is attributed by some not to Pythagoras <note>The ancient Greek philosopher and mathematician</note> but to Epicurus <note>Founder of the Epicurean school of philosophy</note>, as is clear from the book by Plutarch in which he criticizes it. The poet Ovid expressed the sentiment most perfectly [in his work *Tristia*, Book 3, Poem 4, Line 25: 'He who lives well hidden, lives well']." — FABRICIUS <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a prolific German scholar of classical texts</note>.

The *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> under the entry for "Sarapion," quoting the philosopher Damascius <note>The last head of the Neoplatonist Academy in Athens</note>: "The man possessed such an abundance of truthful character and reason, and of that which is customarily expressed in the proverb—but was confirmed by him through his deeds—namely, **'Live unnoticed'** [So Damascius wrote in Photius, page 1037: 'He proved the ancient proverb through his actions']—that none of the men living at that time, neither..."

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the "Life of Proclus" by Marinus of Samaria, specifically focusing on page 38 of the original text. The notes discuss Proclus's character traits—ambition and temper—linking them to classical philosophy and the sayings of the Seven Sages.</meta>

## NOTES ON MARINUS. 99

**P. 38. "He was ambitious"**] “Honorable mention of Proclus’s ambition is also made above on page 28 [Chapter 12].” FABRICIUS <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a German classical scholar and bibliographer who edited Marinus’s biography of the philosopher Proclus.</note>

**P. 38. "Nor would anything great happen"**] “Cicero [In defense of Archias, XI]: <note>original: 'hac laudis et gloriae mercede detracta, quid est quod in hoc tam exiguo vitae curriculo et tam brevi, tantis nos in laboribus exerceamus?'</note> *'If this reward of praise and glory were taken away, what reason is there that we should exert ourselves in such great labors in this so tiny and brief a race of life?'* Truly, as Herodes Atticus says in Gellius [XIX. 12], the mind would languish and grow numb if deprived of the support of this passion, as if stripped of a necessary command. For this reason, Plato said that the desire for glory is laid aside by the soul as if it were the **'last tunic'** <note>original: τελευταῖον χιτῶνα. This famous metaphor suggests that the desire for fame is the very last vice or attachment a person sheds on the path to wisdom.</note>: a point repeated by Simplicius in his commentary on Epictetus [Chapter 14, p. 170 and Chapter 47, p. 440, Schweighäuser edition], and by Tacitus [Histories, IV. 6] when he says, *'even for the wise, the desire for glory is the last thing to be cast off.'* <note>original: 'sapientibus quoque cupidinem gloriae novissimam exui.'</note> However, a wise man does not seek to be praised by others so much as he is delighted if he has done something worthy of the praise of good and wise men. This is also commended by the Apostle: <note>original: 'εἴ τις ἀρετὴ καὶ εἴ τις ἔπαινος, ταῦτα λογίζεσθε'</note> *'If there be any virtue, and if there be any praise, think on these things.'* Philippians 4:8.” FABRICIUS.

Juvenal, Satire X. 141:
“— *for who embraces virtue herself,*
*if you take away the rewards?*” <note>original: 'quis enim virtutem amplectitur ipsam, praemia si tollas'</note>
where Grangaeus notes: Plutarch, fragment edited by Matthaei, p. 128: <note>original: 'δύο ταῦτα ὡσπερεὶ στοιχεῖα τῆς ἀρετῆς εἰσὶν, ἐλπίς τε τιμῆς καὶ φόβος τιμωρίας κτλ.'</note> *“These two are, as it were, the elements of virtue: hope for honor and fear of punishment, etc.”*

**P. 38. **P. 38. "He was easily appeased"**] Obeying, of course, the precept of Periander, <note>original: 'χόλου κρατέειν'</note> **“Master your anger,”** preserved by an anonymous author in an epigram (Adespota 30). A better-known precept of Periander is **"PRACTICE IS EVERYTHING,"** <note>original: ΜΕΛΕΤΗ ΠΑΝ</note> as seen on the base of a <term>herm</term> <note>A squared stone pillar topped with a sculpted head, used in antiquity as a boundary marker or monument.</note> which the most celebrated Mr. Visconti displayed in the Pio-Clementine Museum and recently in his most splendid and rare *Iconographia*, where he cited that other saying of Periander about subduing anger from the sole testimony of this anonymous poet. The commentator on Dio Chrysostom attributes the same sentiment to the Corinthian Sage in different words in Manuscript 2958 [referencing Oration 72, p. 386 of the Reiske edition]: **“These are the Seven Sages, and these are their maxims, which were also dedicated at Delphi:**

*   **Cleobulus of Lindos**, whose maxim is: *'Moderation is best.'* <note>original: μέτρον ἄριστον</note>
*   **Chilon of Lacedaemon**, whose is: *'Know thyself.'* <note>original: γνῶθι σαυτόν</note>
*   **Pittacus of Mitylene**, whose is: *'Nothing in excess.'* <note>original: μηδὲν ἄγαν</note>
*   **Solon of Athens**, whose is: *'Look to the end of a long life.'* <note>original: τέλος ὅρα μακροῦ βίου</note>
*   **Periander of Corinth**, whose is: *'Master your anger.'* <note>original: θυμοῦ κράτει</note>
*   **Bias of Priene**, whose is: *'Most men are bad.'* <note>original: οἱ πλείονες κακοί</note>
*   **Thales**...

100 NOTES ON MARINUS.

"The Milesian <note>original: Μιλήσιος</note>, whose saying is this: 'Give a pledge, and ruin is at hand.' <note>original: ἐγγύα, πάρα δ' ἄτα</note>" Jacobs, in his commentary on the praised epigram (Volume II, page 284), incautiously stated that the epigram in the *Latin Anthology* (Volume I, page 571) was translated from the Greek; for there are significant differences in the sentiments expressed, and to Periander himself is attributed not "master your anger" <note>original: χόλου κρατέειν</note>, but rather "practice is everything" <note>original: μελέτη πᾶν</note>: 
"From Ephyra, O Periander, you teach that all things are to be achieved through meditation." 
Otherwise, in the praised passage of the Scholiast <note>An ancient commentator or annotator of a text.</note>, it is better to write *Mytilenaeus* instead of *Mitylenaeus*. The base of a statue <note>term: herma — a squared stone pillar topped with a sculpted head, usually of Hermes or a famous person.</note> in Visconti’s work at that same location reads:

-> PITTACUS <-
-> SON OF HYRRAS <-
-> OF MYTILENE <-
-> KNOW THE OPPORTUNITY <-

And on a coin of the Mytileneans featuring the portrait of Alcaeus <note>The famous lyric poet of Lesbos.</note>, which was included by that same most learned iconographer <note>referring to Visconti</note>, one can clearly read: ALCAEUS OF MYTILENE <note>original: ΑΛΚΑΙΟΣ ΜΥΤΙΛ</note>. See the venerable elder Larcher in the *Geographical Index to Herodotus*, under the entry "Mytilène."

Page 38. **the turn of a potsherd** <note>original: ὀστράκου περιστροφῇ</note>] "There was a type of game, as Pollux writes, called 'the turn of a potsherd,' and it seems the proverb was derived from this game rather than from ostracism <note>The Athenian practice of voting to exile a citizen by writing their name on a piece of broken pottery.</note>, a type of voting; although Erasmus of Rotterdam is uncertain which of the two it was rather derived from. [*Adages* II. I. 51.]." <gloss>LATIN INTERPRETER</gloss> — "Plato in the *Phaedrus* [at section 40, where Heindorf notes], and in the 6th book of the *Republic* [actually VII, page 521 C], Eunapius in Suidas [under 'Potsherd,' where Kuster notes], Lucian [*Apology for Salaried Posts* I, where see the commentators], Pollux [Book IX, chapter 7], Eustathius on the *Iliad* [Book 18, page 1219], Diogenianus [Century VI, number 95], and Apostolius [Century XIV, number 84, where Pantinus notes]." FABRICIUS. Eunapius, page 81: "the potsherd having flipped over," where you should compare the notes I have made.

Page 39. **he showed his spirit to be like wax** <note>original: κήρινον ἀπεδείκνυ τὸν θυμόν</note>] "As in Horace [*Art of Poetry* 163]: 'Pliant as wax to be bent towards vice' <note>The OCR text 'vitrum' (glass) is likely a mistake for 'vitium' (vice), which appears in the standard text of Horace.</note> etc." FABRICIUS. An unpublished Scholiast on Philostratus’s *Images* (I. 2): "soft like wax and such things, and from these it is applied to character and speech; for wax is softened, and so are character and speech." Proclus, in his *Commentary on the Cratylus* (chapter 75), says of bronze: "being more moldable than wax, it receives the impressions of divine thoughts." Damascius, in Suidas under the entry 'Wax,' following Proclus, says of Asclepiodotus: "he rendered his own voice and the natural organs surrounding it more moldable than wax." The same author, in the *Life of Isidore* (page 1029, line 51), following Plato (*Theaetetus*, section 118), calls the imagination a "waxen impression-block" <note>original: κήρινον ἐκμαγεῖον</note>, evidently because it easily absorbs the images and forms of things. Themistius, in Oration VIII (page 105): "but your..."

NOTES ON MARINUS. 101

...it is possible to observe a nature more easily molded toward virtue than any waxen <note>original: "κηρίου"; the Valesian Manuscript <note>The Valesian Manuscript (Codex Valesianus) refers to a collection of historical excerpts and readings once owned by the 17th-century scholar Henri Valois.</note> reads "κηρίνου", meaning *of wax*</note> impression. The unpublished Mazaris in his *Dialogue in the Underworld* <note>original: "διαλόγῳ νεκρικῷ"; Mazaris was a 15th-century Byzantine author known for his satirical look at the afterlife.</note> says: "Furthermore, by imitating the bait of fishermen, he drew me in—hard and unyielding as I was—and made me softer than lead, or rather, softer than wax."

**P. 39. *He rebuked***] <note>original: "ἐπέπληττε"</note> Manuscript C reads *he struck* <note>original: "ἔπληττε"</note>. Manuscript T reads *he was striking* <note>original: "ἔπληττεν"</note>. This prefix <note>the "epi-" in "epeplette"</note> can be attributed to Themistius <note>Themistius was a 4th-century philosopher and rhetorician.</note>, Oration II, page 236 D, and the simple verb is often augmented by composition: "even a vinedresser and a blacksmith, upon hearing him, had something to carry home with them." The Valesian Manuscript reads *listening to* <note>original: "ἐπακούων"</note>.

**P. 39. *was being carried — of sympathy***] <note>original: "ἐφέρετο — συμπαθείας"</note> These words are missing in the LG <note>Likely referring to the Laurentian and Germanic manuscript families.</note> manuscripts because of the repetition of the word *sympathy* <note>original: "συμπαθείας"</note>. This is a frequent cause of gaps <note>A "lacuna" or gap often occurs when a scribe's eye jumps from one instance of a word to a later instance of the same word, skipping the text in between.</note>, as the transcriber’s eye moves immediately from the first instance of the word to the second. Themistius, Oration II, page 236 C: "often Euripides and Sophocles, and Sappho the beautiful, and the noble Pindar would join in the song." I restore this from the Valesian Manuscript: "often Menander the golden, and often Euripides." Likewise in Oration XXI, page 253 A: "we do not judge a man to be wealthy just because he happens to have a ring." I read this from Valesius’s book: "if someone walking through the marketplace, having put on a gold-plated ring, were to intentionally hold the ring out to those he meets." ¹) Likewise in Oration X, page 130 A: "those who practice philosophy hold equal seats of honor with the generals, while both wisdom and power attend to you." The Manuscript gives this more fully: "in an equal seat of honor, and the bodyguard of wisdom that surrounds you is woven together with the most beautiful weaving." The similarity of the final syllables in *seat of honor* <note>original: "προεδρίᾳ"</note> and *bodyguard* <note>original: "δορυφορία"</note> produced the omission of the words in the middle. Oration II, page 26 B: "not only is your nod a guarantee of safety, but he—" The Manuscript reads: "safety, or rather, toward total happiness." Oration VII, page 97 B: "this man, therefore, was not penalized in money, but having one of his eyes knocked out—" I restore from that same book: "penalized, nor was he deprived of his soldiers, but he himself." Oration XXIV, page 304 A: "these men are so truly their mother's sons and so fond of the dance, that often, having persuaded their mothers—" I supplement from the Manuscript: "that often they even persuade their brothers to go out with them to their usual theaters, and often their mothers—" ²) Oration X, page 132 A. no one...

-> ——— <-

¹) I will note in passing that in the sentence closest to this one, instead of *drunkenness* <note>original: "παροινίας"</note>, the Manuscript has *insanity* <note>original: "παρανοίας"</note>, which Harduinus had correctly conjectured.

²) I will also remove more than one error from the previous sentence: "much varied finery covers her body, and she delights in Greek theaters and accustoms her children from a tender age to public appearances." The Manuscript reads better: "of her much and varied — pushes <note>The translator notes a slight change in the Greek verb from "accustoms" (eithizei) to "pushes" (othizei).</note>."

102               NOTES ON MARINUS

...of the Trojans to escape, but even those not yet born should perish. <note>original: "τῶν Τρώων διαφυγεῖν... ἀπολέσθαι"</note> The Codex reads: *to escape the Trojans, nor any youth whom the mother carries being in her womb, not even this one should escape, but also—.* <note>The author is comparing a manuscript reading to a famous passage from Homer’s Iliad (6.57), where Agamemnon demands total destruction of the Trojans.</note>

Achilles Tatius, Book I, Chapter 18. *They both stood therefore looking at one another; the lover from the mainland vomited.* <note>Achilles Tatius was a 2nd-century Greek novelist; this scene likely describes seasickness during a voyage.</note> Vatican Codex 409 reads: *the lover, and the beloved woman from the islands; whenever therefore the lover vomited.* But this gap was born, I believe, from Boden's negligence; for the omitted words were translated into Latin [in his edition]; from which I gather the full passage is read in other editions.

Aristides, *Sacred Orations*, Book IV, page 329. *Let it be whichever way one wishes, whether this harvest and grace came from the Healer <term>Paian</term> <note>An epithet for Apollo or Asclepius as gods of healing.</note>, and because of this it will be possible* (read *to have been possible* from Codex 1040) *to be saved, or whether this is as dangers will happen at sea, and from them safety.* Codex 1040 is more complete: *to be saved, or whether this must certainly have happened thus, the god having foreknown all things that would happen to signal them. On the one hand, as in the sea—.* The same author, *Sacred Orations*, Book V, page 356. *The words were somewhat like this: "for the good of both kings."* The Codex reads: *for the good of the king, and for the good of both.* These, I believe, are only missing from Jebb's edition, for the Latin translator includes them.

Synesius, *On Providence*, Book I, page 103 B. *It is necessary for everything that is honored to grow; then the love of all education increased.* Codex 1038 reads: *honored, and it is necessary for that which is neglected to perish; and it increased also in education—.* In the same work, Book II, page 127 B. *We shall think him to be in harmony. For how would they be one?* The Codex reads: *we shall think; for in this way one might be saved, and we shall not place the parts as unsympathetic to one another. For how—.* In the same work, page 128 D. *For he serves the judge of the games, from whom also having learned these things, it is necessary to be silent.* The Codex reads: *from whom also having learned, he knows, not having been meddlesome to know, nor because of this having moved the immovable; and having learned, indeed, it is necessary to be silent—.* <note>Synesius was a 4th-century philosopher-bishop; "moving the immovable" is a Greek proverb for disturbing sacred or settled things.</note>

**Page 39.** [Regarding the words] **So great — to be recorded**] Manuscripts C and T read *so great* [in the feminine form]. Manuscript V reads *to be recorded* [as an infinitive]. — Shortly after, **never yet** from manuscripts G, C, and T instead of **never**.

**Page 40. of the life of each**] The printed editions read *of each* [in the singular], which I changed [to the plural] based on manuscripts L, G, C, and T.

**Page 40. without delay**] The Xylander, Hamburg, and Leiden editions read *carelessly*. Manuscript V reads *he cares somewhat*. Fabricius and manuscript C read *without delay*. Manuscript T reads *carelessly*. Coislin Lexicon 393: *Carelessly (Read: without delay); without slowness or postponement.* Compare Suidas. <note>The editor is debating the spelling of 'amellēti' (without delay) versus 'amelēti' (carelessly), which look similar in Greek.</note>

**Page 40. And something also**] Manuscript G reads *And someone*: "taking the abbreviation for the particle 'and' as the letter sigma." SCHAEFER. — Shortly after, I preferred **but as much as there was** from manuscripts L, G, C, and T, rather than **but there was** of the editors.

**Page 41. The succession of Plutarch**] "above on page 29." FABRICIUS. Chapter 12. <note>This refers to the "succession" or leadership of the Neoplatonic school in Athens.</note>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Marinus’s "Life of Proclus," focusing on the nature of friendship and the hierarchy of Neoplatonic virtues.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 103<-

P. 41. **Pythagorean friendship** <note>original: "Πυθαγόρειον φιλίαν"</note>] “Nothing is more celebrated among the ancients than the very close friendship of the Pythagoreans, concerning which see the notes of learned men on Laertius, Book VIII, section 10. To cultivate this friendship all the more sacredly, they would continually remind themselves **not to tear apart the God within them** <note>original: "μὴ διασπᾶν τὸν ἐν ἑαυτοῖς θεὸν"</note>, so that they might not shatter the Divine presence that dwelt in them, as Iamblichus reports in Book I, chapter 33, of his *Life of Pythagoras*.” — FABRICIUS <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a renowned German scholar who edited this text.</note>

P. 41. **fellow student** <note>original: "συμφοιτητὴς"</note>] Found in Syrianus <note>Proclus's teacher and predecessor as head of the Academy</note>: above in chapter 12. But he is called **teacher** <note>original: "διδάσκαλος"</note> in chapter 14.

P. 41. **among those things recorded above** <note>original: "ἐν τοῖς ἄνω ἱστορημένων"</note>] Fabricius reads "being recorded" <note>original: "ἱστορουμένων"</note>. Compare above, page 66. — “Regarding the first kind of friendship, which rested on the kinship of Archiadas with Plutarch <note>Plutarch of Athens, the teacher of Syrianus</note>, see page 29 [chapter 12]; regarding the second, consult page 32 and following [chapter 14]. Furthermore, the Latin translator had wrongly rendered this passage as follows: *for the friendship of these men seemed much more remarkable than those two very rare types of friendship which have been recorded from ancient times.*” — FABRICIUS.

P. 41. **which not even Archiadas** <note>original: "ὃ μὴ καὶ Ἀρχιάδας"</note>] Manuscript V omits the word **and/even** <note>original: "καὶ"</note>.

P. 41. **to those concerning politics** <note>original: "τοῖς περὶ τῆς πολιτικῆς"</note>] Manuscripts LGC read “to those of the [writings] concerning politics.” Manuscript T reads “to those of politics.” — Instead of **of the true things** <note>original: "τῶν ἀληθῶν"</note>, perhaps it should read **of the other things** <note>original: "τῶν ἄλλων"</note>.

P. 42. **having sealed with friendship** <note>original: "τῇ φιλίᾳ ἐπισφραγισάμενοι"</note>] “Pythagoras called friendship the mother of all <term>political virtues</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, these are the practical, social virtues like justice and moderation used in civic life.</note>, according to the testimony of Damascius.” — FABRICIUS.

P. 42. **let us move on to the purifying virtues** <note>original: "ἐπὶ τὰς καθαρτικὰς μετίωμεν"</note>] Manuscripts C and T read “we are moving on.” — “This kind of virtue is the first of the four higher types which are found only in those who practice philosophy, and in men who—as Macrobius says following Plotinus [in *The Dream of Scipio*, Book I, chapter 8]—are capable of the divine. See what was said in the introduction.” — FABRICIUS.

P. 42. **the likeness** <note>original: "τὴν ὁμοίωσιν"</note>] Plotinus, *Enneads* I. 2. 3: “Such a disposition of the soul, by which it thinks and is thus free from passions, if one were to call it **a likeness to God** <note>original: "ὁμοίωσιν πρὸς θεὸν"</note>, one would not be mistaken; for the divine is pure.” And in chapter 5: “But one must state to what extent the purification goes; for thus it becomes clear both what the likeness is to, and with which god the identity is made.” Compare *Enneads* I. 6. 6. Themistius, *Oration* II, page 32 D: “Philosophy is nothing other than **becoming like God as far as is possible** <note>original: "ὁμοίωσις κατὰ τὸ δυνατὸν θεῷ"</note>.” The Valesian Manuscript reads: “the likeness of God according to human possibility.”

P. 42. **which adorn those who possess them while still remaining here** <note>original: "αἳ καὶ μένοντας ἔτι τοὺς ἔχοντας ἐνταῦθα κατακοσμοῦσι"</note>] Thus Fabricius reads. Other editions, such as Manuscript V, read “even if they remain.” The Latin translator either found or restored the word **which** <note>original: "αἳ"</note>. Manuscripts L and G read “if still new.” Manuscript C reads “if a new thing [adorns] those still... here.” Manuscript T reads “if it adorns those **still living** <note>original: "ζῶντας"</note> here.” In Manuscript T, **living** seems to be an explanation of the word **remaining**, rather than a different reading.

104 NOTES ON MARINUS.

But let us read what our author described from Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism, a school of philosophy that heavily influenced Marinus and Proclus.</note>, Ennead I. 2. 2:
“The political virtues, as we said above, truly bring order and make us better, by limiting and measuring desires and measuring all passions in general, and removing false opinions.” <note>original: αἱ μὲν τοίνυν πολιτικαὶ ἀρεταὶ, ἃς ἄνω που εἴπομεν, κατακοσμοῦσι μὲν ὄντως καὶ ἀμείνους ποιοῦσι, ὁρίζουσαι καὶ μετροῦσαι τὰς ἐπιθυμίας καὶ ὅλως τὰ πάθη μετροῦσαι καὶ ψευδεῖς δόξας ἀφαιροῦσαι.</note> I believe that Plotinus’s word “truly” <term>men ontos: a Greek phrase emphasizing reality</term> has turned into “remaining” in the manuscript, and then “still” was added for clarification.

Page 43. **“And these indeed”** [Greek: *hai de ge*] — Manuscript T is without the word “indeed.” Chapter 22: “The things which are indeed contrary.” See Schaefer on Dionysius, page 115. Soon T has “purifying,” P has “separating and releasing.” C has “separating.”

Page 43. **“of the truly lead weights of generation”** — The manuscripts C, T, and V have “lead weights” <note>original: μολυβδίδων</note>, which I have accepted in place of the previously published “leaden.” L has “lead weights” <note>variant spelling</note>. G has “lead weights,” and in the margin “lead weights.” A poet quoted in Plutarch (volume 2, page 75 B) says:
“Vice, having cast around its
Lead weight like a net, pulled [the soul] down.”

In a philosophical sense, “lineage” or “generation” <term>genesis: the material world of birth, change, and decay</term> are taken to mean matter and earthly vices. Proclus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher and the subject of the biography by Marinus being annotated here.</note> in his *Hymn to Hecate and Janus*, verse 9:
“I shall look upon the precious light,
from which it is possible to flee the evil of dark lineage.”

Damascius in Photius, page 1029, 33: “He asked for deliverance from the flooding channel of generation.” The same author, page 1049, 7: “In the nature of Heraiscus the philosopher, there was something that removed the pollutions of generation.” Our author in chapter 22: “purified and rising above generation.” Compare Warren on Hierocles, page 254; Jacobs on Proclus’s *Hymn to the Muses*, verses 8 and 14; Mitscherlich on the cited passage of Proclus; and Fabricius in the introduction, not far from the end.

Page 43. **“flight from things here”** — CTV have “flights.” Plotinus, Ennead I. 2. 3: “Plato says that becoming like God is a flight from things here.” The same author, Ennead I. 2. 1: “Since evils are here and necessarily haunt this place, and the soul wants to flee evils, one must flee from here. What then is this flight? He says it is to become like God.” Compare the same author, Ennead I. 8. 6.

Page 43. **“they arrive”** — Manuscript C has “they arrive” <note>variant spelling</note>. T has “they surpass.”

Page 43. **“by day”** — Fabricius correctly edited it thus once. The Hamburg edition has “during the days.” See above, page 68.

Page 43. **“averting rites”** <note>original: ἀποτροπαῖς (apotropais): rituals intended to turn away evil or bad luck</note> — “By fasting or abstinence from certain foods, chaste prayers, sprinkling, washing, etc. These sacred purifications <note>original: καθάρσια (katharsia)</note> we have illustrated elsewhere by custom.” — FABRICIUS.

Page 43. **“lustral vessels”** <note>original: περιῤῥαντηρίοις (perirranteriois): basins used for ritual sprinkling and purification</note> — Lucian, *The False Prophet*, 23: “How many lustral vessels, how many rivers are needed?” The word is illustrated...

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the work of Marinus, likely his biography of the philosopher Proclus. The editor is comparing various manuscript readings (identified by shorthand like C, T, G, and L) and printed editions (such as the Hamburg or Lyon versions) to establish the most accurate text.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 105<-

By Brodaeus in *Miscellany* Book I, chapter 8. <note>Jean Brodeau (1500–1563) was a French humanistic scholar.</note> Compare Plato's *Cratylus*, page 405 B.

**Page 43. at one time — at another time** <note>original: "ὁτὲ — ὁτὲ"</note>] Xylander and the Lyon edition <note>Xylander was the pen name of Wilhelm Holtzman, a 16th-century classical scholar.</note> read **when — when** <note>original: "ὅτε — ὅτε"</note>, as does manuscript G. But manuscripts C and T and the Hamburg edition read **at one time — at another time** <note>original: "ὁτὲ — ὁτὲ"</note>, as does Fabricius. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius was a famous classical bibliographer and editor.</note> Compare above, page 95.

**Page 44. and to the sea** <note>original: "ἐπὶ θάλαττάν τε"</note>] The Hamburg edition reads **but/and** <note>original: "δέ"</note>.

**Page 44. twice or even thrice** <note>original: "δὶς ἢ καὶ τρὶς"</note>] I have added **or** <note>original: "ἢ"</note> from manuscripts C, T, G, and L. See above in chapter 3: **only twice or even thrice** <note>original: "δὶς μόνον ἢ καὶ τρὶς"</note>.

**Page 44. not only when his age was in its prime** <note>original: "οὐκ ἀκμαζούσης μόνον αὐτῷ τῆς ἡλικίας"</note>] Manuscripts L and G read **not only in his prime** <note>original: "οὐ μόνον ἀκμαζ."</note>. Manuscripts C and T read **not only his prime** <note>original: "οὐ μόνον ἀκμαζ. αὐτοῦ"</note>.

**Page 44. and now at the very sunset of his life** <note>original: "αὐταῖς δὲ ἤδη ταῖς δυσμαῖς τοῦ βίου"</note>] I have added **and** <note>original: "δὲ"</note> from manuscripts L, G, and C. — Hegesander, cited in Athenaeus' *The Banquet of the Learned*, Book 13, chapter 61, says of the playwright Sophocles: **being at the sunset of his life** <note>original: "ἐπὶ δὲ δυσμαῖς ὢν τοῦ βίου"</note>. Procopius in his *Secret History*, page 29: **and she herself, along with Justin, held the sovereignty at the sunset of life...** <note>original: "καὶ αὐτὴ μὲν ξὺν Ἰουστίνῳ ἐπὶ βίου δυσμαῖς τὴν βασιλεία"</note>

<summary>This page contains technical editorial notes comparing different manuscript versions of Marinus's writing, specifically focusing on precise word choices regarding time and the poetic description of old age as the "sunset of life."</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, philology, Greek manuscripts, Sophocles, Procopius, sunset of life, textual criticism</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
106 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;NOTES ON MARINUS.

Theodore Canterus, in his *Various Readings* (Book II, Chapter 21), where he discusses the <term>Castus of Ceres</term> <note>The *Castus* was a period of ritual abstinence or fasting, particularly associated with the goddess Ceres (Demeter) during her festivals</note> at length, uses this passage of our author and writes: "the zealously observed days of <term>kasteia</term>" <note>original: σπουδασθείσας ἡμέρας καστείας; 'days of ritual purity'</note>, having adopted this useless addition from I know not where. In manuscript T <note>The *Taurinensis* manuscript from Turin</note>, the word was entirely omitted. Fabricius, rather than correcting Marinus by using Suidas <note>The *Suda* or Suidas is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world</note>, wants to emend Suidas based on Marinus; Kuster followed the same reasoning, preferring the word <term>hagisteias</term> <note>original: ἁγιστείας; 'holiness' or 'sacred rites'</note>. "It should be written thus from this passage," says Fabricius, "in Suidas under the entry for <term>metrōakai</term> <note>original: μητρωακαί; relating to the festivals of the Great Mother of the Gods</note>, where <term>kasteias</term> is incorrectly read instead of <term>hagisteias</term>. Concerning the Mother of the Gods and her sacred rites brought from Pessinus in Phrygia to Rome, the matter is well known from Livy [Book XXIX, chapter 10 and following] and the writers of Roman Antiquities [Rosinus, Book II, chapter 4]." I am indeed aware that Porphyry, in his *Life of Pythagoras* (page 9), wrote: "concerning the <term>sacred rites</term> <note>original: ἁγιστείας</note> of the gods and the rest of the practices concerning life, he says he both heard and received them from the Magi." Rittershusius illustrates this word in that context; but in our author [Marinus], I preferred the Latin-derived word <note>Referring to *kasteia*, derived from the Latin *castus*</note> provided by the best manuscripts—the Laurentianus and Coislinianus—as well as the one by Canterus and Suidas, which the Turin manuscript almost confirms by its silence.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 45. **Among the Egyptians, unlucky days** <note>original: παρ' Αἰγυπτίοις δὲ ἀποφράδας</note>] "Egyptian days were judged unlucky by Petosiris and other Egyptian astrologers; on these days, it is not helpful to begin buildings nor any works of this kind. See Augustine in his *Letter to the Galatians*, chapter 4; Lilius Gyraldus in the *Fasti*; and Friedrich Lindenbrog on Book XXVIII [page 184, where a notable passage of Augustine is read] of Ammianus Marcellinus; and Salmasius's *On Climacteric Years*." — FABRICIUS. — Compare Suidas under the entry <term>apophrades</term> <note>original: ἀποφράδες; 'unlucky', 'forbidden', or 'inauspicious' days</note>, and the notes to Gregory of Corinth, page 525 of Schaefer’s edition. The Lexicon of Manuscript 1720 defines <term>apophrades</term> as "the forbidden days among the Athenians." Phrynichus, in the unpublished *Sophistic Preparation*: "An <term>apophras</term> man; <term>apophrades</term> days, during which it was forbidden to do anything. It therefore signifies a man who is, as it were, unlucky, out of place, and accursed."

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;— *An unlucky man met me as I was going out,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Looking like the very image of untrustworthiness.*

From this passage Aelian borrows light in his *Letter 15*: "You differ in no way from the unlucky days." ¹)

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 45. **appearances** <note>original: ἐπιφανείας</note>] "By <term>epiphaneia</term> <note>original: ἐπιφάνεια; 'manifestation' or 'visibility'</note> understand in this place the <term>schemata</term> <note>original: σχήματα; 'shapes' or 'configurations'</note> or appearances of the moon." — FABRICIUS.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;1) In this Letter of Aelian, the words: "and this same god will be for you both a <term>Paean</term> <note>A title for Apollo as a healer</note> and will release you from your undiluted bile, having quenched the kindling of your anger with wine," were wrongly attributed by Wagner (page 228) to the fragments of Alciphron.

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 107<-

**Page 45. For every old and new [day]** <note>original: "Πᾶσαν γὰρ ἔνην καὶ νέαν"</note>] The editions read *ennēn* <note>original: "ἔννην"</note>, which doubling of the "n" arose from a misunderstood stroke of the pen. In manuscript C, one might believe *ennēn* was written, but that would be an error. — A Scholium <term>Scholium: an ancient marginal commentary or explanatory note</term> in the Laurentian manuscript states: "the last day of the month, or the day before the first, the Athenians called the **old and new**." <note>original: "τὴν τελευταίαν τοῦ μηνὸς ἡμέραν, ἤτοι τὴν πρὸ τῆς πρώτης ἔνην καὶ νέαν ἐκάλουν Ἀθηναῖοι."</note> — "Compare Proclus's commentary on the verse of Hesiod [Hu. 6. Brunck. 715.] which is also cited by Eusebius, in book 13 of the *Preparation for the Gospel*:

> "First the old, then the fourth and the seventh, a holy day." <note>original: "Πρῶτον ἔνη τετράς τε καὶ ἑβδόμη, ἱερὸν ἦμαρ."</note>

**Old and new** is the thirtieth or last day of any month, the interlunary time <note>the dark period between the old moon and the new moon</note>. It is called **old and new** because it becomes a sort of boundary between the waning and the reborn moon, 'since it happens that on this day the moon ceases and begins to be,' as the Scholiast on Aristophanes's *Clouds* says [v. 1132]." — FABRICIUS. Compare the *Lexicon* of Timaeus and Ruhnken’s notes there on page 103.

**Page 45. of the new moon** <note>original: "νουμηνίας"</note>] Porphyry in the *Life of Plotinus*, chapter 10: "Amelius being a lover of sacrifice, he attended the rituals on the **new moon** and went around to all the festivals." <note>original: "φιλοθύτου δὲ γεγονότος τοῦ Ἀμελίου, καὶ τὰ ἱερὰ κατὰ νουμηνίαν, καὶ τὰς ἑορτὰς ἐκπεριϊόντος"</note> On this, Fabricius notes: "John Meursius in *Greek Festivals* showed that New Moons were sacred not only to the Jews, but also to the Greeks. Marinus also reports this about Proclus, among other things, etc." — Dio Cassius, fragment 120: "While the Romans were performing the inaugural sacrifices at the start of the year, and the magistrates were performing the rites according to ancestral customs <note>The Greek text here is corrupted.</note>." Valesius saw that "magistracy" <note>original: "ἡγεμονίᾳ"</note> was a corruption, but he did not find the cure, and instead corrupted it further by proposing "first fruits" <note>original: "ἀπαρχάς"</note>. Dio himself, in book 45, page 432, indicated the remedy: "When Vionius was performing the inaugural sacrifices **on the new moon**." For "new moon" <note>original: "νουμηνίᾳ"</note> should be restored in place of "magistracy" <note>original: "ἡγεμονίᾳ"</note>. Lucian in *The False Prophet*, chapter 8: "It was the beginning of the year, or rather the third day after the great **new moon**, on which the Romans, according to ancient custom, offer certain prayers for the whole year and perform sacrifices."

**Page 46. festivals, and the — ancestral [customs]** <note>original: "ἑορτὰς, καὶ τὰ — πάτρια"</note>] The editions read "according to the" <note>original: "κατὰ τά"</note>. I preferred the reading of Manuscripts L, G, and C, supported by T, which reads "and the" <note>original: "καὶ τάς"</note>. He will say shortly after: "that it befits the philosopher not to be a worshiper of one single city or of the ancestral customs of only a few." Everyone knows how easily "and" <note>original: "καὶ"</note> and "according to" <note>original: "κατὰ"</note> are swapped. See Schaefer’s *Miscellaneous Notes*, page 47.

**Page 46. of hymns** <note>original: "ὕμνων"</note>] "From this passage of Marinus, it appears that very many of Proclus's hymns have perished. Furthermore, one may conjecture how elegant and full of ancient theology they were from the few that survive; however, none of those remaining are dedicated to any god of the Syrians, Arabs, or Phoenicians."

**FABRICIUS** — Concerning the *Hymns of Proclus* <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a major Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote hymns to various gods</note>, compare Jacobs, *Anthology* vol. 10, p. 272, and our own work below in chapter 26.

**Page 46.** *...containing praises not only of those honored among the Greeks* <note>original Greek: οὐ τῶν παρὰ τοῖς Ἕλλησι μόνον τιμηθέντων ἐγκώμια περιέχουσα</note>] I preferred the word "only" <note>original: μόνον</note> from manuscripts C and T, rather than the published "of the only ones" <note>original: μόνων</note>. Manuscript T reads "placing" <note>original: τιθέντων</note>. Manuscripts C and T read "providing" <note>original: παρέχουσα</note>.

**Page 46. Marnas**] “Concerning Marnas, the deity of Gaza in Palestine, who is the same as Jupiter <note>The Roman king of the gods, identified here with a local Philistine/Syrian deity</note>, one should consult—besides Selden’s excellent book *On the Syrian Gods*—Salmasius’s notes on Solinus and Lampridius’s *Life of Alexander Severus* [p. 202 E.], where Septimius Arabinus exclaims: ‘O Marnas, O Jupiter, O immortal Gods!’ An old edition ridiculously reads ‘O Maria’; others read ‘O deities.’ See also what learned men note regarding Stephanus of Byzantium under the entry ‘Gaza,’ Scaliger on Eusebius and on the fragments of ancient historians, Ezechiel Spanheim’s *On the Use and Excellence of Coins*, Bochart’s *Sacred Geography*, and especially Noris’s fifth dissertation *On the Eras of the Syro-Macedonians*.” **FABRICIUS**. Salmasius cites Jerome in the *Life of Serapion*; but it should actually be written as the *Life of Hilarion*. Jerome says in this book: “...against the magistrate of Gaza, who was devoted to the idol of Marnas.” And not far from there: “The cry of the common people became so great that even the pagans themselves shouted together: ‘Marnas has been conquered by Christ!’”

**Page 46. Asclepius the Lion-Bearer** <note>original: Ἀσκληπιὸν Λεοντοῦχον; Asclepius was the Greek god of medicine</note>] I do not know if this passage can be clarified by these words of Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine polymath</note> regarding the *Oracles*, p. 77: “Among the twelve signs in the heavens, there is one called the Lion, which is said to be the house of the Sun; the Chaldean <note>Refers to the Chaldean Oracles, a set of mystical Neoplatonic texts</note> calls the source, or the cause of the lion-like arrangement of the stars, the ‘Lion-holder.’” And he adds things which I will transcribe here, though they are useless to our current matter, so that I may correct them: “In the rites, he says, if you call upon such a source by name, you will see nothing else in the sky than a lion-like phantom; for the curved mass of the sky <note>The manuscript originally said 'human' (anthrōpou), which the translator corrects to 'sky' (ouranou)</note>—that is, the surrounding vault—will not appear to you.” Dio Chrysostom, *Oration* 63, p. 325: “It seems to me that men also have fortune whenever it is clear, and not dark.” Manuscript 2958 reads “sky” <note>original: οὐνὸς, an abbreviation for οὐρανὸς</note>, which Reiske had correctly guessed. Compare Schaefer on Dionysius, p. 173. In Lucian’s *Timon*, chapter 33, I believe the words “himself” and “sky” have been confused: “he continued strong in mind, living the life of a man and looking toward himself.” Perhaps it should be “looking toward the sky.”

**Page 47. Thyandrites**] This is the reading of the manuscripts and editors before Fabricius, who wrote **Theandrites** <note>Meaning 'God-man'</note>. “I read it thus,” he says, “not Thyandrites, based on Damascius <note>The last head of the Neoplatonist Academy in Athens</note> in the writings of Photius; consult p. 565 of Hoeschel’s edition.” Here are the words of Damascius:
