NOTES ON MARINUS. 109

p. 1061, 3. <note>The author is commenting on a specific passage in a biography of the philosopher Proclus written by his student Marinus.</note> **"And there (in Bostra in Arabia) he came to know Theandrites <note>A local Arab deity often identified with Ares or the Sun</note>, a god of masculine appearance who inspires a virile <note>literally: "not-female"</note> life in souls."** In the case of a name so obscure, I preferred to follow my own manuscripts. <note>original Latin: "In nomine tam ignoto malui Codices meos ſequi."</note>

P. 47. **"Isis, who is still honored at Philae <note>An island in the Nile, the site of a famous temple complex</note>"**] The Laurentian Greek manuscript <note>original: "LG."</note> reads "equal" <note>original: "ἴσον"</note>. Similarly, in Heliodorus Book I, chapter 30, the Venetian Codex 409 has "equal" <note>original: "ἴσην"</note> instead of "Isis" <note>original: "Ἴσιν"</note>. — The CT manuscript reads "is censured" <note>original: "ἐπιτιμωμένην"</note>. Compare what was said above on page 86. — "See Diodorus Siculus, Book I, chapter 22 [where Wesseling comments], and what learned men wrote regarding Lucan, Book X, line 313. See also Henri Valois in the excerpts edited by Hoeschel regarding the *Embassies*, page 208." FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, a famous 18th-century scholar</note> Compare Coray’s notes on Heliodorus, page 256. Add what the most learned man Étienne Quatremère—to whom Oriental literature owes and will owe so much—most recently wrote about this island in his excellent work, *Geographical and Historical Memoirs on Egypt*, volume 1, page 380 and following.

P. 47. **"ready"** <note>original: "πρόχειρον"</note>] I recall a passage of Plato <note>likely referring to the comic poet Plato Comicus</note> in Athenaeus, Book XV, chapter 1, edited thus:
->The flutes should have been **ready** <note>original: "προχείρους"</note> for the girl by now.<-
For the sake of the meter, I would prefer the phrase "at hand" <note>original: "πρὸ χειρὸς εἶναι"</note>. From the phrase "before the hand" (*pro cheiros*) the adjective "ready" (*procheiros*) was formed, just as from "before the city" (*pro poleōs*) comes the adjective *propoleos* in Suidas <note>An 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>, a passage which Kuster unsuccessfully tried to alter, as I shall show below.

P. 47. **"of the whole world"**] "Proclus therefore thought that so many and such diverse sacred rites were not displeasing to God, but that this variety was especially pleasing to Him. This was also declared by Themistius in his *Seventh Oration* to Valens and his *Twelfth* to Jovian, teaching that God is delighted by the variety of opinions concerning divine matters, so that individuals might more greatly revere His majesty, or perhaps because it is difficult to truly know Him. Compare Socrates' *Ecclesiastical History*, Book IV, chapter 32. *Perhaps indeed such variety, by God’s arrangement, produces a certain wonderful beauty in the universe*, says Marsilio Ficino <note>A famous Renaissance Platonist</note> in his book *On the Truth of the Christian Religion*, chapter IV. But there seems also to have been another reason why not only individual philosophers, but also Athens and Rome—which Arnobius calls the 'worshiper of all divinities'—received such varied sacred rites. They hoped that the more rites they bound themselves to, the more they would be approved by God: specifically, the more rites they performed, the more they thought they were appeasing the intermediate gods. They did not doubt that there was an innumerable number and many kinds of these spirits beneath the one supreme God, the God of Gods, who is to be worshiped with a pure mind rather than with external ceremonies. See Celsus in..."

->110 NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

“...Origen <note>A prominent early Christian theologian (c. 184–253 AD).</note> in book 8, page 381: Hence among the Athenians there were so many temples of the Gods, so many altars, even to **unknown gods**, and it was a crime in Athens to slander any foreign God. Philostratus <note>Author of "Lives of the Sophists."</note> in book VI, chapter 2 [section 3, page 232], and following him, Suidas <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> in the entry for *Timasion*: 'For it is more prudent to speak well of all gods, and this especially at Athens, where altars even to **unknown spirits** <note>original: "agnoston daimonon"</note> are established.'

And while throughout the entire empire, provinces, and towns, individual peoples had their own local sacred rites and worshipped their own municipal Gods, the Romans worshipped all of them. They took up the sacred rites of all nations and were initiated into the rituals of every religion, as Caecilius says in Minucius Felix's *Octavius* [chapter 6]. Furthermore, if you ask why only the sacred rites of the Jews and Christians were neglected and ordered to be abolished by the Romans, the answer can be given that the reception of even these was occasionally considered—as under the emperors Tiberius, Hadrian, and Alexander Severus. However, they later thought they could not observe the rites of that God who wished to be worshipped alone without a rival, and who not only despised the rites of other Gods but commanded them to be loathed as impious; this is the cause suggested by Augustine.”
FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), the famous German classical scholar and bibliographer.</note>

**Page 48. But as for pains — by not suffering along with it in the best part of himself]** I wrote *heauto* <note>to himself</note> instead of *en auto* <note>in himself</note> from the CT <note>Referring to specific manuscript codices.</note>. I ought to have written *tō* <note>the (dative)</note> instead of *to* <note>the (accusative)</note> based on a conjecture which Plotinus <note>The founder of Neoplatonism (c. 204–270 AD).</note> confirms. The manuscripts LGCT omit the second “the.” Plotinus in *Enneads* I. 2. 5: “...removing pains, and if that is not possible, bearing them mildly and making them less by **not suffering along with them**.” <note>The concept here is "apatheia," where the higher soul remains detached from the body's pain.</note>

**Page 48. the resolve of the soul]** Longinus <note>Greek author of the treatise "On the Sublime."</note> in chapter 9: “...as if to make them always pregnant with a noble **resolve**.” <term>parastema</term> <note>This term refers to a person’s mental "stature," dignity, or courageous resolve.</note> Compare Ruhnken and Toup on chapter 7, section 2, and Graevius on Lucian’s *The Mistaken Critic*, chapter 5, volume 9, page 465. Suidas in the entry for *Lapsonte*s: “For those who are filled with blood and rushing because of thirst have their **resolve** <note>original: "parastema"</note> in the spring”; that is, their mind. Compare Toup’s *Emendations*, volume 1, page 380.

**Page 48. and he showed]** I added the conjunction from the manuscripts LGCT. Soon after, I provided “for” <note>original: "gar"</note> from the same. Further down, instead of “it happened” <note>original: "egigneto"</note>, the T manuscript uses the spelling *egineto*.

**Page 49. of the paralysis]** Concerning the <term>paresis</term> <note>A term for partial paralysis or weakening of the limbs.</note> of Proclus’s limbs, see below in chapters 26 and 31.

**Page 49. sometimes we read these while present]** From the manuscripts LG instead of “we read.” — “The translator incorrectly read *hotè* <note>at one time</note> and translated it: 'for these things too we sometimes recited when present.' Furthermore, it was a custom of the Pythagoreans to drive away diseases of the mind and body by singing hymns. See Iamblichus, *On the Life of Pythagoras*, chapter 25.” FABRICIUS. Compare Price on Apuleius’s *Apology*, page 105. For the rest, Fabricius does not deservedly start a quarrel with the translator:

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 111

The translation which he criticizes is the best, and should by no means be changed. *Estin hote* <note>original: Ἔστιν ὅτε</note> means *at times* or *sometimes* <note>original: aliquando, nonnunquam</note>. Compare Bos's *Ellipses* under the entry for *chronos* <note>original: χρόνος, "time"</note>. — Shortly after, instead of *alla eti* <note>but still</note>, I wrote *all'* <note>but</note> following the manuscripts LGCT.

**Page 50. *episymbainonta* <note>original: ἐπισυμβαίνοντα, "things happening accidentally"</note>]** Manuscript T reads *epibainonta*.

**Page 50. *eiōthota* <note>original: εἰωθότα, "customary things"</note>]** The published editions read: "of these things happening, such are these things, these are customary." I have punctuated this as Fabricius had proposed. "It could," he says, "also be punctuated thus: 'so as to say regarding each of the occurrences: these are such things, these are customary things.' This is a remarkable example of a mind unshaken and immovable regardless of whatever fate may bring. Thus the Hebrews report concerning a certain Teacher Nahum—who for that reason is called *Gamzu* <note>A nickname derived from the Hebrew phrase below</note> among them—that he was accustomed to say regarding everything that happened to him: *gam zu* <note>original: גם זו</note>, 'even this will turn out for my good.'" <note>Referring to the Talmudic sage Nahum of Gamzu, famous for his optimism and the phrase "Gam zu l'tovah."</note> — FABRICIUS.

**Page 50. *Hoper emoi edokei apophthegma* <note>original: Ὅπερ ἐμοὶ ἐδόκει ἀπόφθεγμα, "Which seemed to me a pithy saying"</note>]** The editions read: *which seems to me, or rather seemed, a pithy saying*. Since I did not perceive the point of this clever refinement, I accepted the simpler and, as I think, truer reading of the manuscripts LGC. In T it is similar, except that instead of *hoper* <note>which</note>, it is *hōsper* <note>just as</note>.

**Page 50. *tēn logikēn einai psychēn* <note>original: τὴν λογικὴν εἶναι ψυχὴν, "that it is the rational soul"</note>]** Manuscripts LGCTV omit the word *psychēn* <note>soul</note>. But I wished to keep it. Below in chapter 24, we find the phrase: "of the rational soul alone." In this whole passage, our author [Marinus] closely follows Plotinus in *Enneads* I. 2. 5: "taking away anger as much as possible, and if possible, entirely; but if not, at least not joining in the anger itself, but letting the involuntary impulse belong to another <note>the lower or animal nature</note>, and letting that involuntary part be small and weak."

**Page 50. *Aphrodisiōn* <note>original: Ἀφροδισίων, "sexual pleasures"</note>]** And these things our author took from Plotinus in the same passage: "the soul itself will not have desire... nor for sexual pleasures; or if it does, they will be natural, I think, and without even the involuntary impulse; or if there is any, only as far as a sudden, fleeting imagination."

**Page 51. *heautēn synagousa kai athroizousa pros heautēn* <note>original: ἑαυτὴν συνάγουσα καὶ ἀθροίζουσα πρὸς ἑαυτὴν, "collecting and gathering herself to herself"</note>]** Damascius in Suidas's entry on *Sarapiōn*: "always collecting and drawing himself together, as much as possible, toward the interior and the more indivisible part." Plotinus *Enneads* I. 2. 5, regarding the soul: "from the body, perhaps even in terms of places, collecting toward herself."

**Page 51. *dokousa* <note>original: δοκοῦσα, "seeming"</note>]** Manuscript C incorrectly reads *dokousan*.

**Page 51. *estraphthai* <note>original: ἐστράφθαι, "to have been turned"</note>]** Manuscript V reads *estrōphthai*. In Chapter 25: "the inward turning of the soul toward the Intellect." Plotinus *Enneads* I. 2. 6: "and temperance is the inward turning toward the Intellect." — Immediately after *mēdamou* <note>nowhere</note>, I deleted the word *de* <note>and/but</note>, following manuscripts CT. Soon after, instead of *syngignesthai* <note>to associate with</note>, LGC read *gignesthai*, and T reads *ginesthai* <note>to become</note>.

<summary>This page consists of scholarly notes on Marinus’s biography of Proclus, documenting manuscript variations and proving that Marinus’s descriptions of the soul’s virtues are direct adaptations of Plotinus’s Enneads.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, Plotinus, Enneads, philology, textual criticism, Fabricius, Nahum of Gamzu, rational soul, Neoplatonism, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
112          NOTES ON MARINUS.

P. 51. "entirely and — to be unaffected" <note>original: *pantē de — apathein*</note>] The manuscripts L, G, and C read "unaffected" <note>original: *apathē*</note>, as does manuscript T, which furthermore omits the word "and" <note>original: *de*</note>.

P. 52. "From indeed such a..." <note>original: *Ek dē tēs toiautēs*</note>] The editions by Xylander, the Hamburg edition, and the Leiden edition use "and" <note>original: *de*</note>. Fabricius and the Manuscripts, except for manuscript T, correctly use "indeed" <note>original: *dē*</note>. „Thus far the earlier editions. What follows now sees the light for the first time." — FABRICIUS. The Vatican Manuscript also goes only this far.

P. 52. "absolutely and easily" <note>original: *apolytōs kai euēniōs*</note>] Ruhnken, in his notes on Timaeus, page 125, reads "without pain" <note>original: *alypōs*</note> instead of "absolutely," which is an ingenious, learned, and perhaps true suggestion.

P. 52. "above these he ascended" <note>original: *hyper tautas anetreche*</note>] This is the reading in manuscripts L, G, C, and T; it is not "theurgical" <note>original: *telestikas*</note>, which Fabricius wrote without authority and—what is most reprehensible—without notifying the reader. I have used "ascended" <note>original: *anetreche*</note> from manuscript T instead of "ascended" <note>original: *anetrechen*</note>. „He ascended to the greater and even to virtues greater than these."

P. 52. "the wand-bearers" <note>original: *narthēkophorōn*</note>] „A well-known proverb: *Many indeed are the wand-bearers* etc., regarding which Erasmus [I. 7. 6.] noted a passage in Plutarch's *Against Colotes*, where he calls Aristodemus of Aegium *not a wand-bearer from the Academy, but a most frenzied devotee of Plato.* This agrees with the praise Marinus heaps upon Proclus here. On the other hand, not only among orators but also among philosophers there are those who, *seeming to themselves to be inspired in many places, are not in a divine frenzy, but are merely playing,* as Longinus says in the treatise *On the Sublime* [chapter 3]." — FABRICIUS. From the *Greek Anthology*, Anonymous Epigram 25: *Many indeed are the wand-bearers, but few are the true Bacchic revelers.* Vatican Manuscript 100 (now in Paris) reads: *Many, you know...* along with this scholion <note>an ancient marginal note</note>: "The term *Bacchus* is an epithet of Dionysus and his followers." Compare the most learned Jacobs on this epigram, Fischer on Plato's *Phaedo* page 290, and Wyttenbach on the same, page 173. Themistius, *Oration* 21, page 254 A: *No wise man is ill-disposed toward his equal, but the mysteries do not allow one who is not lawfully initiated to be considered a wand-bearing Bacchic worshipper.* In the Valerian manuscript, the word "not" does not appear.

P. 52. "was a reveler" <note>original: *ebakcheue*</note>] The reading in manuscripts C and T instead of "was a reveler" <note>original: *ebakcheuen*</note>. Shortly after, I have provided "he became" <note>original: *egineto*</note> from manuscripts G, C, and T instead of Fabricius’s "he became" <note>original: *egeneto*</note>.

P. 53. "just as by sight, by the simple applications of intellectual activity" <note>original: *hōsper de opsei, haplais epibolais tēs noeras energeias*</note>] Fabricius, without any sense that I can perceive and against the manuscripts—and without warning the reader—published "a simple application" <note>original: *haplēn epibolēn*</note>. I restore the reading of the manuscripts; but I myself have changed "by intellectual activities" <note>original: *tais noerais energeiais*</note>, which the manuscripts have (except for G, which has "activities" <note>original: *energeiai*</note>), to the singular. Compare Chapter 23: *...of the mind that provides for all things at once and through a single application.*

The notes focus on correcting Greek terms related to Neoplatonic virtues and the "wand-bearer" proverb regarding true vs. false philosophers.</summary>

### NOTES ON MARINUS. 113

**P. 53. in the divine mind** <note>original: ἐν τῷ θείῳ νῷ</note>] Thus in manuscript T, which I have chosen to follow. Other manuscripts and Fabricius read **in the God mind** <note>original: ἐν τῷ θεῷ νῷ</note>. Perhaps it should be **in God and mind** <note>original: ἐν τῷ θεῷ καὶ νῷ</note>: as in chapter 24: **to act toward the mind and God** <note>original: τὸ πρὸς τὸν νοῦν καὶ θεὸν ἐνεργεῖν</note>.

**P. 53. no longer would** <note>original: οὐκ ἔτ’ ἂν</note>] Fabricius reads **still** <note>original: ἔτι</note>. I have restored the reading of manuscripts L and G. In C and T, it is **not would** <note>original: οὐκ ἂν</note>.

**P. 53. mythical fictions** <note>original: μυθικοῖς πλάσμασιν</note>] Thus Proclus in his commentary on the *Cratylus* <note>A dialogue by Plato regarding the origin of names</note>, chapter 103, at the end: **to trace mythical fictions back to the truth**. Photius, *Library* <note>A 9th-century collection of book summaries</note>, volume 129: **the account of each is full of mythical fictions**. Wyttenbach, in his notes on Julian’s *Oration I*, page 11 D, showed how frequently the words "myth" and "fiction" <note>original: μῦθος and πλάσμα</note> are joined. Longinus, fragment 11: **rather it belongs to the poetic art, which uses many passions and expressions, and indeed myths and fictions**. Agathias, *Epigram* 35: 
**who, speaking timely truths with wise myths and fictions.**
Procopius of Gaza, in Villoison’s *Diatribe*, page 44: **this seemed to me otherwise to be myth and fiction**. Gregory of Nyssa, *Life of Gregory Thaumaturgus*, page 237, Vossius edition: **but for all others, ancestral traditions are myths and fictions**. Photius, *Library*, volume 166: **in such great fictions and myth-makings**. The same author, volume 94, page 235: joins **playful things** with **fictions**.

**P. 53. and he perceived** <note>original: κατεῖδέ τε</note>] Manuscripts C and T, not **and he perceived** <note>original: κατεῖδέν τε; a minor grammatical variation</note>. — Soon after, I deleted **and** <note>original: τε</note> after **to those able** <note>original: δυναμένοις</note>, following the lead of the same manuscripts.

**P. 53. into harmony** <note>original: εἰς συμφωνίαν</note>] “Suidas <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> mentions among the writings of Proclus a **Harmony of Orpheus, Pythagoras, and Plato**. See what we said in the list of Proclus’s writings, number 17.” — FABRICIUS <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, a renowned 18th-century classical scholar</note>.

**P. 53. writings** <note>original: συγγράμμασιν</note>] Manuscripts C and T read **letters/writings** <note>original: γράμμασιν</note>.

**P. 53. fruitful** <note>original: γόνιμον</note>] Thus in the manuscripts. Fabricius reads **noble** <note>original: γεννικόν</note>. — Soon after, instead of **he adopted** <note>original: εἰσεποιεῖτο</note>, manuscripts C and T read **he made** <note>original: ποιεῖτο</note>.

**P. 54. wind-filled** <note>meaning "empty" or "vain," like a wind-egg; original: ἀνεμιαῖον</note>] Fabricius reads **dissimilar** <note>original: ἀνομοιαῖον</note>, which he translates as "diverse from these and disagreeing." However, manuscript G, like the other manuscripts, has the word most clearly and correctly as **wind-filled**, which Wyttenbach already advised should be restored in his *Critical Epistle*, page 56. He points out at the same time the words of Plato which Marinus is imitating, from the *Theaetetus*, section 23, page 151 E: **but come now, let us examine it together to see whether it happens to be fruitful or wind-filled**. See Fischer there, and Wyttenbach in the *Bibliotheca*, volume III, part 1, page 27, as already noted by Heindorf; compare also Wagner on Alciphron, volume 1, page 295.

**P. 54. he refuted** <note>original: διήλεγχεν</note>] Thus in LGCT, not **he refuted** <note>original: διήλεγχε</note>.

**P. 54. each thing — laying down** <note>original: ἕκαστα — καταβαλλόμενος</note>] These words, which are present in all the manuscripts, were omitted by Fabricius. A <term>homoeoteleuton</term> <note>A scribal error where the eye skips from one word to another with a similar ending, causing the text in between to be lost</note> led that most learned editor into error; is it any wonder if unskilled copyists have erred so often?

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations on the text of Marinus, specifically debating manuscript variants and the use of the term "mythical fictions" across various classical and Byzantine authors. It also corrects an error in a previous edition by Fabricius caused by a common scribal mistake.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, Fabricius, Wyttenbach, manuscript variants, mythical fictions, Plato's Theaetetus, homoeoteleuton, philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critical commentary on the "Life of Proclus" by Marinus. The previous pages likely dealt with the earlier chapters of the biography, while this page focuses on textual variants and historical customs mentioned in chapters 3 through 29 of the work.</meta>

114 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;NOTES ON MARINUS.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 54. **with immeasurable industry** <note>original: "φιλοπονίᾳ γὰρ ἀμέτρῳ"</note>] "See above p. 6." FABRICIUS. Chapter 3.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 54. **he explained — the lectures.** <note>original: "ἐξηγεῖτο — πράξεις"</note>] "I have rendered [this as], *he completed the lessons.* For the word *praxeis* <note>The term <term>praxeis</term> (literally "actions" or "deeds") refers here to the specific units or "lectures" into which a philosophical text was divided for teaching purposes.</note> is understood in this way above on p. 20. [ch. 9], and in the manuscript of the lectures of Olympiodorus on Plato's *Alcibiades*: compare Lambecius VII. p. 52." FABRICIUS.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 54. **And he was associated with** <note>original: "Συνεγίγνετό τε"</note>] Fabricius [reads] *but also*; but the Manuscripts read *and*.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 54. **the nocturnal [prayer/study]** <note>original: "τὴν νυκτερινὴν"</note>] "Above p. 26." FABRICIUS. Chapter 11.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 55. **the rising sun, and [the sun] at its zenith** <note>original: "ἥλιον ἀνίσχοντα, μεσουρανοῦντά τε"</note>] Fabricius [reads] *sustaining*; but the Manuscripts read *rising*. From the same [Manuscripts] I added *and*.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 55. **For he was the first** <note>original: "Πρῶτος γὰρ οὗτος"</note>] Thus the Manuscripts L, C, and T, not *he himself* <note>original: "αὐτὸς"</note>, as Fabricius had it, who was following manuscript G, in which it is written incorrectly: for "he was the first" [is better than] "he was himself the first."

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 55. **of souls** <note>original: "ψυχῶν"</note>] "See Proclus on the *Republic* p. 399, Greek edition of Basel; *Theological Elements* number 196; Ficino's excerpts from Proclus on the *First Alcibiades* p. 246 ff. etc." FABRICIUS. — Shortly after, I gave **provident** <note>original: "προνοοῦντος"</note> instead of *thinking* <note>original: "νοοῦντος"</note> based on Manuscripts C and T.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 56. **I dwell [on this] at greater length** <note>original: "ἐπὶ πλεῖον μηκύνω"</note>]. Thus C and T. In L and G it is *at more length*. Fabricius reads *at the greater length*. Chapter 29: *wishing to lengthen*.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 56. **each thing); but he who happens upon...** <note>original: "ἕκαστα)· ὁ δὲ ἐντυγχάνων"</note>] From the punctuation and sentence division I have made, a previously obscure passage becomes clear. In my notes on Eunapius p. 213, I showed how aptly the word *but* <note>original: "δὲ"</note>, after a parenthesis, resumes an interrupted sentence. A change of syntax after a parenthesis is not infrequent.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 56. **and he attained the vision of him** <note>original: "ἐπέτυχέ τε τῆς ἐκείνου θέας"</note>] Fabricius reads *and he attained the vision of her* <note>original: "ἐκείνης"</note> with a note: "otherwise *of him*." I have restored *of him* <note>original: "ἐκείνου"</note> as found in Manuscripts C and T.

&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 56. **The festivals of Plato and Socrates** <note>original: "Πλατώνειά τε καὶ Σωκράτεια"</note>] From this it is clear that Ficino <note>Marsilio Ficino, the 15th-century Renaissance humanist.</note> did not speak accurately enough when he said that the birthday banquets for Plato were discontinued after Porphyry. Jonsius <note>Johannes Jonsius, author of *De Scriptoribus Historiae Philosophicae*.</note> made Ficino's error his own, who in *History of Philosophy* I. 11. 6. handed down many details about these banquets. Porphyry, in his *Life of Plotinus* ch. 2, says: *he did not think it right to sacrifice or feast anyone on his own birthdays, although he both sacrificed and feasted his companions on the traditional birthdays of Plato and Socrates, when also those of the companions who were able were required to read a discourse before those assembled.* Porphyry obeyed this rule, and these are his own words in the same work, ch. 15: *while I was reading a poem at the Platonic festivals.* The same is found in Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel* X. 3: *Longinus feasting us at the Plotinian festivals in Athens*; where Jonsius (in the place cited) and Fabricius (in his notes on Porphyry's *Life of Plotinus* 2) wish to change this to *Platonic festivals* <note>original: "Πλατώνεια"</note> without necessity. For from the passage of Porphyry cited above, we see that even while Plotinus was alive, his own birthdays were being celebrated. They used to celebrate the birthdays of Archilochus and Homer on the same day, which is clear from Antipater of Thessalonica.

<summary>This page provides scholarly commentary and textual corrections for Marinus's biography of the Neoplatonist Proclus. It focuses on clarifying Greek terminology and refuting the historical claim that festivals celebrating Plato's birthday had ceased after the 3rd century.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Johann Albert Fabricius, Marsilio Ficino, Neoplatonism, Plotinus, Porphyry, Platoneia, birthdays of philosophers, manuscript variants</keywords>

NOTES ON MARINUS. [space] 115

In epigram 45, which was not altogether correctly understood by a most learned man <note>The "most learned man" likely refers to a previous editor like Fabricius</note>:
**"Today we pour a libation for the day of Archilochus and manly Homer."**
<note>original: "σήμερον Ἀρχιλόχοιο καὶ ἄρσενος ἦμαρ Ὁμήρου σπένδομεν"</note>

**Page 56. For not without divine inspiration]** I added the word "for" [*gar*] from the CT manuscripts. In manuscript G, it reads "not without." Compare Longinus, *Fragments* VIII, at the end: **"from the inspiration of the Muses."** Callistratus, *Descriptions* 2: **"For indeed Scopas, moved as if by some inspiration:"** where Jacobs (*Exercises* II, page 12) illustrates this word <term>inspiration</term> <note>Greek: "epipnoia"</note> excellently. Dio Chrysostom, *Orations* LIII, page 277: **"For simply without divine fortune, and without the inspiration of both the Muses and Apollo, it is not possible for poetry to become so sublime..."** Anonymous Epigram 116:
**"On account of your insight, O son of Leto [Apollo] of the strong bow, Nicocreon took this four-horned deer."**
I would prefer "inspiration" <note>Greek: "epipnoias"</note> here. Add Casaubon’s discourse on Dio, page 450, volume 2 of the Reiske edition.

**Page 56. to converse]** I have written this by conjecture instead of "to continue." Manuscript T reads "to continue."

**Page 56. like snowflakes]** "Homer, *Iliad* Book 3, line 222, concerning the speech of Odysseus: **'And his words were like wintry snowflakes.'**" FABRICIUS.

**Page 56. of that temperate mouth]** "Another reading: **'most temperate.'**" FABRICIUS. Manuscript T reads "body." I discussed the confusion between the words <term>mouth</term> <note>Greek: "stoma"</note> and <term>body</term> <note>Greek: "soma"</note> in my notes on Philostratus’ *On Heroes*, page 440.

**Page 56. for his eyes seemed to be filled with a certain radiance]** Manuscripts C and T omit the word "his."

**Page 56. the rest of his face]** I spoke about the word "other" <note>Greek: "allos"</note> used in this sense <note>meaning "the rest of"</note> in the notes to Philostratus’ *On Heroes*, page 541.

**Page 57. he saw a light running around his own head]** "Another reading: **'he saw it surrounded by light.'**" FABRICIUS. Manuscript T reads "running alongside." Fabricius uses the order "the head of him." I preferred the word order in manuscripts C and T. — Examples of a similar prodigy are not rare in the books of the ancients. Who does not remember Servius Tullius <note>The sixth legendary King of Rome</note>, whose head the historian Titus Livy (Book I, chapter 39) reports to have burned in the sight of many, or Lavinia (Virgil, *Aeneid* Book 7) seen
**"to catch the fire in her long hair?"**
Less common are the things narrated by Damascius (in Photios, *Library* pages 1040, 1041) concerning the horse of Severus and the donkey of Tiberius sending sparks from their bodies, or concerning Balemer, the father of the famous Theodoric (compare Eustathius on *Iliad* Book 5, volume 3, page 1105), whose body threw out small fires. **"And to me, while dressing and undressing,"** Damascius adds, **"even if this happens rarely, it happens nonetheless that extraordinary sparks jump out, sometimes even producing a noise, and occasionally whole flames shine out—"**

116 ->NOTES ON MARINUS<-

...to touch <note>original: πειν (pein)</note> the garment, yet without burning it. Then he adds that he saw a certain man emitting sparks from his head, and even lighting a flame, whenever he wished, by rubbing against a certain rough garment. Modern philosophers see nothing portentous in these flames: today miracles are neither so easily produced, nor so readily believed, as they were in the age of Damascius and Marinus. <note>Damascius and Marinus were Neoplatonic philosophers of the 5th and 6th centuries; the text likely refers to the "Life of Proclus" where supernatural events were frequently reported.</note>

**Page 57.** *by his explanation*] <note>original: τῇ αὐτοῦ ἐξηγήσει</note> Fabricius reads *by the explanation*. Manuscript CT reads *his*: I have written *his*.

**Page 57.** *and he did obeisance — Rufinus*] <note>original: προσεκύνησέ τε — ὁ Ῥουφῖνος</note> These words are omitted in manuscripts CT because of a "similar ending" <note>original: *homoioteleuton*; a common scribal error where the eye skips from one word to a similar-looking one further down.</note> in the repetition of the name Rufinus. I hardly need to mention that I did not wish to write *he did obeisance* <note>original: προσεκύνησέν τε</note> with the final 'n' as Fabricius did.

**Page 57.** *he had*] <note>original: ἔσχε</note> Thus in CT, not *he had* <note>original: ἔσχεν</note> with the final 'n', as Fabricius has it.

**Page 57.** *let us return*] <note>original: ἐπανίωμεν</note> CT reads *we return* <note>original: ἐπανίομεν</note>. This is the same as above on page 42, where *we go after* <note>original: μετίομεν</note> appears instead of *let us go after* <note>original: μετίωμεν</note>. There is a perpetual confusion [in the manuscripts] between the indicative and subjunctive moods. <note>Indicative states facts; subjunctive expresses wishes or proposals. In later Greek, the vowels 'o' and 'ō' were pronounced similarly, leading to frequent spelling errors.</note> Compare Synesius, *Hymn VI*, line 5:
   *I shall crown you with the wise flowers of hymns.* <note>original: Στεφανώσω σοφοῖς ἄνθεσιν ὕμνων</note>
Codex 1039 has *we shall crown*, which if we easily change to *let us crown*, we will read a verse that flows correctly. What prevents us now from emending the final verses of this hymn?

   *Among the people open up a good report,*
   *Crown it with the flower of soft-spoken Persuasion,*
   *So that my mind might pluck a wave-less leisure,*
   *Nor groaning in earthly anxieties,*
   *But from your high-flowing channels*
   *I may water the mind with the birth-pangs of wisdom.*

I provided the third verse from an emendation I had proposed for Philostratus' *Heroicus*, page 327. Now, in the second verse, the same Codex very correctly gives *crowning* <note>original: στέφων</note>, and in the fourth, *groaning* <note>original: στένων</note>. It also incorrectly has *of persuasion* <note>original: πυθοῦς</note>, and *to soft-spoken* <note>original: πραϋλόγῳ</note>, which is not to be despised, and *with birth-pangs* <note>original: ὠδῖσι</note>.

   *Among the people open up a good report,*
   *Crowning it with the soft-spoken flower of Persuasion,*
   *So that my mind might pluck a wave-less leisure,*
   *Nor may I groan in earthly anxieties,*
   *But from your high-flowing channels*
   *I may water the mind with the birth-pangs of wisdom.*

Certainly, "to water the mind with the birth-pangs of wisdom" <note>original: κατάρδειν νόον ὠδῖσι σοφίας</note> is an excessively harsh and distorted metaphor; and after mentioning "channels," who does not expect "waters"? But Synesius was constrained by the laws of poetic meter, just as the rhythm often drags modern poets toward the absurd or the ridiculous.

**Page 57.** *having already narrated before*] <note>original: ἤδη προϊστορήσαντες</note> Thus Manuscript T. In Manuscript C, *intending to narrate before*. Fabricius reads: *somehow having already narrated*. LG read: *somehow intending to narrate*. Shortly after, I added *the* <note>original: τῷ</note> before *such* <note>original: τοιούτῳ</note> from the Codices.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus's biography of the philosopher Proclus, specifically focusing on textual variations and philosophical references found on pages 58 and 59 of the source text.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 117<-

**Page 58. one thing toward another**] <note>original: "ἄλλου πρὸς ἄλλο"</note> “Compare Plotinus page 15.”
FABRICIUS. <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a classical scholar and the original editor of this text.</note> Plotinus, *Enneads* I, book 2, section 6, page 15: “But if justice is **doing one’s own work**, <term>oikeiopragia</term> <note>A Platonic concept where justice is defined as each part of the soul or state performing its own proper function.</note> is it always found in a multitude of parts? Or is one kind of justice found in a multitude, when there are many parts, while the universal 'doing of one's own work' exists even if there is only one part? True justice, then, is of a thing toward itself, in which there is not one thing and another.” <note>original: "δικαιοσύνη δὲ εἴπερ οἰκειοπραγία, ἆρα ἀεὶ ἐν πλήθει μερῶν; ἢ ἡ μὲν ἐν πλήθει, ὅταν πολλὰ ᾖ τὰ μέρη, ἡ δὲ ὅλως οἰκειοπραγία, κἂν ἑνὸς ᾖ. Ἡ γοῦν ἀληθὴς δικαιοσύνη ἑνὸς πρὸς αὐτὸ, ἐν ᾧ οὐκ ἄλλο, τὸ δὲ ἄλλο."</note>

**Page 58. to act toward Intellect and God**] <note>original: "τὸ πρὸς νοῦν καὶ θεὸν ἐνεργεῖν"</note> Plotinus in the same place: “For the soul, the greater justice is to reach toward Intellect.” <note>original: "τῇ ψυχῇ δικαιοσύνη ἡ μείζων τὸ πρὸς νοῦν ὀρέγειν."</note>

**Page 58. having barely ceased from labors**] <note>original: "πόνων μόγις σχολάσας"</note> Fabricius reads "works." <note>original: "ἔργων"</note> But the manuscripts have "labors," which I have restored. D'Orville, in his notes to Chariton page 409, found "barely from labors" <note>original: "πόνων μόλις"</note> in the Holstenius copy, <note>A transcript or annotated version by the scholar Lucas Holstenius.</note> and "T" <note>A specific manuscript designation.</note> has "barely from labors." "C" has "leisure." Soon before "body," I have added "the" <note>original: "τὸ"</note> according to the manuscripts.

**Page 58. as a kind of idleness of the soul**] <note>original: "ὡς ἀργίαν τινὰ τῆς ψυχῆς"</note> According to the thought of Pythagoras. Iamblichus, *Life of Pythagoras* 25, page 129: “He says also that one should enter a temple wearing a clean garment, and one in which no one has slept; assigning sleep to idleness, and black and reddish colors to it, but giving testimony of purity to the equality and justice regarding reasoning.” <note>original: "λέγει δὲ καὶ εἰς ἱερὸν εἰσιέναι δεῖν καθαρὸν ἱμάτιον ἔχοντα... τὴν δὲ καθαρότητα τῆς περὶ τοὺς λογισμοὺς ἰσότητος καὶ δικαιοσύνης μαρτυρίαν ἀποδιδούς."</note>

**Page 58. and the time for prayers did not yet call**] <note>original: "καὶ οὔπω καιρὸς ἐκάλει τῶν εὐχῶν"</note> So the manuscripts, except that manuscripts "L" and "G" have "not yet." <note>original: "οὔπω"</note> Fabricius wrote, without even warning the reader: "and it was already the time called." <note>original: "καὶ ἔω πω καιρὸν ἐκάλ."</note> I have restored the reading of the manuscripts.

**Page 58. being upon a bed**] <note>original: "ἐπὶ κλίνης ὢν"</note> “Another reading: reclining.” <note>original: "ἐπικλινής"</note> FABRICIUS. Chapter 31: “while he was lying upon the bed.” <note>original: "κειμένου αὐτοῦ ἐπὶ τῆς κλίνης"</note> Nicetas Choniates page 5 D: “he approaches the man cast upon a bed.” <note>original: "πρόσεισι τῷ ἀνδρὶ ἐπὶ κλίνης ἐῤῥιμμένῳ."</note> Libanius, *Epistle* 551, page 268: “she was upon a bed in chains, and while she cried out her body was being abused.” In the Paris manuscript 3002, "her" is omitted, which no one will miss. Soon, instead of "was finding," <note>original: "ηὑρίσκε"</note> manuscripts "C" and "T" have "found," <note>original: "εὕρισκε"</note> as above in chapter 16.

**Page 59. during the day**] <note>original: "μεθ᾽ ἡμέραν"</note> Fabricius, as is his custom, reads "after many days"; <note>original: "μεθ᾽ ἡμέρας"</note> but the manuscripts and the Holstenius copy mentioned by D'Orville (to Chariton page 409) read "during the day." Compare above, page 68.

**Page 59. following**] <note>original: "ἑπομένην"</note> This is the reading of the manuscripts; Fabricius reads "consequently." <note>original: "ἑπομένως"</note>

**Page 59. turning**] <note>original: "στροφὴ"</note> Compare the notes above on page 111.

**Page 59. an untouched and undistracted disposition**] <note>original: "ἀνέπαφος καὶ ἀπερίσπαστος διάθεσις"</note> Fabricius reads "unstable." <note>original: "ἀπερίστατος"</note> I have restored the reading of the manuscripts. Van Lynden, in his work on Panaetius page 99, noted that "undistractedly" <note>original: "ἀπερισπάστως"</note> is frequently used by the Stoics, which is confirmed by the index of Epictetus in the Schweighäuser edition. Marcus Aurelius III. 6: “give no place to any other thing, toward which if you once incline and turn aside, you will no longer be able to prefer that good which is truly your own and undistracted”; <note>original: "μηδενὶ χώραν δίδου ἑτέρῳ... οὐκ ἔτι ἀπερισπάστως τὸ ἀγαθὸν ἐκεῖνο τὸ ἴδιον καὶ τὸ σὸν προτιμᾷν δυνήσῃ"</note> where Gataker <note>Thomas Gataker (1574–1654), a noted English scholar and theologian who edited Marcus Aurelius.</note> comments.

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations on Marinus's "Life of Proclus," comparing the text against works by Plotinus, Iamblichus, and various classical manuscripts. The editor focuses on correcting previous errors by Fabricius and clarifying Neoplatonic concepts like justice and the "undistracted" soul.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Iamblichus, Fabricius, Proclus, Neoplatonism, textual criticism, justice, Marcus Aurelius, manuscripts</keywords>

118 NOTES ON MARINUS

### Page 59. <term>and he put forward courage as his helper</term> <note>original: "ἀνδρείαν δὲ τὴν ξυνέριθον τ. προὐβάλλετο." This refers to Proclus's practice of virtue. Marinus was the student and biographer of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus.</note>] The manuscripts read *andrian* <note>original: "ἀνδρίαν," a variant spelling of "ἀνδρείαν" (courage)</note>, which I felt compelled to change so that the spelling would remain consistent throughout the book: see above, page 71. Fabricius reads *proballeto* <note>original: "προβάλλετο," a slightly different verb form meaning "he put forward"</note>: the manuscripts offer a more correct reading. And so Ruhnken wrote, citing this passage of ours in his notes on Timaeus regarding the entry **Helpers: fellow-workers** <note>original: "Συνέριθοι· συνεργοί"</note>, where he illustrated the word **helper** <note>original: "συνέριθος" (synerithos)</note>. Theophylactus Simocatta, *Letter 24*: "I shall be a **helper** to the tragedy." Manuscript 3047 reads: "be [to] me," which no one who reads the letter will immediately accept, even though Theophylactus says in *Letter 59*: "be a **helper** to me at the noon hour, Coriannus." Theophylactus delighted in this word in *Physical Problems*, page 1: "Just as the cicada was a **helper** in the melody for Eunomus the Locrian." *Letter 83*: "Possess the Cretan dog as a **helper**." *History*, page 3 A, regarding **reason**: "it is the best **helper** of nature." Add also Wernsdorf on Himerius, page 719.

<margin>+</margin>
### Page 59. <term>having emulated the passionlessness of that toward which he looked, that being passionlessness by nature</term> <note>original: "τὴν ἀπάθειαν τοῦ πρὸς ὃ ἔβλεπε ζηλώσας, ἀπαθοῦς ὄντος ἐκείνου τὴν φύσιν." In Neoplatonic thought, "apatheia" (passionlessness) is the state of being unmoved by earthly emotions, mimicking the divine nature.</note>] From manuscripts C and T, *eblepe* <note>original: "ἔβλεπε"</note> instead of *eblepen*. The same manuscripts have "according to nature" <note>original: "κατὰ τὴν φ."</note>. We can do without the preposition, and Plotinus does not have it. Plotinus, *Enneads* I. 2. 6: "And courage is **passionlessness** through likeness to that which it looks upon, which is **passionless** by nature."

### Page 59. **According to Plotinus** <note>original: "κατὰ Πλωτῖνον"</note>] "Plotinus, *Enneads* Book I, chapter 2, at the end." — FABRICIUS <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, a renowned 18th-century scholar and editor.</note>. Here are Plotinus’s words: "Living entirely not the life of a good man, which **political virtue** <note>Virtues concerning social and civic life.</note> requires, but leaving this behind and choosing another, that of the gods; for the likeness is to these, not to good men."

### Page 60. **He lived with such virtues** <note>original: "τοιαύταις μὲν ἀρεταῖς συνεβίω"</note>] The manuscripts read thus, and not *tautais* <note>original: "ταύταις" (these)</note> as Fabricius has it. — Athenaeus, Book 12, 71: "He **lived with** wisdom for nearly eighty years." Plutarch, *Cicero*, section 29: "No one would have expected a man who had **lived with** so much education." Philostratus, *Heroicus*, page 106: "**having been associated with** popular leadership since youth"; my friend Bastius very aptly conjectures "**having lived with**" <note>original: "συμβεβιωκότα" (symbebiokota)</note>. See the *Addenda to Gregory of Corinth*, page 924, and compare Schaefer on the same, page 1030, on the word **to live with**.

### Page 60. **Going through the treatises of the ancients** <note>original: "τὰς τῶν ἀρχαιοτέρων ἐπεξιὼν πραγματείας"</note>] Perhaps it should be "to the treatises of the ancients" <note>suggesting a change to the dative case</note>. Chapter 22: "**going through** the writings of the older ones." Chapter 23: "**to one going through** his treatises." Chapter 33: "if I should wish to **go through** everything in this way." Gregory of Nyssa, *Life of Gregory Thaumaturgus*: "**to go through** all the miracles performed by him in order." Dio Chrysostom, *Oration 53*, page 274, in another sense regarding Homer: "**recounting** adulteries and strifes and rivalries concerning the gods"; where Reiske seems to doubt the correctness of this verb's usage, but wrongly.

The editor compares various manuscript readings and cites parallel usages in other classical authors like Plotinus, Plutarch, and Theophylactus Simocatta.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus (written by Marinus), specifically providing textual criticisms and historical context for chapters 26 through 28. It maintains the continuity of comparing various manuscript traditions (identified as CT, LG, and G).</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 119<-

P. 60. **"for not arriving first"** <note>original: διὰ τὸ μὴ φθῆναι</note>] So in manuscripts C and T. Fabricius reads **"nor to arrive first"** <note>original: μηδὲ φθάναι</note>: and manuscripts L and G read **"to arrive first."** Immediately after the word **"years,"** manuscripts C and T omit the word **"him."**

P. 60. **"Domninus"**] “Photius and Suidas <note>The *Suda* (often called *Suidas* in older scholarship) is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient world.</note> [in the entry for *Domninus*] report many things about Domninus from the writings of Damascius.” FABRICIUS. Compare *Greek Library*, volume 3, page 171.

P. 61. **"either the works of Orpheus"**] Fabricius reads **"for indeed the works of Orpheus."** In manuscript G, the strokes of the pen are very obscure; someone, perhaps Gudius <note>Marquard Gude (1635–1689), a famous German classical scholar and manuscript collector.</note>, had written **"for"** <note>original: γὰρ</note> above the line, which Fabricius then adopted. But manuscripts C and T have the best reading, which I have restored. — Next, after the word **"since,"** manuscripts C and T provided the word **"but."** — Then, instead of **"the same things,"** C and T incorrectly have **"their things."** I wrote **"and this very thing"** <note>original: τοῦτό τε αὐτὸ</note> based on L, G, C, and T, instead of **"but this very thing."** In C and T, instead of **"itself,"** one reads **"to her,"** which was hardly worth noting. — Next, manuscript T has **"therefore having taken"** instead of **"but therefore having taken."** You see here the word **"therefore"** <note>original: οὖν</note> used after a long interruption in the sentence by incidental remarks; I have written something on this matter regarding Eunapius, page 213, and we have another example below in chapter 32.

P. 61. **"both to his works on Orpheus — and to those of Porphyry and Iamblichus"**] C and T omit the word **"both."** — “Suidas mentions the commentaries of Syrianus and Proclus on the oracles and the poems of Orpheus. The work of Porphyry *On the Philosophy from Oracles* is praised by Eusebius, Augustine, Firmicus, and others [cited by Holstenius in *The Life of Porphyry*, chapter 10]. Just as in other matters, Iamblichus followed Porphyry in this area as well and illustrated the oracles [cf. *Greek Library* IV. ch. 28. §. XI.], whom Psellus <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine monk and scholar.</note> in his exposition of the *Chaldean Dogmas* [in the final book] adds to Porphyry, Proclus, and Plotinus, who, as he says, accepted these <term>oracles</term> <note>original: λόγια (logia); specifically the *Chaldean Oracles*, a set of mystical texts used by late Neoplatonists as sacred scripture.</note> as divine voices, without requiring logical proof.” FABRICIUS. — Soon after, the manuscripts provided the word **"the"** before **"Chaldeans."**

P. 61. **"nurtured on the oracles"**] C and T read **"dwelling among the oracles."** — Themistius, *Oration* XXI, page 248 D: **“one who happens perhaps to come from a cook or a baker, nurtured on small measures and cups.”** The Valesian manuscript has **"knuckles"** instead of **"cups."**

P. 62. **"the divinely inspired Iamblichus called them theurgical"**] “Iamblichus, both in other lost writings such as *On the Gods*, and in the work *On the Most Perfect Chaldean Philosophy* (of which Damascius praises the 27th book in his work *On First Principles* [cf. *Greek Library* IV. ch. 28. §. XI.], a manuscript in the Johannine Library of Hamburg from the Holstenian legacy), and also in the book *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*, which survives, and in which one should see the notes of Thomas Gale, a most learned Englishman, where there is more on <term>theurgy</term> <note>Theurgy refers to "divine work" or rituals intended to invoke the presence of gods or achieve union with the divine, seen by late Neoplatonists as superior to mere intellectual philosophy.</note>.” FABRICIUS. Below in chapter 28, **"theurgical virtue"** is called **"greater and more perfect."**

P. 62. **"having summarized"** <note>original: συνελὼν</note>] Manuscript T has **"having come together."** — Next, the word **"the"** before **"greatest things"** is from C and T.

120 NOTES ON MARINUS.

P. 62. **applied himself to the divinely-delivered oracles** <note>original: εἰς τὰ θεοπαράδοτα λόγια κατεβάλετο</note>] The manuscript T reads "was applying himself." — Proclus, in his commentary on the *Cratylus*, chapter 107, mentions **"the divinely-delivered rumors."** Psellus, in his work on the *Oracles*, p. 70, says: **"there are names among each nation delivered by the gods, having an ineffable power in the sacred rituals."** — Shortly after, manuscripts C and T provide a different word order: **"completing these in [these] years."**

P. 62. **to foretell** <note>original: προαγορεύειν</note>] Fabricius reads "foretelling." Manuscripts C and T read "to address." Manuscript G reads **"to foretell,"** which I have adopted. The infinitive in this expression is the only correct form, and our author uses it elsewhere. Chapter 28: **"for he seemed in a dream to say..."** — **"having cried out, he seemed to say these things."** Chapter 30: **"for it seemed to the philosopher to frequent in a dream."** Chapter 32: **"the god seemed to visit him in a dream."**

P. 62. **the great Plutarch**] "Plutarch of Athens, of whom we spoke above in chapter 12." <note>Plutarch of Athens (died c. 432 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher, not to be confused with the more famous biographer Plutarch of Chaeronea.</note> — FABRICIUS. — Manuscript T omits the article "the."

P. 63. **of those composed** <note>original: συγκειμένων</note>] So read G, C, and T; not "composed" in the singular <note>original: συγκείμενος</note> as Fabricius had it.

P. 63. **He lived** <note>original: Ἐβίω</note>] I have written this based on the Manuscripts instead of Fabricius's **"He lived with."**

P. 63. **before**] "Above on page 8." — FABRICIUS. Chapter 3.

P. 63. **but the [remaining] five [years] no longer with strength**] Manuscript T reads "and the years no longer strong." And Dorville, in his notes on Chariton, p. 606, thought that the word "years" had fallen out of the text; but we can do without this word. Manuscript C reads **"strong."**

P. 63. **washings** <note>original: περιχυμάτων</note>] Fabricius's version translated this as *labors*, which I had to change. <term>Perichymata</term> <note>literally "pourings-around"</note> are the frequent ritual washings used by the superstitious philosopher: compare chapter 18.

P. 63. **to be relaxed** <note>original: παρεῖσθαι; likely referring to a state of paralysis or being weakened</note>] Compare above, page 110.

P. 63. **he spent time with his companions** <note>original: ἑταίροις συνεγίγνετο</note>] The Manuscripts and Fabricius read "others" <note>original: ἑτέροις</note>; but Fabricius preferred "companions," since he translated it as *friends*. Regarding this very frequent confusion between the words for "others" and "companions," see above, page 69. — Manuscript T reads "spent time," which also has the same spelling variation shortly after in the same word.

P. 64. **concerning the [explanations] of Hegias**] Manuscripts C and T read "upon." — "Damascius on Hegias [found in the Suda]: **'while he was still a youth he had high hopes for himself, and showed everyone that he was not far behind the great Plutarch. Therefore Proclus judged him worthy, while he was still young, to hear the Chaldean oracles. For a certain love of learning and reasonableness (or perhaps "attentiveness") belonged to him by nature.'**" — FABRICIUS. — Shortly after, I added the word "of" <note>original: τῶν</note> after "all" from the Manuscripts.

P. 64. **of the truly golden chain of the lineage from Solon**] Fabricius read "of the one having perished," joining a participle of this form with "chain," and translating it as "of the chain which [perished] long ago..."

### NOTES ON MARINUS. 121

...withdrew himself from the eyes of men. This negligence in such a great man is wondrous beyond measure. The manuscript G, which he was using, and the manuscripts LCT <note>These letters refer to specific codices or handwritten copies of the manuscript used by the editor.</note> say **from Solon** <note>original: ἀπὸ Σόλωνος</note> expressly. — "Eunapius, writing about Porphyry [page 9]: 'But Porphyry was like a kind of **Hermetic chain** <note>original: Ἑρμαϊκή τις σειρὰ</note> reaching down toward men through his diverse learning.' Regarding this passage, we have noted many more things [which I only wish we still had!]. Damascius, quoted in the works of Photius [page 1057]: 'Proclus was fearing for the truly golden chain of Plato, lest it should leave the city of Athena <note>Athens</note> to us.'" **FABRICIUS.** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736) was a German classical scholar and the primary editor of these notes.</note> Marinus transferred the word **chain** <term>seira: a Neoplatonic term for a succession of divine or philosophical influence, like a spiritual lineage</term>—which the Platonists use in a philosophical sense to describe the continuous connection of divine influences passed down to us—to the family of Hegias and their origin derived from Solon. Compare Jacobs on the *Greek Anthology* 10, page 277. Our author later in chapter 28 relates that Proclus clearly saw "that he belonged to the **Hermetic chain**." Look for the passages of Proclus in his commentary on the *Cratylus*, chapter 25.

**Page 65. by the cubit** <note>original: κατὰ πῆχυν</note>] "As elsewhere, so here too Damascius imitated this in his *Life of Isidore* [page 1057]: 'For it was not easy to advance, nor was he to be increased **by the cubit** <note>This is an idiom meaning to grow in massive, measurable leaps</note>, just as Theodorus of Asine increased under Porphyry.' The learned man Andreas Schottus translated this incorrectly as 'to progress with precision' <note>original: ad amussim</note>." **FABRICIUS.** Zosimus, book 4, chapter 41: "Every form of luxury and licentiousness increasing **by the cubit** (as the saying goes) in every instance." Eunapius, page 53: "increasing by many cubits in every kind of wisdom." Suidas: "He increased **by the cubit**; a phrase used for those who make progress toward better things." "The philosopher continued his advancement without hindrance, and he increased **by the cubit**, so to speak, quite simply until the **household** <note>original: πανοικίου</note> misfortune." I have accepted Toup’s conjecture of 'household' instead of 'panic' <note>original: πανικοῦ</note>: furthermore, I believe it should be written, 'so to speak, quite simply, until...' Compare Junius, *Adages* 4, 30. — Shortly after, I added the word **the** before **on the one hand** from the Manuscripts.

**Page 65. and more**] I added the word **and** <note>original: τε</note> from the Manuscripts.

**Page 66. with a threat**] Thus the Manuscripts. Fabricius wrote 'with a threat' <note>using a contraction: μετ’ ἀπ.</note>. The manuscript L has 'with a threat' <note>variant spelling: μετὰ ἀπηλῆς</note>. — Shortly after, for **thinking**, I would have preferred either **I think** or **I was thinking**. — Then the Manuscripts have **having been persuaded** <note>original: πεισθέντος</note>, not Fabricius’s version **having been persuaded** <note>variant spelling: πειθέντος</note>.

**Page 66. and there were his commentaries on Orpheus**] The manuscripts LG have "and there were and." LCT rightly have **his** <note>original: αὐτοῦ</note>, not **to him** <note>original: αὐτῷ</note>, which Fabricius perhaps took from manuscript G. — "This is what Suidas praises among the writings of Proclus as *On the Orphic Theology*. Indeed, throughout his works, both in his published books and in his **unpublished** <note>original: ἀνεκδότοις</note> commentaries, Proclus praises and explains Orphic verses and oracles, and everywhere shows that he attributes a great deal of importance to them." **FABRICIUS.**

<meta>This page continues the critical commentary on the "Life of Proclus" by Marinus of Neapolis, specifically focusing on page 67 of a previous edition (likely Fabricius's). It provides textual variants and explains Neoplatonic ritual terminology.</meta>

122                         ### NOTES ON MARINUS

**P. 67. virtue]** <note>original Greek: ἀρετὴν</note> Manuscript G reads "best" <note>original: ἀρίστην</note>, from which Fabricius published "of the best." I have restored "virtue" <note>original: ἀρετὴν</note> from manuscript CT. — Shortly after, I provided "more perfect" <note>original: τελεωτέραν</note> instead of "more complete" <note>original: τελειοτέραν</note> based on manuscripts GCT.

**P. 67. he stood]** <note>original Greek: ἔστατο</note> Thus in manuscript G. Fabricius uses "he stood." Manuscript L reads *istato*. Manuscript CT reads *istato*. — Shortly after, instead of Fabricius’s "on the one hand" <note>original: καταθάτερος</note>, I wrote "on the other hand" <note>original: κατὰ θάτερον</note> based on CT and Fabricius's own marginal notes. GL read "on the one hand."

**P. 67. of the two properties in divine things]** Fabricius reads "he went out" <note>original: ἔξη</note>. "Alternative readings: 'he said' <note>original: ἔφη</note> or 'he went out' <note>original: ἔξη</note>: and a little before 'on the other hand'." FABRICIUS. Manuscript CT reads "he went out." — "Regarding divine properties, which Marinus here says are twofold: the first kind consists of those by which the nature of the Gods is perfected in itself, and in the contemplation of which the wise take delight and are perfected. The other kind consists of those which flow from the Gods into lower things, and by participating in which the <term>theurgists</term> <note>Practitioners of 'god-working' or ritual magic aimed at divine union.</note> can themselves become 'fathers of gods' <note>original: θεοπάτορες</note> and perform <term>theurgy</term> for others." FABRICIUS.

**P. 67. he placed]** <note>original Greek: ἐτίθετο</note> Thus in the Codices. Fabricius used "he set" <note>original: ἔθετο</note>.

**P. 67. sustainings]** <note>original Greek: συστάσεσι</note> This term will be clarified by these words from <term>Olympiodorus</term> <note>A 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> in his commentary on the *Phaedo* (p. 289, Fischer edition): "In the sacred rites, first came the public purifications; then, following these, the more secret ones; after these, the <term>sustainings</term> <note>technical term for the establishment of a ritual connection with a deity.</note>; then the initiations; and finally the highest visions."

**P. 67. spinning-disks]** <note>original Greek: στροφάλοις</note> Thus in GCT. L reads *strophalais*. Fabricius published "whirls" <note>original: στροφάλιγξι</note> and translated it as *entanglements of words*, adding this little note: "Thus in the <term>Chaldean Oracles</term> <note>A sacred text of the 2nd century AD central to theurgy.</note> are often called the words full of mystery concerning the Gods and divine matters." This change was not necessary, and the meaning of the word seems to be something else. A Chaldean Oracle says: "Work around the <term>Hecatic spinning-disk</term>." Regarding this, <term>Psellus</term> <note>An 11th-century Byzantine scholar and commentator.</note> says (p. 74): "The Hecatic spinning-disk is a golden sphere, enclosing a sapphire in the middle, turned by a leather strap, having sacred symbols throughout it; by spinning this they made their invocations. They were accustomed to call such things <term>iynxes</term> <note>Ancient magical tools, often spinning wheels or birds tied to wheels, used to draw spirits or love.</note>, whether they had a spherical, triangular, or any other shape (Codex 2832: 'or some other' shape)." Compare Nicephorus's notes on Synesius, p. 364 A. Suidas: *Strophalinx* means a whirling; and 'in a whirling.' This example seems to be taken from an Oracle cited by Proclus in his commentary on the *Cratylus*, chapter 52.

**P. 67. pronunciations]** <note>original Greek: ἐκφωνήσεις</note> "A manner of pronouncing and expressing." FABRICIUS. It should be understood as referring to those "barbarous names" <note>Sacred, non-Greek names believed to hold innate power.</note> which the Oracle forbids changing: "Never change the barbarous names." Regarding this, Psellus says (p. 70): "That is, there are names among every nation delivered by the gods, having an unspeakable power in the sacred rites." Among these names, Psellus lists "Se..."

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations on Marinus's biography of Proclus, debating specific word choices and explaining theurgical concepts like the Hecatic spinning-disk and the use of sacred "barbarous names" in ritual.</summary>
<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, theurgy, Chaldean Oracles, Hecate's spinning-disk, barbarous names, Psellus, Olympiodorus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Marinus’s "Life of Proclus," focusing on theurgical practices and textual variations between different manuscripts and the edition by Johann Albert Fabricius.</meta>

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 123

...Seraphim, Cherubim, Michael, Gabriel. <note>original Greek: "ραφείμ, Χερουβείμ, Μιχαήλ, Γαβριήλ"</note> Add Nicephorus’s notes to Synesius, page 364 A.

P. 68. **Asclepigenia** <note>original: "Ἀσκληπιγενείας." She was an Athenian philosopher and theurgist who taught Proclus.</note>] Consult Fabricius’s introduction.

P. 68. **Nestorius the Great** <note>original: "Νεστορίου τοῦ μεγάλου"</note>] I have provided this reading from the Manuscripts: Fabricius incorrectly gave **the great things**. <note>original: "τὰ μεγάλα"</note>

P. 68. **theurgical guidance** <note>original: "θεουργικὴ ἀγωγὴ." Theurgy refers to rituals intended to invoke the presence of or communicate with divine beings.</note>] “Proclus seems to have treated this **guidance** in two books *On Guidance*, which Suidas <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world.</note> records among his writings, though translators mistakenly render the title as *On Education*. In the same work, Proclus also seems to have mentioned his own **mystic vision** <note>original: "ἐποπτείας," referring to the highest stage of initiation in Greek mysteries</note>, which Marinus mentions shortly after: so that perhaps the phrase **in a separate work** <note>original: "ἐν ἰδίῳ συγγράμματι"</note> could be translated as ‘in a single work dedicated to this subject.’” — FABRICIUS.

P. 68. **But before these things** <note>original: "Πρὸ δὲ τούτων"</note>] Thus in manuscripts L and G. In C and T, it reads **before these things, however**. <note>original: "πρὸ τούτων δέ"</note> Fabricius has **furthermore** <note>original: "πρὸς δὲ τ."</note>, which he had translated as ‘besides.’ — Soon after, manuscripts C and T read **Chaldaeans**. <note>original: "Χαλδαίοις"</note>

P. 68. **things seen with one’s own eyes** <note>original: "αὐτοπτουμένοις"</note>] Thus correctly in manuscripts G, C, and T, instead of Fabricius’s misspelled *autoptoumenois* <note>original: "αὐτοπτοουμένοις"</note>.

P. 68. **having moved a certain iynx...** <note>An "iynx" was a magical spinning wheel or "bullroarer" used by theurgists to attract divine influence or command the elements.</note>] “Regarding the **iynx**, one should consult, among others, Nicephorus’s notes on Synesius’s *On Dreams*, pages 360, 361, [363]. Suidas also mentions that Julian the Chaldaean Theurgist and Arnuphis the Egyptian brought down rain through magical power [under the entry *Julian, son of...*]. But there will be a place to say more about **miracles** <note>original: "θαυμασίοις"</note> of this kind in the *Treatise on the life, philosophy, and writings of Empedocles the Pythagorean*, who was called the **averter of winds** <note>original: "ἀλεξάνεμος"</note>, because by a similar artifice he could restrain the winds and keep them in check.” — FABRICIUS. See the passage of Psellus cited above regarding **whirling wheels** <note>original: "στροφάλοις"</note>, and Price’s notes on Apuleius’s *Apology*, page 87.

P. 69. **Talismans against earthquakes** <note>original: "Φυλακτήριά τε σεισμῶν"</note>] “A similar thing was the **talesma** <note>A consecrated object or "talisman" believed to have protective powers.</note> or magic capsule of Ablaccon; John Malalas, in book 10 [page 301] of his *Chronography*, reports that when this was placed, it kept Antioch free from the overflows of the Parmenius river and from barbarian incursions.” — FABRICIUS. Lambecius used this passage, which was then unpublished, in his notes on Codinus, number 7, page 139, where he illustrates the point.

P. 69. **he attempted** <note>original: "ἐπειράθη"</note>] The manuscripts have **he attempted**. Fabricius has *epeirasthe* <note>a variant spelling of the same verb</note>.

P. 69. **concerning his own station, he produced verses** <note>original: "περί τε τῆς αὑτοῦ λήξεως στίχους ἐξέβαλεν"</note>] I wrote **his own** <note>original: "αὑτοῦ"</note> instead of *autou*. Manuscript T incorrectly reads **of the word** <note>original: "λέξεως"</note>. Ausonius, in his poem *The Moselle*, line 80, translates the word **station** <note>original: "λῆξις," which in Neoplatonism refers to one's divinely allotted rank or destiny</note> into Latin thus:
“...he does not end it, to whom the care of the second
**Lot** [*Sortis*], and the protection of the sea-trident fell.”
In place of Fabricius’s **he received** <note>original: "ἐξέλαβεν"</note>, manuscripts C and T have **he produced** <note>original: "ἐξέβαλεν"</note>, which I have adopted. Before this reading was known, I had thought about restoring...

124&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ->NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

the proper word in these cases is *cried out* <note>original Greek: ἐξέλακεν</note>, and I proposed that conjecture for Eunapius p. 189, where I also corrected the translation by Fabricius. With *cast out* <note>original: ἐξέβαλεν</note> one can compare the word *flung* <note>original: ἔῤῥιψεν</note> used by Euripides in these verses found in Stobaeus, volume 2, edited by Heeren, p. 336, 4.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The first one who spoke, not with an untrained mind,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*flung* this word, whoever first made it new:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;that Fortune fights on the side of the prudent.
Euripides, *Iphigenia in Tauris*, 976:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;then Phoebus, taking a voice from the golden tripod...
Learned men have conjectured the word should be *having cried out* <note>original: λακών</note>. But perhaps the reading of the Manuscript, which in this passage of our author gives *cast out* <note>original: ἐξέβαλεν</note>, provides a way to correct Euripides, suggesting we should read *having cast* <note>original: βαλών</note>. One may compare the not uncommon expression "to fling words."
&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 69. *he flies about*] <note>original: πωτάσκεται</note> Manuscript CT: *he used to fly about* <note>original: πωτάσκετο</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 69. *of the fire-crashing revelry*] <note>original: πυρισμαράγου θιασεύης</note> Fabricius read *of the fire-crashing divine nature* <note>original: πυρισφαράγου θεασείης</note>. All Manuscripts have *fire-crashing* <note>original: πυρισμαράγου</note>, which I have restored. Fabricius wrote *divine nature* based on a guess. Manuscripts L and G have *you might be divine*. CT has only *you might go*. The word I have provided, *revelry* or *membership in a sacred troop* <note>original: θιασεύης</note>, is a term used elsewhere by Proclus in his *Hymn to the Sun*, line 21:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and from your gentle-giving, evil-averting *revelry*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;the Healer sprouted forth.
&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 70. *breathing*] I wrote this <note>original: πνείουσα</note> following the Manuscripts instead of Fabricius’s version <note>original: πνεύουσα</note>. — Soon after, Manuscript CT reads *the mind having spread out*. I have given *drawn by a torch* <note>original: πυρσοέλκτος</note> from Manuscripts G, C, and T, instead of Fabricius’s *torch-drawn* <note>neuter form: πυρσοέλκτον</note>. In the following verse, Fabricius wrote *of the immortals*; but I had to restore the reading of the Manuscripts, *of the immortal woman* <note>original: ἀθανάτης</note>, which is indeed more obscure, but no less certain for that reason. For in these mystic pronouncements, obscurity and absurdity are primary and necessary elements. <note>The author suggests that "mystic" or "theurgic" texts were intentionally difficult or ungrammatical to reflect their divine, non-human origin.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 70. *he might be of the Hermetic chain*] <note>The "Chain of Hermes" refers to a lineage of teachers and souls inspired by the god of wisdom.</note> Fabricius wrote *to be*. The Manuscripts offered *he might be*. Regarding the *chain* <note>original: σειρᾷ</note>, see above on page 121.
&nbsp;&nbsp;P. 70. *of Nicomachus*] „Understand this to be Nicomachus of Gerasa <note>A famous Pythagorean mathematician</note>, on whose *Introduction to Arithmetic* another certain Proclus of Laodicea, a High Priest, wrote a commentary, if we believe Suidas. Moreover, it is not surprising in a Platonic and Pythagorean philosopher that he dreamed the soul of a famous philosopher who had died long ago had fallen to him, and that he believed his own dream. But who would not marvel at Jerome Cardano writing thus about himself in his *Dialogue on Death*: „You cannot deny that a part of the matter (of our body) consists of several portions, so that in me, for example, there is one part of Galen, another part of Iris, another of Themison, and another of Maximus Planudes. These men, therefore, whether you like it or not, have risen again in part.“

It also explores the Neoplatonic belief in the transmigration of souls, noting that Proclus believed he was the reincarnation of the mathematician Nicomachus.</summary>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 125<-

"...in us. But what confusion? Yet how much more worthy of divine wisdom is it that these same [souls] should fully return after several centuries?"
FABRICIUS. <note>original Latin: "in nobis. Sed quae confusio? At quanto dignius divina sapientia eosdem plene post aliquot secula redire?"</note>

**Page 71. "if anyone should have"**] <note>original Greek: ἄν τις ἔχοι</note> Codex C has "has." Codex T has "I have." — A little later, Fabricius had omitted the word **"activities"** <note>original: ἐνεργήματα</note> after **"theurgical."**

**Page 71. "And of one other"**] <note>original: Ἑνὸς δὲ ἄλλου</note> It is said "one other" just as one says "a certain one." Porphyry in the *Life of Plotinus* says: "this man, he said, is the thief, pointing out **a certain one**." Lucian in *The Lapiths*, 34: "with so many philosophers present, not even by chance could one see **a certain one** without fault; but some did shameful things, while others (more correctly in Codex 1041: 'but others') said even more shameful things." Longus, Book II, p. 47 of Sch.: "and **a certain one**, picking up a dog-collar." Musaeus, line 64:
> "and **a certain** eye of Hero’s,
> laughing, bloomed with a hundred Graces."
Add Aesop, from the edition of the most learned Furia, f. 53, 56, 65, 127. Synesius used the phrase "one is no one" <note>original: εἷς μηδείς</note> in *On Kingship*, p. 3 D: "and I agree with you that such a greatness of rule is present to **no single person**."

**Page 71. "Asclepigenia — Plutarcha"**] Codices C and T read "of Plutarch."
"This Asclepigenia was the daughter of Asclepigenia Plutarcha—the daughter of Plutarch mentioned in the previous chapter—and of Archiadas, who is called the **descendant** <note>original: ἔκγονος</note> of Plutarch above on p. 29 [ch. 12], meaning son-in-law, as we noted at that passage."
FABRICIUS.

**Page 71. "to the fathers"**] Compare above, p. 75.

**Page 71. "to heal the impossible"**] <note>original: ἰάσασθαι ἀδυνάτῳ</note> Philostratus in *On Heroes*, p. 4: "the sea is **not easy to sail**," where the Scholiast <note>an ancient commentator</note> notes: "to be sailed." In a note attached to this passage, I illustrated that method of taking the infinitive in a passive sense. Compare the illustrious Gail on Thucydides, Book I, ch. 42, p. 49. Dio Chrysostom, Oration XIII, p. 422: "some strange oracle and **not easy to interpret**." Sophocles in *Philoctetes*: "for they are **piteous to nourish**" <note>meaning 'piteous to be fed'</note>.

**Page 71. "painfully"**] <note>original: ὀδυνηρῶς</note> Fabricius had "painful." I have restored the spelling of the Codices. Next, I added **"as was likely"** from the Codices and from Holstenius in D’Orville’s notes on Chariton, p. 413. Instead of **"despairing"** <note>original: ἀπογινωσκόντων</note>, I wrote the corrected form <note>original: ἀπογιγνωσκόντων</note> from Codex C.

**Page 72. "to the last anchor"**] "Erasmus [Adages I. 1. 24] mentions 'to loosen the sacred anchor' <note>a proverb meaning to resort to the final hope or resource</note>." FABRICIUS.

**Page 72. "to the savior"**] <note>original: ἐπὶ σωτῆρα</note> "Just as the Gods (compare Reinef. *Inscriptions*, p. 29), and among them Aesculapius <note>Asclepius, the god of healing</note> (as soon seen in our author; see Spanheim on Aristophanes' *Plutus*, 1176), and then kings, princes, and doctors (for example in Aristaenetus I. 13), so all <term>benefactors</term> <note>original: evergetae; refers to the Greek practice of 'euergetism' where wealthy individuals funded public works</note> and those who bring help to afflicted circumstances are commonly called **saviors** <note>original: σωτῆρες</note> by the ancients. See the illustrious Spanheim’s fifth dissertation on the excellence and use of coins."

<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, Asclepigenia, Plutarch the Younger, Fabricius, Greek grammar, Savior (Soter), textual criticism, Erasmus, philology</keywords>

NOTES ON MARINUS. 127

And give, O King, unshakable prosperity from lovely piety,
if you are willing; for you can accomplish everything
easily, you who possess mighty and infinite strength.
But if anything destructive comes to us by the spinning spindles of fate
under star-whirling threads,
you yourself ward this off with your great force.
<note>This is a poetic prayer to a deity (likely Helios or Apollo) found in Marinus's biography of the philosopher Proclus. Original Greek: Ὄλβον δ’ ἀστυφέλικτον... τόδε ῥιπῇ.</note>

P. 73. **as a god**] <note>original: ὥστε θεὸς</note> Regarding the use of *as* <note>original: ὥστε</note> instead of *just as* <note>original: ὥσπερ</note>, see the notes on Longus, page 333, by Schaefer, who does not easily tolerate this usage in Marinus. In Longus, Book I, page 7, the phrase "fearing she would be outdone by the sheep" was left untouched by the excellent man <note>referring to the editor, likely Villoison or Courier</note> who oversaw the splendid edition of this romance writer, printed in 1802 by the most famous printer P. Didot. Villoison's suggested correction to *just as* <note>original: ὥσπερ</note>, already accepted by Schaefer, was also accepted by Courier, a man most learned in Greek, in his brilliant and most complete edition of Longus. However, it should be noted that the best manuscripts he used, the Florentine and the Vatican, both contain the original word *as* <note>original: ὥστε</note>.

P. 73. **he was walking**] Fabricius wrote "he walked" <note>original: ἐβάδισεν (aorist tense)</note>; but the manuscripts G, L, and C have "he was walking" <note>original: ἐβάδιζεν (imperfect tense)</note>, which I have preferred.

P. 73. **dissolved**] <note>original: λελυμένην</note> Manuscripts C and T read "forgotten" <note>original: λελησμένην</note>.

P. 74. **good fortunes**] "Marinus had said earlier on page 4 [chapter 2] that Proclus was surrounded by and overflowed with the goods of fortune on all sides. To this, add what Photius reports from Damascius (page 536 [p. 1057]): the income of the school over which Proclus presided was significantly increased by various legacies, so that the philosopher became wealthier from it by more than a thousand coins—that is, <term>thalers</term> <note>A silver coin used throughout Europe for centuries; the commentator is using it here to give a contemporary equivalent to ancient currency.</note>—every year." FABRICIUS.

P. 74. **the father — the grandfather**] "The father refers not to the man who had biologicaly fathered him, but to the one who had instructed him; and 'grandfather' is used for the teacher of one's own teacher. See Theophilus Raynaud's work *On Sacred Paternity*, volume 11 of his collected works." FABRICIUS. — Shortly before the word "himself" <note>original: αὐτὸς</note>, I removed "and" <note>original: καὶ</note>, which is not found in the manuscripts.

P. 74. **from Sophocles**] "Pausanias, in the first book of his *Description of Greece* (Attica), chapter 21, mentions a statue of Sophocles placed in the theater at Athens, and shortly after in the same chapter he discusses the nearby temple of <term>Asclepius</term> <note>The Greek god of medicine and healing.</note>. Philostratus, in book 3, chapter 5 [chapter 17, p. 109] of the *Life of Apollonius*, records that Sophocles' hymn to Asclepius used to be sung in Athens. The same Pausanias says in chapter 20: 'The most ancient sanctuary of Dionysus is near the theater.' He discusses the Acropolis in chapter 26; to this add Meursius's *Cecropia*." FABRICIUS.

P. 75. **school of thought**] <note>original: αἵρεσις</note> "See above, page 6." FABRICIUS. — Shortly after, I wrote "he showed" <note>original: ἐδήλωσεν</note> instead of the shorter form <note>original: ἐδήλωσε</note>, following the manuscripts.
