128 ->NOTES ON MARINUS<-

**Page 75. In the Parthenon** <note>original: ἐν Παρθενῶνι</note>] “The Temple of Minerva <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Athena</note> in Athens, built by the architect Ictinus around the time of Pericles, as witnessed by Pausanias in his *Attica* and *Arcadia*.” — **FABRICIUS** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a famous classical scholar and previous editor of this text</note>

**Page 75. By those who move even the immovable things** <note>original: ὑπὸ τῶν καὶ τὰ ἀκίνητα κινούντων</note>] I have added the word *and* <note>original: καὶ (kai)</note> from the Manuscripts. A marginal note in Manuscript C says: *He seems to me to be hinting at us Christians.* “He censures the Christians, by whom he had also hinted the temple of Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine; his temple in Athens was a major site of pagan worship</note> was overturned a little earlier. Moreover, Marinus writes with the same faith that Minerva commended her own image to Proclus as Zosimus (in Book 5) records: that Alaric, when about to occupy Athens, was deterred by a vision of an armed Minerva walking the walls of the city and Achilles standing before the fortifications. Meursius explodes this little fable in *Fortuna Attica* (last chapter), as does Baronius at the Year of Christ 395.” — **FABRICIUS**. *To move the immovable* <note>original: τὰ ἀκίνητα κινεῖν</note> was a proverb, regarding which see Erasmus (I. 6. 61) and Henri Estienne’s *Schedules* (I. 22). It is a Platonic expression. Compare Heindorf on Plato’s *Theaetetus* (p. 419) and the *Scholia on Plato* (p. 238). Porphyry says in *On Abstinence* (I. 4): *those who speak against justice say it is thrown into confusion and that the immovable is moved.* Compare the passage from Synesius above (p. 102) taken from the Manuscript.

**Page 76. As is necessary** <note>original: ὡς χρὴ</note>] I have given *as* <note>original: ὡς (hos)</note> instead of *that* <note>original: ὅτι (hoti)</note>, following the Manuscripts. — Soon after, instead of *to prepare beforehand* <note>original: προπαρασκευάζειν</note>, Manuscripts C and T have *to prepare* <note>original: παρασκευάζειν</note>.

**Page 76. Manifestation of the god** <note>original: ἐπιφάνεια τοῦ θεοῦ</note>] Chapter 32. *The god of the Adroteni welcomed him kindly with manifestations.* — *The manifestation was somewhat divine.*

**Page 76. From which** <note>original: ἀφ’ ἧς</note>] “Namely, from the manifestation.” — **FABRICIUS**. On the contrary, it refers to the **head**. <note>original: κεφαλῆς (kephalēs)</note>. The disease had begun from the head. The relation of the pronoun is entirely obvious. A similar explanation of a pronoun in the text of Sopatrus himself (prolegomena to Aristides, column 3) was not to be accepted: *When Antoninus sent him, he came saying that, "I did not meet you for this reason, because I do not need the things which others need," instead of money.* I am completely deleting these last three words, placed in the margin by a diligent reader who was explaining the word *which* <note>original: ὧν (hōn)</note> to himself, and they should be returned to the margin. I will similarly attempt Demosthenes in the *Funeral Oration* (§ 1): *The city, knowing... that all desire is for virtue and praise, thought they should honor them with these words, from which these things might best happen for them.* Here too, I believe the word *words* <note>original: λόγων (logōn)</note> should be eliminated, as it is a gloss <note>An explanatory note added by a later scribe that accidentally slipped into the main text</note> on the pronoun *which*.

**Page 76. Of a certain respite** <note>original: ἀνακωχῆς τινὸς</note>] Compare Pierson on Moeris (p. 86).

**Page 76. Completely** <note>original: τέλεον</note>] Fabricius edited this as *end* <note>original: τέλος (telos)</note>. I had to restore *completely*, which Manuscripts G, C, and T contain. Nothing was noted from the Laurentian manuscript.

7.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s "Life of Proclus," focusing on textual variants between different manuscripts and previous editions, specifically the edition by Fabricius.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 129<-

**P. 77. worthy of memory]** <note>original: "μνήμης ἄξια"</note> Instead of "memory" <note>original: "mnēmēs"</note>, which is the reading found in the Manuscripts, Fabricius wrote "however" <note>original: "mentoi"</note>, undoubtedly through a slip of the pen. In his notes on Chariton (p. 604), Dorvillius proposed either "memory" in place of "however," or, if "however" is kept, he suggested "however, of memory."

**P. 77. through sympathy, not without tears]** <note>original: "ὑπὸ συμπαθείας οὐκ ἄνευ δακρύων"</note> “Because of his great sympathy <term>sympathy: here meaning a spiritual or emotional resonance</term> regarding divine matters [etc.] see below, p. 83 [chapter 32].” — **FABRICIUS**.

**P. 77. the disease of arthritis]** <note>original: "ἀρθρῖτις νόσος"</note> Fabricius published this as *arthritēs*. However, the Florence, Gotha, and Copenhagen manuscripts <note>abbreviated as LGC</note> read *arthrītis*. Manuscript T reads *arthrytis*. The Dorvillius Manuscript (in notes to Chariton, p. 625) reads *arthrītis*, which I have adopted. Mitscherlichius, in his notes on the *Hymns of Proclus* (p. 17), having found *arthrīpēs* in the London edition—which was a typographical error for *arthrītēs*—proposed *arthrōdēs* <note>joint-like</note> disease. The Dorvillius Manuscript uses the word *nousos* <note>an archaic/Ionic form of "disease"</note>. This Ionism, which Marinus does not typically use, is better attributed to Heraclitus, whose fifth letter begins: "We are sick, Amphidamas, with the disease <note>original: "nouson"</note> of dropsy." Manuscript 1760 also uses *nouson*. You should likewise restore *nouson* instead of *noson* from that same Manuscript at the beginning of the sixth Letter.

**P. 77. loving and accustomed to]** <note>original: "φιλοῦσα καὶ εἰωθυῖα"</note> Manuscript T reads *eiōtheia*. “An ancient author of a small book on <term>podagra: gout, specifically in the foot</term>, translated into Latin by Musurus, says: ‘this disease renders itself so domestic and familiar that, if faith is to be placed in the writings of the ancients, it becomes hereditary and is taken up by great-grandchildren through a series of successions, unless a safe and sober diet and frequent, well-timed purgings offer a remedy.’” — **FABRICIUS**.

**P. 77. perceiving]** <note>original: "αἰσθόμενος"</note> Fabricius’s edition has "burning" <note>original: "aithomenos"</note>, which Dorvillius corrected from his Manuscript (notes on Chariton, p. 588). The London edition correctly reads "perceiving," as do my Manuscripts. — Shortly after, I wrote "from some people" <note>original: "para tinōn"</note> (plural), which is found in Manuscripts G, C, and T, instead of Fabricius’s "from someone" <note>original: "para tinos"</note> (singular). The Laurentian manuscript is silent here.

**P. 77. small tablet]** <note>original: "πτυγμάτιον"</note> Here and below, Manuscript C reads *pygmation*. Manuscript T twice reads *pogmation*.

**P. 78. of healing]** <note>original: "παιώνειος"</note> Fabricius has *paiōnios*. I have accepted the spelling found in the Manuscripts.

**P. 78. of the disease.]** <note>original: "νόσου"</note> Thus the Manuscripts, not *nousou* <note>the Ionic form</note> which Fabricius published and which is found in the Dorvillius volume (notes on Chariton, p. 625).

**P. 78. to reveal something clear to him, having slept he saw]** <note>original: "φῆναί τι αὐτῷ σαφὲς, καθευδήσας εἶδε"</note> Manuscripts C and T read "to reveal *someone* <note>original: "tina"</note> to him clearly, having slept..."

**P. 78. and he saw]** <note>original: "εἶδε δὲ"</note> Manuscript T reads *eide* <note>without the movable 'n'</note>. Manuscripts G, C, and T read "indeed" <note>original: "dē"</note> instead of "and/but" <note>original: "de"</note>, which Schaeferus approves of; however, as we have seen above, "and/but" <note>original: "de"</note> is aptly placed after a parenthesis.

**P. 78. leaning over toward the legs]** <note>original: "ἐπικύψαντα εἰς τὰ σκέλη"</note> Fabricius wrote *skela*. I have provided what is read in Manuscripts C and T. An unpublished Scholiast on Philostratus...

130 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->NOTES ON MARINUS.<-

<note>Marinus of Neapolis was a 5th-century philosopher and biographer of Proclus.</note>

**Illustration I. 2.** The leg: the part from the ankle to the knee; shins: the front parts of the legs; calves: the back parts. <note>original: "σκέλος, τὸ ἀπὸ τοῦ ἀστραγάλου μέχρι τοῦ γόνατος· κνῆμαι, τὰ ἔμπροσθεν τῶν ποδῶν, ἀντικνήμια, τὰ ὄπισθεν."</note> — "More correctly, Eunapius, speaking of Iamblichus, denies that he wished to write more paradoxical and more monstrous things <note>original: "παραδοξότερα καὶ τερατωδέστερα"</note>, and that he handed down those very things which he had produced out of fear, lest perhaps they were mere inventions, even if supported by the testimonies of those who affirmed they had seen them with their own eyes." **FABRICIUS.** <note>Johann Albert Fabricius was a famous 18th-century classical scholar.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P. 78. ...refusing to kiss the knees... to kiss]** Fabricius read "having refused," which I have corrected based on the Manuscripts. Compare Abresch’s *Readings on Aristaenetus*, page 76.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P. 79. ...he arrived at a deep old age]** Compare the note on Eunapius, page 171. An anonymous author on <term>physiognomy|the study of a person's character or personality from their outer appearance</term> in Manuscript 2991 A. says: "Wrinkled cheeks in a man indicate wickedness, unless caused by deep old age." But why shouldn't I include the entire short work? I believe there are many scholars and students of <term>conjectural science|theories based on observation and inference rather than hard proof</term>—in which <note>Johann Kaspar</note> Lavater became famous—who will read these things with pleasure.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->**The Natural Character-Signs of Man.**<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A pointed head is full of folly. A small head is senseless, even if it is round. A large head is full of knowledge, and especially so if it is round. Not every bald man is wise, just as not every man with a thick beard is.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;High eyebrows are a sign of haughtiness. Eyebrows that are frequently raised indicate conceit. <margin>1)</margin> One eyebrow being raised while the other is lowered is a sign of curiosity. <margin>2)</margin> To have no eyebrows at all is a sign of a quick-tempered person. To have very thick eyebrows is a sign of a soft character. To have eyebrows that are widely separated is a sign of both a quick-tempered and a cowardly person.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Black eyes are a sign of a good person, if they are somewhat large. Eyes with double pupils are a sign of an unstable and fickle person, especially if the double pupils are in the same eye. Bloodshot eyes, if they are otherwise healthy, are a sign of courage. Yellowish eyes indicate <unclear>madness</unclear> <margin>3)</margin>. Eyes that have a little blue-gray but more white indicate a greedy character, a lecher, and a dishonest person. Constantly rheumy <note>watery or crusty</note> eyes produce foolishness. Sharp, fierce eyes are a sign of instability, if they are always moving. Naturally distorted eyes that are not caused by [disease...]

***

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;1) The Manuscript reads "of conceit."
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;2) I wonder if this explains the passage in Philostratus’s *Heroicus*, page 188, in the description of Paris: "the one eyebrow rose above the eye."
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;3) A gap in the text. It seems it should be filled with "suffering" or "disease."

<meta>This page continues a series of observations on physiognomy—the ancient practice of judging a person's character from their physical appearance—specifically commenting on a work related to Marinus.</meta>

->### NOTES ON MARINUS. 131<-

... [of character], it is a sign of courage. Eyes that turn away from the light, unless caused by discharge <note>original: λήμης; referring to rheum or "sleep" in the eyes caused by infection or irritation</note>, are a sign of malice and a lack of manliness. Eyes that feel heavy indicate a person who is negligent. Eyes that are frequently covered by the eyelids indicate cowardice and a difficult disposition. If the right eye is larger than the left, it signifies one who chooses what is good. If the left eye is larger than the right, it indicates an evildoer and a wicked person. If someone closes the right eye while speaking, unless it is done by some trick, they are dishonest; $^1$) they are less so, however, when they close the left. Eyes that look intently $^2$) and without blinking indicate an impudent and shameless person, especially if the person is a woman. If someone closes one of their eyes while speaking, it indicates that they possess wickedness and a meddlesome nature. Eyes that do not look upward indicate that the soul possesses a sense of shame.

Deep hollows under the eyes in both a man and a woman indicate a base person; and especially in the case of women, if these areas are black, they indicate that the woman is in a state of natural impurity <note>This likely refers to menstruation or the postpartum period, which ancient writers often classified as a state of ritual or physical "uncleanness."</note>. If these areas are full and protruding, they indicate a person of frivolous thoughts. However, if someone has these deep hollows while they are fasting, it is nothing unusual.

Cheeks the color of blood indicate a person who is soft, slow, and well-meaning; but if they are thick and well-nourished, they indicate, above all, a coward. Cheeks that are sallow <note>original: κιῤῥαὶ; tawny or yellowish-pale</note> and thin indicate a person who is shifty, prone to anger, abrupt, and difficult, unless they became that way through the practice of self-restraint <note>The author allows that a "difficult" appearance might actually be the result of asceticism or religious fasting.</note>. Deep cheeks indicate a difficult and shifty person. Wrinkled cheeks in a man indicate a wicked person, unless they are caused by extreme old age. Cheeks that are bare of hair <note>meaning a man who cannot grow a beard or is naturally hairless on the face</note> indicate an utterly wicked person.

132 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ->NOTES ON MARINUS<-
<note>original Latin: "NOTAE IN MARINVM." These are likely scholarly commentaries on a text by or about a figure named Marinus.</note>

If someone has a **mole** <note>original Greek: φακὸν (phakon) – literally a "lentil," used to describe a mole or freckle</note> on their lips, they are ill-tempered and a liar; if one has this near the eye, they are envious; if one has this on the hand, they are ungenerous, and especially so if it is on the right hand.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A large nose is a sign of a **cunning person** <note>original: πολυμήτου (polymētou) – a term famously applied to the clever hero Odysseus, meaning "of many schemes"</note>; a small nose is a sign of a wicked person. A knobby nose is a sign of an irritable person. An eagle-like nose <note>original: ἱερακοειδὴς (hierakoeidēs) – hawk-like or aquiline</note> is a sign of one who loves power, a boaster, a fool, and a narcissist.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A **forked beard** is a sign of a sensible person. A sharp beard is a sign of simplicity. A beard growing long hair is a sign of a mindless and arrogant person, especially if the hair is thick. A beard having thin and short hair <unclear>***</unclear> <note>The source text contains asterisks here, indicating a gap or missing word in the original manuscript</note>. A beard that is completely hairless is a sign of a wicked and shifty person.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A **curved nose** is a sign of an arrogant person.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A **long neck** is a sign of simplicity, provided it is on a well-proportioned body. A short neck is a sign of courage. A neck leaning to the right reveals a straightforward person. A bent neck, unless caused by an ailment, reveals a person of counsel and reflection. A neck leaning to the left reveals an evildoer and a wicked person. A neck tilted back and stretching upwards reveals a vain character.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Shoulders** that move continuously, unless due to disease, show that a person is uneducated. Broad shoulders show a courageous man, while narrow ones show a weak and fading one.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Large and fleshy muscles** indicate a strong man; conversely, small and lean ones indicate the opposite.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Long forearms**, if they are not in proportion to the rest of the body, are a sign of cowardice; short ones that are not in proportion are a sign of strength.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Large, bony wrists** indicate a strong man; small ones indicate a weakling.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Fleshy palms** <note>original: Ταρσοὶ χειρὸς (tarsoi cheiros) – referring here to the broad surface or "frame" of the hand</note> indicate a soft and weak man; those with little flesh and lean indicate a strong man.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Smooth nails** are a sign of a straightforward person, while knobby ones are a sign of a base person; healthy-colored <note>original: σαρκοειδεῖς (sarkoeideis) – literally "flesh-like" or pinkish</note> nails are better than black or tawny ones.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A **broad chest** is a sign of courage and wisdom. A hairy chest indicates courage; it also partakes of some anger, as does one that is entirely hairless.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A man’s **lean breasts** are a sign of strength and health; larger ones are a sign of softness and sluggishness.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; A man’s **swollen belly** indicates that the owner is soft, slow, and difficult to manage. A belly that seems to "stick to the kidneys" <note>An ancient idiom for a very flat or hollow stomach</note> is a sign of great strength.

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 133<-

Fleshy hips and buttocks indicate someone who is physically strong but lazy, while lean buttocks indicate a person skilled in a <term>craft</term> <note>original: "technē," referring to a specialized trade or art</note>.

Short and fleshy shins indicate a person who is both vigorous and fast; the same is also true of those whose shins are sinewy and lean.

Large and bony ankles indicate a strong person, while their opposites indicate a slack or weak character.

Feet that walk straight indicate a capable person; however, those who walk headlong or with twisted feet are uneducated and senseless.

Feet that are out of proportion <note>specifically, too large for the rest of the body</note> indicate a foolish and incapable person; those described as <term>crescent-shaped</term> <note>likely referring to flat feet or a specific curve of the sole</note> indicate an unlucky and unfit individual.

A body completely free of hair indicates an irritable temper; the same is true for one that is excessively hairy. The middle state is the good one.

Feet that are beyond the proper measure and proportion indicate either great strength or folly; this sign must be weighed and judged against the other indicators of the body.

A narrow waist is a sign of an unmanly person, while a wide waist shows courage; the same rule applies to the shoulders.

A man who is short, hunched, thin-haired, and stooped—resembling an ape in character—is meddlesome, wicked, troublesome, greedy, and fond of arguing. A man who resembles a woman in appearance is unmanly, soft, and small-minded. A person with a <term>sallow</term> <note>original: "chloros," which can mean pale green, yellow, or sickly pale</note> complexion, whether man or woman, is irritable and defensive. A person with a <term>fiery</term> <note>original: "pyrros," meaning red-haired or ruddy-faced</note> complexion, man or woman, is irritable, murderous, and a babbler of idle theories. A person of very small stature is boastful, pugnacious, and troublesome. A man who is tall and lean is prudent and courageous, provided he has other fitting traits; but if he is excessively lean and tall, he is foolish and weak. A man with a narrow waist and fleshless loins is, for the most part, weak and mindless. A man whose entire back is in proportion to his shoulders is courageous. A short-armed man is rarely courageous. A man who naturally resembles a <term>eunuch</term> <note>referring here to physical softness or lack of secondary male characteristics</note> is most base. A man who constantly guffaws and laughs is most senseless. A man who merely smiles while laughing is sensible. A man who is pensive is best in counsel and speech. A man who never laughs at all, unless commanded to do so, is worthless. A man who excessively adorns himself is either lewd or uneducated. A man with a face that is too "pretty" is licentious or foolish. A man who is rash in speech is prone to errors. A man of few words is quick-witted and accurate when he speaks. A man with excessively coarse hair is foolish, though you might occasionally find such a man to be courageous. A man who swaggers and acts haughty when he walks is foolish and idle. A man who, while walking, leans forward...

<meta>This page continues the detailed philological commentary on Marinus’s biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, comparing various manuscript readings against earlier printed editions.</meta>

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 135

**P. 80. "and saying"** <note>original Greek: καὶ λέγοντας</note> — I have added these two words from the Manuscripts. Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a prolific scholar who produced an earlier edition of this text.</note> had not neglected to translate them.

**P. 80. "at the temple"** <note>original Greek: πρὸς τῷ νεῷ</note> — So the Manuscripts. Fabricius, who wrote "to the god" <note>original: θεῷ (theō)</note> by a slip of the pen, nonetheless translated it as "temple."

**P. 80. "to those very persons"** <note>original Greek: αὐτοῖς ἐκείνοις</note> — Fabricius wrote "they themselves" <note>original: αὐτοὶ (autoi)</note>, which I have changed following the Manuscripts.

**P. 81. "therefore from these"** <note>original Greek: ἐκ τούτων οὖν</note> — Regarding the use of the word "therefore" <note>original: οὖν (oun)</note> after a parenthesis, see the discussion above on page 119.

**P. 81. "having been recorded and [besought]"** <note>original Greek: ἱστορημένοις καὶ δεηθ.</note> — I provided "having been recorded" <note>original: ἱστορημένοις (istorēmenois)</note> from the Manuscripts instead of "being recorded" <note>original: ἱστορουμένοις (istoroumenois)</note>, which is what Fabricius has. Compare the note above on page 66. Additionally, the word "and" <note>original: καὶ (kai)</note> had to be added based on the Manuscripts.

**P. 81. "What then?"** <note>original Greek: Τί δαί;</note> — [Or] "What's that?" <note>original: Τί δέ;</note>. Aristaenetus I. 22 uses "What's that?"; Valckenaer, in his notes on the *Phoenician Women* line 621, would prefer "What then?"; though he changes other elements in this passage of Aristaenetus less necessarily, and the reasoning of Abresch is pleasing. Basil the Scholiast <note>A "scholiast" is an ancient or medieval commentator who wrote explanatory notes in the margins of manuscripts.</note> on Gregory Nazianzen, in the unpublished Codex 573, folio 26a, writes: "Technical writers record the conjunction following interrogative particles with the 'ai' diphthong." I owe this reference to my friend Bast <note>Friedrich Jacob Bast (1771–1811), a scholar known for his expertise in Greek paleography.</note>, who diligently examined the best of Basil’s Scholia. Compare also Coray on Heliodorus page 361, and Fischer on Plato’s *Euthyphro* 4, page 15.

**P. 81. "Have you not heard Iamblichus?"** <note>original Greek: Ἰαμβλίχου οὐκ ἀκήκοας</note> — "Proclus could not have literally 'heard' Iamblichus <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a foundational Neoplatonist philosopher who died nearly a century before Proclus (412–485 AD) was born.</note>, given the great interval of time separating him from Iamblichus's era; rather, he learned these things from Iamblichus’s writings, such as perhaps the work *On the Gods* or a similar book. Furthermore, Aristotle (not the 'Stagirite' <note>The author clarifies this is not the famous Aristotle of Stagira, but a different ancient writer of the same name who wrote a work called the *Peplus*.</note>) in the *Peplus* calls the sons of Asclepius celebrated by Homer—Podalirius and Machaon—'participants in the gods'; and Lycophron relates that the Daunians, sleeping on sheepskins at the tomb of Podalirius, received oracles through their dreams. What wonder is it, then, that these same Gods showed themselves even to the people of Adrotta, and chose their homeland to be honored by their presence, even if they were unknown and less recognized by the common people? Moreover, whoever believes that Achilles rose from his tomb for the sake of Apollonius <note>Referring to Apollonius of Tyana, a 1st-century sage and wonder-worker; his biography by Philostratus was often compared to the lives of saints or philosophers.</note> and informed him about various matters, as found in Philostratus Book IV, chapter 5, will also easily be persuaded that Proclus was taught by the god Asclepius regarding those protective Gods of the Adrotteni. Indeed, the same person will with less trouble digest similar fables in the [Christian] legends, in which Saints are said to have indicated the location of their monuments or relics to pious men through dreams. What follows immediately—that Proclus was praised by a deity in specific words—may seem less incredible here because of what they relate about Saint Francis being praised by the mouth of Christ. But it cannot be said..."

136          NOTES ON MARINUS.

,, [One] can see how great a similarity exists between pagan fables and those of the Papacy <note>The author is comparing the "miracles" of Catholic saints with those of ancient non-Christian philosophers to suggest they are equally mythological.</note>, which, to avoid being tedious, I shall declare with one further example. Maffei reports that [Ignatius] Loyola <note>original: "Loyolam"; the founder of the Jesuit Order (1491–1556).</note> prayed with such fervor of spirit that his bodily weight was snatched up nearly four cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger (approx. 18 inches or 45 cm).</note> high from the earth. Similarly, Philostratus, in Book 3, Chapter 5 [c. 17], affirms concerning the Brahmans <note>original: "Brachmanibus"; ancient Indian philosophers and priests.</note> that during their religious and mystical dances they were lifted two cubits <note>original: "εἰς δίπηχυ" (eis dipēkhu)</note> into the air. But lest you complain further with Meric Casaubon (in his book *On Enthusiasm*) that the Brahmans were inferior to Loyola, here are the words of Eunapius [p. 13], by which he testifies that Iamblichus <note>A late Roman Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) famous for his work on theurgy or "god-working."</note> was raised high from the earth more than ten cubits: *that while praying to the gods he was raised up more than ten cubits from the ground, as it seemed.* <note>original: "ὡς εὐχόμενος τοῖς θεοῖς μετεωρίζηται μὲν ἀπὸ τῆς γῆς πλέον ἢ δέκα πήχεις εἰκάζεσθαι"</note> Bodin, in Book 2 of the manuscript *Colloquium Heptaplomeres*, says: *I remember, when I was in Spain, that Magdalena de la Cruz, the head of the Monastery of Córdoba, obtained a pardon from Pope Paul III; she had slept with a dark nightmare demon for more than 30 years, yet she displayed such integrity of life and religion that she was carried high off the ground during the sacred rites as if by an angel—which Porphyry narrates also happened to Iamblichus while he was performing the rites.*"
FABRICIUS. <note>Refers to Johann Albert Fabricius, a renowned 18th-century German classical scholar.</note>

   P. 82. **speaking**] <note>original: "λέγοντες" (legontes)</note> So in Manuscripts G, C, and T. Fabricius’s edition has **it is said**. <note>original: "λέγονται" (legontai)</note>

   P. 83. **Because of the**] <note>original: "Διὰ δὲ τὴν" (Dia de tēn)</note> Fabricius reads **Because also of the**. <note>original: "δ. δ. καὶ τὴν" (dia de kai tēn)</note> But the Manuscripts do not have the "also" [and]; and Fabricius himself above on page 129 cited these words without the conjunction.

   P. 83. **he was entirely moved to tears**] <note>original: "πάντως εἰς δάκρυα ὑπήγετο" (pantōs eis dakrya hypēgeto)</note> Manuscripts C and T read **all** <note>original: "πάντας" (pantas)</note>. But the common reading is correct. Compare the beginning of chapter 31.

   P. 83. **I would wish**] <note>original: "ἐθέλοιμι" (ethelomi)</note> Manuscript C reads **I wish**. <note>original: "θέλοιμι" (thelomi)</note>

   P. 83. **Pan, the son of Hermes**] <note>original: "τὸν Πᾶνα τὸν τοῦ Ἑρμοῦ" (ton Pana ton tou Hermou)</note> Fabricius reads **that of Hermes**. <note>original: "τὸν Ἑρμοῦ" (ton Hermou)</note> Manuscripts C and T read **Pan of Hermes**. <note>original: "τὸν Π. τοῦ Ἑ."</note> I ought to have kept both Articles. Compare the note on Philostratus's *Heroica* page 343 and on Eunapius page 230.

   P. 83. **salvation, which he obtained**] <note>original: "σωτηρίαν, ἧς ἔτυχεν" (sōtērian, hēs etykhen)</note> Manuscripts L and G read **of salvation**. <note>original: "σωτηρίας" (sōtērias)</note> Fabricius has **obtained** [without the final 'n'], but the Manuscripts have **obtained** [with the final 'n']. <note>The editor is noting a minor grammatical variation (movable nu) common in Greek manuscripts.</note>

   P. 83. **from his mother**] <note>original: "παρὰ τῆς μητρὸς" (para tēs mētros)</note> ,, Above on page 45 [chapter 19], Marinus had narrated that Proclus purified himself assiduously every month with the rites of the Mother of the Gods." <note>Referring to Cybele, the Phrygian deity.</note> FABRICIUS.

   P. 83. **indeed especially**] <note>original: "δὴ μάλιστα" (dē malista)</note> Manuscript T is without the "indeed" [dē].

   P. 84. **of which**] <note>original: "ὧν" (hōn)</note> Manuscripts C and T read **such as**. <note>original: "οἷον" (hoion)</note> — Soon, instead of **I have now quite** <note>original: "ἔχω γε νῦν πάνυ" (ekhō ge nyn panyu)</note> Manuscript T has **I have quite indeed**. Manuscript C has **I have indeed quite**. This lack of the "now" [nyn] reminds me of a passage in Themistius suffering from a similar defect in Oration II, page 28 D: *my money is ridiculous, and my poverty great, and the droplet is less than...* (The Manuscript... <note>The sentence cuts off at the bottom of the page.</note>

# NOTES ON MARINUS. 137

Valesianus <note>referring to the scholar Henri Valois</note> inserts "the Ocean," not only as many as I possess (the Codex adds "now"), but also as many as his father once [did].

**P. 84. the unexpected nature of the writing**] I do not clearly see how that could have been able to hinder the memory. — A little below, C and T <note>Manuscript designations</note> have **and** before **maternal**.

**P. 84. not without divine possession**] "Suidas <note>The Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> repeats these words from Marinus verbatim in the entry for 'Proclus.'" FABRICIUS; and also in the entry for <term>possession</term> <note>original: katakōchē; a state of being "held" or inspired by a god</note>. In the first passage he has "at hand," in the second "in hand." The lexicographer explains "possession" as "seizure," and brings forward a passage of Damascius in Photius p. 1033, 25: "that it is a divine possession, gently opening and purifying the eyes of the soul." Proclus in *Platonic Theology* Book I, chapter 26, p. 63: "simply all the activities of divine possession." Plato in the *Ion*, p. 82, Müller edition: "for not by art, nor by knowledge do you say what you say about Homer, but by divine fate and possession." Our author above in chapter 23: "not without divine inspiration," in the same sense.

**P. 84. concerning the goddess he revealed**] Manuscript C has "the" <note>masculine article</note>. Suidas has "concerning God." Fabricius has "he revealed" <note>slight spelling variant</note>. The Codices have "he revealed."

**P. 84. Attis**] Manuscripts G, C, and T have "Attes." It seems more correct to be "Attis." Compare the commentators on Lucian, *Dialogues of the Gods* XII. 1., Reimar on Dio Cassius Book LXI, §. 134., and Brunck on Proclus’s *Hymn to the Sun*, verse 25. — "See Julian’s *Fifth Oration*, which is on the Mother of the Gods, and Sallust in the book *On the Gods*, chapter 4." FABRICIUS.

**P. 84. to be disturbed**] Timaeus’s *Lexicon*: "Disturbs: upsets, moves. To disturb within: to move slightly, to upset"; where see the note of the most learned editor. Compare Wolf on Libanius’s *Epistle* 1285.

**P. 85. secretly**] Thus the Manuscripts, not "of secret things," which the Fabrician edition has.

**P. 85. Running over—the—successes**] Fabricius wrongly has "of the" <note>genitive vs. dative case</note>. Compare above p. 96. The same editor has "successes" <note>with a movable 'n'</note>, which I have changed based on Manuscripts C and T.

**P. 85. The beginning became for us—the whole**] "Plato, Book VI of the *Laws* [p. 614 D. Lyon Edition]: 'For the beginning is said in proverbs to be half of every work, and we all praise a good start every time, but it is, as it seems to me, more than half.' This is what we commonly say:
-> *The one who has begun well has half the deed done.* <-
But different from this is the Hesiodic proverb, which with..."

87. "from the"] Manuscript G reads "from those." Soon after, instead of "he was born," manuscripts C and T read "they were born." Manuscript G reads "he was held" <note>original: ἐπέχθη</note>: [as written].

Page 87. "it fell neither among the last nor in the middle"] Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a renowned German classical scholar and bibliographer</note> did not transcribe the first four words, yet he translated them. Manuscript T reads "middle." Manuscript G reads "in the middles."

Page 87. Sun in Aries, at degree] Manuscript C reads Sun 9. Manuscript T reads Sun. Manuscripts G, C, and T use the abbreviation "deg." <note>original: μ, for μορίῳ, meaning portion or degree</note> and so on for each individual zodiac sign. Soon after, manuscripts C and T are without "26." Manuscripts C, T, and G have small lines placed over the numeral letters <note>a standard practice in Greek to distinguish numbers from words</note>. Compare throughout Fabricius's *Prolegomena* <note>introductory remarks or preface</note>. Delambre <note>Jean Baptiste Joseph Delambre (1749–1822), a famous French astronomer and historian of science</note>, a man who is among the very few equally skilled in both Greek literature and astronomy, did not refuse to recalculate—for my sake, as he kindly favors my studies—this <term>horoscope|a calculation of planetary positions at the time of birth</term> of Marinus and the one found in Fabricius's *Prolegomena*; he noted that, since...

NOTES ON MARINUS. 139

Since the position of the sun in the text of Marinus is not given correctly, it should be read as ☉ in <term>Aquarius</term> <note>original Greek: Ὑδροχόῳ</note> at 20 degrees and 26 minutes, which the <term>Fabrician Prolegomena</term> <note>Introductory notes by the scholar Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736)</note> contains; and having dismissed other variations of no great importance, he concludes that <term>Proclus</term> <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher who lived in the 5th century AD</note> was born in Byzantium in the year 412, on February 8th, at nine o'clock in the morning—that is, three hours before noon.

P. 87. ☽ <term>Gemini</term>] <note>original: Διδύμοις</note> Manuscript T has ☽.
P. 87. ♄ <term>Taurus</term>] <note>original: Ταύρῳ</note> Manuscripts G and T have "and Taurus." Manuscript C has "6 degrees Taurus."
P. 87. ♃ <term>Taurus</term>] <note>original: Ταύρῳ</note> Manuscript C has "7 degrees Taurus." Manuscript G has "7 degrees Taurus." Manuscript T has "χ Taurus." And instead of 41, manuscripts C and T have 44.
P. 87. ♂ <term>Sagittarius</term>] <note>original: Τοξότῃ</note> Manuscript C has "9 degrees Sagittarius." Manuscript T has "9 degrees Sagittarius, 29 minutes."
P. 87. ♀ <term>Pisces</term>] <note>original: Ἰχθύσι</note> Manuscripts C and T have ♀ in Pisces.
P. 87. ☿ <term>Aquarius</term>] <note>original: Ὑδροχόῳ</note> Missing in Manuscript G. Manuscripts C and T have "Aquarius": both without the planetary sign.
P. 88. <term>Ascendant</term>] <note>original: Ὡρόσκοπος; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at the time of birth</note> Manuscripts C and G have the letter φ. Manuscript T has ♃. — After 19, Manuscripts C, T, and G have 40.
P. 88. <term>Midheaven</term>] <note>original: Μεσουράνημα; the highest point in the sky at the time of birth</note> Not found in Manuscripts C and T. Manuscript G has ♃.
P. 88. <term>Aquarius</term>] <note>original: Ὑδροχόῳ</note> Manuscripts C and T have <term>Hydrechoos</term> <note>A variant spelling of Aquarius</note>.
P. 88. <term>In the 124th year</term>] <note>original: τῷ δ' καὶ κ' καὶ ρ' ἔτει</note> I have added the word "year" from Manuscripts L, C, and T. Manuscript C has "in the 126th year." Manuscript T has "in the 124th and into the 126th year."
P. 88. <term>From the reign of Julian</term>] <note>original: ἀπὸ τῆς Ἰουλιανοῦ βασιλείας</note> A <term>Scholium</term> <note>An ancient marginal note</note> in Manuscripts L and G reads: "He is not speaking of Julian the Apostate <note>The Roman Emperor Julian (reigned 361–363 AD)</note>, but of a successor magistrate in Athens chosen by lot." Fabricius, using this Scholium in his Prolegomena, writes "an archon <note>a high official</note> chosen by lot." However, the Scholiast is in error, as Fabricius himself correctly noted in the same place.
P. 88. <term>And of the worship</term>] <note>original: Καὶ θεραπείας</note> I added the conjunction "and" from the Codices <note>handwritten manuscript books</note>, and shortly after, the word "the" before "Athenians" from the same sources.
P. 88. <term>Still surviving</term>] <note>original: ἔτι περιών</note> He speaks this way again a little further down. The Scholiast of Philostratus’s *Heroicus*, page 304, says: "surviving... is used instead of living, as in: the man survives."
P. 89. <term>Wandering about</term>] <note>original: περινοστῶν</note> "Pilgrimages." — FABRICIUS.
P. 89. <term>Souls</term>] <note>original: ψυχὰς</note> I added this from the Codices. — Immediately after, Manuscript T has "of those set apart."
P. 89. <term>Of those who practiced philosophy</term>] <note>original: φιλοσοφησάντων</note> "All Saints." — FABRICIUS.
P. 89. <term>Most holy</term>] <note>original: εὐαγέστατος</note> In Fabricius's printed edition, it appears as "most accursed" <note>original: ἐναγέστατος; a likely printing error where a single letter changed 'holy' to 'cursed'</note>.
P. 89. <term>Of men</term>] <note>original: ἀνθρώπων</note> "All Souls." — FABRICIUS.
P. 89. <term>He performed the sacred rites</term>] <note>original: ἀφωσιοῦτο</note> Chapter 38, "I may perform the sacred rites." On this word, see Wyttenbach’s notes on Plato’s *Phaedo*, page 127.
P. 90. <term>Carried out for burial</term>] <note>original: ἐκκομισθὲν</note> Heraldus illustrates this term in *Adversaria*, page 24, and Kirchmann, who seems to have benefited from him, in *On Funerals*, Book II, Chapter 1. The verb occurs quite frequently in this sense.

# 140 NOTES ON MARINUS.

"To carry out" <note>original Greek: *ekpherein*</note>. Plutarch in *On Education*, chapter 17: "Lifting him up on high, we shall carry him out as quickly as possible." The sentence that follows in Plutarch is not entirely sound. "Someone even brought down a prostitute and acted as a pander for his wife, and has cut away and plundered the provisions for his fathers' old age." I would read it as "stolen away" <note>original: *perikeklophe* instead of *perikekophe*</note>.

**P. 90. in the suburbs** <note>original: *proasteiois*</note>] I added this from the Manuscripts. An unpublished <term>Scholiast</term> <note>an ancient or medieval commentator who wrote notes in the margins of manuscripts</note> on Philostratus’s *Images*, preface: "Suburbs (*proasteia*), the areas before the walls of the city, which are also called gateways." From this, the reading "before the city" <note>original: *propoleos*</note> in Suidas is confirmed, about which Kuster <note>Ludolf Kuster, a 17th-century scholar</note> was doubtful. — "It was a custom used by almost all nations in the past to bury the dead not in the city itself, but in suburban soil. See Chiflet in his explanation of the inscription of Geminia, a Mother of the Sacred Rites. Concerning the Athenians, Servius Sulpicius in a *Letter to Cicero* [4.12] says: 'I could not obtain from them a place for burial within the city (for Marcellus), because they said they were prevented by religion; nor indeed had they granted it to anyone before.'" — FABRICIUS <note>Johann Albert Fabricius, a renowned 18th-century scholar and original editor of this text</note>.

**P. 90. On Lycabettus**] Manuscripts C and T read "Kabettus." "Lycabettus is a very well-known mountain in Attica, mentioned in Aeschylus, Aristophanes, and others." — FABRICIUS. See also Meursius, *On the Peoples of Attica*.

**P. 90. Of Syrianus** <note>Syrianus was a Neoplatonist philosopher and the mentor of Proclus</note>] "Similarly, Polemon and his student and successor Crates were buried in a common tomb, as Diogenes Laertius testifies [4.21], citing an epigram by Antagoras, which is also read in the seventh book of the *Anthology* [and in Brunck’s *Analecta*, volume 1, page 260]." — FABRICIUS.

**P. 90. double**] "The word *Machpelah* <note>original Hebrew: *Makpelah*</note> in Genesis 23:9 was certainly something else; the Greek translators called it the 'double cave' <note>original: *spelaion diploun*</note>, or a cave which had a double opening and an entrance from two sides, and which, being 'double-mouthed' <note>original: *amphidymos*</note>, had a double gap. Salmasius, in his notes on Solinus [page 1208 D], notes that the size of that crypt was shown by this. But in this passage, Marinus mentions a 'double tomb' <note>original: *theken diplen*</note>, that is, a small burial place that was large enough to hold the bodies of two men." — FABRICIUS.

**P. 91. since at any time—lest it be contrary to what is fitting**] Manuscript T reads "since ever." Fabricius reads "lest it *the* contrary to what is fitting." I have deleted the first "the" <note>the Greek article *to*</note>, which does not appear in manuscripts C or T. Perhaps, so that nothing is lost, the word "something" <note>Greek: *ti*</note> should be substituted. Wyttenbach, in his *Critical Letter*, page 56, proposed "somewhere" <note>Greek: *pou*</note>. — Proclus feared that there might be something unseemly or lacking in dignity in this enclosing of two bodies in one tomb. To be sure, the ancients took the greatest care that "alien" <note>meaning belonging to others or outsiders</note> bodies were not placed in tombs destined for specific individuals—

It includes technical debates over Greek and Hebrew terms for "double" tombs and suburban locations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s 'Life of Proclus', specifically focusing on textual corrections and interpretations of funerary inscriptions and divine omens.</meta>

->NOTES ON MARINUS. 141<-

...and the inscriptions are full of threats and curses against those who, contrary to the wishes and commands of the deceased, might bring another person's body into their tombs.
      <note>original: "Λοιπὸν φυλάσσου, μή τιν’ ἐνθήσῃς τάφῳ"</note>
      *“Furthermore, take care that you do not place anyone else in the tomb,”*
says—to use only one example for now—that Crispinus, who calls himself in the <note>original: "ἐπιγρ. ἀδέσπ." (epigr. adesp.)</note> anonymous epigram 694, a <term>stolarch</term> <note>A commander of a naval fleet.</note> of the Pontic ships:
      <note>original: "Ἐξηρέτμοις πτέρυξιν ἐξηγαλμένων"</note>
      *“Exalted by wings made of oars.”*
In passing, I will say what I think about that last word: it seems to have come from the hand of the editor, not from the author himself. For Muratori, who is the source of the inscription, has *ēgaimenos*; I believe I can more easily and truly correct this corrupted word by reading *ēglaismenōn* <note>original: "ἠγλαϊσμένων" — meaning "glorified" or "radiant."</note>.

   **Page 91. “the same” [ton auton]** Fabricius and the manuscript C read <note>original: "τὸν αὐλόν"</note> *the flute*. This absurd reading gave birth to an absurd translation by Fabricius and his note regarding flutes, which I have refrained from describing. Nor did he see the sense in <note>original: "ἐνεθυμήθη"</note> *considered* or *pondered*. Wyttenbach, in his *Critical Epistle* (p. 56), proposed *ton auton* [the same], which I have adopted, and he translates it excellently: *Syrianus <note>Proclus's teacher and predecessor as head of the Academy.</note> himself appeared to him in a dream, rebuking him because he even dared to contemplate such a thing.* But later I noticed that manuscript T has *äylon* <note>original: "ἄϋλον" — meaning "immaterial" or "incorporeal."</note>, which is not without probability. Otherwise, whether using *auton* [him] or *äylon* [immaterial], something seems to be missing for the integrity of the passage. Perhaps: *it seemed he saw him (or his immaterial self) in a dream...*

   **Page 91. “threatening” [epapeilounta]** In Themistius’s 20th Oration (at the beginning), this word should be restored: *He is angry and threatens* (thus the Valesian Codex for the edited *threatens* <note>The note clarifies that the manuscript uses a stronger compound verb "epapeilei" rather than the simple "apeilei."</note>) *that he will do some terrible things to me.* Harduin wrote *to do*, imprudently changing Petavius's writing.

   **Page 91. “I became a Lycian” [genomēn Lykios]** Brunck, in his *Analecta*, published it as *Lycian I became*, giving no reasons for the change, as is his custom. Verse 4: Fabricius reads *Aite*; which I corrected from Brunck and the Codices. Fabricius reads *eeis*; Manuscripts C and T read *eeis* <note>different Greek spelling</note>; Brunck reads *eeis* <note>another spelling variant</note>.

   **Page 91. “divine signs” [diosēmeiai]** Compare the Scholia to Philostratus’s *Heroicus* (p. 526). Emperor Julian, in letter 27 (p. 399 D), says: *I was traveling from Litarbae (Codex 2755 reads Litarkōn) to Beroea, and Zeus signaled that everything was favorable ¹), showing a clear divine omen [diosēmia].*

***

   **1)** In an Aesopic Fable, which Rochefort provided in *Notice des Manuscrits* (vol. 2, p. 723), we read: <note>original: "ἀπαιώτερον τὸ ὑπὸ τῶν οἰκείων πάσχειν τι δεινὸν ἢ ὑπὸ τῶν ἀλλοτρίων"</note> *it is more "apaioteron" to suffer something terrible from one’s own people than from strangers.* Rochefort saw that the word *apaioteron* was corrupt but could not heal it. I read *apaisiōteron* <note>meaning "more ill-omened" or "more unlucky."</note>. [The same correction was proposed by the recent editor of Aesop, Heusinger (p. 149). — SCHAEFER.]

### 142 NOTES ON MARINUS.

(The Manuscript reads <term>divine sign</term><note>original Greek: "διοσημείαν" (diosēmeian)</note> following a correction); **"having stayed there for a day, I saw the Acropolis and sacrificed a white bull to Zeus in a kingly manner; I spoke briefly to the council about piety"**: The Manuscript [reads] <term>signs from the gods</term><note>original Greek: "θεοσημείας" (theosēmeias)</note>, which should be noted, and it seems to be a better reading.

**Page 92. during the day]** Thus the Manuscripts and Holsten’s edition in Dorville’s notes on *Chariton*, page 409. Fabricius, as is his habit, reads "after days." See above, page 68.

**Page 92. Darkness]** "Everywhere in ancient history such eclipses are recorded, where night seemed to stretch out during the day, and the moon appeared with the stars. See Herodotum, Book 7, chapter 37. See also Julius Obsequens <note>A 4th-century Roman author who compiled a list of prodigies and wonders.</note>, pages 32, 54, and 66 in Scheffer's edition. This specific eclipse mentioned by Marinus happened in the year 484 AD, on the 14th of January <note>original Latin: "XIX. Cal. Febr." — the 19th day before the Kalends of February</note> at sunrise." **FABRICIUS**. Compare Fabricius’s *Introduction*, and Burigny in the *Academy of Inscriptions*, volume 31, page 142.

**Page 92. of the first year]** "From this, I seem to rightly conclude that this biography was written and published by Marinus not long after Proclus's death; certainly not even a full year had passed after him, because he speaks of this eclipse as a future event." **FABRICIUS**.

**Page 92. signs]** "Pindar, addressing the sun being stolen away in the day <note>referring to a solar eclipse</note>:
'Do you bring a sign of some war, 
[or the withering of fruit], 
or the overwhelming strength of snow, 
or destructive rebellion, 
or the emptying of the sea upon the plain, 
or frost upon the earth, or a southern summer 
wet with raging water, 
or will you flood the land and make 
the race of young men a thing of 
lamentation from the start?' 
This passage of Pindar, which was excellently corrected by the most eminent Joseph Scaliger <note>A famous 16th-century French religious scholar and philologist.</note> in his notes on *Propertius*, Book 3 (elegy 4), escaped the notice of Jean Hardouin in his notes on Pliny, Book 2, chapter 12." **FABRICIUS**. Compare the note on Philostratus's *On Heroes*, page 526.

**Page 92. he says]** Who? Fabricius suggests "they say"; not a bad suggestion: for I remember that "he says" <note>Greek: φησὶ (phēsi)</note> often occurs in place of "someone says." Compare the notes of Morus and Weiske on Longinus, Chapter 2, Section 1, page 228. Synesius, in letter 79, page 227 D: "I do not know for what things I am paying such penalties, and if, as **he says**, we were previously objects of envy to any of the gods." The same author in letter 101, page 239 D: "But adorn, **he says**, the Sparta you were granted." <note>This is a reference to a famous Greek proverb meaning 'make the best of the lot you have been given.'</note> Here the name of the writer from whom the proverb was taken is withheld. Julian, in letter 29: "Syloson has returned," <note>Referring to the brother of the tyrant Polycrates of Samos; the phrase became a proverb for an unexpected return of an old power or person.</note>

<keywords>Marinus, Proclus, solar eclipse, 484 AD, Pindar, Fabricius, Joseph Scaliger, textual criticism, Greek proverbs, piety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Marinus’s biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus (412–485 AD). The author is examining specific word choices and textual variants found in different medieval manuscripts (Codices).</meta>

### NOTES ON MARINUS. 143

He says <note>original: "φησὶ"</note>, "to <note>Codex 2755 reads "towards"</note> Darius": this is, of course, Herodotus **speaking**. Plutarch, in his *Consolation* §. 34, writes: "she <note>Fate</note> is already calling her," **he says**, "to herself." Here Wyttenbach explains the phrase "he says"—which had bothered other learned men—as meaning, "as the poet says from whom this was taken." John Chrysostom, in *On Education* p. 25 (Combefis edition), writes: "A man," **he says**, "if he has long hair, it is a dishonor to him." It is Paul, of course, who **says** this in 1 Corinthians 11:14. Likewise, in the *Praise of Diodorus* near the beginning: "For he will not judge by appearance," **he says**, "nor will he convict by hearsay." This is Isaiah (11:3), whom **he says** <note>The author uses "he says" to introduce a quote without always naming the source, a common habit in ancient texts that this commentator is clarifying.</note>.

**Page 93. "to the one who wishes"** <note>original: "τῷ βουλομένῳ"</note>] I added the article from the Manuscripts. — Shortly after, instead of "those who had been associated with" <note>original: "συγγεγονότων"</note>, the reading in the Manuscripts, Fabricius had written "those associating with." — Then, "from many places."

**Page 93. "in summary"** <note>original: "ἐπὶ κεφαλαίων"</note>] Fabricius and edition G read "summarily" <note>original: "ἐπικεφαλαίων"</note> as one word. TC read "to a heading," which is more correct. Schaefer suggested it should be written as two words, adding this note: "Write it as 'in summary' <note>original: "ἐπὶ κεφαλαίων"</note>, meaning briefly. See Dionysius of Halicarnassus, Volume I, page 369 in Reiske's edition, and Volume V, page 386, compared with the note on Volume I, page 296." Compare also Matthaeus on Demosthenes’s *Funeral Oration* §. 3, page 97.

**Page 94. "On the Theaetetus"**] A lost work <note>Proclus wrote many commentaries on Plato's dialogues; this one on the *Theaetetus* has not survived to the modern era.</note>.

**Page 94. "they caused the Oracles and the Timaeus to be circulated"**] I added "the" <note>original: "τὸν"</note> before "Timaeus" based on the Manuscripts. Regarding the word "circulated" <note>original: "φέρεσθαι"</note> in this sense, see my note on Eunapius, page 157, and the Appendix to Bast’s *Letter*, page 33. Iamblichus, in *The Life of Pythagoras*, chapter 5, section 25, says: "of this man, even the writings on athletics are **circulated**." Cicero, in *Brutus* VII. 27: "before Pericles, of whom certain writings are **circulated**." Quintilian, in *Institutes*, preface 7: "two books on the art of rhetoric were already being **circulated** under my name." Aulus Gellius, book V, chapter 14, regarding Apion: "his books are **circulated** with no small fame." Otherwise, it has turned out very well for us that this ridiculous wish of Proclus went unfulfilled <note>Proclus famously claimed that if he were in power, he would destroy all ancient books except the *Chaldaean Oracles* and Plato's *Timaeus*, to prevent them from confusing the uneducated.</note>.

**Page 94. "Proclus — of the god"**] Edition G has only: "Proclus, or On Happiness." The Editor seems to have added the last parts from his own mind. Manuscripts C and T lack this entire closing statement. I have no notes on this from Manuscript L.

Manuscripts C and T place an epigram at the end of the *Life of Proclus*, which is stitched together from two published poems—one under Marinus's name, the other among the anonymous poems <note>original: "ἀδέσποτα"</note>, number 594. Here is the complete epigram as it appears in the Manuscripts:

->Always performing deeds pleasing to the immortal gods,<-
->Marinus also wrote these things with a pious mind.<-
->And this work is a surpassing tribute to your divine head,<-
->Blessed Proclus; because Marinus is a living image<-
->of all the immortals, a helper to pious mortals.<-

<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, philology, manuscripts, textual criticism, Plato's Timaeus, Chaldaean Oracles, epigram, Greek literature</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
144 NOTES ON MARINUS

In place of your sacred presence, you left a soul-saving protection, who, singing of your life that delighted the gods, wrote these memorials of your virtues for those yet to come. <note>original: "ἀντὶ τεῆς ἱερῆς κεφαλῆς ψυχοσσόον ἄλκαρ..." – This is a poetic tribute to the philosopher Proclus, written by his successor Marinus or a contemporary.</note>
**Commonly, people attribute the first two verses to Marinus, and the remaining ones to an anonymous author. But these eight verses proceed better and more aptly when joined together in this way, and I believe they should ultimately be attributed to a single author based on the authority of the Coislin and Turin <term>Codices: Hand-written manuscript volumes.</term>. The Turin manuscript prefixes the epigram with "Verse of Marinus";** <note>original: "στίχος Μαρίνου"</note> **and the Vatican Codex ascribes the name of Marinus to the last six verses. But I could never persuade myself that Marinus could have spoken such magnificent words about himself. Pride and boasting are indeed not absent from the character of philosophers—especially such <term>theurgists: People who practiced a form of "high magic" intended to invoke the gods.</term> and <term>thaumaturges: Miracle-workers or wonder-workers.</term>—but they at least put on a show of certain modesty, and their self-love is not without a sense of shame. They might feel such things about themselves, but they would appear far too ridiculous if they actually said them. I would suspect that the anonymous author was someone from the Platonic school, perhaps Isidore or Damascius.**

***

[The remainder of the page contains faint, illegible text bleeding through from the reverse side.]

**ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS.**

p. 3. l. 21. for "writers" <note>original: γραφέων (*graphéōn*)</note> substitute "those who are writing" <note>original: γραφόντων (*graphóntōn*)</note>.
p. 4. l. 6. Read "precursors" <note>original: πρόδρομα (*pródroma*)</note>.
p. 12. l. 15. Instead of "when — when" <note>original: ὅτε — ὅτε (*hote — hote*)</note> I intended to place "at one time — at another time" <note>original: ὁτὲ — ὁτὲ (*hotè — hotè*)</note>, as also on p. 15. l. 24.
p. 13. l. 17. "Argives" <note>original: Ἀργεῖοι (*Argeioi*)</note>. Read "Andrians" <note>original: Ἄνδριοι (*Andrioi*), referring to people from the island of Andros</note>.
p. 15. l. 23. "from which the separation of the soul occurs" <note>original: ἀφ' ὧν τὸ χωρίζεσθαι συμβαίνει τῇ ψυχῇ (*aph' hōn to chōrizesthai symbainei tēi psychēi*)</note>]
A Platonic expression. See Plato’s *Phaedo*, section 12. Nonnus in his *Historical Synopses* I. 16. regarding that famous Cleombrotus <note>Cleombrotus of Ambracia was a youth who reportedly jumped to his death after reading Plato's arguments on the immortality of the soul to reach the afterlife sooner</note>: "learning that the separation from the body is better for the soul, he threw himself from the wall and died; so that his soul, having departed, might indeed be separated from the body." Pseudo-Diogenes in an unpublished letter: "Let the migration from here be a concern to you; and it will be, if you would practice dying—that is, to separate the soul from the body while still living . . . . Practice philosophy and consider what death is to you (perhaps: *what death is not so much to you*), and what is according to nature and what is according to law; for in this alone is the soul separated from the body," etc. This letter of Diogenes is one of twenty-two other unpublished ones which I recited in a session of the French Institute <note>The Institut de France, a learned society in Paris</note>, accompanied by a dissertation in which I attempt to demonstrate the pseudonymity <term>pseudonymity: the practice of writing under a false name; here, the author argues these letters were not actually written by the philosopher Diogenes the Cynic</term> of the masked letter-writer. The text of those letters and the dissertation will eventually be found in the *Memoirs of the Institute*. Furthermore, the first words of this letter should be compared with Libanius, page 256 of Morel’s edition: "while living, one must practice philosophy and make life a memory of death." Where one should absolutely restore "practice" <note>original: μελέτην (*meletēn*)</note> from the *Rhodonia* of Chrysocephalus; the most learned editor left this reading in a note, though it was worthy of the main text itself.
p. 16. chapter 20. l. 2. Read "he was doing for the..." <note>original: ἐποίει τῷ (*epoiei tōi*)</note>.
p. 20. l. 8. Read "courage" <note>original: ἀνδρίαν (*andrian*)</note>.
p. 22. l. 12. "to him" <note>original: αὐτῷ (*autōi*) in the dative case</note>, which was left instead of "of him" <note>original: αὐτοῦ (*autou*) in the genitive case</note> as I intended, now pleases me more, and I very easily bear that this correction was neglected <note>The author is expressing that the "mistake" actually sounds better to him now than his original intended correction</note>.
p. 25. chapter 32. l. 4. Insert "him" <note>original: αὐτῷ (*autōi*)</note> after "to the one being at a loss" <note>original: ἀποροῦντι (*aporounti*)</note>.
p. 26. l. 9. Insert "and" <note>original: καὶ (*kai*)</note> before "the one having requested" <note>original: δεηθέντι (*deēthenti*)</note>.

146 ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS.

Page 26, chapter 33, line 2. Instead of "of Hermes" <note>original: *tōn Hermou*</note> I intended to have written "the [statue] of Hermes" <note>original: *ton tou Hermou*</note>. The matter is very slight and uncertain.

Page 66, line 5 from the bottom. I should have noted that "was well-prepared" <note>original: *eupareskeuasto*</note> is a reading belonging only to Fabricius <note>Johann Albert Fabricius (1668–1736), a prolific German classical scholar.</note> or his printer.

Page 67, line 19. Maximus of Tyre <note>A Greek rhetorician and philosopher of the 2nd century AD.</note>, Dissertation 24, section 3. "A twofold thing, one part attaining virtue, the other naturally joined with wickedness; people naming it with one voice 'Love' <note>original: *erōta*</note>, thus calling both the god and the disease [by the same name], they adorn themselves, etc." I would prefer to punctuate the text this way: "...naming it, thus calling [it] Love..."

Page 67, line 5 from the bottom. Basil, Bishop of Isauria, *Praise of Saint Stephen*, page 298 in the Combefis edition: "the clay-like body <note>A metaphor for the mortal human body.</note> had been dissolved according to the common law of nature."

Page 68, line 12. Schaefer <note>Gottfried Heinrich Schäfer (1764–1840), a German classical philologist.</note> has again advised on restoring the word "heat" <note>original: *kaumatōn*</note> in the notes to the Scholia of Apollonius of Rhodes, page 36, where "toilsome" <note>original: *kamatēron*</note> and "burning" <note>original: *kaumatēron*</note> are interchanged, just as here "of labors" <note>original: *kamatōn*</note> and "of heat" <note>original: *kaumatōn*</note> are confused.

Page 72, line 10. Add Plutarch, *Life of Philopoemen*, 6; Maximus of Tyre, Dissertation 26, section 5; Arrian, *Discourses of Epictetus*, book 1, chapter 10, section 1.

Page 72, line 22. Instead of page 63, read page 60.

Page 75, line 22. I should have written "having tried" <note>original: *peirathenti*</note>, so as to follow the manuscripts and remain consistent with my own choices: for in chapter 28, I preferred the form "he tried" <note>original: *epeirathē*</note>. Compare Wagner’s notes on Alciphron <note>A 2nd-century Greek author known for his fictional letters.</note>, book 2, chapter 3, section 14.

Page 81, line 15. Similarly, the letters *upsilon* [υ] and *iota* [ι] are interchanged. <note>In later Greek, these vowels began to be pronounced identically (like "ee"), leading scribes to frequently misspell words by swapping one for the other.</note> Maximus of Tyre, Dissertation 25, section 1: "nor anyone else of those trusted because of their ruling power." Davis <note>John Davies (1679–1732), an English classicist.</note> introduced a preposition from the manuscripts, with poor judgment; it should be deleted entirely. When the scribe, wanting to write "power" <note>original: *dynamin*</note>, had written the first syllable *dy* <note>original: *dy*</note>—which is pronounced with the same sound as the syllable *di* <note>original: *di*</note>—through a slip of the pen using an *iota* [ι], he immediately realized he had made a mistake. Not wanting to spoil the elegance of the manuscript by erasing the syllable *di*, he wrote "power" <note>original: *dynamin*</note> again. Another less learned scribe, copying this manuscript, did not notice that *di* here was to be completely ignored; by adding an *a* [alpha] to make it a preposition <note>Creating the Greek word *dia*, meaning "through" or "because of."</note>, he believed himself to be a wonderful critic. The same reason for interchange exists between *iota* [ι] and *epsilon-iota* [ει]. Alexis <note>A Greek comic poet.</note> in Athenaeus, book 1, section 38, is read thus, probably correctly emended:

"For I think this is one of the marks of the ill-bred:
to walk out of rhythm in the streets,
when one could walk gracefully; for which [grace] no one
ever exacts a fee from us, nor can one receive
honor from others by paying for it."

But the omission of the word "must" or "it is necessary" <note>original: *dei* or *chrē*</note> for the verb "to receive" seems to me to have something too harsh and rough here. The meaning [is] more easily...

**p. 91. l. 18.** Reinesius made the same error regarding Eunapius, p. 411 of my edition.

**p. 92. l. 32.** A fitting comparison is Iamblichus, *Exhortation to Philosophy* ch. 2. p. 20 of the good edition which we owe to Kiesling: *just as the lesser mysteries must be handed down before the greater ones, so must education precede philosophy.* <note>Original Greek: ὡς πρὸ τῶν μεγάλων μυστηρίων τὰ μικρὰ παραδοτέον, καὶ πρὸ φιλοσοφίας παιδείαν. This reflects the ancient view that general education (Paideia) was merely a preparation for the "higher" truth of philosophy.</note>

**p. 93. l. penultimate.** Read *Mind*. <note>Original Greek: Νοὸς (Noos).</note>

My friend Heiler, who is planning an edition of Julian, should look into this.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**page 116. line 30.** Read "I groan" <note>original: στένω</note>. — **line 31.** after "with birth-pangs" <note>original: ὠδῖσι</note> add: These things being established, I am of the opinion it should read:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**page 119. line 16.** Concerning "therefore" <note>original: οὖν</note> see Coray on Plutarch’s *Cato the Younger*, Chapter 32, Volume 5, page 330.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**page 123. line 8.** from the end. Add: see page 75 with the additions.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**page 137. line 8.** And under the word "to disturb" <note>original: θράττειν</note>, Suidas—as Toup points out in *Emendations*, Volume 1, page 292—presents the final words of this chapter: "so as not to (so written) be disturbed — of laments" <note>original: ὡς μὴ θράττεσθαι — θρήνων</note>. Toup [suggests] "dismissed..." <note>original: ἀπεμ-</note>

<meta>This page continues a series of scholarly corrections and additions (Addenda et Corrigenda) to a previously published work, likely a commentary on a Greek text. The author is refining definitions, correcting typos, and citing additional supporting evidence from other classical scholars.</meta>

->ADDITIONS AND CORRECTIONS. 149<-

The phrase <note>original: "φαινόντων θρήνων"</note> "appearing laments" he translates as *absurd and inconsistent dirges, disagreeing with one another*; which seems to be correct. Compare Schweighaeuser's Lexicon of Polybius under the entry for *Apemphainein* <note>meaning to be discordant or absurd</note>. Hesychius <note>an ancient Greek grammarian</note> writes: *Apemphainonta*: unsuitable; from the failure to show a likeness. On this point, Alberti—who could have cited the words of Suidas <note>a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>, "It is unsuitable; it is unlike"—preferred instead to bring forward similar examples from what he calls an "unpublished Lexicon" on Dionysius the Areopagite. But in this, he was mistaken. That Lexicon was included in Maximus's *Scholia* <note>ancient explanatory comments</note> on Dionysius the Areopagite, which G. Morellius printed in Paris in the year 1562. Page 137, line 31. Compare Schaefer on Aristophanes' *Plutus*, page 508.

**p. 138. l. 22.** Read *syllēdēn* <note>meaning "collectively" or "in short"</note>.

**p. 139. l. 24.** Compare also Fabricius, *Greek Library*, volume 9, page 364.

**p. 140. l. 6.** I utterly regret this attempt <note>the author is retracting a previous scholarly suggestion</note>, and *perikekophe* <note>meaning "has cut all around" or "mutilated"</note> seems to be the best reading.

**p. 140. l. 12.** Compare above, page 109. — **l. 22.** and Fischer in the index to Aeschines under the word *Lykabēttos* <note>a hill in Athens</note>.

**p. 142. l. 1.** The words *diosēmeia* and *diosēmia* <note>meaning "signs from Zeus" or "omens"</note> are frequently swapped. Coray <note>Adamantios Korais, a famous Greek scholar</note> on Plutarch's *Life of Cato the Younger*, chapter 42, prefers the spelling with the letter "i" <note>iota</note>; I would prefer the version with the diphthong <note>"ei"</note>, especially in prose writers.

**p. 142. l. 4.** The word *Theosebeias* <note>piety</note> arose from the similarity between the letter μ <note>mu</note> and the letter β <note>beta</note>, which Bast noted in his *Commentary on Paleography* <note>the study of ancient handwriting</note>, page 708. To this, add Bentley on Horace's *Satires*, Book II, satire 2, line 123. It was first written, or seemed to be written, as *theosemeias* <note>divine signs</note>, which by a poor correction was changed into *theosebeias* <note>piety</note>. Kuster's edition of the Suidas Lexicon explains *Diosēmia* <note>a sign from Zeus</note> as *theosemia* <note>a divine sign</note>.

**p. 142. l. 37.** Add Liebel on Archilochus, page 103.

**p. 143. l. 10.** What I said here regarding the word *phēsi* <note>"he says"</note>, I have mostly repeated, by a slip of memory, in my notes on Eunapius, page 418, where I discussed this expression again and brought forward other points besides. Meanwhile, compare Davies on Cicero's *Tusculan Disputations*, Book I, chapter 39. Aphthonius in the *Progymnasmata* <note>rhetorical exercises</note>, page 24, edition by Commelin: "Daphne," *he says*, "sprang from the Earth and Ladon." But here I think we should read *phasi* <note>"they say"</note>: for the discussion concerns poets in general.

<summary>This page contains technical philological corrections regarding Greek terminology, paleographic errors (such as confusing the letters mu and beta), and bibliographic updates for a scholarly edition.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, Greek linguistics, paleography, Hesychius, Suidas, Adamantios Korais, Dionysius the Areopagite, classical scholarship</keywords>

-># Front Cover<-

The volume is bound in an antique style, featuring a decorative cover of **marbled paper** <note>Marbling is a traditional method of aqueous surface design which can produce patterns similar to smooth marble or other kinds of stone.</note> with intricate, swirling patterns in dark brown and black over a lighter tan background. The edge of the cover is finished with a vertical strip of **worn, reddish-brown leather**, which constitutes part of the book's spine. 

There is no legible text present on this cover. <note>In historical bookbinding, the exterior covers were often left blank or decorated with patterns, with titles usually appearing on the spine or an internal title page.</note>
